The Kept Place

The Kept Place

Riarhin IX

Part I The protected guest

Chapter 1 The other copy

Margery had written the last part on the back.

Riarhin turned the sheet over before finishing the first page. There was still room beneath the last line there, enough for a name and a farewell. On the back the writing was larger.

She went back to the beginning.

Riarhin,

There are people here who will hear an unwelcome answer from Jaennis and no one else. I cannot tell you how much still depends upon that. Perhaps you know.

Mattie asked me to walk with her yesterday. We had arranged it in the morning. Jaennis had a bad afternoon and asked me to stay.

When I went upstairs, Mattie was taking off her walking shoes. She said she had forgotten too.

She had not forgotten.

I meant to speak to Jaennis afterward. She was pleased to see me. She put her hand against my face and asked why I looked so tired.

I stayed the night.

I love her. Please understand that before you answer me.

I want you to come. I am frightened of what you will think I am asking.

Margery

The carrier was waiting in the passage. He had been paid to wait for an answer, if she wished to send one, and had told the innkeeper so with a confidence that had already cost somebody a bowl of stew.

Riarhin laid the letter face down.

Across the table Coryd was repairing a strap. She had a needle between her teeth and both thumbs pressed against the leather. She looked up when Riarhin rose, then went back to the strap. There was a fire in the little parlour, though the afternoon was warm; somebody had brought in wet wood, and the room smelled of it.

Rumours from Valora had come with other travellers. A merchant had praised the dowager for persuading three quarrelling officers to accept the same payment day. His companion said her niece’s appointment had been promised, then quietly given to somebody who could live closer to the palace. Neither knew the terms of the appointment. Both agreed that the young queen was considerate beyond her years.

Riarhin had asked whether the roads were open. They were. She had asked nothing else.

She went to the door and told the carrier there would be an answer in the morning.

“I’m meant to leave tonight.”

“Then go.”

He glanced at the letter behind her. “I could wait.”

“For the morning rate?”

He admitted that there was one. Riarhin paid it. He went downstairs before she changed her mind.

Coryd took the needle from her mouth.

“Bad news?”

“I don’t know.”

“Who is it?”

“Margery.”

She waited for something more. Riarhin sat down.

“She took care of the child. Afterward.”

“Matylde.”

“Yes.”

Coryd put down the strap. “What does she want?”

“For me to come.”

Riarhin turned the sheet over again. Margery had once held her wrist beside a window, asking leave to remain. Riarhin had worn Bea’s face. Later she had sent Saikou to bring Margery to a child whose parents she was going to kill. She could remember every word she had used to explain why Margery was the right person. She had not asked Margery.

“Do you want to?” Coryd asked.

“No.”

“Have you told her that?”

“Not yet.”

Coryd picked up the strap, then set it down again.

“Are you going to?”

Riarhin rubbed the crease in the letter with her thumb. On the journey she had kept Edrune’s petition in the same pocket as the replies from the provincial office. She had supplied an account; she had offered money for the storage. Each answer had come back to the same point. There were questions she must attend to answer, and no protection under which she could safely attend.

She took those papers out now.

Coryd looked from them to Margery’s letter. “Are they asking for the same thing?”

“No.”

“Then answer both.”

Riarhin found a clean sheet. She began by saying that she could not come without public protection. She read the sentence, left it where it was and wrote beneath it that the queen might grant a hearing for the northern households. There would have to be a public undertaking, authenticated by people with something to lose if it failed. She would ask Edrune’s guarantor to pursue it. Margery was not to promise anything in Matylde’s name.

She stopped before signing.

I will not come to kill her, she wrote.

She watched the ink settle into the paper. Then she added, I cannot promise you will like what I say.

In the morning she gave the carrier the sealed answer and another letter for the northern guarantor. He asked which should go first. She gave him the money for a second carrier.

It took weeks to bring back the queen’s seal.

Edrune had brought her own copy of the promise.

She laid it beside Riarhin’s on the posting-house table, between a bowl of boiled eggs and a wet place left by somebody’s cup. The seals differed. The words did not.

Riarhin read the first paragraph again.

Outside, a boy was striking a wheel rim with a wooden mallet. He had been striking it for some time without producing any change that she could hear. A man told him to stop. The boy struck it once more, lightly, and stopped.

“You thought I might alter it?” Riarhin asked.

“I thought you might remember the part that interested you.”

Coryd broke an egg against the table. She looked at Edrune, then at her sister, and began removing the shell.

The carter had a broad face reddened by travel and a narrow white mark beside one eye. She wore her hair wound in a cloth at the back of her head. The cloth had once been yellow. Her coat hung over the chair behind her, drying at the shoulders and still wet at the hem, and beneath it she wore a waistcoat with one fastening replaced by a knotted cord. She kept touching the cord when she spoke, testing whether it held.

“I remember all of it,” Riarhin said.

“Yes. That was what they told me.”

Edrune moved the bowl out of the way. A little salt fell from its rim onto the queen’s name. She brushed it off with the back of a finger.

“Then you remember we haven’t asked her to say they were all innocent.”

Riarhin looked at the two documents. The protection named her, described the hearing and allowed sixty days from the first presentation to a royal officer. There were ten further days to leave by the appointed road once the hearing closed. The old accusations remained. New offences would receive no protection from the instrument.

Under the queen’s seal, three men had witnessed the undertaking. One represented the northern petitioners. Another had guaranteed a portion of the money presently keeping the Crown’s soldiers fed. None had been chosen because he liked her.

“I know what you asked.”

“Then don’t improve it when they let you speak.”

The boy outside resumed work. This time somebody was holding the rim for him. The sound changed.

Edrune’s timber had been cut in the north by men whose permission to sell it had subsequently been questioned. By then she had contracted to carry it south. The river yard below Valora would not release it; the man collecting storage had written three times to tell her that the charges continued. A fourth letter suggested that a local buyer might relieve her of the difficulty. The buyer was related to the man who had signed the hold.

Some of the timber belonged to households examined after the killings. Some belonged to a woman who had married into one of them six years later. Nobody disputed which trees she had owned before her marriage. The yard keeper said the order made no distinction.

“I can pay the storage,” Riarhin said.

Edrune picked up an egg and put it down again.

“You could have sent that.”

“I offered to.”

“You did. We asked for something else.”

There was a child asleep across two chairs near the hearth. A carter lifted her carefully while his wife gathered the blankets. The child woke and began asking whether they were leaving. Her father said soon. She asked again before he had finished saying it.

Riarhin folded her copy along the existing creases. She had received the summons beside a window looking onto a yard where Harissa was arguing with a woman who claimed that a repaired hem had never fitted properly. She had read it twice while the argument continued.

Now she put the document inside her coat.

“Tell me which account you don’t want me to improve.”

Edrune drew a second folded sheet from beneath the first.

“My cousin. He gave the knight a horse. He knew there was trouble. He knew the knight was riding without the Duke’s permission. Afterwards he said he hadn’t known either thing. We aren’t asking the queen to believe that.”

“What are you asking?”

“That his daughter can sell the trees she planted with her mother. That somebody hears what he did before deciding what she may do.”

Riarhin held out her hand for the sheet. Edrune did not immediately give it to her.

“The knight was Berent,” Riarhin said.

“I know his name.”

She surrendered the paper.

Coryd set half the peeled egg beside Riarhin’s hand. Riarhin ate it while reading. The yolk was warm and dry; she had to reach for the water. At the bottom of the sheet, in a different hand, somebody had supplied the daughter’s age when the horse was lent.

She had been two.

Edrune took the paper back when Riarhin had finished.

“She can write her own name now,” she said. “She wrote to the keeper. He returned the letter to her father because he said she wasn’t old enough to enter a contract. Her father wrote. They told him he was under restriction.”

“Who planted the trees?” Coryd asked.

“The mother planted the first rows. The girl helped when she could carry a sapling without dragging it. They aren’t all ready to cut. Only the older stand.”

“Then she hasn’t lost all of them.”

“Not yet.”

Edrune held the paper near the light, checking whether the damp had reached it. Riarhin had a sudden image of the girl, whom she had never met, being told by several adults that she still had a great deal to be grateful for.

“Is her father coming?”

“He can’t leave the holding without leave. The leave is one of the things we’re asking about.”

“Her mother?”

“Keeping the holding going.”

“So you came.”

“I own the carts.”

Edrune looked at her for a moment, then relented enough to add, “And she’s my cousin’s child. I would like to see her enjoy something before she’s asked what it cost.”

Coryd wiped egg from her thumb. Outside, the wheel rim rang sharply and the boy cried out. All three women turned. The man with him said, too loudly, that this was why he had been told to keep his hand out. There was a pause, then a softer voice asking to see the finger.

Riarhin pushed her chair back.

Edrune caught the edge of the documents before the movement could drag them from the table.

“Someone is with him.”

Riarhin sat again.

Coryd watched her with the infuriating patience her family had acquired. A year earlier Riarhin would have gone out before anybody could advise her. A great many years earlier she would have remained seated without turning. She could not identify the point between those habits at which she might simply finish an egg.

Edrune counted the pages, tapped them straight and wrapped them in a piece of oiled cloth.

“Did they ask you to come before we did?”

“The summons came after your letter.”

“That wasn’t my question.”

“No.”

“But somebody was thinking of it.”

“Margery asked me to come. Jaennis supported the summons.”

“Jaennis is the queen’s grandmother?”

“By marriage. Not by blood.”

Edrune considered the distinction and dismissed it with a slight movement of her hand. “Will she help?”

“I don’t know.”

“Will you ask?”

“If asking will help the petition.”

“That sounds like a person saving room to do something else.”

Riarhin looked across the table. “I have other people to see. I won’t put your petition aside for them.”

“Then find out after the clerk has our names.”

Coryd laughed, unexpectedly and with pleasure. Edrune looked at her, uncertain whether she had been mocked, and Coryd shook her head.

“I’ve been trying that tone for months.”

“Does it work?”

“Occasionally.”

Riarhin took the remaining half egg. “I’m here.”

“Yes,” Edrune said, and for the first time her voice softened. “You are.”

They settled the day’s road. Edrune’s carts would move slower than the riders once they reached the better surface. She preferred that they remain together until the first royal checkpoint. Riarhin agreed. Coryd asked whether this meant they could leave before somebody bought all the bread. The innkeeper, passing with a tray, said that he had more in the oven and that no guest of his need frighten herself about bread.

His wife, behind him, raised three fingers at Coryd. Three loaves. Coryd went to buy two.

The checkpoint stood where the road divided around a toll house and joined again beyond a cattle pen. Water lay in the hoofmarks. Two drovers were disputing whether a calf carried in a wagon counted as a separate animal, and a woman with a basket of hens was waiting for both to stop so that somebody could attend to her.

Riarhin dismounted before reaching the bar.

The officer took the protection without looking at her. He had been listening to the drovers. At the second line he looked up.

“Your name.”

“Riarhin.”

He looked at her ears, then at the sword.

“Stay there.”

Coryd’s horse moved its head towards a hen protruding from the basket. Coryd pulled it away. The hen made a furious, private sound.

A notice was nailed beneath the toll house’s little roof. Rain had reached the lower part and carried the ink into dark feathers. The top was legible. Riarhin had seen copies of it years earlier, sometimes with a poor drawing, sometimes without one. This had no drawing. It named the woman called Riarhin and anyone knowingly giving her shelter.

The officer came out with a clerk.

He read the protection aloud.

The drovers stopped arguing. The woman with the hens moved her basket to the other hip. Riarhin kept her hands clear of her belt. The effort of standing still in that particular way made her conscious of every person within reach.

When the officer reached the departure provision, the clerk interrupted to ask whether the sixty days began today or from the date of signing. They consulted a sentence near the foot of the page. The officer read it twice.

“Today,” he said.

The clerk wrote the date on the back of the retained copy.

“You’ll receive an escort at the next station.”

“I was told it would be here.”

“They were told the next. I’ll send someone.”

“I’ll wait.”

He studied her.

“You may pass.”

“I understand. I’ll wait for the escort.”

Edrune had remained beside her cart, one hand laid on the horse’s shoulder. She nodded once when Riarhin looked towards her.

The clerk wanted to put up the new notice. There was no space beneath the roof except that occupied by the reward. He asked the officer what to do.

“Take the old one down.”

“Shall I discard it?”

“No. Bring it inside.”

He worked the nails out with a knife. Riarhin watched him fold the paper carefully with the writing inward. The woman with the hens asked whether, now that they had finished with whatever this was, somebody would count her birds.

Chapter 2 Fields outside the city

They approached Valora between fields still showing the pale stubble of the harvest. In the lower ground, men were clearing a drainage ditch. The wet earth came up in black slabs, and each slab made a sound Riarhin could hear from the road. A child was carrying water to the workers in a pail too large for her. She stopped every few paces and changed hands.

Their escort had three horses and four men. The youngest rode behind another soldier with his knees spread awkwardly around the saddle. At the first halt they quarrelled about which of them had eaten onions.

“It’s the horse,” the younger said.

“The horse has better manners.”

“The horse won’t lend me its place.”

Their leader let them continue until the talk became a contest about whose wife would recognize the smell. Then he told both to get down and walk beside the animals.

He had given his name as Kerros. He wore a plain cloak over serviceable armour, and the leather at his wrists had been repaired by someone who cared more for its strength than its appearance. When Riarhin offered her hand, he examined the letter she held in the other one.

After a moment she lowered it.

At noon they ate in the yard of an inn whose rooms were occupied by a wedding party. A bridegroom’s uncle had fallen asleep on a bench with a sprig of green tied to his coat. Every person who passed added another piece to it. By the time he woke, he looked as though he had been left under a hedge all summer.

The landlord apologized for the noise and brought them stewed beans, bread and a bowl of sour plums. Kerros would not sit with Riarhin. He took the corner near the gate, from which he could see the road.

Coryd watched him go.

“He could manage to be civil.”

“He is.”

“He hasn’t spoken six words to you.”

“He read the order. He’s keeping it.”

Coryd tore her bread. “You needn’t defend him to me.”

“Then don’t make me.”

Her sister looked up sharply. Riarhin put the plum she had taken back in the bowl. They sat in silence while a woman came out to shake a cloth from the wedding room. Crumbs landed near Coryd’s boot.

“I wasn’t asking for a quarrel,” Coryd said.

“I know.”

“Do you?”

Riarhin rubbed the salt from her fingers.

“He served under Tylan.”

“He told you?”

“I recognized the clasp. He saw me recognize it.”

Across the yard, the uncle woke to laughter and began discovering the decorations on his coat. He pulled off a trailing branch and used it to threaten a young man who had not been responsible for that particular addition.

Coryd ate another mouthful of bread.

“You might have said.”

“I might.”

A man at the adjoining table had heard enough of their escort’s conversation to identify a former regiment. He asked whether the men had received their last payment.

“Some of it,” Kerros said.

“All of the last one. Half of the one before.”

“Then you’ve done better than we did.”

The man laughed. He had one stiff hand and held his cup against his chest while he drank. His daughter stood behind his bench, winding and unwinding the end of a ribbon.

“Lady Jaennis found the rest,” he said. “My brother said we’d never see it. Said we’d spent it twice already in our heads. We paid the roof man.”

“How is the roof?” Riarhin asked.

The man looked at her for the first time.

“Still up.”

“Then he was worth paying.”

“He thinks so. Came back in the rain to tell me.”

His daughter laughed into her hand.

There had been meetings, the man said, and an officer who travelled from house to house explaining which promises had security behind them and which did not. One provincial lord had refused to sign until his own retainers were included. Another wanted an old toll restored. The man could recite the sums due to his company but did not know what the toll had been called.

“Wouldn’t have got it without her,” he said. “The little lady. She listened.”

Riarhin nodded.

He asked whether they were going to the city. Before she could answer, Kerros rose and said it was time to move.

They were still fastening the bags when the bride came into the yard.

She was a broad-faced woman in a good blue gown, furious with someone behind her. A man followed carrying a chair. Another came with a little stringed instrument wrapped in a cloth. The bride told him to stop being so careful with it and play something before everyone went home sober.

“Your father says—” the man with the chair began.

“My father can come out here and say it.”

He did. For several moments three people spoke at once. Riarhin gathered that the house had been promised to the couple after the wedding, that the promise included the large front room and that the father had just remembered he liked sitting in it. The bridegroom remained at the door with his mouth open, looking from his wife to the chair as though the trouble might be located in its construction.

The woman who had been shaking crumbs over Coryd’s boots went to him and spoke into his ear. He nodded. She spoke again. He went inside and returned with two cups.

“Sit with us tonight,” he told the father. “In the morning we’ll carry your chair where you want it.”

The father said he had no objection to the house. Nobody had suggested he did. The bride looked at her husband.

“Not in our room,” she said.

The bridegroom went red. His father laughed; so did everyone who had been pretending not to listen. The instrument was unwrapped. A tune began uncertainly and then found its feet.

Coryd leaned her elbows on the gate. “We could stay another quarter-hour.”

Kerros said they could not.

“The horses aren’t ready.”

“They will be.”

He turned away. Coryd stayed at the gate until the youngest escort came to help her with the bridle. He had drawn the short straw again and was walking the first stretch. She offered to let him ride behind her.

His eyes went to Kerros.

“I have to ask.”

“Then ask.”

He did. Kerros studied Coryd’s horse, Coryd and the young man, then said they could exchange mounts. The youngest could have the officer’s spare saddle for the next leg, while Kerros rode behind his corporal.

“You don’t have to do that,” Riarhin said.

“I wasn’t speaking to you.”

She shut her mouth. The men made the change with practised speed. Kerros took the rear place without complaint, though his knees looked as uncomfortable as the younger man’s had done. Coryd thanked him. He nodded to her.

At the last moment the bride came over with a cloth-wrapped parcel. She held it out to Coryd.

“You’ll need something sweet after that road.”

Coryd accepted with obvious pleasure. “Thank you. And congratulations.”

The woman’s eyes moved to Riarhin. Recognition did not come at once. Her husband appeared behind her. He recognised the escort badge first, then Riarhin.

He put his hand on his wife’s elbow.

“Come back.”

She looked at him. He shook his head once.

Coryd held the parcel between her hands. She began to offer it back. The bride turned, saw the movement and frowned.

“I gave you that.”

“Yes.”

“Then don’t stand there with it.”

She went back to the music, taking her husband with her by the wrist. Riarhin could hear them speaking as they reached the door. His voice was low. Hers was not. The words were lost beneath the tune.

On the road, Coryd opened the parcel. It contained five little honey cakes, misshapen and sticky at the edges. She ate one, offered one to Riarhin and asked Kerros whether the others might be divided among his men.

“After the next halt.”

“They’ll know you have them.”

“They know a good many things they don’t immediately get.”

The youngest rider laughed. The corporal carrying Kerros told him he could be put back on the road as readily as he had been taken off it. They began a dispute about whose horse had suffered more from the arrangement.

Riarhin ate the cake. There was a piece of nutshell in it. She took it out and held it on her palm until they passed a ditch.

The city entrance had a lane for carts and a narrower way for riders. Edrune had gone ahead with the slow freight, but they found her still waiting beside a wagon whose owner insisted that the load was personal baggage. It included six new stools, three chests and a wrapped mirror. The officer collecting the entry dues had evidently heard the claim before.

Riarhin was taken through the narrow way. A man on the wall called down her name. The men below had already seen the protection, but the call turned heads in the cart queue.

A child asked his mother who she was. The mother pulled him against her skirt.

The mirror in the wagon showed Riarhin a narrow piece of herself: one eye, the edge of an ear, the brown shoulder of her coat. The cloth slipped when the carter shifted his weight, and the piece widened. She looked away.

A woman left the queue and approached Kerros. She carried a basket of folded linen on her head, steadying it with one hand.

“Is the queen going to hear us too?”

“Who?”

“Those of us with petitions.”

“Take yours to the office named on the notice.”

“I did.”

“Then you’ll have a day.”

“I had a day. They moved it.”

Kerros looked towards the gate officer, who came down to speak with her. The woman refused to leave until he told her whether the new notice concerned all petitioners or only the northern houses. He read the heading aloud. Only the named houses.

“Thought so,” she said.

Riarhin almost asked what the woman’s petition concerned. The woman had already turned her back. The basket swayed once and steadied beneath her hand. She resumed her place beside the wagon with the mirror.

Kerros waited for the clerk to return the pass. Riarhin watched a narrow boy push a handcart between two wheels with barely an inch to spare on either side. He neither looked at her nor stopped whistling.

When the pass came, she put it inside her coat before mounting. The gate officer kept his eyes on the seal until the paper disappeared.

Their lodging stood on a busy street beyond the palace’s outer precinct. It had two stairs, a yard occupied by somebody else’s laundry, and a rear door whose lock had been replaced so recently that the wood around it remained pale.

Dame Solenne was waiting in the front room.

She rose when they entered. She had a square jaw, dark hair cut above the collar and an ink mark on the side of one hand. Her coat fitted her shoulders properly and had no ornament that could catch on a sword hilt.

“I’ll show you the rooms first,” she said. “Then we’ll discuss the arrangements.”

Riarhin would have preferred to reverse the order. She followed her upstairs.

Two rooms opened onto a short passage. Solenne indicated which belonged to Riarhin and which to Coryd. A guard would occupy the chair at the stairhead, with relief every four hours. Visitors were to give their names below. The rear door was for fire and household work. They were not forbidden to use it, but the guard must be told.

Coryd looked into her room.

“The bed’s narrow.”

“Yes.”

“Is it meant for a person?”

“The previous guest managed.”

“Did you fold him first?”

Solenne almost smiled.

“I can ask for another mattress.”

“Ask for a shorter guest.”

Downstairs, the officer laid out the conditions of palace attendance. Riarhin would arrive by the eastern entrance, present the pass issued for that day and surrender the sword until she left. Small knives were also to be declared. Coryd would have separate permission if summoned; her relationship to Riarhin was not an admission in itself.

Riarhin put Remorse on the table, still in its scabbard.

“Who keeps it?”

“The officer at the inner door.”

“I want a name.”

“You’ll get one at each attendance.”

“And a mark I can recognize on the binding.”

“You may make it yourself.”

Solenne waited while she considered the rest.

“Are these arrangements yours?” Riarhin asked.

“They are issued through my office.”

“That wasn’t what I asked.”

“The queen approved them. Lady Jaennis suggested another lodging. Her own household.”

“And you?”

“I thought this less difficult to explain.”

Riarhin looked around the front room. A picture had left a darker rectangle on one wall. Somebody had removed it before she arrived.

“I agree.”

Solenne noted the acceptance.

When she had gone, Coryd opened the bundle containing Riarhin’s good coat. She held it up by the shoulders.

“This?”

“Yes.”

“You’ll look like somebody attending a burial.”

Riarhin took it from her and shook out the sleeves.

“I won’t look like somebody returning to a party.”

Their first visitor arrived before the cook had finished telling them which shelves they might use.

He was a young man from a family Riarhin did not know. He introduced himself through the half-open door, bowing to both women and then to the guard, as if uncertain whose permission mattered most. His mother wished to offer them supper. She had heard of Riarhin’s arrival and would be honoured to receive her privately, away from the difficulties of court.

Riarhin waited for him to name the mother.

He did. The guard wrote it down. Riarhin had heard the family named in connection with a grant of land after the northern campaigns. She remembered no more than that. The young man watched her face for recognition and supplied several details which did not help.

“Did your mother know me before?”

“She knew of you.”

“As Aveline?”

His ears reddened.

The cook appeared behind Riarhin carrying a pot. He moved to let her pass and nearly stepped into the guard’s chair.

“My mother thought you might prefer not to be obliged to explain yourself at every table.”

“That was considerate.”

“Then I may tell her—”

“Tell her I must decline.”

His face fell. He unfolded the invitation and checked it again.

“I have come to attend the queen,” she said. “I shall have limited time.”

“Another evening?”

“Not during this visit.”

He bowed and left with the careful pace of someone refusing to hurry from an embarrassment.

Coryd waited until the guard closed the door.

“Was there something wrong with him?”

“No.”

“With his mother?”

“I don’t know.”

“Then—”

“She would have received me quietly. Other people would have heard. They would wonder what I offered her which I hadn’t offered them.”

“Would you have offered anything?”

“Not necessarily.”

Coryd folded her arms. Riarhin rubbed her forehead.

“I am trying not to begin that way.”

Her sister looked towards the window. “We shall have a great many evenings in these two rooms.”

“You can go.”

“She didn’t ask me. She asked your sister.”

Riarhin sat on the bed. It creaked under her weight.

“I know.”

“I’m not saying you should have accepted. I’m saying I have to live beside it when you refuse.”

Downstairs the cook called to ask whether either of them disliked onions. Coryd called back that one of them was presently determined to dislike everything, but that onions might be tried.

Riarhin took a pillow from the bed and threw it. Coryd caught it against her chest, laughing, then held it out of reach when Riarhin asked for it back. The guard outside cleared his throat.

“Nothing,” Coryd told him. “A small domestic victory.”

She returned the pillow and went down to help with the plates.

Margery came the next morning, before the officer arrived for Riarhin.

The guard announced her properly. She gave her name and waited while he went upstairs; Riarhin heard her thank the cook for a chair. By the time Riarhin came down, Margery had taken off her gloves and was turning them inside out to dry a damp place at the fingers.

She stood.

Riarhin had prepared to greet an older woman. She had not prepared for the familiar way Margery put out her hand, stopped short of touching and waited.

Riarhin took it. Margery’s eyes filled. She looked annoyed with herself and let go before either of them had to decide whether there would be an embrace.

“Thank you for coming.”

“The hearing—”

“Yes. I know.”

They sat at the kitchen table. Coryd came down, was introduced, and took her breakfast into the yard. The guard remained in the passage, near enough to be called. Margery watched Riarhin notice where he stood.

“They have made this difficult for you.”

“It ought to be difficult.”

Margery bent a finger of the glove back into place.

“Mattie was glad about the petition. She asked to read the earlier accounts herself.”

“Does she know you wrote?”

“She knows I asked you to attend. I told her I wanted you to answer her too.”

“Has she seen your letter?”

“No.”

Riarhin waited.

“Neither has Jaennis.”

In the yard Coryd asked the cook where to put the bowl. The cook told her to bring it through the kitchen like anyone else. Margery smiled at the voice, then looked back at Riarhin.

“I wrote on a bad morning.”

“Was any of it wrong?”

“No. But there are other mornings.”

She laid the gloves together, smoothing one over the other.

“She took Mattie out the following day. They came back with their shoes ruined. Both of them. Jaennis had to have her knee wrapped and sat telling everyone it had been worth it.”

“That sounds like a good day.”

“It was.”

Riarhin could not tell whether Margery wanted her to ask another question or stop asking altogether. She poured water into the cup the cook had left. Margery accepted it and drank too quickly.

“How long?” Riarhin asked.

Margery looked up.

“You and Jaennis.”

“Years. It didn’t begin all at once.” She touched the cup’s wet rim. “At first we talked about Veralyn. Nobody else would talk about the things I remembered. They wanted the queen, or they wanted the monster. Jaennis remembered a woman who couldn’t leave a loose buckle alone. She would do it up while she was arguing with you.”

Riarhin remembered that too. She kept the memory to herself.

“Then we had other things to talk about,” Margery said. “I liked making her laugh.”

Her mouth had softened. Riarhin waited.

“I haven’t come to take that away from you.”

“You can’t know what you will take away.”

“No.”

Margery looked towards the open door. “I’d forgotten how you do that.”

“What?”

“Leave me to hear what I’ve said.”

Riarhin reached for her own cup and found it empty. She filled it rather than answer.

“I don’t want Mattie questioned about every afternoon she spends with her,” Margery said. “Or everyone watching to see whether she is sufficiently unhappy. She likes being there.”

“I’ll be speaking to her about her parents.”

“I know.”

“You asked me to come.”

Margery’s eyes rose to hers. “Yes. I did.”

Neither moved until the escort knocked at the outer door. Margery put on her gloves. One finger remained turned in; she struggled with it, pulled the glove off and carried both in her hand.

At the door she stopped.

“Don’t let my letter be the first thing you tell her about us.”

“It won’t be.”

Margery thanked her again. This time Riarhin could not answer before she had gone.

Chapter 3 What the settlement bought

The man who received Remorse put a cloth beneath it before laying it on the shelf.

Riarhin watched him write his name on the slip. She made her own mark across the tie and asked him to keep the small knife separately. He did so without comment.

At the turn of the passage she looked back. He was already attending to somebody else’s cloak.

The palace smelled of warm wax, floor soap and bread. A boy was carrying a basket of rolled napkins towards the kitchens. He moved aside when he saw Solenne and waited with his shoulder against the wall. His attention remained on the basket; one roll had begun to unwind and threatened to fall.

Riarhin remembered another boy and another basket. She passed this one without speaking.

The working audience was held in a room smaller than she expected. There were chairs around a long table, but no raised seat. The queen’s place had a cushion laid on it, which she moved aside before sitting down.

Matylde wore green. The colour made Riarhin’s stomach tighten before she saw that it was a different green, pale where the window lit the sleeve. The girl had her father’s shape around the eyes. She was taller than the image Riarhin had permitted herself to retain.

Jaennis sat beside her, small and neat in a dark gown. She had aged. The change was not in one feature; it lay in the care with which she turned her head, the pause before reaching for something placed beyond her hand. When she saw Riarhin, her expression became familiar enough to hurt.

Fayd stood near the far window.

He greeted her as though they had last met at an inconvenient dinner.

“Your journey was manageable?”

“Yes.”

“Good.”

He returned to the paper in his hand.

Riarhin bowed to Matylde. She had practiced how far, and still felt the motion become too deliberate. The queen told her to rise, waited until she had done so, and asked whether the protection had been honoured on the road.

“It has, Your Majesty.”

“Any difficulty?”

“None requiring a change.”

Matylde looked towards Solenne.

“The station escorts had different instructions about where to meet,” Solenne said. “It was corrected.”

“Then correct the copies.”

“I have.”

The girl nodded. For a moment she seemed about to say something more, then glanced at Jaennis and took up the next paper.

A councillor introduced the preliminary business. The petitioners’ accounts would be heard in five days. Riarhin’s statement must be delivered beforehand in a form distinguishing what she had ordered from what she had subsequently learned. There would be questions. She was not to approach witnesses privately to settle their answers.

“I haven’t,” Riarhin said.

“I am telling you what is required.”

She inclined her head.

The councillor turned to a payment that had been withheld from a discharged soldier. Fayd objected to the proposed means of releasing it. The provincial guarantee covered service within a named company; this man had been transferred before discharge, and extending the guarantee would invite claims on behalf of others.

“He was transferred because he could no longer ride,” Jaennis said.

“The reason isn’t disputed.”

“Nor is the service.”

“The money pledged to one service cannot simply be called another.”

Jaennis rested her hand on the table.

“His former captain will attest that the injury occurred before the transfer. The guarantor has accepted that distinction in two other cases. Shall I send to him?”

“You may,” Fayd said. “Or the office responsible could make the same request.”

“It did. Six weeks ago.”

The councillor began to explain the delay. Matylde interrupted.

“Is the man still waiting here?”

Everyone looked at her.

“He has been obliged to remain available,” the councillor said.

“Where?”

“In lodgings near the Hall.”

“Who is paying?”

The councillor consulted his paper, though the answer was evidently not on it.

Jaennis touched the edge of Matylde’s sleeve.

“A good question.”

Matylde looked pleased. Riarhin saw her lose the next words while receiving the praise.

“Ask,” the girl said at last. “And send the first request again. From the office. With the other two cases attached.”

Fayd bowed his head. Jaennis smiled.

The next paper concerned a road crew whose wages had been assigned to the same provincial account. The councillor began reading. Matylde let him reach the first sum, then asked him to stop.

“If the man is waiting here, can we see him?”

“The discharged soldier?”

“Yes.”

“He hasn’t been summoned.”

“Someone can summon him.”

Jaennis said, “He may find it difficult to come at short notice. We could have him brought tomorrow.”

“Then tomorrow. But tell him what we have decided now.”

Fayd moved towards the clerk. “I can send—”

“From the office,” Matylde said.

He paused. “From the office.”

Riarhin watched him take his hand off the back of the clerk’s chair. The clerk looked first at him, then at the queen, then wrote the instruction in full before making the shorter copy for the messenger.

The work went on. A damaged causeway required stone from a quarry whose owner claimed exemption from royal calls. Nobody disputed that there had been an exemption. They disputed whether it applied to supplying stone, transporting it, or both. The road crew was sitting idle while the correspondence travelled between two houses whose owners could have met in a morning.

Matylde wanted them brought together. The councillor explained that one was attending a family burial.

“After the burial.”

“He is likely to remain with his sister.”

“For how long?”

“That has not been specified.”

The girl closed her eyes for a moment. “Then ask him.”

Riarhin could remember men waiting with tools while an order caught up with the work it was meant to permit. There was a winter road in her memory, mud closing over a boot almost as fast as she lifted it. She had once settled such an argument by taking the carts and leaving the owner to complain afterwards. Someone had still had to answer the complaint. She had ridden on before learning who.

Matylde asked what would happen if the repairs waited another month.

“The road will remain passable to light traffic.”

“And carts?”

“By the other crossing.”

“How far?”

“An additional half-day.”

The queen looked at the sums again.

“Include that. When you ask him to come. Tell him what every load is doing while he waits to decide.”

Jaennis reached for the paper. “I know his sister. A note from her may be received more kindly.”

“That would help,” the councillor said at once.

Matylde watched the paper pass between them. She did not object. Riarhin saw her glance at Fayd, who had resumed his place by the window. He gave no sign of having seen.

Before the audience ended, a messenger returned about the soldier. The man had paid for his lodging by doing light work in the stable. He had fallen behind because his hand had become infected again. The innkeeper was not threatening to turn him out, but wanted someone to say who would meet the account.

Jaennis took out a small purse.

Matylde put her hand on the table. “From the household allowance.”

“My dear, it is only—”

“From mine. Record it against mine until his claim is paid. He should know where it comes from.”

The messenger looked at the purse. Jaennis closed it and asked the clerk to make the payment before the innkeeper found another reason to send a letter.

The queen returned to the road account. She had missed her place. Riarhin saw her search for it without asking anyone to point.

At the end of the audience, Solenne took Riarhin towards a side door while the councillors gathered their papers. Jaennis came with them.

For several steps nobody spoke.

“She’s prepared,” Riarhin said.

“She works.”

“I could see that.”

“Could you?”

Jaennis stopped beside a window. Below it, men were carrying chairs across the court. One had taken three and was refusing to put any down. His companion waited to see which would fall first.

“I thought you ought to come,” Jaennis said.

Riarhin had expected an accusation.

“She has had accounts from everybody. Men who served her mother. Men who wished they had opposed her sooner. Women who know exactly what ought to have been done now that there is no danger in saying it.”

“And you.”

“Yes.”

Jaennis’s gaze remained on the chairs.

“She should hear from the person who chose to leave her without them.”

Riarhin felt the window’s warmth against the side of her face.

“She has my account.”

“She has a sheet of paper.”

The man below lost one chair. His companion caught it and said something that made both stop laughing.

“Will you attend when she questions me?” Riarhin asked.

“If she wishes.”

“And if she doesn’t?”

“Then I shall be elsewhere.”

Jaennis looked at her then.

“I have had rather more practice at staying with her afterwards.”

Solenne took her out through the lower court. The chairs had been collected. Where the man had dropped one, a servant was rubbing a pale scrape from the paving with a wet rag.

“Will the questions be written?” Riarhin asked.

“Some. Her Majesty may ask others.”

“Will there be an account kept?”

“If she asks for one.”

“If she doesn’t?”

“Then it is a private audience.”

They stopped beneath the arch while a group of councillors crossed ahead of them. Riarhin recognised one from an earlier court: more flesh at the neck now, a better coat, the same habit of holding a paper folded so that only he could read its heading.

“Your officer remains outside?” she asked.

“Yes.”

“And who remains with the queen after I leave?”

Solenne looked at her.

“Whoever she chooses.”

The officer waited while the councillors cleared the arch.

“If she asks whether I loved her mother?”

“You answer.”

“If the answer is complicated?”

“Then you’ll have something in common.”

Riarhin let out a short breath. Solenne began walking again.

At the inner door the custodian brought Remorse down from the shelf. A man collecting a cloak saw the hilt and stopped in the middle of putting his arm into a sleeve. He said something to his companion. The companion looked at Riarhin, then quickly away.

She took the sword without touching the man’s sleeve, though it lay across part of the counter. The custodian waited for him to move it before giving back the knife.

Outside, the discharged soldier was sitting on a bench.

Riarhin knew him from the description given in council: the damaged left hand, the old cavalry boot cut low at the top to accommodate a stiff leg. He had come without waiting for tomorrow. A little girl sat beside him holding a paper bag which had once contained food and now contained crumbs. She was bending one corner into the shape of a beak.

“Are they still in there?” he asked Solenne.

“The sitting is over.”

“They sent for me.”

“For tomorrow.”

He unfolded the note. The word was there. He stared at it, then held it lower, as though another angle might change the day.

“I was at the stable when it came.”

Solenne crouched to look at the note with him. Riarhin stayed beside the bench. The girl studied her ears frankly, then went back to the folded paper.

“They said I should bring him,” the child told Solenne. “I asked twice.”

“You did right.”

The soldier closed his eyes. “They don’t pay her when she isn’t carrying.”

“What does she carry?”

“Whatever they give her. Plates. Linen. She’s at the inn.”

Solenne looked at the girl. “You’re not his daughter?”

“No, dame. He’s too old.”

The soldier laughed despite himself. “Thank you.”

“I mean you could be my grandfather.”

“Better and better.”

Solenne rose and spoke to the guard. He went inside. The girl opened the paper beak over one finger and made it snap at the soldier’s good hand. He caught it and held it until she could pull free.

Riarhin asked whether he had served in the north. He nodded, then saw her face properly.

He drew his hand back from the paper bird.

The girl folded the bird flat and put it away.

“I’m here for the money owed to me,” he said.

“I know.”

“That’s all.”

Riarhin moved away from the bench. Solenne returned with a page carrying a written confirmation for the innkeeper. The soldier’s lodging would be met until the hearing. The girl’s lost afternoon was included as the cost of bringing him. Solenne read both provisions aloud.

The child wanted to know whether that meant she could keep the coin or must give it to the innkeeper.

“You give him the part covering the work he pays you for,” Solenne said. “He isn’t to take it twice.”

“Will you write that?”

The officer asked for the pen again.

Riarhin waited by the gate. She heard the soldier ask whether the queen had really read his case. Solenne said that she had. The child asked how old the queen was. The soldier told her not to be impertinent, but Solenne answered.

“That’s not so old,” the girl said.

“No,” Solenne said. “It isn’t.”

Chapter 4 The place at supper

Deliane had given the minister a limp he did not possess.

Vaelrie objected on the ground that any competent imitation should preserve the original man’s few advantages. Deliane replied that she could manage his voice or his legs, and the company must choose.

“His legs,” somebody said.

“You have never heard his voice,” Vaelrie answered. “They should pay her to leave it out.”

Matylde laughed so suddenly that she had to put down her cup.

Riarhin sat three places away, beside a woman who had known Aveline.

The woman had made the recognition at the door, giving the name enough volume to reach the people behind her. Riarhin had answered with her own. After a pause, the woman had said that she remembered having been told another one.

“Yes,” Riarhin said.

She had not added that the face was different. The woman had eyes.

Now they were seated together. A dish of small fish lay between them, crisp at the edges and dressed with green herbs. Riarhin offered it. The woman took two without looking at her.

Jaennis had placed the company loosely enough that conversation could cross the table. There were men among them, older officers and younger relatives, and a visiting woman whose husband had sent six increasingly urgent letters asking when she intended to return. She described the letters while a knight supplied the voice of an abandoned household dog.

“He says the steward cannot find the key.”

“Who will scratch behind my ears?” the knight said.

“He says the accounts require my attention.”

“Who will scratch the other ear?”

“He says his sister is coming.”

The knight put down his spoon.

“You must go. I withdraw everything.”

Even Riarhin’s neighbour smiled.

Coryd was seated near Deliane. She had begun the meal stiffly, uncertain what to do with the extra spoon. Deliane took it from her setting and laid it beside her own.

“I steal these,” she said. “It saves me buying presents.”

Coryd relaxed enough to tell her that the lodging’s bed had been designed for a man who had offended his carpenter.

Across the table, Matylde and Isbray were using counters to reconstruct a disputed game. Isbray maintained that the queen had moved twice. Matylde said that anybody who spent that long thinking ought to expect the rest of the world to proceed without her.

Jaennis watched them with pleasure. She ate little. Twice she moved a dish nearer someone who had been reaching without asking. The second time, Matylde did it before she could.

“Thank you, darling.”

The girl’s shoulders settled. Riarhin noticed because she had not known they were raised.

A porter came to the door and waited. He was a thickset man with a grey beard cut close beneath his chin. Jaennis asked what he needed.

“A woman from the lower house. About the room promised to her son.”

“Orven, this is supper.”

“She says she has been to the office.”

“How often?”

“Four times.”

Jaennis closed her eyes briefly.

“The cooper’s widow?”

“Yes, my lady.”

“Ask Deliane to give her the note we kept. The second drawer.”

Deliane rose. Matylde looked towards her.

“What room?”

“For an apprentice taken into the household service,” Jaennis said. “His father’s old master spoke for him.”

“Why did the office refuse?”

“It appears nobody wished to say yes.”

The queen followed Deliane with her eyes until she had left.

Riarhin’s neighbour leaned towards the fish.

“Aveline was less quiet.”

“She had more reason to be agreeable.”

The woman looked at her fully for the first time.

“I beg your pardon,” she said. “You received someone who was lying to you. I know.”

“You know that much.”

“Yes.”

After a while the woman passed her the bread.

When the meal was being cleared, Matylde asked whether she could speak to Riarhin alone the following afternoon.

“You needn’t ask me,” Jaennis said. “But I would have a little time with you first. There are questions which can be put less painfully if you know where they lead.”

“I’ve written them.”

“Then we can look at them.”

“I meant alone.”

The room had not fallen silent. Somebody was still telling the knight how to imitate the minister’s walk. Nevertheless, Riarhin could feel the nearest people listening.

Jaennis touched Matylde’s hand where it lay beside the folded napkin.

“Of course.”

Isbray reached under the table for a counter she had dropped. Matylde watched her disappear beneath it and then looked back towards Jaennis.

“We were going to finish.”

“I know. I have hardly had you to myself today.”

“We can do it another time.”

“Only if you wish.”

Matylde gathered the counters. Isbray emerged with the missing piece and began to object, then looked at the girl’s face and closed her mouth.

“How considerate she is,” the visiting woman said to Riarhin’s neighbour.

Jaennis smiled and drew Matylde’s chair a little nearer so that the servants could pass.

Riarhin rose when the others did. She had accepted no private invitation. She had spoken under her own name. In the passage, waiting for Coryd, she found herself checking whether she had made the woman beside her angrier by apologizing.

Coryd arrived carrying an apple Deliane had given her.

“I like that one,” she said.

“So do I.”

“You sound surprised.”

Riarhin looked back towards the lit room. Matylde was beside Jaennis, listening with her head inclined. Isbray had put the counters into a little bag and was trying to pull the knot tight with her teeth.

“I’m tired,” Riarhin said.

Coryd bit into the apple and looked back into the room.

“I’m not.”

Riarhin followed her gaze. Deliane was being urged to give the minister another ailment. The knight had offered his own hat as a prop and was protesting that he had meant it to be worn, not sat upon. Two women near the window were laughing with their foreheads almost touching.

“You could remain,” Riarhin said.

“I know. I’d rather you did too.”

“Why?”

“Because you keep looking as if we ought to be thrown out. It’s making me wait for it.”

Riarhin turned the end of her glove between her fingers. Beyond the open door Matylde and Isbray were leaving with their attendants, the younger girl still trying to tie the bag of counters. Jaennis walked beside the queen as far as the stair. Their voices disappeared down it. For the first time all evening, people began choosing their places without checking where Matylde would sit.

The visiting woman with the absent husband looked out at the sisters.

“Have you been dismissed? We haven’t.”

Coryd held up her apple. “Provisioned.”

“How efficient. Come back. The wine is still arriving, which I take as permission.”

She gave her name as Madra and insisted that they sit beside her. Her husband was the man who had sent six letters. She had been married to him for nineteen years and had received variations on the same letters every time she left him for longer than a week.

“He could come,” Riarhin said.

“He would hate it.”

“Then perhaps he ought to stay where he is.”

“That is what I write. He finds it heartless.”

The other woman from the window leaned over Madra’s shoulder and kissed her cheek. Madra turned at the last moment so that the kiss landed on her mouth. The woman drew back, laughing, and looked at the sisters.

“She’s telling you she has a tragic marriage.”

“I’m telling them I have a husband.”

“Much the same story when you tell it.”

“Sivelle has been widowed twice,” Madra said. “She considers this an accomplishment.”

“Only once. The second man married someone else. He’s still alive, to her increasing surprise.”

Coryd moved her chair to make room. Sivelle thanked her and sat with the careless grace of a woman who knew how much space her skirts required. She was perhaps in her thirties, with a strong nose and a crease beside one eye deeper than the other. Her smile favoured that side. She took Madra’s cup, tasted the wine and gave it back.

“Too sweet.”

“Then stop drinking it.”

“I keep hoping.”

Across the room Deliane had begun a recitation. The original was a solemn address about the quality of the roads. Her version concerned the passage of the minister’s horse through a door too narrow for its dignity. The knight supplied the horse, stamping once and looking offended. Coryd laughed with her whole face.

Riarhin watched her and felt a sharp, unexpected envy. She had brought her sister to a city where every second face recalled something Riarhin ought to answer for. Coryd had found a chair and a woman who liked her laugh.

Sivelle caught her looking and raised an eyebrow.

“Am I being examined?”

“No.”

“What a pity. I should like to know whether I’ve improved.”

Madra pushed her shoulder. “Don’t start. She’ll think you mean it.”

“I sometimes do.”

Jaennis returned as Deliane reached the horse’s refusal to enter the council chamber. She stood in the doorway until the room noticed her, then said that if the minister were half so entertaining she would invite him twice a week.

Deliane dropped the performance and went to her and took Jaennis’s hand. The older woman smiled up at her, and Deliane bent to kiss her. Riarhin looked down at her own cup.

Madra did not.

“You’ve missed the best part,” she said.

“I have heard the best part several times. She performs it when I’m trying to read.”

“Your reading is dull,” Deliane told her.

Jaennis touched the sleeve at Deliane’s wrist. “Your minister is getting broader.”

“So is the minister. I work from observation.”

The room rearranged itself around them without anyone giving an instruction. A chair became available. Someone put a lamp closer. The knight surrendered his recovered hat to a servant and sat on the floor with his back against a bench.

Sivelle asked Coryd whether she had ever ridden in a race. Coryd said she had raced people who had not realised it until she passed them. The knight said that this was the only kind of race worth winning and asked what she had ridden. They began comparing horses, then falls. Madra had fallen through a hedge into somebody else’s washing. The knight claimed to have gone over his horse’s head and landed standing. Sivelle asked whether the horse had applauded.

Riarhin laughed before she remembered not to draw attention. Jaennis heard and looked pleased.

“There. We have found something she can bear.”

Riarhin met her eyes. “A man lying about a horse is familiar ground.”

The knight objected loudly. He demanded witnesses, was told he should have brought them and appealed to Coryd, who said that she had only just met him and would hate to begin with perjury.

They played a game with slips of paper after that. Each person wrote a complaint in the manner of somebody at court, and the rest guessed whose it was meant to be. Riarhin declined a pen but agreed to guess. She knew too few of the new people and remembered the old ones too well; twice her answers silenced a joke she had thought harmless. The third time she let Coryd answer for both of them.

Deliane’s complaint was unmistakably Jaennis’s. It concerned a woman who insisted on leaving hairpins wherever a bare foot might discover them. Jaennis read it aloud without changing a word, then asked whether she might be given the actual pins, which had been taken from her dressing table.

Deliane looked guilty. Everyone laughed. She put a hand into her sleeve and brought out three.

“Thief,” Jaennis said.

“Careless woman,” Deliane answered.

The pins lay between them on the table. Later Riarhin saw Jaennis put one back into Deliane’s hair, fixing a loose piece which had fallen over her eye. Deliane stayed still for her, though she had been arguing a moment before. When Jaennis finished, she caught the older woman’s fingers and kissed them.

Sivelle, who had watched too, made a small face into her cup. Coryd asked what it meant.

“Nothing. I wanted that position by the fire.”

“There are two chairs.”

“It isn’t always the chair.”

Madra flicked a folded paper at her. “Behave.”

Sivelle caught it, unfolded it and read the complaint, which described a woman who declared herself heartbroken whenever somebody else was given a larger pear. She looked up at Madra.

“You promised to burn that letter.”

The laughter went on for some time.

When the sisters finally rose, Coryd had accepted an invitation to see the house Sivelle had inherited from her first husband. It needed a new stair rail. She had also agreed to go riding if a suitable horse could be found. Riarhin could not tell which invitation had come first or whether either had been the point.

At the door Sivelle touched Coryd’s sleeve.

“You can send word if you’d rather not.”

“I’d rather.”

“That was obscure.”

“I’d rather come.”

Sivelle smiled. Coryd smiled back without trying to hide it.

The escort was waiting in the outer court. Riarhin apologised for the delay. The man said that his duty ended when she reached her lodging, not at a particular hour, but she could hear the fatigue in his voice. She sent the kitchen’s unused parcel out to him before they left.

Coryd walked beside her through the cold streets, swinging the empty apple cloth from one hand.

“What?” she said, when Riarhin glanced at her.

“Nothing.”

“You can say something.”

“I was going to ask whether the stair rail was badly damaged.”

Coryd laughed so loudly that the guard ahead turned round.

“It could be falling into the street,” she said. “I haven’t seen it yet.”

Chapter 5 The person who did it

The sheet was worn at the folds.

Matylde laid it on the small table between them and held one corner down with a glass weight. She had brought no attendant into the room. Solenne stood beyond the open outer door, far enough away to preserve the privacy of ordinary voices.

Riarhin waited to be offered a chair.

Matylde looked up, seemed to remember that this was something she must do, and pointed to it.

“You wrote this.”

“Yes.”

“When?”

“Afterwards. Before I left the city.”

“I know afterwards.”

Riarhin folded her hands loosely in her lap.

“I don’t remember which day without going through the days.”

“Will you?”

She did. She began with the morning after the deaths, the lodging, the wounds, the people whose names she did not say aloud. Matylde watched her while she worked backwards and forward again. The silence grew long enough that Riarhin wanted to supply an approximate answer.

“The day Margery’s request reached us,” she said. “Several days after. I could be exact if I had the letters.”

“So you had time.”

“Yes.”

The girl moved the glass weight.

“My father barred the door.”

“Yes.”

“Was it my door?”

“Yes.”

“Why?”

“To prevent anyone reaching you that way.”

“Anyone. Or you?”

Riarhin looked at the paper.

“Me.”

Matylde’s eyes filled, but she did not wipe them. She waited until she could speak without the breath catching.

“You say the last thing he said might have been my name.”

“I couldn’t hear it clearly.”

“Say what you heard.”

Riarhin closed her eyes for a moment. The linen stair returned with a precision she had learned to endure: the pressure through her feet, the light against the wall, Tylan’s weight, the sound which had not become a word she could honestly retain as certain.

She tried to give the sound.

Matylde listened, and then asked her to say it again.

The second attempt was worse. Riarhin could hear herself beginning to shape it towards the name.

“I can’t do that accurately.”

“You just did.”

“I tried. I shouldn’t have repeated it.”

The girl pushed the weight away. It travelled farther than she intended and struck the lip of the tray at the edge of the table.

“You can remember everything else.”

“Some things I never heard properly.”

Matylde looked towards the open door.

For a moment Riarhin thought she would call someone. Instead she took a breath and unfolded the paper completely.

“Mother was looking at my door.”

“Yes.”

“Did she say anything about me?”

“Not that I can give you as a message.”

“I didn’t ask for a message.”

“No.”

“What did she say?”

“She said, ‘You brought it to me.’ She meant the amulet.”

“Did she ask you to take it away?”

“No.”

“Did she ask you why you had come?”

“No.”

Matylde looked at the window. Riarhin waited. The glass caught the light so that she could see the girl’s reflection more clearly than the garden beyond it.

“What was she doing?”

“She had made room to fight. Moved the furniture aside. Lit the candles. She was waiting when I came back from the stair.”

“Was she frightened?”

“I couldn’t tell.”

“What could you tell?”

“That she knew what she was doing. That she meant to stop me.”

Matylde’s mouth tightened, almost with satisfaction.

“I went into the room,” she said. “She attacked. I could hardly get near her.”

“Then how?”

Riarhin looked at her. The question had been spoken quietly. She could refuse its detail, offer the account already written, say that the girl ought not to have the image. She had made enough decisions about what Matylde should be left with.

“I cut the ribbon holding the amulet. It fell. She looked at it and then came at me again.”

“Because it was gone?”

“I don’t know what she felt. She could still fight. Nothing stopped when it touched the floor.”

“You said it made her do things.”

“I have said it affected her. I cannot tell you where its influence ended.”

The girl pressed the edge of the sheet against the table.

“Then finish.”

“She was driving me back towards the fire. I threw a knife to make her turn her sword. I used the hearthstone to keep her from turning clear. I went in with Remorse.”

“Your sword.”

“Yes.”

“The one they take from you outside.”

“Yes.”

Matylde looked towards the outer door. Riarhin could not tell whether she was imagining the sword or the man keeping it. The girl’s eyes came back to her.

“And she looked at my door.”

“Yes.”

“Did you say anything?”

“No.”

A coal shifted in the hearth. Both of them heard it. Matylde stared at the glass weight, at the little flaws suspended in it, then put it back on the paper.

“Where is the amulet now?”

“Out of the kingdom. In a place built to contain it.”

“Can it be brought back?”

“Not safely.”

“You took it.”

“Yes.”

“And now you tell me I cannot have it.”

“Yes.”

The girl looked at her for a long time.

“Why shouldn’t I have you killed?”

Riarhin heard a footstep in the passage. It stopped beyond the door.

“Because you promised I could attend and leave.”

“That is your answer?”

“It is why I came under your protection.”

“Not because you thought I ought to be allowed?”

“I thought you ought to ask me what you wanted answered.”

“I asked this.”

“You could order it.”

“And you would fight.”

“Yes.”

There was no useful way to make the word gentler.

Matylde pressed the heel of her hand against one eye, angrily, as if the tears were an interruption introduced by somebody else.

“You didn’t ask me.”

“No.”

“You decided about them. You decided about me. And now I have to keep my promise because you have come here with it.”

“Other people have come on the strength of it too.”

“You brought them into it.”

Riarhin stopped.

She had been about to explain why the petitioners’ reliance was different. The girl had not asked about the difference. She was sitting with both hands flat beside the account of her parents’ deaths, trying to keep them still.

“Yes,” Riarhin said.

Matylde leaned back.

“Did you think you would die?”

“I knew I could.”

“That isn’t what I asked.”

“I hoped to get out. We had made arrangements.”

“You had made arrangements for afterwards.”

“Yes.”

“And for me?”

“Margery.”

“That’s one person.”

“She was the person I trusted to come to you.”

Matylde folded the sheet along the wrong crease, saw what she had done, and opened it again. Her hands were shaking now. Riarhin kept hers in her lap.

“People keep telling me what I must remember,” Matylde said.

Riarhin waited.

“They say I must remember that she loved me. Or that the army would have marched. Or that people suffered. As if I would lose one thing the moment I looked at another.”

She moved the sheet away from the glass weight. The corner curled upwards and she flattened it with a finger.

“I would like someone to answer the question I asked.”

“Ask it.”

“I did. About afterwards.”

Riarhin breathed in. She remembered counting the people who might reach the nursery, making the route possible for Saikou, trusting Margery to know what to say when the child asked. She had not imagined a girl at this table. She had imagined the night, the following morning, enough time for someone else to take charge.

“I arranged for you to be found by someone you knew. I did not arrange your life after that.”

“Why not?”

“I could not.”

“You could arrange for other people to be killed.”

“Yes.”

Matylde looked towards the door again. This time Riarhin heard the breath escape her before she brought it under control.

“Do you want someone?” she asked.

“No.”

“Water?”

“No.”

Riarhin sat still. A small bruise showed at the base of the girl’s thumb, probably from one of her games. She had begun looking for something harmless to think about. She made herself return to Matylde’s face.

“Did any of them refuse?” the queen asked.

“The people with me?”

“Yes.”

“They had choices. I asked things of them which made those choices difficult.”

“That’s the sort of answer the councillors give.”

Riarhin felt a flash of anger. The girl was determined to make every answer insufficient. Then she remembered how carefully she herself had worded it.

“I sent people to places where I knew they might die. I needed them to go. I did not want them to refuse.”

“Did you tell them that?”

“Yes.”

Matylde rubbed the bruise with her other thumb. “Was that frightening? To have you tell them?”

Riarhin thought of Cadan asking for instructions, of Amari’s face, of the man whose name had been taken so he could live. She could not put them all into the room. She would not make them into an answer chosen for the girl.

“Sometimes.”

“Were you glad when they agreed?”

“Yes.”

The queen’s eyes filled again. She leaned forward as if about to be sick. Riarhin lifted a hand, stopped it halfway and set it on the chair arm.

Matylde saw.

“Don’t.”

“I won’t.”

They sat until the girl could straighten. A sound came from beyond the outer door: someone approaching, Solenne’s low reply, retreating steps. The queen listened to those steps go with an attention which seemed to hurt her.

“Did you think I would forgive you if you came?”

“No.”

“Did you hope?”

Riarhin looked at the sheet between them.

“I hoped I could answer without making it worse.”

“You haven’t.”

“No.”

Matylde folded the paper again, this time along its proper lines. She held it in both hands for a moment before putting it in the little case beside her chair.

“You may go.”

She rose.

At the door, the girl spoke again.

“Do you regret it?”

Riarhin turned back.

There were things she could have said about regret, decisions and necessity. She had heard herself say versions of them in other rooms. Matylde had already endured enough of her distinctions.

“I wish you had your father,” she said. “And I would still have tried to stop your mother.”

“Go.”

Riarhin went.

Chapter 6 The question brought back

The officer with the sword was changing the cord on somebody else’s parcel. Riarhin gave him her slip and waited.

Her hands had begun to shake after leaving the room. She rested them on the edge of the counter, then took them away because the movement was visible against the wood.

Jaennis sat in an adjoining chamber with a small table drawn beside her. There was a covered dish on it and two cups. She was speaking to a woman about curtains. The woman held a sample against the light and asked whether the paler part would fade first.

Jaennis saw Riarhin and did not interrupt the discussion.

The officer checked the mark on the tie. He held Remorse out with the hilt towards her. She buckled it on, thanked him and stepped back to make room for a man carrying a sealed box.

Matylde came into the passage.

Jaennis stopped speaking before the girl reached the door.

“My love.”

Matylde’s face changed. Riarhin saw the effort of it: the mouth preparing one thing, the eyes finding Jaennis, the first thing abandoned.

“I wasn’t as upset as you thought,” she said.

Jaennis held out her hand.

“You don’t have to be brave with me.”

“I know. I don’t want you to worry.”

The curtain woman gathered her samples without being asked. Matylde went inside. The door closed.

Riarhin stood until the man with the box asked her to move.

At the lodging, Coryd was sitting on the floor with a basin between her feet. She had washed her hair and was combing water out of it onto a towel whose original colour could no longer be confidently named.

“Well?”

Riarhin unfastened the sword.

“She asked.”

“And?”

“I answered.”

Coryd put down the comb.

“Come here.”

“I don’t need—”

“Then stand there being useful to nobody.”

Riarhin sat on the bed. Her sister finished drawing the comb through a tangle, set it down and came to sit beside her. For a while they listened to somebody arguing over the laundry in the yard.

“She asked why she shouldn’t kill me.”

Coryd’s hand closed around the wet towel.

“What did you say?”

“The promise.”

“Good.”

“It didn’t feel good.”

“It wasn’t meant to. It was meant to keep you alive.”

Riarhin laughed once, without pleasure.

Coryd leaned her shoulder against hers. The wet hair touched Riarhin’s sleeve. She left it there.

After a while Coryd said, “My mother used to say I would catch a fever doing this.”

“Did you?”

“Not that I remember.”

“You’d remember.”

“Yes. She knew that too. It never stopped her saying it.”

Riarhin could see Bea with a towel over one shoulder, turning from the mirror to tell her not to sit in the window while her hair was wet. She had forgotten none of it. The angle of the window, the little chip in the mirror’s frame, the damp ends of her own hair cold against her neck: all were available. Bea’s hand had held something. A pin. Riarhin had been taking pins from the table and arranging them in rows. Her mother had reached for one and found the table empty because her daughter had wanted all of them.

“She would be old,” Riarhin said.

Coryd did not ask who.

“Yes.”

“I can’t make her old.”

Her sister moved away just enough to look at her. Water from her hair marked the shoulder of Riarhin’s coat.

“I’ve tried,” Riarhin said. “I know what it looks like when people age. I’ve seen enough of them. But it’s never her. It’s something I’ve put on her.”

Coryd touched the wet place on the sleeve with two fingers, as if it might be dried that way.

“Mine has lines beside her mouth now. She hates them. She says they make her look as though she’s about to object.”

“Is she?”

“Usually.”

Riarhin laughed. The laugh escaped roughly and became something else before she could contain it. Coryd put her arm around her. Neither spoke for a while.

The laundry argument continued below. Someone had taken another woman’s good cloth down before it was dry. Someone else insisted the line belonged to her brother. A child offered an account so detailed that both women told her to go inside.

Eventually Riarhin straightened. Coryd handed her the corner of the towel which was still dry.

“Does your mother know where you are?”

“She knows I came with you.”

“What does she think of that?”

“She told me not to let you decide when I was hungry.”

Riarhin looked at her. Coryd kept a straight face for several seconds, then failed.

“I did write,” she said. “Before you ask. And she wrote back. You needn’t arrange a courier to prove you’re allowing me contact with my own family.”

Riarhin rubbed her eyes with the towel.

“I wasn’t going to.”

“You were wondering.”

“Yes.”

They went downstairs together. The cook had saved them two portions and was prepared to be angry if they had already eaten. Coryd told her, with feeling, that she need have no fear of that.

The following morning, Riarhin was asked to return for clarification of two dates. She prepared the relevant part of her account and brought it in a folder whose corners had been repaired with cloth.

Matylde received her in the same room. The paper from the day before was gone.

“I ought not to have asked in that way,” the girl said.

Riarhin had not yet sat down.

“What way?”

“As though there was something you could say to make it different.”

“There may be things I can tell you.”

“I didn’t mean to make you defend yourself.”

“You didn’t.”

The queen looked unconvinced.

Riarhin took the chair when it was offered. Beyond the window, a workman was tapping at a loose pane. The small repeated noise gave her something to wait beside.

“Do you still want an answer?”

“Yes.”

It came quickly. Matylde looked towards the door.

A page appeared before she could continue. Jaennis was waiting with the councillor who had travelled from the provincial meeting. They had only a little time before he must leave.

Matylde rose, then stopped.

“Leave the dates.”

Riarhin laid the folder on the table.

“May I return for it?”

“I’ll have it sent.”

The girl went. The page held the door for Riarhin, who passed through after her and waited while he decided which passage she ought to use.

Margery was waiting below the records stair when the page finally found Riarhin’s escort.

“She wants to see you again?”

“She asked me to leave the dates.”

“The rest?”

“What rest?”

Margery glanced at the page. Riarhin sent neither him nor the guard away.

“Jaennis sat with her for a long time yesterday,” Margery said. “She was badly shaken.”

“I expected that.”

“Did you need to tell her everything?”

Riarhin looked at her.

“She asked me to describe his last breath.”

Margery’s hand went to the stair rail.

“What did you say?”

“I told her what I remembered.”

“Oh, Riarhin.”

The page shifted the folder beneath his arm. Riarhin heard the paper slide inside it.

“You asked me to answer her.”

“I didn’t know she would ask that.”

“Neither did I.”

Margery was crying, quietly and with an angry effort to conceal it. Riarhin wanted to apologise. She wanted, more fiercely, to give the letter back and go north.

“I can’t make her father’s death easier to hear,” she said. “You know why.”

Margery covered her mouth. The guard looked away.

After a little while she lowered her hand.

“No. That wasn’t fair.”

Riarhin said nothing. The apology had not made her less angry.

“I promised to sit with her this afternoon,” Margery said. “Jaennis says we should let her rest.”

“Has Matylde asked to rest?”

“I haven’t seen her yet.”

Margery waited, and Riarhin knew she was waiting to be told to go upstairs. She adjusted her coat instead. The page asked whether he should take the folder to the clerk now.

“Yes,” Riarhin said.

He left. Margery looked after him, then turned back towards the stair. Riarhin went out with her escort. Before she reached the court she heard Margery asking an attendant whether the queen still wanted her at the agreed hour.

Her account took the rest of the day.

She wrote the names of people who had helped her, then crossed out the names not required to answer the particular question. She distinguished the people she had asked from the people who had been asked by others. At one point she found herself using a passive construction so that nobody seemed to have ordered a thing she had ordered.

She tore that sheet in half.

Coryd looked up from the stocking she was mending.

“Bad?”

“Convenient.”

Her sister made a small noise and returned to the heel.

Riarhin began again. Outside, the guards changed. She could tell which man had come on by the way he set his cup on the stair rail. The new one always put it too near the edge.

She had nearly finished when a knock came. Edrune stood below with her coat over one arm and two fresh letters in her hand. The yard had added another week’s storage. The petitioners’ spokesman from the northern guarantor wanted to know whether the hearing would go ahead on the appointed day.

“As far as I’ve been told,” Riarhin said.

“As far as you’ve been told,” Edrune repeated.

“I’ll ask in the morning.”

“Ask tonight. We have people waiting on the answer.”

Riarhin looked at the unfinished page, then reached for her coat.

Part II An appointment of her own

Chapter 7 A house with visitors

The cook would not show them the kitchen until somebody answered about the flour.

Solenne said the purpose of the visit was to establish what the house required.

“Flour,” the cook said.

“How much?”

“The amount I’ve already paid for.”

They stood in the entrance while a man carrying two mattresses attempted to pass them sideways. Deliane took one mattress from him, discovered that it was heavier than she expected, and told Riarhin to stop watching her fail. Between them they got it through the door.

The royal guest house had a generous front and a much less generous interior. Rooms had been divided to make more rooms. A passage ended in a locked cupboard which, according to the laundress, had once been the back stair. Someone had put a screen around a bed in a room still being used to store candles. The sleeper had complained of the smell, which the laundress considered unreasonable in a man who had arrived with three unwashed dogs.
