Deliane listened to each complaint and asked names. She wrote them on a sheet already crowded with other names, then turned the sheet over and began again.

“You’ll lose them,” Riarhin said.

“I’ll copy them.”

“When?”

“This evening, when you won’t be there to ask.”

Coryd laughed. Deliane looked pleased, then caught sight of the cook still waiting in the doorway.

“The flour,” she said. “Come with me.”

They went into the kitchen. A boy was scraping a pot with a piece of wood. He had worked through the blackened crust at the centre and was now lifting it in large, satisfying pieces. The cook took the scraper from him before he could damage the metal.

Deliane examined the bill. The flour had been bought for guests admitted under a royal direction. The direction named the guests and the dates but gave no authority for the expense. The cook had assumed that feeding them was implied.

“It was,” Solenne said.

“Then somebody should imply it to the miller.”

Solenne took the bill and put her own name beneath a note on the back. She instructed Deliane to retain a copy and the cook to bring the original to her office the next morning.

“Will I have to wait?”

“Yes,” Solenne said. “But you won’t have to explain it again.”

The cook considered this insufficient and accepted it.

Upstairs, Deliane allocated a quiet room to an elderly petitioner and was immediately told that it was over the place where the ash cart stopped before dawn. She looked out of the window, apologized and changed the allocation. The laundress gave her a less hostile account of the remaining rooms.

A woman on the landing asked whether the quiet room was the only one with a door which locked.

Deliane turned. “They should all lock.”

“Should they?”

She was older than Riarhin first thought, with a fine network of lines around the mouth and white hair pinned beneath a travelling cap. A young man stood behind her carrying two boxes. One had split at the corner. He held it against his chest to keep it together.

“My daughter is upstairs,” the woman said. “We were told that there would be women on that floor. Now there are men going past at all hours.”

“The men sleep at the other end,” the laundress said.

“They walk past.”

“They have to get there.”

Deliane asked to see the door. Riarhin followed when the woman moved to include her.

The daughter was sitting on a bed with a basket of stockings. She was fully grown, tired and visibly irritated by her mother’s arrival. The door did lock from inside. It did not lock from outside, which was the mother’s concern.

“She leaves her things.”

“Then take them with me,” the daughter said. “There’s very little you haven’t looked through.”

Her mother pretended not to hear. “She came to attend a hearing. I don’t want her frightened while she waits.”

“I’m not frightened.”

“The woman next door said a man tried her latch.”

“He tried the wrong room.”

“That’s what he said.”

Deliane stood at the open door, testing the latch with one finger. She asked the young man with the boxes to put them down. He looked at the mother first, then obeyed.

Riarhin went to the window. Below it was a kitchen roof with no easy route beyond; the alley on the far side was too narrow to turn a ladder. She looked back at the room. The daughter had seen her inspecting it.

“I’m not planning to escape,” she said.

“I was looking at who could get in.”

“Oh.”

She returned to her stockings. Her mother wanted a guard posted at the stair. Deliane asked who would pay him. The mother said the Crown ought to provide protection to people brought there by its business.

“It provides a porter,” the laundress said. “He went home an hour ago because nobody told him the change.”

Deliane looked towards the stair.

“We’ll alter the doors first,” she said. “You’ll have a key. I’ll have a second kept in the office. We will knock and wait for an answer unless there is smoke, water or reason to believe someone is hurt.”

The mother began to ask for a copy. Deliane turned to the daughter.

“Do you want her to have one?”

The room became very quiet.

“No.”

“Then you may let her in when you choose.”

The mother’s face reddened. “I have paid for half her journey.”

The daughter dropped the stocking into the basket. “And told everyone on the other half.”

The young man took a small step towards the door. Deliane moved aside to let him pass, but he only wanted to stop being directly between the two women.

“I won’t settle that for you,” Deliane said. “The room is hers while she stays. I’ll arrange the key.”

“When?” the daughter asked.

“Before evening, if we can get the man who keeps the locks. Until then, take anything you cannot replace to the office. You may collect it without explaining why.”

The daughter nodded. Her mother would not look at Deliane. Riarhin followed the others out and heard the argument resume before they reached the stair.

The laundress waited until they were below it.

“You’ve made an enemy.”

“Which one?”

“The mother. The daughter’ll come round when she needs something.”

Deliane took out her crowded paper. She looked at it, folded it and put it away again.

“Where does the locksmith live?”

“Above the old bakehouse.”

“I’ll go.”

“You’ll be there till supper. He’ll tell you about his leg.”

“Is it a bad leg?”

“It’s a long story.”

Coryd offered to see whether the door could be made to take a common lock, which would spare them waiting for something specially fitted. Deliane accepted. Riarhin watched her go back up with the laundress, already discussing the width of the wood. Deliane remained on the bottom step, rubbing her thumb over the ink on her forefinger.

“Was that foolish?” she asked.

“The key?”

“Promising it today.”

“You may have to sit through the story.”

Deliane laughed. “I meant the rest.”

“The woman will complain.”

“I know.”

“Then you’ve allowed for it.”

Deliane looked towards the stair. Above them the mother was still speaking. The daughter answered once, sharply. A door closed without being slammed.

“I’ve asked people to wait all my life,” Deliane said. “I could hear myself going to say it.”

She went to find the porter who should have been relieved.

Coryd knelt beside a chest whose lower runner had split. She drew it out, examined the grain and asked who had tried to mend it last.

“The man who doesn’t come now,” the laundress said.

“That was wise of him.”

“Can you do it?”

“Yes. I need dry wood and a different fastening. And someone must empty it.”

“What would you charge?” Deliane asked.

Coryd named a sum. Deliane looked at the chest, then agreed.

Riarhin waited until they were alone beside the upper window.

“You want this house?”

“The post.”

“It appears to contain several people who dislike you already.”

“They dislike the last three people who promised to come back. I’m wearing all of them until I prove otherwise.”

“You could stay where you are.”

Deliane folded the paper carefully and put it inside her sleeve.

“Yes.”

Below, the man with the mattresses had stopped to drink from a pitcher. Water ran down his chin. He wiped it on his shoulder and took up another bundle.

“Does Jaennis know?” Riarhin asked.

“That I’ve applied? Yes.”

“And?”

“She thinks I deserve something worthy of me.”

Deliane’s tone had altered very little. Riarhin waited.

“This has work I can do, a salary I can ask to have paid, and a door at the end of the day. I consider it worthy.”

“Will you leave her household entirely?”

“I’d like to have supper there. I’d like to stay sometimes. I’d like to stop being asked about a visitor while I’m trying to take my stockings off.”

She looked at Riarhin.

“You needn’t look solemn about that. The stockings are sometimes the best part.”

Riarhin laughed. Deliane smiled and went downstairs to tell the cook when she would return.

Riarhin followed more slowly. A room beside the stair had been left open, and an old man sat inside trying to thread a lace through a boot. His fingers could not find the hole. He looked up as she passed.

“Could you?”

She went in and knelt.

The boot was good leather, old and carefully kept. The end of the lace had frayed until it filled the hole without passing through. Riarhin took the little knife she had declared at the palace and cut it clean, wetted the tip and fed it through. The man watched her hands.

“You could have put me in that room,” he said.

“Which?”

“The one over the ash cart. I wouldn’t have minded.”

“You’d have been awake before dawn.”

“I’m awake before dawn.”

She gave him the lace.

“What would you have minded?”

“Not knowing they were going to tip the thing. It sounds like the roof coming down if you don’t know.”

Riarhin sat back on her heels. “I’ll tell her.”

“I already did.”

“Then she’ll know.”

He nodded. He could make the knot once the lace was threaded. She watched him do it, resisting the impulse to take it back and finish.

In the kitchen Deliane was arguing about the guests’ arrival times. The cook wanted everyone served at one hour. Deliane said that the hearings did not finish at one hour. The cook replied that the fire did not last all day without wood, and that she had been promised enough wood for two cookings, not six.

Riarhin stood in the doorway until Deliane asked whether she wanted something.

“The man upstairs wants warning when the ash cart comes. He doesn’t need a different room.”

Deliane looked towards the cook. “There. One thing less.”

“He still wants breakfast,” the cook said.

“And you still want wood. I’m writing it.”

The two women bent over the paper. Riarhin went out to the yard, where Coryd had persuaded a boy to show her the dry timber store. The boy was naming every tool he could see with the pride of someone who had recently been taught their names. Coryd listened and corrected only the one he was about to pick up by the blade.

Riarhin waited beside the door. For once, nobody required anything of her. She watched the boy pull out a straight length of wood and offer it for inspection. Coryd tested it against her eye, nodded and asked him to carry the other end.

Chapter 8 The hour for the game

The seamstress had pinned Riarhin’s sleeve too tightly at the shoulder.

Riarhin lifted her arm. The woman clicked her tongue, removed two pins and asked whether she intended to climb something in the garment.

“I intend to be able to move.”

“Everyone says that. Then they complain of folds.”

“I won’t complain.”

“You say that too.”

The fitting room opened onto a courtyard where Matylde and Isbray were playing a game with wooden rings. A post had been driven into a tub of sand at one end. Isbray stood closer to it than the chalk line allowed.

“I can see your foot,” Matylde said.

“Then stop looking.”

“If I looked away, you’d carry the ring over and put it on.”

“I hadn’t thought of that.”

“You did it yesterday.”

The seamstress’s apprentice looked out of the window and laughed. Her mistress told her to attend to the hem. She obeyed without losing her interest in the game.

Riarhin stood on a low board while the seamstress marked the coat. She had chosen a brown cloth that would travel well and disclose little at a distance. It made her look older than the clothes in which she had first come to court. That was acceptable. She wanted the difference to be visible without inviting questions about it.

Matylde threw three rings in succession. The first struck the post and bounced away. The second landed flat. The third caught around it and spun down.

Isbray accused her of having moved the tub.

“How?”

“While I was looking at my foot.”

“You were looking at my foot.”

“A mistake. I’ll watch the tub.”

A messenger crossed the court and waited. Neither girl attended to him until he had cleared his throat twice.

Isbray had been asked to accompany a visiting lady to see a horse selected for her brother. The lady would be leaving shortly. Her household considered it a valuable introduction.

“We haven’t finished,” Matylde said.

“The carriage is waiting.”

“Tell it to wait a little longer.”

The messenger looked miserable. Riarhin recognized the moment at which a person carrying an instruction became the person required to defend it.

Jaennis appeared beneath the arch behind him.

“I thought Isbray would enjoy it.”

“I would,” Isbray said. “After.”

“The lady cannot delay her journey.”

Matylde turned a ring between her hands.

“Then she shouldn’t have arrived when we were playing.”

The apprentice bent over the hem. The seamstress kept her face still with visible effort.

Jaennis did not rebuke the girl.

“I’ve had something brought for us. A man who makes little figures move with water. You remember wanting to see them?”

“Yes.”

“Then we needn’t waste the afternoon.”

Isbray put down her rings. She promised to come back and finish. Matylde said she did not mind. The words were flat enough that the messenger looked at the ground.

When the girl had gone, Jaennis held out her hand for the remaining ring.

“You understand these things better than most people twice your age.”

Matylde surrendered it.

“May we choose a day next time? A day nobody needs her?”

“Darling, there are arrangements you are still too young to judge.”

The girl looked at the empty chalk line.

The seamstress asked Riarhin to turn. By the time she could see the courtyard again, Jaennis had gone to fetch the maker and Matylde was kneeling beside the bench. She reached beneath it, found one of Isbray’s rings and rubbed the dust off on her skirt.

She slipped it into her pocket before anyone returned.

The maker arrived with his son and a wooden case longer than either could carry comfortably alone. He had expected a table. The table provided was too narrow. He explained this three times before Jaennis asked two servants to bring another and made everyone move to give them space.

Riarhin had finished the fitting, but the seamstress needed to check the coat after she walked in it. She was told to wait until the pins had been replaced. From the doorway she could see the case opened and a little metal garden lifted out.

There were trees made from thin beaten plates, a bridge and a house no bigger than a fist. At one side stood a wheel. The boy poured water into a reservoir. His father adjusted a screw, then another, and set a small brass bird on a wire above the trees.

For a moment nothing happened.

The boy looked at his father. His father looked at the wheel with the fixed dislike of a craftsman betrayed before witnesses.

“Too much water?” Matylde asked.

“No, Majesty.”

“Not enough?”

“Quite enough.”

The bird jerked, turned completely upside down and remained there.

The maker shut his eyes. Matylde began laughing. She put her hand over her mouth, then took it away when the boy laughed too.

“I beg Your Majesty’s pardon,” the maker said.

“No. Leave it. I like him.”

“He is meant to fly.”

“He has made a decision.”

Jaennis laughed with her. The maker’s shoulders lowered a little. He asked permission to reset the wire and, receiving it, turned the bird upright. This time the wheel moved. Water ran beneath the bridge, turned an unseen weight, and a little door in the house opened. A figure emerged carrying a hammer. It struck a bell once and went in again.

Matylde leaned close. “How does he know when?”

The maker explained the float. He did it badly at first, using the names of parts she had not seen. She asked him to show her. He removed a panel. Jaennis watched the girl’s concentration.

“Your father would have taken it apart before breakfast,” she said.

Matylde’s hand stopped above the exposed mechanism.

“Would he?”

“And insisted he could put it back.”

“Could he?”

“Usually with someone standing beside him looking worried.”

The girl smiled. The maker, who had begun looking worried on his own account, smiled too. He let her lift the float carefully between finger and thumb. The hammer stopped halfway through its movement.

Riarhin watched her discover that she could make it strike too soon, then too late, and then at the proper interval. She forgot Isbray for several minutes. Jaennis had chosen well.

A woman arrived at the arch and waited to be noticed. Jaennis went to her, leaving Matylde absorbed in the little house. The woman spoke quietly. Her face was angry; then ashamed of having been angry; then, after Jaennis answered, relieved. Riarhin could not hear the words.

When Jaennis returned, Matylde asked, “Is it settled?”

“For now.”

“She’ll be all right?”

“I hope so.”

Matylde reached for Jaennis’s hand without looking away from the garden. “You shouldn’t have to do everything.”

Jaennis’s fingers closed around hers.

“I have you.”

The girl looked up. She moved nearer Jaennis so that their shoulders touched, though this made it harder to see the float.

The maker replaced the panel. His son poured in more water. They demonstrated a second figure, a woman drawing a bucket from a well, and Matylde asked whether the two could be made to meet. The maker said that would require a different arrangement. She asked how long it would take.

Jaennis told her that the man had already brought a wonderful thing and should not be sent home with another week’s work because the queen had a new thought. Matylde apologised immediately. The maker protested that he would be honoured to attempt it.

“Another day,” Jaennis said.

Riarhin returned to the fitting room when the seamstress called. The apprentice was trying not to look through the doorway. Riarhin stepped aside so she could see the bird, now flying its little circle quite properly, until her mistress called her back.

Jaennis had company by the time Riarhin came out with the altered coat.

Matylde had gone upstairs with the maker’s son to see the little garden put back in its case. In the court below, a man was waiting with a flat leather wallet held beneath his arm. Jaennis introduced him as Councillor Tessart. He had been on the council when the old household commissions were renewed, and wished to speak about the northern hearing.

Riarhin had seen his signature on Edrune’s papers.

He looked at her coat, then at the folded bundle in her hand. She had expected the usual glance at her ears or the unmarked place where her sword would hang. His attention to the clothes was more deliberate.

“An extended visit, then.”

“As long as the hearing requires.”

“I hope it will require very little.”

Jaennis smiled. “You have both said how much you would like these questions answered. I thought it would be pleasant to begin from an agreement.”

Tessart turned towards her. “Some questions have already been answered.”

“Then you can bring the answers.”

“I intend to.”

“Do bring the earlier papers as well. Matylde is becoming very particular about what went before.”

He looked at Riarhin again.

“The hearings were conducted under authority which the Crown has not repudiated.”

“Nobody has asked you to repudiate the Crown,” Jaennis said. “I certainly haven’t.”

She offered him the chair recently occupied by the maker. Its cushion had fallen sideways. He put it straight before sitting, laid the wallet across his knees and undid its straps. Riarhin remained standing. The escort was waiting beneath the arch; she had no reason to stay, except that Jaennis had placed a cup for her on the table.

Tessart said the northern guarantee might become difficult if the hearing were understood to excuse earlier disloyalty. Some of the money came from people still waiting to be repaid for service during Veralyn’s reign.

“I am sure they will tell us,” Jaennis said.

“They have told me.”

“Then you must speak for them. Before the queen decides.”

He took a paper from the wallet. Riarhin recognised the arrangement of its paragraphs before she could read the words: the renewed restriction, copied with the dead steward’s name. Tessart laid his hand on it.

“And the other families? The families who lost someone when this woman came here before?”

Riarhin felt the old sharp willingness to answer enter her voice before she spoke.

“They can ask me.”

Tessart did not look at her. “Through whom?”

“Through the appointed officer,” Jaennis said. “I have asked that their requests be heard.”

“All of them?”

“All that can properly be put to her. She will be attending for weeks.”

The paper disappeared beneath Tessart’s hand as he slid it back into the wallet. He had heard an invitation. Riarhin was not certain whether it was the one Jaennis meant him to hear.

A servant came with a message about the supper arrangements. Jaennis waved it away without looking. She was watching Tessart buckle the wallet. When he took his leave she gave him both her hands and thanked him for being so frank.

After he had gone, the servant approached again. This time she listened, decided which table could be moved and sent him away.

“You enjoyed that,” Riarhin said.

“Did I?”

“Yes.”

Jaennis laughed. It was a younger sound than Riarhin expected from her.

“He hasn’t brought me anything I didn’t know in months. At least today he was trying.”

“Those orders concern people who can’t sell their own trees.”

“I know what the orders concern.”

The laughter had gone, but the animation remained. Jaennis moved Tessart’s untouched cup beside her own and looked at the damp ring beneath it.

“He would like you to say something unforgivable before the hearing. Do try not to oblige him.”

“And you?”

“I should like Matylde to hear him speak as plainly as he has just spoken to us.”

She called the servant back and asked that the northern guarantor be seated beside Tessart at supper. Not opposite; beside. They had been avoiding each other for too long.

Riarhin took her coat and went to the waiting escort.

Chapter 9 Who may answer

The clerk asked a question intended for the man beside Edrune. Riarhin answered it.

The clerk put down his pen.

“Was I unclear?”

“No.”

“Then I will ask it again.”

Edrune looked at her without expression. Riarhin leaned back.

The man had once carried messages for a northern official. His name was Velden, and he wore a good coat that had belonged to a larger person. The shoulders stood away from him. Whenever he moved, one cuff slid over his fingers and had to be pushed back.

He knew who had ordered the horses searched at the yard where Berent changed mounts. He had delivered the instruction. He could describe the weather, the official’s meal and the dog sleeping beside the hearth. When asked what the instruction had said, he stopped.

“Will he see this?”

“The account will be entered,” the clerk said.

“Will he see it?”

“He may be required to answer it.”

Velden pulled the cuff back.

“My wife is still in his house.”

“As a servant?”

“With our youngest.”

The clerk made a note. Riarhin waited for him to say something useful and was angry when he asked the original question once more.

Velden shook his head.

It was almost imperceptible. The clerk saw it and set the pen down again.

“We’ll stop there.”

Outside, Riarhin offered to arrange for Velden’s wife to leave. He looked so frightened that she stopped speaking before she could explain how.

“She doesn’t want to leave,” he said. “She has the dairy.”

“I meant if she were in danger.”

“She has the dairy,” he repeated.

Edrune took his arm and led him towards the bench in the shade. Riarhin stayed where she was until the clerk came out carrying a jug. He gave it to her.

“Water might be more use.”

She carried it over.

Velden drank without lifting his eyes. Edrune had taken off her coat and put it between him and the cold stone of the bench. He seemed not to have noticed.

The clerk came out again carrying his paper. Riarhin prepared to object. He did not ask another question about the order.

“Who is paying for your room?”

Velden looked up. “I am.”

“Have you applied for the witness allowance?”

“I didn’t know there was one.”

The clerk sighed, then saw the man flinch and checked himself. “That is our fault. I’ll have it entered.”

“Will my name go back to the house?”

“It will go on the allowance list.”

“Who sees that?”

The clerk took a moment to answer. “The people who pay it. The people who check that it was paid. I can’t promise nobody else will ask.”

Velden turned the cup between his palms.

“Then I won’t.”

Riarhin looked at Edrune. Edrune shook her head slightly. Let him, the motion said; or wait. Riarhin was not certain which, and for once the uncertainty kept her from moving.

The clerk sat on the low wall opposite them. His robes pulled at the knees and he arranged them with the distraction of a man who had stopped remembering that he wore them.

“We can enter the claim and withhold collection until you decide.”

“Will you enter why?”

“That you wish to decide later. Nothing more unless you ask.”

Velden nodded. The clerk made a brief note.

“Would it help to have your wife asked separately what she wants?” Riarhin said.

The man looked frightened again, but this time remained facing her.

“She’ll say she’s well.”

“Is she?”

“Mostly. She has a good room. The mistress likes her. Our youngest gets milk every day.”

“Then what are you afraid will happen?”

Velden opened his mouth. His shoulders rose as he tried to draw breath. He leaned forward suddenly, putting the cup on the ground before it could spill.

Edrune laid a hand flat between his shoulder blades.

“Wait. There’s no question now.”

Riarhin knelt in front of him. His lips were pale. She asked whether he had a pain in his chest. He shook his head. When she asked him to look at her, his eyes moved past her to the patch of sun on the wall.

“Cold,” he said.

The day was mild. His hands, when she touched them, were dry and icy.

The clerk went for help. By the time an attendant arrived with a blanket Velden could speak again. He apologised to each of them separately. Edrune interrupted the third apology to ask him whether he preferred water or something warm. He chose warm.

Riarhin remained kneeling after the others moved away. She remembered the halt in his speech inside the room, how closely it had resembled a man deciding whether honesty would cost him too much. She had been angry with him for letting fear choose. The cold remained in her fingertips.

When Jaennis’s name was suggested, later, she raised no objection.

The woman who asked about the dead guard came that afternoon.

She had sent a note requesting an account in the presence of an officer. Solenne attended herself. Riarhin was grateful for the formality until the woman placed a small repaired glove on the table.

“My brother,” she said.

Riarhin did not recognize it.

“He was in the lower passage.”

“I didn’t see the men there.”

“Your people did.”

“Yes.”

The admission left her briefly without the next question.

“Was he told to stand aside?”

“I don’t know.”

“Was he armed?”

“I don’t know.”

“Did you ask?”

Riarhin looked at the glove. A piece of darker leather had been sewn across the base of the thumb.

“No.”

The woman drew it back towards herself.

“He had a daughter. She’s married now. Her husband says we should leave it. There are people who say your quarrel was with the queen and therefore—”

She stopped. Solenne waited.

“Was it necessary to kill him?”

“I can’t tell you that.”

“But you told them to stop anyone coming.”

“Yes.”

The woman stood before Riarhin could say anything else.

“I wanted that entered.”

“It will be,” Solenne said.

“I don’t want her money.”

“Nobody has offered it.”

“I know how these things are finished.”

She put the glove into her bag and left. Riarhin remained seated while Solenne read back the brief account of the meeting. Every answer sounded poorer the second time.

At the door, an older man was waiting for the woman. Riarhin could see him through the gap before the guard closed it. He took the bag from her and listened while she spoke. She shook her head at something he said.

“Who is that?” Riarhin asked.

Solenne went to look. By then both had moved beyond the window.

“I’ll ask.”

Riarhin picked up the pen and signed beneath the account.

Chapter 10 The reason given in confidence

Matylde had read Deliane’s application.

She had written three questions in the margin and crossed out one. Deliane answered the remaining two, including the question about how many guests could be housed without moving the staff into the room beneath the eaves.

“Fewer than the last return claimed,” she said.

“How many fewer?”

“Six, if you wish them to remain people.”

Matylde smiled. Solenne did not, but made a note.

Riarhin was present because Edrune’s party had been proposed for rooms in the house while the hearing continued. She stood beside the window, listening to an argument about blankets, and was beginning to think the morning might be useful without becoming painful.

Then Jaennis asked whether Deliane had considered who supplied the wine.

Deliane’s face altered.

“I have.”

“His brother still acts for the house.”

“I know.”

Matylde looked from one woman to the other.

“Whose brother?”

Jaennis explained.

She did not raise her voice or use a word that sounded scandalous. She described a man who had once enjoyed Deliane’s confidence and afterwards used his knowledge to obstruct her employment. His brother had access to the guest house’s deliveries. It would be unwise, she said, to place Deliane where the same influence might reach her again without examining the arrangements.

Deliane stood very still.

“You needn’t be embarrassed,” Jaennis told her.

Riarhin looked at the queen’s crossed-out question. It concerned repairs to the upper stair.

“Should we wait?” she asked.

“A little care now,” Jaennis said.

“I’ve changed the supplier,” Deliane said.

“Temporarily.”

“Subject to the appointment.”

“Which is why we should examine it.”

Solenne asked for the replacement supplier’s name. Deliane gave it. The appointment was held pending confirmation that the new arrangement would be reliable.

Afterwards, Riarhin found Deliane in the passage pressing the edge of her application against her palm until it bent.

“Is he still a danger?”

“Yes,” Deliane said. “To anyone who lets him be.”

“Then she was right to ask.”

Deliane looked at her.

“She could have asked me.”

Riarhin said nothing.

“I knew about the brother. I knew about the wine. I had written to another supplier before I asked for the post. She knows that too.”

“Then why—”

“Please don’t make me explain what just happened while it’s still happening.”

Riarhin stepped back.

Deliane smoothed the application, making the crease worse, and went downstairs.

Riarhin found the replacement wine merchant herself that afternoon, then stopped outside his shop.

The door stood open. A boy was rubbing chalk from an old price board. Inside, two men were lifting a cask onto a cradle while a woman told them that if they cracked the tap she would take the loss from both their wages. They were laughing at something she had said before Riarhin arrived.

She could go in. She could ask whether the merchant had agreed to supply the guesthouse, whether he knew the former supplier’s family and what pressure he expected. She was good at making people answer the question beneath the one she asked. The appointment might be helped by what she learned.

Deliane had not asked her to do it.

Riarhin stood long enough that the boy offered to fetch his mother.

“No, thank you.”

“Did you want a bottle?”

She almost bought one to account for standing there. Then she thought of carrying it back to Deliane as proof that she had done nothing intrusive.

“No.”

The boy shrugged and went on cleaning the board.

At the lodging, Coryd was trying to persuade a knot to leave a piece of wood. She looked up when Riarhin came in empty-handed.

“Good walk?”

“Pointless.”

“Those can be good.”

Riarhin sat beside the window. Her sister turned the wood over and tried the other side.

After a while she said, “I owe someone an apology.”

“Then I suggest you give it to them.”

“She asked me to stop.”

“Then stop first.”

She took off her gloves. Outside, a pair of girls were sharing a roasted root from a paper twist. One took too large a bite; the other snatched it back, indignant, and they went on down the street arguing with their mouths full.

When Deliane came to collect the repaired runner two days later, Riarhin asked whether she could say something about their last conversation. Deliane looked wary but nodded.

“I answered you as if she had discovered a danger you hadn’t considered. I didn’t ask what you had done.”

“No.”

“I’m sorry.”

Deliane shifted the wood beneath her arm. “I did love him, you know.”

Riarhin waited.

“The man. Before. I wasn’t always waiting for him to become somebody I could explain to other people. For a while he was funny, and he listened, and I liked the way he held a cup with two fingers as if it were beneath his dignity to be thirsty.”

She smiled despite herself, then seemed to resent the memory.

“Jaennis remembers the things I said when it was worst. Sometimes I think those are the only things anyone will let me have of it.”

“You needn’t tell me the rest.”

“I wasn’t going to. I was going to carry this across the city without being told it was too heavy.”

Riarhin stepped away from the door.

Deliane left. That evening Riarhin found herself trying to hold a cup with two fingers. She almost dropped it.

The wine merchant came to the guesthouse with a woman and a child, neither of whom Deliane had expected.

Riarhin was there with Edrune to inspect the proposed petitioners’ rooms. She saw the merchant in the yard, a heavy man wearing good shoes which he kept checking for mud. The woman carried a tied packet under her arm. The child was a boy old enough to resent being called one. He had a brush tucked through his belt.

“My wife,” the merchant said. “She keeps the orders.”

Deliane offered the woman her hand. The merchant looked pleased. His wife looked relieved to have the packet taken from her before he could begin explaining what was in it.

They went into the kitchen, where the cook set out cups with the resigned air of a woman whose table was being used for things which prevented dinner. The merchant produced two sample bottles. His wife laid out the written terms.

No one mentioned the previous supplier until Deliane did.

“You understand why the deliveries must be made by your people.”

“Yes, mistress.”

“And why a substitution has to be agreed here.”

“Yes.”

The boy looked down. His mother caught the movement.

“What?”

“Nothing.”

She said his name, too softly for Riarhin to hear.

“The cart,” he said.

The merchant began to speak. His wife held up a hand.

“What about it?”

“We don’t always use ours.”

“I know that.”

“When the axle went, we hired the other house’s.”

Deliane put the bottle down.

“The previous supplier’s?”

The merchant spread his hands. “Carts are hired. We can’t keep an extra one for a broken axle.”

“And the driver?”

“Sometimes their man, sometimes ours.”

His wife looked at the terms, then at Deliane.

“We can specify the driver.”

“Will he bring anyone with him?”

“A boy to help.”

“Which boy?”

The merchant looked annoyed. “Are we to name everybody who touches a cask between our cellar and your door?”

“No,” Deliane said. “Everybody who comes through the door.”

His wife nodded, then paused. “That will affect days when our man is ill.”

“Send word. We’ll take the wine at the outer yard gate and carry it in.”

The cook, who had been listening from the hearth, said, “Which people?”

Deliane turned to her. There was a silence, followed by a short laugh from the merchant’s wife. Deliane smiled despite herself and asked the cook which days she could spare someone. The answer was none of them, but after further discussion it became two afternoons, provided the flour was not coming then.

Riarhin tasted the wine while they worked it out. It was rough, sound and better suited to feeding tired people than impressing a court. Edrune drank hers and asked what it cost without water added. The merchant looked offended. His wife answered promptly.

They wrote the driver’s name and a replacement’s name beneath the terms. The boy would make the deliveries himself for the first month. He looked up at that, pleased with the responsibility until his father told him the casks would not become lighter for having his name written down.

When the terms were ready, Deliane read them aloud. She stumbled over a measure and asked the merchant to explain it again. He did, this time without the impatience which had made Riarhin ready to dislike him.

The cook wanted the empty bottles. The merchant said they were included in the price only if the contract was accepted. Deliane signed. The cook took them before the ink dried.

At the gate the merchant’s wife paused beside Riarhin.

“I know who you are,” she said.

Riarhin waited.

“My husband wanted you asked to leave.”

“Why didn’t he ask?”

“I told him the lady was appointing a wine merchant, not choosing her acquaintances to please him.”

“Thank you.”

The woman did not smile. “I didn’t do it for you.”

She went to help her son secure the empty case. The boy had used too much rope. She let him finish before showing him where one fastening would have done.

Inside, Deliane was copying the names for the porter. Riarhin put her cup down beside her.

“You made a better arrangement than I would have.”

Deliane kept writing. “You’d have bought another cart.”

Riarhin laughed. “I might.”

“I haven’t the money. It helps me think.”

She looked up at last. “Don’t tell anyone I said that. They’ll reduce the salary.”

Chapter 11 Still here in the morning

Coryd returned to Jaennis’s household to collect the piece of wood Deliane had promised for the chest runner. Riarhin accompanied her to ask about two visitors named in the guest-house returns.

They found Deliane at breakfast with her hair unbound and a man’s coat around her shoulders. The man wanted it back. She told him he should have thought of that before leaving it beside a cold woman.

“I left it beside a chair.”

“An error. Look at what you’ve married.”

He turned to Coryd for support. Coryd said that in her experience chairs could not be trusted.

The room was full of people preparing to go somewhere. A lady had lost an earring. A young officer had found an earring but was withholding the news until the lady finished describing him as useless. Two women were discussing a mare in terms that made Riarhin wonder, until one mentioned a fetlock, whether they meant a third woman.

Sivelle came in wearing the other of the missing earrings.

The lady who had lost the pair stared at her. Sivelle touched her ear, remembered and began laughing. She took it off and held it out.

“I thought we agreed these were mine for the evening.”

“The evening ended some hours ago.”

“An austere view.”

Madra came after her with a letter. She had opened it so roughly that one edge was torn almost to the writing. She tried to tuck it away when she saw the sisters and then, apparently deciding she would have an audience whether she wished it or not, sat down heavily.

“Seven,” she said.

“Your husband?” Deliane asked.

“My husband’s sister. He has given up direct communication and sent artillery.”

The knight held out his hand. Madra refused him the letter.

“She wants me home for the choosing of a tutor.”

“For the boy?”

“For the household dog, if she has her way. The boy will be expected to learn by observation.”

Riarhin watched the laughter arrive a little late.

“How old?” Coryd asked.

“Eleven. Quite old enough to tell three adults what he thinks of their decision, which is why they would like me there to make him civil.”

“Do you want to go?”

Madra looked at her. “I want to choose the tutor.”

“Then go and choose him.”

“And while I’m there, settle the winter servants. And the steward’s quarrel with the mill. And whether my husband’s sister may put her own woman over the linen.”

Sivelle sat beside her and took the torn letter without asking. Madra let her.

“Write that you will come for six days.”

“He’ll make it seven.”

“Then leave on the fifth. Confuse him.”

Madra laughed this time, but only briefly. She pulled at the tablecloth until a cup moved.

“I’ll miss the riding.”

“We’ll be here when you return,” Deliane said.

“Will you?”

Deliane looked down at her borrowed coat. Sivelle refolded the letter along a new crease, hiding the tear.

Madra said, “I didn’t mean—”

“I know.”

“You might still come to supper.”

“I intend to.”

“Good. Somebody has to tell me what you all said while I was away.”

Sivelle put the letter back into Madra’s hand. “All of it unkind. Save yourself the trouble and imagine the worst.”

Madra leaned against her shoulder for a moment. Sivelle kissed the top of her head. Riarhin looked out of the window, giving them what privacy she could without leaving the table.

Below, Orven was trying to persuade a man to stop unloading the wrong cart. The man, offended by the implication that he could not recognise a cart, continued unloading it while he argued.

Deliane gave Coryd the promised wood and answered Riarhin’s questions without consulting a book. She had stayed the night, she said, because the conversation about the appointment had needed more time.

“And did it?” Riarhin asked.

“It had more time.”

Jaennis came in carrying the missing earring. The officer looked betrayed by fortune. She handed it to the lady and asked Deliane whether she had eaten anything warm.

“I’m wearing his coat.”

“That won’t sustain you.”

“It has sustained him for years.”

Jaennis laughed and put a hand briefly on her shoulder before taking the chair beside her. Deliane leaned towards her without thinking. Riarhin looked away.

A household officer appeared with an altered list of duties.

“Since you’re staying, my lady, I thought we could settle the visitors for next week.”

Deliane took the list.

“I haven’t said I’m staying.”

The officer’s expression changed slowly.

“I understood—”

“From whom?”

He looked at Jaennis.

“I said the matter required further consideration,” she said. “Which it does.”

“The girl from the western house has been told she needn’t come,” the officer said. “To assist with the correspondence.”

“Tell her she is still wanted,” Deliane said.

“She has accepted something else.”

Nobody spoke for a moment. The young officer surrendered the chair he had been occupying and left with an exaggerated concern for the time.

Deliane laid the list flat.

“I will need a replacement here. That was why I gave notice when I did.”

“And we shall find one,” Jaennis said.

“Before I leave.”

“Yes.”

“On the date in my application.”

Jaennis withdrew her hand from the table.

“If everything is ready.”

Deliane looked at Riarhin.

“Would you leave us?”

Riarhin had wanted the names of the two visitors confirmed in writing. She put the paper away and rose. Coryd had already gathered the wood.

In the yard, her sister balanced the length across her shoulder.

“That looked unpleasant.”

“Yes.”

“Was it?”

“She asked us to leave.”

Coryd glanced back at the door.

“People ask that for more than one reason.”

“I know.”

They walked back through the crowded street. Riarhin kept clear of a man carrying a bundle of reeds and nearly collided with a woman she had been avoiding for a different reason. The woman stared, then turned sharply away.

Coryd watched her go.

“Another?”

“Yes.”

“How many?”

Riarhin took the heavier end of the wood from her.

“I didn’t count them at the time.”

They carried the wood halfway back before Coryd said, “Sivelle invited me for tomorrow.”

“The stair?”

“And supper.”

“Will there be people there?”

“Probably. She lives in a city.”

Riarhin shifted the wood higher. Her sister glanced at her.

“You could ask what you mean.”

“I don’t know her.”

“I’m hoping to improve on that.”

There were a great many things Riarhin could have said about the household, favours and women who knew how to ask for them. The scene at breakfast gave her material enough for an unpleasantly persuasive warning. Coryd had been at the same breakfast.

“Tell the guard where you go,” Riarhin said.

“I will.”

“That is an instruction about the protection, not about—”

“I know.”

They reached the lodging. The wood had rubbed a mark into the shoulder of Coryd’s coat. Riarhin brushed it with her hand before she thought, and Coryd stood still to let her finish.

Her sister did not return for supper the following evening.

She sent a note. The stair was worse than expected; the meal was better; she would remain the night and return after breakfast. The boy who brought the note waited because Coryd had instructed him to bring back any answer. Riarhin asked whether the household was busy. He said that he had only seen the kitchen and that it had seemed so.

She gave him a coin and wrote that the note had arrived. She added that the weather was turning and she ought to borrow a cloak. Then she crossed out the last line, wrote the note again and sent him away.

The cook had made enough for two. Riarhin ate one portion and asked whether the other could be kept. The woman told her it could be eaten by the guard, who was no less hungry for not being her sister.

“Of course.”

She took the plate to the stairhead. The guard thanked her and moved his cup from the edge of the rail. She had said nothing about it. It gave her an unreasonable sense of being known.

Later she sat by the window with the book she had brought for the journey. She read the same page three times. In the house opposite, someone was teaching a child a song. The child sang each line too soon, eager to reach the part in which something went wrong with a goose. The teacher kept beginning again.

Riarhin laughed once, alone in the room, and found that it hurt.

She took out the little bundle of letters she carried apart from the business of the journey. Ailsa’s writing had changed when she was tired. The letters sloped less, the spaces widened, and she put the date at the end rather than the beginning. Riarhin could recognise an evening from three words. She did not need to unfold the page she chose. She unfolded it anyway.

Outside, the song reached the goose at last. The child shouted the line. The teacher gave up and laughed with him.

Riarhin put the letter on the bed beside her. She lay down in her clothes for a while, and when the lamp began to smell she got up to trim it.

Coryd returned the next morning with her own cloak and a missing button. She was smiling at something which had happened before she entered the room. Riarhin recognised that private smile and looked back at her breakfast.

“The stair?” she asked.

“Abominable.”

“The supper?”

“Excellent.”

“And—”

Coryd leaned over and kissed her sister’s temple. “You may stop there.”

Riarhin reached for the bread.

“I saved none.”

“Good. I already ate.”

Her sister paused at the open window, listening to the child across the street begin again. Then she went upstairs. A few moments later she began humming the goose song. Riarhin stared at the stair, then laughed so hard that the cook came to see what had happened.

Chapter 12 The two sheets

The hearing began with Councillor Tessart, who wanted Riarhin tried before anyone asked him to surrender a profitable order.

He said this politely. He had brought copies of the original commissions and a letter from a provincial superior attesting to his diligence. He laid them out in an order that made his argument seem older than the request to hear it.

“If these associations are to be excused,” he said, “the Crown must first determine whether the acts they supported were excusable.”

Edrune leaned forward.

“My cousin’s daughter was two.”

“The order concerns the holding.”

“She planted trees.”

“The holding supported a household under inquiry.”

Matylde lifted her hand. Edrune stopped, breathing through her nose.

Riarhin had been told to stand when questioned. She was already standing. The room’s heat had gathered under her collar, and one of the seams newly altered for her movement was rubbing against the side of her neck.

“What did you ask of the northern households?” the queen said.

Riarhin answered.

She named the help she had sought and the people to whom she had spoken. She distinguished Berent’s earlier journey from her later plans. When asked whether she knew the help could put households in danger, she said yes.

“How much danger?”

“I couldn’t have told them all of it.”

“Did you tell them what you could?”

“Not always.”

The councillor asked the clerk to mark the answer. Riarhin could see him preparing the next question before the pen had finished moving.

“Did you consider that the men administering those holdings might have duties to perform?”

“Yes.”

“And that those duties could include withholding passage or property pending inquiry?”

“Yes.”

“Then you cannot object to the existence of the orders.”

“I object to what some of them are still being used to do.”

He lifted one of the papers. “This was renewed lawfully.”

“Who was asked before it was renewed?”

“The responsible office.”

“Who at the holding?”

He looked at the heading. The paper named a steward dead for four years. Riarhin knew because Edrune had shown her the burial entry at the posting house. She waited while the councillor found the attached copy.

“The current occupants had a means of petition.”

“They used it.”

“And they are being heard.”

“Today.”

He set the paper down with a little more force than needed.

Matylde asked Edrune how long ago the petition had first been sent. Edrune gave the date, then took out the copy of the receipt. The clerk compared the two. The dates agreed.

“Why wasn’t it heard then?” the queen asked.

“It was referred.”

“To whom?”

The answer required another paper. While it was fetched, the man whose family remained under a later restriction asked to speak. He had travelled with Edrune but had said little on the road. Riarhin knew him chiefly by his habit of checking the cart straps whenever anyone stopped talking.

“My brother was questioned,” he said. “They found nothing. He was released. The hold stayed.”

“Released without finding,” the councillor said. “That is not the same as cleared of association.”

“What would clear him?”

“The production of an adequate account.”

“He gave one.”

“Which was not sufficient to—”

“He died.”

The councillor stopped.

The man swallowed. “After. Not there. At home, last spring. He kept saying if he could get the man who questioned him to write one line, one line saying what he still wanted, he could answer it. There isn’t anyone left to answer now except me.”

Riarhin looked down. She had not known about the death. The man’s reserve on the road had been easy to respect because she had been preoccupied with her own arrival. Now every missed opportunity to ask presented itself, exact and useless.

Matylde asked for his brother’s account to be found. The clerk wrote a name in the margin. The man sat, rubbed both hands over his face and asked Edrune whether she had any water.

A servant brought a cup before she could reply. He drank half and passed it to her. She drank too. Riarhin watched this small exchange while the council returned to the terms of the order.

Edrune looked down. Riarhin had wanted her to look angry. The lowered head was worse.

The man with the commissions asked whether it was reasonable to leave orders in force when the principal offender admitted that the extent of her dealings was uncertain.

Matylde put the account of the killings on one side of her working space and the petitioners’ papers on the other.

“These are different questions.”

“They arise from the same offence.”

“Then we shall know which question we are answering.”

She proposed suspending the restrictions until new hearings could be held. Solenne asked whether the suspension included acts alleged after Riarhin had left the country.

Matylde hesitated.

“The restrictions on these people.”

“Some of the instruments name later acts. Some name no person at all, only a holding. We need to say which are suspended.”

The girl’s face reddened. Jaennis moved her chair slightly, preparing to speak.

Matylde turned back to Solenne.

“Can you separate them today?”

“With the clerk.”

“Do that. We will hear the ones you can identify tomorrow.”

Fayd, near the far end of the table, made a small mark beside something in his notes.

The hearing adjourned without the broad decision Riarhin had hoped for. Edrune gathered her papers and asked the clerk where she should wait for the revised list.

Riarhin followed her into the passage.

“I should have said—”

Edrune turned.

“What?”

Riarhin stopped.

There had been several things she might have added. None would alter the age of the child or the timber spoiling below the city.

“Nothing.”

“Then leave it for tomorrow.”

Edrune went to find the clerk. Riarhin waited for her sword with the seam still rubbing her neck. She could feel the place becoming sore. She had paid for the coat and had insisted on the fitting, and this was still how it sat.

The man whose brother had died was waiting outside the gate. He had missed the party going back to the guesthouse and was unwilling to ask for directions while people from the hearing remained within earshot.

Riarhin asked whether he meant to walk with them. He nodded and fell into step beside the escort, leaving a little distance between himself and her. Coryd was elsewhere that afternoon. There was nobody to make the first ordinary remark.

At the corner, a cart blocked the street while two men tried to lift a cracked barrel onto it. The escort stopped. Riarhin heard the petitioner sigh.

“Would you rather go round?” she asked.

He shook his head. “There’ll be another one.”

They waited. One of the men lifting the barrel lost his grip. The other swore, then apologised to an old woman who had said nothing about it. The petitioner stepped forward, put a shoulder beneath the rim and helped them get it up. He returned rubbing a mark from his sleeve.

“You’ve worked with those,” Riarhin said.

“We all have where I come from.”

“Where exactly?”

He told her. She knew the valley and the road entering it, but not the village. He seemed to recognise the distinction when she repeated the name of the crossing instead.

“My brother had the little mill below it.”

“Does his family still have it?”

“His wife. On paper.”

The cart moved. They walked beside it until the street widened.

“The wheel’s sound,” he said. “The stones need dressing. I could do the work if the men would come. They want to know whose permission matters. If they take my money and somebody says it wasn’t mine to pay, where does that leave them?”

“Who says that?”

“The man whose mill is two miles down.”

Riarhin looked at him. He shrugged.

“He’s not wicked every day. His daughter sends apples when they have more than they can keep. He helped bring my brother home. He’d still like the mill.”

The escort glanced back, interested despite himself. The petitioner noticed and stopped speaking. Riarhin did not press him.

At the guesthouse the cook had put out food for people returning from the hearing. She asked whether the man wanted a portion kept for his companion. He looked confused, then remembered that he had agreed to share a room with a witness who ate late. He said yes.

Riarhin sat opposite him while the bowl was brought. He thanked her for walking back, very formally.

“Will you be all right here?”

“Yes.”

She rose. Then he said, “You don’t have to find something to say about him.”

Riarhin stood with a hand on the chair.

“My brother. People want to know whether he was a good man. I can see them looking for the part that’ll make the answer easy.”

“What do you tell them?”

“Depends what day they ask.”

He smiled, unexpectedly. It lasted only a moment.

“He was a nuisance. He kept other people’s tools and complained they never lent him any. I want his wife to have the mill without having to make him better than he was.”

“I’ll remember that.”

He nodded, then looked down at his food. She left before he had to thank her again.

At the lodging, Riarhin set the copied orders on the table and looked for the man’s brother among them. She found the name. The inquiry described his associations, his refusals and the insufficiency of his answers. There was nothing about the millstones. She wrote the village and the mill’s location on a separate sheet and laid it on top.

Part III The queen’s instruction

Chapter 13 A person who can speak

Velden wanted Edrune present.

He asked for Riarhin afterwards, almost apologetically. She had been there, he said, on the road at the time. If he became confused, she would know which day he meant.

She agreed and then asked whether he understood that she might not remember the same part of it.

“I don’t need you to remember it for me.”

“No.”

“I need somebody not to say I imagined the weather.”

The morning in question had been bitterly cold. He had delivered an instruction about searching horses and then stood outside for nearly an hour because the official wished to question him again before releasing him. He had been made to swear that no account of the household’s private directions would pass his lips without leave. At the time it seemed an ordinary condition of service. Afterwards, whenever he approached a prohibited subject, he felt the yard’s cold enter his chest.

Jaennis listened without interrupting.

They were in a small workroom, not one of the rooms in which she received visitors. A kettle stood beside the hearth. On the table lay a white thread, a shallow dish and a pair of little shears. Riarhin had seen less equipment used for things she would not have permitted in the room.

“What do you want to be able to say?” Jaennis asked.

“The instruction.”

“All the household’s affairs?”

“No.”

“Then we needn’t ask for all of them.”

Velden looked relieved. Edrune moved her chair nearer his without touching him.

“Who was there when you swore?” Jaennis asked.

“The steward. A man from the household office. Two others.”

“Had the room been prepared?”

“I don’t know what you mean.”

“Was there something you were asked to hold, or drink, or touch?”

He looked at the kettle. “There was a cup. We all drank.”

“Do you remember the cup?”

“Yes.”

“Tell me about it.”

He described a plain earthen cup with a chip on its lip. It had been passed first to the man who held the written direction. Velden had expected him to drink and been surprised when he only put his fingers around it. When it came to Velden the liquid was still warm. He had been grateful for that; his hands had been numb from standing outside.

Jaennis listened. She did not ask whether he had known what the warmth would cost.

“Are you still in that service?”

“No.”

“Were you released from the oath when you left?”

“I was paid.”

“That is not what I asked.”

“I know. I don’t know the other answer.”

She nodded and moved the dish nearer him.

Riarhin disliked how readily she was accepting the room. The kettle, the thread, the woman’s hands at work: she had seen comfort used to lead people somewhere before they understood they had begun to move. But Velden’s breathing had steadied. He was looking at Jaennis as if she had asked the first sensible questions of the visit.

“What happens after?” Riarhin asked.

Jaennis looked at her. “After he can speak?”

“After whatever you do.”

“He will be able to give the account he has chosen to give. If the former household objects, the queen’s protection of a witness will have to be made effective by her officers. I cannot cut their displeasure out of the world.”

“And the other promises?”

“Remain other promises.”

Velden asked, “Will I forget what I said?”

“No.”

“Will I feel that I didn’t say it?”

Jaennis paused. “You may still wish you hadn’t. That part belongs to you.”

He looked at Edrune. She kept her hands in her lap.

“I want the instruction heard,” he said.

“Then say only that much now.”

He tried. The cold came into his chest. Riarhin saw it in the way he drew his shoulders close, as if defending a little warmth inside his coat. Jaennis put the thread across his palm without closing his fingers over it.

“You needn’t force it.”

He stared at the white line.

“Does it need blood?”

“No.”

His relief was so naked that Riarhin looked away. On the shelf behind Jaennis stood a row of cups, each different from the others. One had been mended at the handle. She counted them without intending to and stopped when she reached six.

Jaennis asked for the wording of the oath. He gave it in short portions, pausing to recover his breath. She repeated none of it. When he had finished, she asked him to say his wife’s name.

He did.

“Her place in the dairy won’t be changed by this,” Jaennis said. “We must attend to that separately. You understand?”

“Yes.”

“The man may still be angry.”

Velden nodded.

She waited until he looked at her again.

“Shall I?”

“Yes.”

The white thread burned badly at first. Jaennis held it lower in the flame. A small bright point moved along it, leaving a shape which should have fallen and did not. She spoke quietly enough that Riarhin heard the sounds without being certain of the words.

Velden pressed both hands against his chest.

Riarhin moved before she meant to. Edrune held up a hand.

The ash fell into the dish.

Velden coughed. He bent forward, coughed again and looked at the floor between his boots.

Jaennis gave him water. He drank, spilling some down his coat.

“The order was to find the grey horse,” he said.

No one spoke.

“The knight had changed horses. They knew where. I was told to have the men wait until he came out of the house, and if he wasn’t alone—”

He stopped and put a hand to his mouth.

Jaennis waited.

“If he wasn’t alone, they were to take whoever came with him.”

He looked at Edrune. The astonishment on his face made Riarhin’s own throat tighten.

“I can say it.”

“Yes,” Edrune said.

He began to cry, awkwardly and with angry little attempts to stop. Jaennis brought a cloth and knelt beside his chair. Riarhin saw the effort it cost her to get down. Velden noticed too and tried to help her rise before she had finished offering the cloth.

“Leave me a moment,” she said, smiling. “We are both being difficult.”

He laughed through the tears.

Afterwards she arranged for an officer to inquire discreetly into his wife’s position. She gave him the name and told him where to ask for the man. At the door, she touched his sleeve.

“If anyone troubles you, come to me first.”

“I will.”

Velden waited in the outer room after the working, though nobody had asked him to.

Jaennis had gone to rest. The little shears lay on the table where she had put them down. Riarhin had seen the effort it cost her to stand, and the small shake of her hand when she reached for the kettle. Deliane had looked at her, taken the kettle and asked everybody to give her a few minutes.

Now Velden sat with his coat folded across his knees. He could speak. He did not know where to begin.

Edrune asked whether he wanted to eat. He said he ought to give the account while it was possible.

“It will still be possible after a meal,” Riarhin said.

“Will it?”

She did not know enough to promise. She looked towards the door. Deliane emerged carrying the used cups.

“Lady Jaennis says you should eat,” she told him. “She’ll send word to the clerk.”

Velden stood immediately.

Riarhin saw Edrune notice it too. Neither said anything. Deliane led them to a room where a small meal had been laid out for people working through the afternoon. There was cheese, a dish of beans and the heel of a loaf. Velden took less than anybody else until Edrune cut another slice and put it on his plate.

“You’ll be hungry.”

“I don’t want to take—”

“They keep a kitchen.”

He smiled and ate.

The first thing he wanted to tell them concerned the dog. Riarhin almost interrupted; the instruction about the horse seemed more urgent. She caught herself as Velden described the animal sleeping beside the official’s hearth, its paws twitching against the tiles. He had watched it while he waited to be questioned again. A servant had stepped over it carrying the meal. The dog woke just long enough to see whether anything would fall, then went back to sleep.

“Warm,” he said. “I kept thinking how warm it was.”

Edrune broke a piece of cheese with both hands. She gave him half. He had finished his story about the dog before he noticed she had done it.

The instruction came after. Search the grey horse first. If the knight had changed mounts, take the people travelling with him. Wait for someone who would know what to ask. Velden said the words without stopping. Then he stopped anyway, astonished to have reached the end.

Riarhin could see Berent on the road as he had been, younger than the man he would become, trying to look like an ordinary traveller while carrying a message which could cost him his life. She kept that memory to herself. It would not help Velden to know all the people his instruction might have reached.

“That’s it,” he said.

Edrune nodded. “That’s what you came to say.”

He began to laugh. The laugh broke once and resumed, and then he was crying into the hand that held his bread. Edrune moved his plate so it would not fall. Riarhin went to the door and told the clerk, who had arrived while they ate, to give them a little time.

“How long?”

“I’ll come for you.”

The clerk looked past her, saw the man at the table and nodded.

When she returned, Velden was wiping his face with a cloth Deliane had brought. He asked Riarhin whether she would stand near the door while he gave the account, where he could see her without looking at the people writing.

“Yes.”

He ate the rest of his bread. Edrune refilled his cup. They finished the meal before calling the clerk.

The next morning the clerk asked whether the grey horse had been searched before or after the household’s other horses.

Velden looked at Edrune.

“Should I tell her first?”

“Who?”

“Lady Jaennis.”

The clerk set down his pen. Riarhin watched Velden pull back the cuff of the borrowed coat.

Chapter 14 Where the money went

Fayd’s office had moved.

Riarhin found him in a room whose window faced a blank wall across a narrow court. The light was poor. He had placed his table sideways to obtain what he could of it, and the arrangement made visitors turn awkwardly before reaching the chair.

“You’ve lost your view,” she said.

“I wasn’t paid for looking out.”

Edrune remained standing until he invited her to sit. She took the chair nearest the door.

The papers they required were already laid out. Fayd had marked the original commissions, subsequent renewals and the local directions issued in their names. Some renewals had copied descriptions of households whose occupants were dead. In one case the wrong holding had been named for four years because two properties shared a boundary and the clerk had copied the neighbour’s account.

“You knew?” Riarhin asked.

“I know now.”

“When did you know?”

He looked at her.

“Which error?”

She pushed the paper back towards him.

“The people who received these didn’t have that luxury.”

“No. That is why I proposed a permanent office capable of hearing corrections.”

“With power to enter every holding and question every household.”

“With power to obtain an answer.”

Edrune tapped the corner of her freight bill.

“Would it have obtained mine?”

Fayd read it. He read the attached storage demand and the offer from the local buyer. Then he drew another paper from the stack.

“The buyer’s brother married into the guarantor’s household.”

“Would it have obtained mine?” she repeated.

“Possibly.”

She leaned back.

Fayd did not appear offended.

“Lady Jaennis could have sent a note,” he said. “It would probably have released the timber within a week.”

“At what point would I have known to ask her?”

“At the point someone introduced you.”

“And the next load?”

He spread his hands slightly. Edrune looked at Riarhin, irritated to discover that her own questions had been made useful to an argument she did not entirely trust.

The regency settlement had ended several extraordinary powers and preserved others under named offices. Fayd had argued for broader continuation. Jaennis had obtained consent for a narrower arrangement by promising that unresolved cases would receive attention. Some did. Riarhin thought of the cook’s flour, the apprentice’s room, the soldier whose lodging was consuming the money owed to him.

On the shelf behind Fayd stood a small box bearing the old seal. Its lid was open. Inside lay the impression used to mark orders issued during the regency, struck through and rendered unusable. He had kept it where visitors could see it.

“Was that your idea?” Riarhin asked.

He followed her glance. “The clerk wished to know whether it should be melted.”

“And you wished people to remember.”

“I wished no one to claim an old impression was a new order. It is easier to show them this.”

Edrune looked from the box to the papers. “Were these made under it?”

“Some. Others under the emergency council before my appointment. More recently, under ordinary provincial powers.”

“So who could have stopped them?”

“At each renewal? Several people. Ask which one had both the means and the reason.”

“You had the means.”

Fayd turned one of the papers towards himself. For once he did not answer at once.

“I had means which were needed for other things. I chose where to use them.”

“Was my cousin’s holding ever among the things you chose?”

“No.”

Riarhin saw Edrune receive the answer. She had come for a cartload of wood and found herself sitting before a man for whom the years of obstruction had been one unchosen item among hundreds.

“Then what brought her back?” Riarhin asked. “Jaennis.”

“She had never ceased to have people here.”

“That wasn’t what I meant.”

“I know. She returned with an offer people wished to accept.”

He found the army settlement beneath a leather weight. It had several seals. Riarhin recognised two, though their holders had changed since she last saw them used. She asked which one guaranteed the pay of the men at the northern forts.

“The first instalment? This one. The rest depended on the others remaining in the agreement.”

“Could you not obtain it?”

“Not on terms I would accept.”

“What terms?”

“The end of my extraordinary appointment. A return of specified levies to provincial collection. Matylde’s confirmation of certain officers whom I would have preferred to replace.”

“And she accepted.”

“She obtained their acceptance of the queen in return.”

He said it without an ironic emphasis. Riarhin had been expecting one.

“Did you advise her against it?”

“Yes.”

“Matylde?”

“Yes.”

“Did she listen?”

“She listened. Then she signed.”

Edrune looked at the seals. “Did the men get their money?”

“Enough of it to make the next promise credible.”

She nodded slowly. Fayd set the settlement aside.

A knock interrupted them. A clerk entered to say that the man sent to inspect a bridge had returned without the measurement. The stream had been too high. Fayd asked whether he had brought the local mason’s account. He had. Fayd asked for that instead and told the clerk to send the inspector home; there was no use keeping him while the water decided whether to fall.

The clerk waited.

“What else?”

“The warrant needs a different countersign now.”

Fayd held out his hand for it, read the direction and crossed out the place where his own signature would once have been enough. He wrote a name above it and gave it back.

After the door closed, Edrune asked whether that meant another day’s waiting.

“Yes,” Fayd said.

She almost laughed. She did not quite manage it.

“What do you want me to tell her?” she asked.

“The queen?”

“Yes.”

“What you have found.”

“And what would you like me to recommend?”

He smiled then.

“A process that survives your departure.”

The outer clerk came back with another packet. It bore the same northern names, but its covering page concerned money offered to guarantee army pay. Fayd took it and passed the covering page to Edrune.

“Did your guarantor tell you who would carry his share if he withdrew?”

“No.”

“Tessart’s associates have offered to.”

“With the money they’re taking to keep my timber in the rain?”

“Some of it may be borrowed.”

“That will comfort the trees.”

Riarhin watched Fayd’s clerk follow the page with his eyes.

“Are you permitted to give her that?” she asked.

“It is an offer to the Crown. She may ask why it names her hearing as a condition.”

“And you would like her to ask where Jaennis can hear it.”

“I would like someone to ask while the money can still be refused.”

Edrune folded it into her papers. “I’ll choose when.”

Fayd inclined his head.

“Will you bring back your office through this?” Riarhin asked.

“I will ask for the power to inspect the guarantees.”

“Jaennis will oppose you.”

“She already has. She thinks Tessart’s friends will expose one another if they are allowed to compete.”

“Will they?”

“Some. The most foolish will give the clever ones time to agree what they remember.”

Riarhin looked at the packet he was still holding.

“You sound as though you’ve tried it.”

Fayd smiled. The clerk kept his eyes on the paper.

Riarhin gathered the copies.

“I’ll recommend that she hear the people asking for one.”

“That will take longer.”

“She has already started.”

At the door, Fayd said her name.

She turned.

“There are people who regard your protection as a personal insult.”

“I’ve met some.”

“Not all of them will trouble you with an account of their feelings.”

“Do you have a name?”

“Several. Hostility isn’t evidence of an intended act.”

“Send the names to Solenne.”

“I have.”

Outside, Edrune waited until they were beyond the narrow court.

“Was that a warning?”

“Yes.”

“Was it useful?”

“I’ll know when I speak to Solenne.”

Edrune adjusted the strap of her bag.

“He could make a woman grateful for being told it’s raining while standing in it.”

Riarhin looked at the cloudless strip of sky above the roofs and laughed.

They met Cedric in the public court.

Riarhin had not seen him enter. He was standing beside a stone basin with a man from the old household office, listening while the other man pointed out someone crossing beneath the far arch. When he turned, the mark came into view before the rest of his face.

He had aged around it. The scar had drawn one part of the skin tight enough that his ordinary expression looked faintly amused. He wore a good coat with no current household badge and a sword which he had been permitted to retain in the outer court. Riarhin’s was in the custodian’s keeping.

Kerros moved closer to her without touching her arm.

Cedric saw the movement. His gaze went from the escort badge to Riarhin and rested there.

“Under the queen’s protection.”

“Yes,” Kerros said.

“I wasn’t asking you.”

“You have your answer.”

For a moment the two men looked at one another. Riarhin knew the space between them: three strides, wet paving beside the basin, a servant carrying a load behind Cedric who would have nowhere to go if the first movement frightened him. She did not put her hands together. She left them loose at her sides.

Cedric looked back at her.

“I was told you would return as yourself.”

“I have.”

“It saves people remembering which courtesy they owe.”

The clerk beside him made a small movement, neither agreement nor protest.

“Audemar has brought an account,” Cedric said. “Someone ought to read it.”

Edrune stood a little behind Riarhin with her freight papers held against her body.

“Is there business you wish to put before the hearing?” Riarhin asked.

Cedric smiled. “A great deal. I’m considering where to begin.”

“Then write it down.”

“Will you answer?”

“The queen will decide what she hears.”

“That sounds familiar.”

He moved aside. It was the correct courtesy to an escorted guest. Kerros waited until the path was clear, then told Riarhin to come.

She passed within an arm’s length of Cedric. The temptation to look directly at the scar was almost physical. She looked at his eyes instead. They had not changed as much as the rest of him.

Behind them he said, to the clerk, “Take it to the office. They still know me there.”

Riarhin heard the words clearly. They might have concerned the paper in the man’s hand. She made herself keep walking.

At the inner door Kerros asked whether she wanted to change the afternoon’s arrangements.

“No.”

“Think before answering.”

She looked at him. His tone had not been insolent. He was asking her to perform the part of his work he could not do for her.

“Keep the escort as agreed. Tell Solenne whom we met.”

“I will.”

Edrune waited until they were beyond Cedric’s hearing.

“You know him.”

“Yes.”

“Did he help keep these orders in force?”

“He has helped enforce orders. I can’t tell you which without checking.”

“That wasn’t the question I wanted to ask.”

“I know.”

Edrune glanced at her. “Then I’ll ask the clerk.”

Riarhin was grateful. She was also ashamed of the gratitude. At the sword counter she had difficulty making the fingers of her left hand close properly around the hilt. She asked for a moment. The custodian stood back and let her fasten the belt herself.

She gave Solenne the whole encounter that evening. Kerros had already done so. The accounts differed in one respect: he had heard Cedric say which courtesy they owe, while she had initially repeated whether they owe any courtesy. Solenne pointed to the difference.

Riarhin closed her eyes. The words returned, the inflection with them, Cedric’s mouth pulled slightly by the scar.

“Your officer is right.”

Solenne corrected the page.

“Do you think he meant a threat?”

“I think he wanted me to hear one.”

“That isn’t the same thing.”

“No.”

“I’m asking you because you know him.”

Riarhin looked at the page. “I know what I did to him.”

Solenne waited. Riarhin gave the account of the brand without offering the justification first. The officer asked whether she had held the Road Warden’s office at the time. She had. Whether the punishment was within its ordinary use. It had not been an ordinary punishment.

“What would you call it?”

“Mine.”

Solenne looked up. Riarhin heard the neatness of the answer and disliked it.

“I chose to hurt him where he would carry it,” she said. “I wanted him afraid of trying me again.”

The officer wrote without comment. When she had finished, she asked Riarhin not to meet Cedric without an officer present.

“I don’t intend to meet him.”

“He may intend to meet you.”

Riarhin accepted the instruction. On her way out, she saw Cedric’s name added to a list which already held several others. Some were people she had expected. One was a woman who had been kind to her under the borrowed face. She looked at that name longest.

Chapter 15 Without a guarantee

The queen’s clerk had separated the papers into four groups.

Matylde moved one document from the third to the second after asking a question about the date. The clerk checked it and agreed. Riarhin watched the girl’s pleasure at the small success and hoped she would not try to repeat it to feel it again.

Jaennis offered guarantees for three households whose connections she could establish. It was a useful offer. One of the petitioners began thanking her before Matylde had answered.

“Your account will be entered,” the queen said.

“I can speak for them.”

“Yes.”

“Then these need not wait.”

Matylde looked at Edrune.

“Would your cousin’s daughter come under that guarantee?”

“No, Your Majesty.”

“Why not?”

“She doesn’t know her.”

Jaennis’s face remained composed.

“I could inquire.”

“I want the officer to inquire,” Matylde said. “The one appointed to hear them.”
