The provincial guarantor objected to the expense. He had accepted a limited contribution on the understanding that existing assurances would suffice. A fresh hearing, with travel and officials, would fall outside that understanding.

Edrune took out the page Fayd had given her and asked permission to have it read. The guarantor leaned towards her, one hand extended; she passed it to the queen’s clerk instead.

The replacement money depended on the northern restrictions remaining in force until the Crown had reviewed all associated claims. Tessart said that was ordinary prudence. Edrune asked whether his associates would pay the storage while that happened.

He answered the queen instead of her.

Matylde listened, then returned the paper to Edrune. “We have not accepted this offer.”

“Your Majesty will require the money from somewhere,” Tessart said.

Jaennis’s fingers moved against her mouth. Riarhin had seen her conceal a smile that way at supper. The guarantor saw it too and sat farther from Tessart.

“Then what will you contribute?” Matylde asked.

He gave a figure smaller than the one before them.

The girl looked at her clerk. He looked at the paper and did not help her by pretending the difference was slight.

They adjourned for an hour.

Riarhin found herself in the passage with the petitioners. Edrune sat on the floor because there were no chairs. Velden remained standing until she pulled his sleeve and pointed to the place beside her.

After a few minutes the clerk came for Riarhin and Edrune. The queen wanted to know how many households could be heard in one journey. Edrune asked whether the queen wanted a hopeful answer or a useful one. The clerk said, with a tired smile, that he had been sent specifically for the useful one.

The smaller room had not been prepared for a meeting. Matylde sat at a table laid for her midday meal. A servant was removing the covered dishes while the councillors found places for their papers. The girl reached beneath one cover, took a piece of bread and ate it while listening.

A councillor proposed meeting the missing expense from the expected sale of a Crown lease. Fayd asked whether the buyer had paid his first instalment. He had not. Jaennis suggested a personal approach to the original guarantor, whose wife had received assistance from her household during an illness.

“He won’t wish to seem ungrateful,” she said.

Edrune looked at Riarhin, then back at the queen, waiting for her answer.

Matylde swallowed her bread.

“If he agrees for your sake, does he decide which houses are heard?”

“He will want to know that his money is used carefully.”

“That wasn’t the question.”

Jaennis’s face closed a little. “I can ask him.”

The girl looked down at the plate. Riarhin saw the apology approaching. This time Matylde took another piece of bread and chewed before speaking.

“How much is actually available?”

The councillor named a sum. Fayd corrected the date on which part of it would become available. The councillor accepted the correction with a look he did not trouble to conceal.

Edrune had been standing beside the window. She put her packet on the edge of the table.

“Send one officer as far as the first crossing. Have the people beyond it come there.”

“Several are restricted from travel,” the clerk said.

“Give them leave for the hearing.”

“Some cannot leave the work.”

“Then hear the ones who can and set a day to go to the others. If you wait until the journey is perfect, you will still be discussing it when the river rises.”

“How many could come?” Matylde asked.

Edrune named five with confidence, two more with conditions. One woman would need somebody to remain with a sick father. One man would require a declaration that leaving the holding did not concede the disputed possession. Edrune knew these things because she had asked where her next load was coming from and why people could not promise it.

Riarhin found herself listening with the same close attention she once gave scouts. There was a route here, but she had not walked it.

Matylde asked whether a hearing at the crossing would satisfy the promise made to the petitioners.

“Some,” Edrune said.

“Would it satisfy you?”

“If the timber is released now. If the others get a day. If you name who comes back when the day arrives.”

The councillor began to object to committing an officer beyond the quarter. Matylde pushed her plate aside.

“Then name the office. People can die or be ill. I understand that. I want there to be somewhere to send when nobody comes.”

Fayd spoke for the first time in several minutes.

“That can be done.”

Riarhin looked at him. He had not said who should control the office. She could see the omission costing him something.

The remaining expense came from a reduced guarantee and a portion of the queen’s household allowance. Jaennis asked whether the allowance was already committed. Matylde said some of it was. They went through the commitments one by one. The queen surrendered a proposed enlargement of the musicians’ gallery and retained the payment to a household whose roof had failed. The carpenter working on the gallery would have to be paid for timber already cut.

The councillor found another source for that smaller sum. He had known of it all along, Riarhin suspected, but she did not ask. Matylde thanked him and made the clerk record it before anyone could think again.

When they returned to the larger chamber, the new officer’s first journey had been shortened. The people beyond the crossing would receive dates for later hearings. Two petitioners were visibly angry. Edrune went to sit among them and began explaining who could travel.

Matylde named the councillor who had found the alternative funding. She thanked him in front of Jaennis.

Then she gave the decision.

Specified restrictions would be suspended. Claims concerning later acts would remain for separate hearings. The river yard was to release Edrune’s contracted timber without requiring an admission about the original household inquiry. Storage charges would be heard as a distinct dispute.

The guarantor who had withdrawn part of his support bowed as though he had helped improve the arrangement.

Edrune waited until the decision was complete.

“May I have copies for the yard, Your Majesty?”

“You shall.”

“With the officer named.”

“Yes.”

The girl almost smiled.

After the others had begun to leave, Jaennis leaned towards her.

“You managed that very well.”

Matylde’s face opened. She began describing the funding difficulty, then stopped when Jaennis asked whether Margery had enjoyed their walk the previous afternoon.

“Yes. I was going to tell you.”

“You needn’t account for every moment.”

“I know. We only went as far as the old garden.”

Riarhin, gathering her copies, heard the explanation continue while the clerk waited with the order that needed the queen’s mark.

The copies were not ready when Riarhin reached the lower passage. Edrune had gone to find the other petitioners. Through the open door of the copying room she could see Matylde still at the table, turning the edge of a wafer between finger and thumb while two clerks argued about the order in which the releases should be entered.

Riarhin would have passed if the girl had not seen her.

“Wait. This concerns the yard.”

She went in. Jaennis had left; her cushion stood on its edge against the wall. Without the councillors the room looked smaller, though none of its furniture had moved. Matylde motioned Riarhin towards a chair and asked the older clerk to begin again.

Some receipts from the northern stores had been pledged against a remount account. Horses had been bought for a company waiting to take up a western posting. Until the restrictions on the stores were heard, the receipts counted as part of the guarantee. If the receipts were disputed, the buyer could decline to meet the second payment. The horses themselves remained in the seller’s field.

“Have we paid for them?” Matylde asked.

“A reservation. They are due to be taken up at the end of the week.”

“Then use the reservation.”

“That keeps them until the day. It doesn’t buy them.”

The younger clerk unfolded an additional sheet. The company captain had expected an advance before the men left their present quarters. The same buyer was named on it. Riarhin leaned forward, then sat back. The clerk had put the sheet before the queen, not her.

Matylde noticed.

“What?”

“Will they need the horses before the inquiry can finish?”

“Their captain says they do,” the older clerk said.

“Ask the officer who gave him the posting.”

“We can send.”

Matylde looked at the two papers. “How many days?”

“Until the answer? Three, if the officer is where we believe he is.”

“And the money is wanted before then.”

Neither clerk answered. It was on the page.

The girl laid the wafer down. She had softened one edge with the heat of her hand. When she lifted her finger it clung for a moment, and she had to scrape it off against the dish.

“Have the horses been promised to somebody else?”

“Not yet.”

“Tell the seller to hold them until we have the answer.”

“On whose credit, Majesty?”

She looked up sharply. The older clerk lowered his head but did not withdraw the question.

“Mine.”

“He will ask whose purse pays if you decide against taking them.”

Matylde looked once towards Jaennis’s empty chair, then asked whether the treasury could guarantee the hold. The younger clerk began counting money which had already been committed. His colleague stopped him and named the uncommitted balance. It would meet two days of the seller’s charge for keeping the horses apart from the rest of his stock. After that, more would be needed.

“Two days,” Matylde said. “Send today. Tell the captain we have not cancelled the purchase.”

“Or assured it,” the older clerk said.

Matylde’s mouth tightened. “Tell him that too.”

The clerk made a note. Then he asked whether the ordinary payment authority should continue while the disputed part was checked. Matylde read the condition again. Riarhin watched her follow it with one finger, stop at the mention of the restrictions and return to the beginning.

“If we let them pay under this, they will say we accepted the condition.”

“They may.”

“Then hold that authority until we have another.”

“Your Majesty can accept the risk and make payment subject to the hearing.”

“And if they refuse the hearing afterward?”

“That would have to be pursued.”

Matylde looked at Riarhin. For a moment Riarhin wanted to agree. She had seen what the continuing restrictions had done; she had watched Edrune carry the same papers from room to room. She could give the girl the answer she wanted and leave the clerks to make it practicable.

“I don’t know their agreements,” she said.

“You know what men do when their pay doesn’t come.”

“Yes.”

“What do they do?”

“Some borrow. Some leave. Some stay and make it somebody else’s misfortune.”

“Would you have paid?”

“I used to have a man to tell me which money was real.”

The girl looked away. Riarhin had not meant to speak of Braedyn. The older clerk gathered the captain’s letter and left the payment direction before her.

“Two days is what we can offer without a fresh undertaking,” he said.

Matylde signed the direction. The clerk sanded it and took it away while the ink was still bright at the edge of her name.

Outside, Edrune had found Riarhin’s copies. She stood beneath the window with one hand inside her coat, keeping them dry from a thin rain that had begun without anyone noticing. She passed them over and asked what had delayed her.

“A payment.”

“Yours?”

“No.”

“Then come. The yard shuts when the light goes.”

Riarhin followed her. Behind them a messenger crossed the court at a run, the new direction held beneath his cloak.

Chapter 16 The timber under hold

The yard keeper read the release and asked who would pay for the spoiled pieces.

Edrune stood beside a stack of timber darkened at the ends. She had pulled a loose cover back to show where water had gathered. Beneath it, the wood smelled sour.

“That is part of the claim,” Solenne’s officer said.

“Against whom?”

“To be determined.”

“Then I won’t have it said I released sound timber and received a complaint afterwards.”

Edrune picked up a short piece and struck it against the edge of the stack. The softened end broke.

“Would you like to call this sound before witnesses?”

The keeper took the piece from her. He looked angry and worried in equal measure.

His men had obeyed the hold. They had moved the timber twice to make space for other cargo. One cover had been taken for a consignment whose owner could obtain an answer more quickly. The keeper said he had requested another. The request was in the office.

“Bring it,” the officer said.

They waited.

Coryd walked along the stack with a yard worker, separating the pieces that could travel from those that would break during loading. Riarhin stayed with Edrune. It was difficult to resist going after the clerk who had gone to find the request. The man had been absent long enough to alter something if he intended to.

“You’ll make him slower,” Edrune said.

“I haven’t moved.”

“You’ve been moving in your head since he went.”

The request existed. It had been sent, copied and left unanswered. The officer recorded it, took statements on the condition of the load and authorized release without settlement of the storage claim.

The keeper insisted on a witness from his own staff. Edrune agreed. The first cart was loaded while the others argued over how to describe the damaged ends.

By the time Riarhin left, Edrune had climbed onto the cart and was shouting at a man who had tied a fastening she disliked. Her anger sounded healthier than the quiet she had carried into court.

The replacement escort met them where the yard road joined the lane above the river.

There were two men. One wore the correct badge. The other held a folded direction with a seal Riarhin recognized from the household office. They said Solenne had changed the return route because a delegation was blocking the eastern entrance.

Riarhin asked for the officer’s name.

The man gave it.

“Who issued the pass?”

“The same office.”

“Who put it in your hand?”

His irritation was convincing. That made her more cautious.

Kerros had remained with them from the morning. He took the direction and read it. His jaw tightened.

“This isn’t my relief.”

“We were told—”

“Then we will ask again.”

The second man moved towards Coryd’s horse. She turned the animal before his hand reached the bridle. The first man looked past Riarhin, towards the low wall beside the lane.

She dismounted on the far side.

The blade struck the saddle where her leg had been.

The horse drove sideways into the wall, trapping Riarhin’s scabbard between its flank and the stone.

She had the reins in her right hand. The animal’s head went up, dragging her arm with it. She moved before the next cut, taking the horse’s movement through her shoulder rather than trying to stop it. The blade struck stone where her fingers had been.

“Back,” she said to the horse, too quietly for anybody else to hear.

It heard the familiar sound. Its forefeet shifted. She gained a hand’s breadth between herself and the wall, enough to bend the left knee and kick at the man coming round its head. Her heel caught him above the knee. He grunted and kept coming.

Kerros’s sword passed between them.

“Behind me.”

Riarhin could not get behind him. The lane narrowed at the cart post, and Coryd’s horse was there, turning in a frightened circle while the second man tried to catch its bridle. Coryd had one foot free of the stirrup. The other held for a moment too long. Riarhin saw her sister tip, catch the saddle with both hands and land hard enough to stumble.

The second man reached for her.

Riarhin pulled at her sword. The horse pressed her against the wall again. She stopped pulling, took the scabbard in her left hand and pushed the animal’s shoulder with her own. Its hide was hot through the cloth of her coat. She could smell the cut leather of the saddle.

A boy at the yard entrance shouted for someone to stop the horses. Nobody had yet seen the blade clearly enough to know what else was happening.

Coryd caught the second man’s sleeve and used his reaching arm to turn him into the post. He struck her in the face with his free hand. Her head went back. The blow made a hollow sound which Riarhin would remember afterwards, separately from the rest.

“Leave her,” the first man said.

It was an order to his companion. It gave Riarhin the fraction of attention she needed.

She let go of the reins.

The horse went past her, squeezing between the wall and Kerros, who had to change his footing to avoid being knocked down. Riarhin came off the stone with Remorse free. The sword’s point stayed low for the first step, where it would not meet Kerros’s arm as he recovered.

The attacker saw it. He changed his intention at once. His weight went towards the river side, trying to pass Kerros and reach Riarhin before she had room for a cut.

Kerros moved with him.

The two blades met. The attacker’s point slid along the officer’s weapon and opened his forearm above the repaired leather cuff. Kerros’s hand held for one breath. Riarhin saw it begin to fail.

She stepped into the space on his right. Remorse rose between the man’s face and the place he wanted to go. He checked, turned the point aside and tried a shorter cut at her hand. She gave him the distance, moving along the wall rather than towards the open road.

Behind him the yard boy was still shouting. A worker had picked up a loading pole and did not know whom to threaten with it.

“Stay there,” Riarhin told him.

The man with the blade glanced back. That glance cost him ground. She advanced one step and brought her point across his way out to the yard, leaving the lane behind him open.

He understood.

Coryd struck the second man with the runner wood. It broke against his shoulder. He fell to one knee, more astonished than disabled, and she hit him again with the shorter piece. He went down beside the post. She put her knee between his shoulders before he could roll and dragged his wrist away from the knife he had not managed to draw.

The first attacker began to retreat.

Riarhin followed for two steps, keeping Remorse level. There was room now. She could move freely, and he knew it. His gaze flicked towards Kerros, then back to her. Blood dripped from the officer’s cuff onto the lane.

The attacker turned and ran.

She could have followed him. There was clear ground for six paces and then a turn.

Kerros dropped his sword.

Riarhin turned back.

Coryd had the other man down, one knee between his shoulders, demanding that he stop trying to rise. He was saying something about the wrong woman. Riarhin ignored him and cut a strip from Kerros’s sleeve.

“Hold here.”

He pressed his uninjured hand where she put it.

“Harder.”

“I’m holding it.”

“Harder.”

He swore at her. She was grateful for the force of it.

The yard keeper came running with two men. Riarhin told him to fetch a healer and keep the folded direction dry. He stared at the blood, then took off towards his office, shouting for a boy.

Coryd looked up from the man beneath her.

“He says he only came to talk.”

“Let Solenne hear him.”

Kerros’s hand slipped. Riarhin put hers over it and pressed until he cried out.

Chapter 17 The unfinished account

Jaennis offered rooms before the blood had dried on Riarhin’s coat.

The message reached the lodging while Kerros was being treated downstairs. The healer had asked for hot water, clean linen and enough silence to work. Coryd supplied the linen. Riarhin, unable to supply silence, went to the front room and read the offer twice.

The household could protect her more effectively. Her meetings would be easier to arrange. The queen was distressed by the attack and wished the protection she had granted to be made real.

There was nothing in the message Riarhin could fairly call a threat.

She sent thanks and declined.

Solenne arrived before the messenger had left.

“You will have more guards here.”

“Yes.”

“You won’t walk out because you find them inconvenient.”

“No.”

“And you will tell me if somebody offers you another arrangement.”

Riarhin handed her the message.

Solenne read it and set it down.

“I meant privately.”

“So did I.”

The officer looked tired. She sat without waiting to be offered the chair.

“The captured man says he believed you were to be brought to an unwitnessed meeting. He admits knowing you hadn’t agreed to it.”

“Who asked him?”

“He has given a name.”

“Which?”

“I will check it before I give it to you.”

“Cedric.”

Solenne’s expression did not change enough to count as an answer.

Riarhin felt anger arrive with an almost pleasant clarity. She could put a face to the intention. It was an old face, altered by time, with a mark she had put there herself.

“Don’t,” Solenne said.

“I haven’t done anything.”

“I can see you preparing to.”

Below, Kerros cried out again. Riarhin looked towards the floor.

Solenne rose.

“I will tell the queen what happened. You will remain available. If this is who you think it is, he will be questioned by people who haven’t already decided what to do with him.”

Riarhin waited until the officer’s steps had passed down the stair, then followed her far enough to hear the healer calling for another lamp.

Kerros sat in the kitchen with his forearm laid on a folded cloth. The cook had cleared the table, put the supper pots on the floor and forbidden everyone to step backwards. A guard held the lamp. Another stood at the door trying not to look at the wound.

“You,” the healer said to Riarhin. “Can you hold a light without moving it?”

“Yes.”

“Then take his. He keeps following my hands with it.”

The guard surrendered the lamp with relief. Riarhin stood where she was told.

The cut was longer than it had seemed in the lane. Blood had hidden the edges. Now she could see the parted muscle and the pale movement beneath it when Kerros tried to bend his fingers. The healer told him to stop trying until asked.

“Will it mend?” Kerros said.

“The flesh will mend.”

“That isn’t what I mean.”

“I know. I can’t answer the rest while I’m looking at this part.”

Riarhin kept the lamp still. Kerros watched her face rather than his arm. She made herself think about the flame, the oil, the distance between the lamp and the healer’s fingers. He asked her to bring it closer. She did.

When the stitching began, Kerros put his good hand flat on the table. His fingertips pressed against the wood. He swore once, very quietly, and then asked the cook whether she had anything to drink.

“Water.”

“Something worth asking for.”

“After,” the healer said.

The cook stood beside him with the water anyway. After another stitch he accepted it. She held the cup because his good hand had become occupied with gripping the edge of the table.

“Some of my men at Hollin would have laughed themselves sick to see this,” he said.

The young guard at the door looked up. “You were there?”

“Not where the songs put me. In a ditch. We spent half the afternoon waiting for somebody to tell us whether the men coming over the hedge were ours.”

“Were they?”

“Eventually. The first ones weren’t.”

The guard grinned before remembering the arm. Kerros saw him catch himself.

“Don’t look so solemn. It’s my arm.”

Riarhin felt a painful affection for him then, unwanted and without any claim on his return. He had barely spoken to her on the road.

The healer finished the last stitch. He dressed the wound, secured the arm and said that assisted mending might help once the damage had settled enough to judge, provided an appropriate practitioner could be obtained. Kerros asked how soon he could hold a rein. The healer told him how soon he could ask that question again.

When they were left alone for a moment, Riarhin set the lamp down.

“I can pay for—”

“The queen will pay.”

“Yes.”

He looked at her. “I don’t mind you asking. I want it entered where it belongs.”

She nodded.

“They used our badge,” he said. “And a real seal.”

“I saw.”

“I’d have let them speak to you longer if you hadn’t asked who put the paper in his hand.”

“You looked at the relief.”

“After.”

He was angry with himself. Riarhin knew the search he was making through the moments before the attack, placing an earlier suspicion here, a better angle there. She had done it while he was bleeding.

“You kept him from me.”

“I was told to.”

“Yes.”

He leaned back. The cook brought a little spirits in a cup and set it beside him without asking the healer. Riarhin stood to leave.

“Don’t go after him,” Kerros said.

She stopped.

“The one who ran. You go out alone, and every man who stayed at his post will wonder what he was doing there.”

Riarhin had meant to make inquiries. She had already thought of the man at the yard who might know where the attacker went. She let the plan go, slowly.

“I won’t.”

Kerros took the cup in his good hand. “Good. I’m too tired to follow you.”

Coryd’s cheek had swollen by evening.

She had put a wet cloth against it and was sitting beside the fire, complaining that she could no longer drink without tipping the cup like an infant. Riarhin brought a shallower cup. Coryd tried it, conceded that it helped and declined to thank her while she was looking so pleased with herself.

“I wasn’t pleased.”

“You had found a problem you could solve with crockery. You were radiant.”

Riarhin sat opposite her. “Let me see.”

“The healer saw.”

“I know.”

“He said it will bruise.”

“I know.”

“Then look at something else.”

Riarhin looked at the fire. A piece of wood had fallen away from the others and was smoking. She moved it with the poker. Coryd lowered the cloth.

“I’m still here.”

Riarhin nodded.

“You don’t need to keep checking.”

“I saw him hit you.”

“Yes. I was there.”

The anger in her sister’s voice made Riarhin look up. Coryd was pressing the cloth too hard against the cheek, as if determined that it should hurt on her own terms.

“I want to be angry with him,” she said. “You’re making it difficult by sitting there as if you’ve done it.”

Riarhin let the poker rest against the hearth. She could feel a dozen replies arranging themselves, all of them accounts of how much danger she had brought into Coryd’s life. She kept them to herself.

“What would you like?”

“For someone to ask whether I hit him properly.”

“You did.”

“Thank you.”

“With the first blow you—”

“No lesson.”

Riarhin laughed, and Coryd laughed too, then swore because it hurt.

Sivelle came an hour later with a small pot, a packet of food and a temper. She gave her name below, waited for the guard to announce her and demanded entry so loudly that the cook came out to watch.

“I am permitted to visit a friend with a swollen face,” she told the guard.

“You are. I have to announce you.”

“You have. Now I am announcing that I’m cold.”

Coryd called down that she could come up. Sivelle passed the guard with an apology delivered as briskly as the complaint. She stopped when she saw the bruise.

“Oh, you poor—”

“Don’t,” Coryd said.

Sivelle put the things down, crossed the room and kissed the uninjured side of her face. “Very well. You magnificent fool.”

“That will do.”

Riarhin rose. Sivelle glanced at her and then at the cup.

“Has she eaten?”

“She can answer,” Coryd said.

“Has she?” Sivelle asked Coryd.

“A little.”

“Then she can eat a little more while I tell her what Madra wrote.”

Riarhin went downstairs. The guard asked whether she was leaving. She said no, only sitting somewhere else, and took a chair in the kitchen. The cook gave her a knife and a basket of roots. For half an hour she cut away spoiled places while the cook complained about the cost of good oil.

Upstairs the women talked. Riarhin could not hear the words, only the rise and fall of the voices and once Coryd’s laughter cut short by pain. She cut another root and discovered that she had removed more sound flesh than bad. The cook took it from her and said she would use that one in the pot.

When Sivelle came down, she stopped beside Riarhin’s chair.

“The ointment is for the skin when the swelling begins to go down. Not near the eye.”

“I heard the healer.”

“Then you can remind her when she decides she has a better idea.”

Riarhin looked up. “I’m not trying to keep her from you.”

Sivelle blinked. “I didn’t think you were.”

“I thought—”

“I know. You do a great deal of it.”

She bent to pick up her empty basket.

“She’s coming to my house tomorrow, if the officer permits the journey. I have a broad chair and nobody there has seen the attack. We may spend the afternoon talking about curtains.”

“She hates curtains.”

“Excellent. We’ll have something to agree about.”

After she left, Riarhin took the roots to the cook. She had finished most of the basket. The woman inspected them, found nothing worth objecting to and put them in the water.

The next day Coryd went. Riarhin helped her fasten the cloak without touching her face.

Matylde received her two days later under a more conspicuous escort.

The queen asked about Kerros first. The wound would heal, Riarhin said. Whether the hand would work properly would take time to know.

“He was doing what I told him to do,” Matylde said.

“Yes.”

A councillor began to suggest that the invitation had produced foreseeable disorder. Matylde let him finish.

“Would you have had him permit it?”

“No, Your Majesty.”

“Then he will have what he needs.”

Jaennis supported the order. Riarhin saw no pretence in her concern. When the business ended, Matylde wanted to speak about the freight release, which Edrune had reported in a letter containing both thanks and three complaints.

Jaennis asked about the girl’s second visit to Margery instead.

“I had thought we would hear about that together.”

“I was going to tell you.”

“I wasn’t asking for an account.”

“I know. I’m sorry.”

The apology came so readily that Riarhin looked at Solenne. The officer was attending to another paper. A servant who had approached with a message withdrew quietly.

Later, when Riarhin was alone with the queen for the few permitted minutes, Matylde asked whether Margery had been afraid of Veralyn.

“You should ask Margery.”

“I’m asking you.”

“I can tell you what I saw. I can’t tell you what she felt.”

“Everyone says that when they don’t want to answer.”

“Sometimes it’s the answer.”

Matylde pushed Edrune’s letter aside.

“You may go.”

Riarhin bowed and went. That evening a page brought a brief apology for the dismissal. It contained no withdrawal of the question.

The second copy of the changed escort direction came from the west gate.

Solenne brought it late, with a short account from a household sergeant who was now angry enough to insist that every word he had said be recorded. He had been sent to meet an expected dispute near the gate after the afternoon hearing. Six men, no drawn weapons, a closed inner door behind the royal guest. If complainants tried to force their way through, he was to hold them until the queen’s officers arrived.

Riarhin read the hour twice. By then she had already been in the kitchen holding the lamp above Kerros’s arm.

“They were waiting for me?”

“They were waiting for an incident involving you.”

“Who sent them?”

“Jaennis’s travelling officer. He says she wished to prevent a scene.”

“Did she tell you?”

“No.”

Solenne took the paper back before Riarhin could begin pointing at it.

“The sergeant was told there might be petitioners who would not accept the conditions of your hearing. He was not told to let them attack you. He had orders to keep you inside.”

“After they tried.”

“That is one of the questions.”

Below them the cook dropped a pan. Riarhin’s shoulders moved before she could stop them. Solenne heard it too and waited for the cook’s oath before continuing.

“Someone gave the household a time and a place. Someone gave your attackers a different one. I want the men who gave both.”

“Has the queen been told?”

“Yes.”

“All of it?”

“The papers are on her desk.”

Riarhin looked towards the window. Nothing could be seen there beyond the opposite wall and the corner of the street. She had spent the day wanting Cedric within reach. Now she wanted Jaennis to look at Kerros’s hand while she explained where she had intended her men to stand.

Solenne folded the direction.

“You have an audience tomorrow. Let her read tonight.”

Chapter 18 An escort nobody asked for

Riarhin asked Coryd to walk ahead of the queen’s proposed outing and see which doors opened onto the lane.

Her sister looked up from the chest runner she was finishing.

“Why?”

“So we know.”

“Who asked us?”

“Nobody has to ask me to notice a door.”

“You didn’t ask me to notice one. You asked me to go there first.”

Riarhin took the shaving Coryd had handed her without meaning to and put it on the pile beside the work.

“There was an attempt to kill me.”

“Yes. Will Matylde be safer if you tell more people where she intends to go?”

“I wasn’t going to tell people.”

“You were going to send me.”

The argument stopped because Riarhin could see the rest of it and did not want to say it aloud.

She went to Solenne.

The officer listened, then became angrier than Riarhin had expected.

“You are not responsible for this household.”

“I know.”

“Then stop arranging it through your sister.”

“I came to tell you I had stopped.”

“Good.”

Riarhin waited a moment longer than she needed to.

Solenne asked her to identify the particular access she had been concerned about. Riarhin did so. The officer noted it and said she would decide what to do. Their meeting ended there.

Coryd had received a letter.

She read a portion aloud while Riarhin took off her boots. Harissa had accepted work she had previously sworn she would never accept again. Linias had found somebody willing to repair the instrument properly and was quarrelling about the proposed materials. Zemia had supplied an address and had not written to the person herself. The writer thought this worth mentioning.

Coryd folded the letter and looked at the runner.

“I miss people who tell me when I’m being tiresome.”

“I can do that.”

“You think you can.”

Riarhin leaned back against the wall.

“Do you still want to leave with me?”

Her sister did not answer immediately.

The pause hurt. Riarhin let it continue.

“Yes,” Coryd said. “When this is done. I don’t want to live in a room where a man has to be told each time I go downstairs.”

“Neither do I.”

“You’d arrange it for someone else quickly enough.”

“Yes.”

Coryd resumed smoothing the wood.

“Then we should both leave,” Riarhin said.

Coryd set the letter down between them.

“Do you want to?”

“Yes.”

“Then why do you sound as if leaving is something you intend to do after you’ve put the whole city in order?”

Riarhin looked towards the window. The street had quieted, but she could still hear a wheel turning somewhere, its loose fitting striking once with each rotation.

“Because somebody should have done this sooner.”

“That isn’t an answer about you.”

“It usually is.”

Coryd laughed without amusement. “I know. That’s why I’m asking.”

Riarhin took up Harissa’s letter. There were three places where the pen had caught in the paper. She could picture her sister stopping to scrape it clean, irritated by an object which had behaved perfectly until the moment she needed it. She missed the sound of that irritation. She missed knowing which footsteps would arrive before she looked up.

“I don’t like being here,” she said.

“Neither do I, much of the time.”

“I don’t like what I remember here.”

Coryd waited, picking a little dried glue off her thumb.

“Sivelle has asked me about the stair at her house.”

“Does she want you to mend it?”

“She would like me to. It needs more than mending. It needs taking apart.”

“How long?”

“Three weeks. Perhaps longer.”

Riarhin looked at the tools on the table. Coryd had bought two since they arrived. They were wrapped in cloth which Riarhin had not seen before.

“Would she pay you?”

“Yes. She said we ought to settle that before we find ourselves quarrelling over a stair when we mean something else.”

“She’s sensible.”

“Sometimes.”

Coryd smiled down at the glue on her hand. Riarhin found that she wanted to hear the story behind the smile, and knew that asking would make it disappear.

“I haven’t agreed,” Coryd said.

“Because of me?”

“Partly.”

Riarhin put the letter aside. She had no sudden, generous answer. She had been counting on having Coryd beside her at the gate and on the road beyond it. She would have thought to ask whether the tools needed a better wrapping before she thought to ask whether they were travelling at all.

Riarhin leaned forward, elbows on her knees. “Do you want to go home before me?”

“I might.”

It had not occurred to her that Coryd might say it. She had offered the choice with an image of her sister declining already prepared.

“All right.”

“You look appalled.”

“I’ll arrange—”

“Riarhin.”

She stopped. Coryd began to laugh. After a moment Riarhin did too, though it hurt a little.

“I could speak to Edrune,” Coryd said. “She has carts going north. There are people on them. They will presumably not all perish without your direction.”

“They might get there late.”

“They’ll have each other to complain to.”

Riarhin wiped a hand across her face. She wanted her sister with her. She had wanted it so fiercely at times that she had disguised it as a calculation of Coryd’s safety. She did not know how to ask without laying that whole history on the table.

“Stay a little longer,” she said.

Coryd watched her.

“Because you want me?”

“Yes.”

“Not because you need someone who can fit through a window?”

Riarhin smiled despite herself. “The window here is quite broad.”

Coryd picked up one of the stockings Riarhin had pulled off and threw it at her. Riarhin caught it and laid it beside her boots.

“A little longer,” Coryd said. “Then we decide again.”

Riarhin nodded. She reached for the letter and began reading Harissa’s account of a neighbour’s disputed washing line aloud, until Coryd objected that she was using entirely the wrong voice for the neighbour.

Outside, a carriage passed. Riarhin got up before she could stop herself and looked through the gap beside the curtain. It was carrying a family she did not know. A small boy had his face pressed against the glass, distorting his mouth for the entertainment of someone inside.

She stepped away from the window.

The woman waiting at the guard office had brought a saddle instead of a petition.

It lay across her knees. One stirrup leather had been taken off, and the polished places on the seat were pale with years of use. Riarhin had come to confirm the next day’s escort. She knew the saddle first: an old army pattern, made to carry more than a rider, with the stitching repaired at the front where a roll had rubbed through. Then the woman turned it over and she saw that the wooden tree had split.

“Don’t put it on that bench,” the guard told her.

“I didn’t.”

“You will when you get tired.”

“Then bring another bench.”

The guard looked at Riarhin with relief and asked for her paper. She gave it to him. The woman held the saddle between her boots while she flexed the fingers which had been supporting it.

“Your horse do that?” Riarhin asked.

“An ox cart. It was left where it shouldn’t have been.”

“Was anybody on it?”

“My husband. He got off in time.”

She looked at the escort waiting beside Riarhin, then at her face. The interest left her expression. She lifted the saddle back onto her knees.

“I know you,” she said.

Riarhin let her have the silence which followed. A clerk came out, took her escort paper from the guard and said the officer would see them both. The woman rose too quickly, caught the saddle against her hip and swore.

They went into a room with a low window overlooking the exercise yard. Solenne was standing with an army captain whose boots had been cleaned so roughly that mud remained in the seams. The woman put the saddle on the floor between them.

“This one belongs to us,” she said. “Before anyone writes it down with the rest.”

The captain’s face reddened.

“I told her she needn’t bring it.”

“He told me they’d remember. They didn’t remember the tent last winter.”

Solenne asked her name for the record. She gave it, then spelled the name of her husband, who served in the company whose remounts had been held. Riarhin listened while the clerk wrote. The woman was neither a deputation nor the company’s laundress. She made and repaired harness for men who could not wait for the quartermaster. There were debts marked inside the cover of her work book beside the measurements.

The remount seller had honoured the two-day hold. On the third morning he sold six of the promised horses. The captain had bought four inferior animals from another dealer with money lent by officers. Those animals would carry loads. They would not do the work of the six he had lost.

“The answer about the posting arrived this morning,” Solenne said.

“Then it was only two days late.”

“I understand.”

“Do you?” He stopped, glanced at the clerk and began again. “My men are to ride west. They haven’t been asked whether they can do without the horses.”

The woman with the saddle opened her book. She had lent money towards the four animals. It was the money set aside for leather, rivets and two apprentices’ wages. The captain had signed for it. He had not denied the debt. She wanted to know whether the Crown’s refusal to pay the purchase account meant that his signature was now only his own.

Riarhin saw the captain look towards the door. There was no one there to rescue him from the question.

“It is my own,” he said. “You know where I live.”

“I know your wife has sold her good mare.”

Solenne sent for someone from the treasury. While they waited, the captain went to the window. The harness maker remained beside her saddle, turning the corner of a page over and back. Riarhin knew the worn indentation which repeated work could make in the pad of a thumb. This woman had one at the side of her forefinger, deep enough to hold the black of the leather dressing.

“Did the queen know?” she asked Riarhin.

“About the horses? Yes.”

“About our money?”

“She couldn’t have. You hadn’t lent it.”

“She could have asked where we’d get it.”

Riarhin took the accusation without attempting to improve its terms. The woman went back to her book.

The treasury clerk arrived with the original authority and a copy of the hold. He recognised Riarhin from the small room. It was the younger of the two; he looked older with his collar undone. He explained that the first instalment of the army settlement had been paid. This was a purchase promised against the next guarantee, and that guarantee had depended upon the northern restrictions.

The captain turned from the window.

“I do not want the queen to put another farmer under an order for my benefit. I want the horses I was told to prepare to receive.”

“We haven’t the balance today.”

“Then change my posting.”

“That is not my office.”

“Get the man whose office it is.”

The harness maker laughed once. It was an ugly sound, made worse by how quickly she swallowed it.

Solenne told the guard to send for the posting officer’s deputy. Then she asked the captain how many days he could remain where he was. He had already given notice to the owner of the field. Another company was to use it. The families would go into the old counting shed if its roof could be made sound. His men could sleep outside. The horses could not eat an order.

Riarhin had heard men make such speeches to win a better ration. She waited for the captain to look at her and see whether it had worked. He never did. He was counting the days on his fingers while he spoke to Solenne, correcting himself when the woman reminded him that their present meal contract ended before the field tenancy.

“Three,” he said. “We can stay three days if somebody gives the owner a reason to let us.”

The clerk offered a letter promising payment when the new guarantee was settled. The harness maker pushed her book towards him.

“Write it here too.”

“I have no authority to—”

“No. I know.”

She drew the book back. The captain sat on the edge of the window ledge without asking and covered his face. Nobody spoke for several breaths.

Solenne asked whether Riarhin could identify what had been said when the hold was authorised. The clerk would require a separate account. She agreed. It meant she would have to return the next morning; she asked that her escort be confirmed for that journey as well.

As she left, the woman stopped her.

“Lady Jaennis could have kept them.”

“The horses?”

“The seller. She knows his mother. His mother lived on her money while their house was being repaired.”

Riarhin did not know whether that would have been enough. She could see the woman did, and that contradicting her would only make the room colder.

“Has anyone asked her?”

“The captain wouldn’t. He said we were to go through the office.”

Outside, Coryd was waiting with a bag of apples. She had bought them for the lodging and eaten one on the way. She offered the bag. Riarhin took an apple and held it without biting.

“What happened?”

Riarhin told her about the horses.

“Can you help?”

“I can say what I heard.”

“That wasn’t what I asked.”

Riarhin looked back at the office. Through the window she could see the captain standing again. The harness maker had taken the saddle to the table and was showing the clerk where to write its marks so that nobody could take it for company property.

“I don’t know.”

Coryd hooked the bag more firmly over her arm. They walked home with the escort. Riarhin ate the apple when they reached the market, though by then she had bruised it with her thumb.

Chapter 19 The horses already sold

The remount captain came to the palace before the queen had eaten.

Riarhin met him in the covered court, where her escort had been told to wait. He was wearing the same boots. This time there was no mud on them at all. Somebody had worked at the seams with a stick until the leather showed pale around every stitch.

“She’s seeing us,” he said.

“Yes.”

“Do you know whether her ladyship will be there?”

“Which?”

He gave her an impatient look.

“I haven’t been told.”

He had brought a sergeant whose left shoulder sat higher than his right. The sergeant carried a folded cloth under his arm and watched the gate instead of the doorway through which they would be called. When a groom brought a horse across the court he looked at its forefeet, then away. Riarhin wondered whether he was one of the men who had put money into the purchase. He caught her looking and asked whether she had served under the old Marshal.

“With him. For a time.”

“He inspected my troop once. Made us change the girths. Said the stores weren’t to call a thing fit because it had a number.”

Riarhin could remember Tylan’s hand against a horse’s flank. She had been trying not to remember his hand on the sword.

“Did they change them?”

“We changed them. He sent the storeman out to watch us do it.”

The captain took the sergeant by the sleeve and drew him aside. Riarhin heard the beginning of a rebuke, quiet and furious. She said they need not apologise. Both men looked at her. Neither had intended to.

The room prepared for them had a table long enough to keep everyone apart. Matylde sat at its end with a piece of bread untouched beside her. Jaennis was already there. The harness maker stood near the treasury clerks, her book wrapped in cloth against the rain. A deputy from the posting office looked as if he had been taken from his bed. He bowed to the captain with an apology which did not quite reach words.

The facts took little time. The two-day hold had expired. The answer confirming the posting had arrived after it. Six horses had gone to another buyer. Four replacements had been bought on private loans. The company had permission to remain in its field for three more days, but the owner had withdrawn the meal contract. He would not provide further food on a signature which had already disappointed him.

“How many people?” Matylde asked.

The captain named the soldiers. The harness maker named the others.

“Are those all dependants?” a councillor asked.

“Some work for us,” the captain said.

“Are they required to remain?”

“My wife isn’t required to remain anywhere,” the captain said. The harness maker looked up from her book. “She could take her tools and go home. She won’t, while there’s work done and unpaid.”

The captain had said my wife before Riarhin understood the relation between them. At the guard office they had spoken so much like adversaries that she had failed to see it. The sergeant put the folded cloth on the table. Inside were two old stirrup leathers, a worn bridle and a list.

“These are the things we can repair before we leave,” he said. “If we’re leaving mounted.”

Matylde asked the deputy whether the posting could be delayed. He said the company already at the western post had been promised relief. Its captain had released men on leave against that promise. Some had begun their journeys. He could recall them, but not before the relief was due.

“Then send a different company.”

“There is one ready to march. They are foot.”

“Could they hold the post?”

“The buildings, Majesty. The patrol road is forty miles.”

The girl leaned back. Riarhin saw her count the days again without moving her fingers. Jaennis had not spoken. She sat with the palms of her hands resting on the table, waiting while the others brought the problem to the same place.

At last Matylde asked her whether she could help.

“Yes.”

The answer came without a pause. The harness maker looked towards her with such naked relief that Riarhin felt ashamed of having expected a performance. Jaennis named a family who could guarantee the seller, and another whose horses were at grass within a day’s journey. Neither belonged to Tessart’s circle. The first had accepted her word before. The second had received a favour from Tylan and would wish to honour his daughter.

“Will they meet the captain’s private debt too?” Matylde asked.

“They can be asked.”

“And the meal contract?”

“I will write to the owner.”

The treasury clerk asked whether the undertakings could be addressed to the office. Jaennis looked at him.

“They can be copied wherever you wish.”

“Accepted there,” he said.

“They will answer me.”

Matylde pressed a thumb against the rim of her plate. Riarhin watched the crumbs gather beneath it. Nobody could tell the harness maker to be content with the principle of a better arrangement. Her purse had paid for horses standing in somebody else’s field, and one of her apprentices had a father waiting for the boy’s wages.

The queen asked the captain what he needed that day.

“Authority to stay where I am. Food until we leave. Assurance that my officers won’t carry the purchase debt into their next posting.”

“All of it today?”

“Food today. The owner has given notice. He isn’t withholding what we’ve bought.”

“Then take that first,” Jaennis said. “I can have the food agreed by dinner.”

The captain looked at Matylde. He had been brought to the edge of an answer and was expected to stand there while the queen decided who should give it.

Matylde turned to the older clerk.

“If she writes, can you accept the undertaking when it comes?”

“If its terms are sufficient.”

“What terms?”

He began to explain the right of recovery. Jaennis interrupted.

“The man knows me. He will feed them. Must we keep them here while you settle the words?”

The harness maker shut her eyes. Her husband stared at the bridle on the table. Riarhin remembered an army waiting for a cart whose owner wanted the receipt altered before he unloaded it. She had threatened him. They had eaten. Years later she still did not know whether he had ever been paid.

“Have the letter written,” Matylde said. “But don’t offer him the disputed receipts. Or another house’s hearing.”

“I had no intention of doing either.”

“And let the office see the answer.”

Jaennis called for paper. The servant had already put some beside her, anticipating this better than the council had. She wrote while the posting deputy outlined a temporary patrol arrangement. The foot company could hold two stations and send messengers along the short road. Mounted patrols would be reduced. It would leave a stretch of road unwatched until the mounted company could take over.

“Is that dangerous?” Matylde asked.

“Yes,” the deputy said.

“More dangerous than sending these men without what they need?”

He took a breath. “I would not recommend that.”

“Then do the other thing. Name the stretch to the local officers. Don’t tell them the road is watched if it isn’t.”

The deputy bowed. The sergeant touched the worn bridle as though he might fold it away, then waited.

The harness maker asked about her loan.

The treasury clerk explained that the Crown had not authorised the captain to borrow from her. The woman’s face hardened. The clerk stopped.

“He bought horses required for service,” Matylde said.

“At a price we haven’t examined.”

“Examine it.”

“We can reimburse the purchase if it is accepted. The interest—”

“There isn’t any,” the woman said. “He is my husband.”

The clerk looked at the book. “Then why enter it as a loan?”

“Because my apprentice isn’t his soldier. Because when a captain dies somebody takes his equipment and tells the widow which debts belong to the company. Because I knew you would ask.”

She had raised her voice. She put her hand on the book and forced it flat. Her husband reached towards her, then let his hand drop.

Matylde looked at the clerk until he wrote the loan as a separate claim. It was still a claim. No money changed hands.

Riarhin was asked to give her account of the two-day direction. She repeated the alternatives offered, the warning about the seller’s charge and the queen’s refusal to accept the condition attached to the original money. Matylde watched her throughout. When she finished, the younger clerk added that they had sent the enquiry by the ordinary messenger. An express rider might have returned in time, but none had been ordered.

“Could you have asked?” Matylde said.

“Yes, Majesty.”

“Why didn’t you?”

He looked at the older man, who did not answer for him.

“We had already exceeded the travel allowance.”

The girl lowered her head. For a moment Riarhin thought she would cry. Instead she asked how much the express would have cost. The clerk told her. It was less than the officers had borrowed to buy one of the replacement horses.

Jaennis put down her pen.

“You cannot expect to know all these things yet.”

Matylde did not look up. “I can expect to be told.”

“You were told there was a difficulty.”

“I was told there were two days.”

“And you chose.”

The words were gentle. Matylde took the letter Jaennis had written, read it and passed it to the messenger.

“Send this by a rider who can come back today.”

The harness maker closed her book. Her husband gathered the worn tack. As they rose, Matylde asked them to wait.

“I should have sent the express.”

The captain bowed.

“If the answer had come in time, would the horses have remained?”

“We could have argued with an order for the posting in our hands. I don’t know whether we would have won.”

She nodded. They left together. The sergeant carried the bridle under his arm this time and left the stirrup leathers for the stores officer to inspect.

In the court the harness maker asked Riarhin whether she thought they would be paid.

“Yes.”

“When?”

“I don’t know.”

“My apprentice is leaving. His father found him a place with a man who pays on the day.”

“Will you have him back?”

“If I ask. If the place is bad. I hope it isn’t.”

She put her hand through her husband’s arm. He leaned towards her until their shoulders touched. They went out without waiting for the sergeant, who had stopped to adjust a buckle on his boot.

Riarhin remained until her escort came for her. She checked the date on the next permission, then asked which officer would confirm it if the queen’s business changed.

Part IV People who stay

Chapter 20 The evening after the ruling

The fountain in the winter hall had been filled with wine for the occasion, but nobody had considered how one was meant to drink it. The cups provided had broad, decorative handles. They caught on the stone lip. A man of importance tried to dip his cup, wet his sleeve to the elbow, and spent the rest of the evening explaining that he had been demonstrating the difficulty.

Coryd watched his third demonstration.

“He has nearly taught it to me,” she said.

Riarhin moved her wine away from her sister’s hand.

“I wasn’t going to do anything.”

“Then it won’t inconvenience you.”

They stood together beside a screen of painted reeds. Beyond it, several women were discussing a marriage with the close attention usually given to an infected tooth. One maintained that the husband had excellent shoulders. Another asked what use these were to anybody except his tailor. The bride’s aunt said that shoulders were very useful indeed and declined, with a smile which suggested a store of particular instances, to explain further.

The warmth reached Riarhin through her coat. Someone had overfilled the braziers; a footman went from window to window admitting strips of cold air which the guests immediately moved to avoid. At the far end of the hall, two men were laying down boards to cover a place where the floor had lifted. One kept his hand against the lower man’s head whenever a guest stepped across them.

The ruling had released eleven timber loads and restored passage to five households. There had been congratulations in council. Edrune had sent a list of the boards too far gone to sell. Riarhin still had it folded in her pocket. She had put it there to remember to give it to the clerk, and now the edge touched her thigh whenever she shifted her weight.

Jaennis had arranged the evening. There was no business announced, no summons to listen, and enough food to make it possible for a guest to spend half an hour occupied by something besides the person beside them. Baked pears stood in shallow dishes. A cook was carving a spiced ham near the doors. The bread was warm.

Riarhin took a piece and found that she was hungry.

“Good,” Coryd said.

“What?”

“You’ve been looking at it as if it ought to produce a warrant.”

A man laughed behind them. Riarhin turned. He was young, red about the ears, and already sorry that he had made himself noticeable.

“My sister,” she told him.

“I guessed.”

“From the resemblance?” Coryd asked.

“From the way she didn’t stab you.”

Coryd laughed. Riarhin found herself laughing too. The young man relaxed enough to give his name, which she missed because a board fell flat on the floor behind him. He worked with the royal horses. He had come to see whether the woman with the green ribbons was dancing; the woman had told him that she would decide when she saw who asked her.

“Ask before somebody better does,” Coryd advised.

He considered this.

“I thought that might be her meaning.”

“Quick fellow,” Riarhin said.

He grinned and went.

She watched him find the woman. He bowed, caught the end of a ribbon in his button, and had to stand very close while she unfastened it. The woman’s friends turned their backs with a concerted delicacy which made Coryd put down her cup.

“I like them.”

“You haven’t met them.”

“I like them already.”

Across the hall, Matylde was trying to prevent Isbray from explaining something. Isbray got three words out each time before the queen interrupted her. Their companion, a boy wearing a new collar too tight for him, listened with the fixed expression of someone determined not to learn what had happened.

Jaennis approached the group carrying a little dish. Matylde took something from it without looking and put it in her mouth. A moment later she turned, smiling, to say something to Jaennis. Riarhin could not hear. She saw the older woman touch the back of the girl’s hand, receive a second smile and move away.

Vaelrie came round the reed screen with a torn piece of bread in one hand.

“Do you mean to stand here all evening?”

“It’s a useful wall,” Riarhin said.

“They’re all useful walls. You needn’t have the dreariest.”

Vaelrie had silver among the hair at her temples. It suited her. Riarhin remembered the younger face and had the uncomfortable sense, briefly, of arriving late at somebody else’s life. Vaelrie saw where she was looking.

“Yes,” she said. “Time. Dreadful business. You should try it.”

Coryd made a small choking sound into her wine.

Vaelrie took Riarhin by neither arm nor hand. She tipped her head towards an empty space beside the music, waited for a decision, and went there with them.

For a while they spoke about horses. Vaelrie had received a letter from Ouen describing a mare which had bitten three grooms and nearly killed a stallion. He seemed very pleased with it.

“He says she has spirit,” Vaelrie said.

“Does he intend to ride her?”

“He intends other people to ride her and tell him about it.”

“Wise.”

“He’ll be delighted you think so. Nobody has said it to him this year.”

Deliane joined them carrying the queen’s abandoned dish. She had a pear stain on the cuff of her sleeve. Vaelrie took the last sweet from the dish and Deliane pretended to slap her wrist.

“That’s for Her Majesty.”

“Then she’ll have to execute me.”

The words reached Riarhin before Vaelrie remembered who stood beside her. There was a tiny, wretched pause. Deliane looked at the floor. Coryd looked directly at Vaelrie.

“The kitchen has more,” Riarhin said.

Vaelrie nodded. “I’ll petition it.”

She went to do so. Deliane remained with the empty dish between her hands.

“She didn’t mean—”

“I know.”

A chair scraped behind them. Riarhin felt the timber list against her leg again.

“Your rooms?” she asked.

Deliane’s face changed. “The second chimney draws. We smoked out the first floor discovering it. A woman came from the linen office and told us we were opening the wrong vent. She then asked why nobody had asked her before moving all the beds.”

“Why hadn’t you?”

“Because I didn’t know she existed.”

“And now?”

“Now she has a key and considers me an idiot.”

“Serafine would have given her two,” Riarhin said, and was surprised by the name in her own mouth.

“Two keys?”

“And let her find someone else to consider an idiot.”

Deliane smiled, but her thumb moved along the empty dish’s rim.

“You knew her?”

“Yes.”

“What was she like?”

The warm room, the food, the music came back around Riarhin with an almost painful sharpness. She could see a different table, a woman whose courtesy had always required her to sit more carefully. There was a whole city behind that remembered face.

“She would have disliked my answering for her.”

“Very well. I shall be left in ignorance.”

“You won’t be the first.”

Deliane laughed and took the dish back to the queen.

Later Jaennis asked Riarhin whether she had eaten. Riarhin said that she had. Jaennis offered to have something wrapped for Coryd, who had spent most of the evening talking and would be hungry by the time they returned to their lodging. The observation was accurate. Riarhin accepted.

A woman waiting beside Jaennis said that her carriage was ready at last; she could leave for the southern road in three days if the weather held. Jaennis asked whether the larger travelling case had been repaired. The woman said yes, owing entirely to the man Jaennis had sent, and insisted again that there would always be a place for her if she wished to come.

“Not this time, Aluen,” Jaennis said. “Write when you reach your sister.”

Riarhin stepped aside to let them speak. Matylde passed her on the way to Isbray, then stopped.

“Will you tell Solenne I am staying another half-hour?”

“She’s beside the musicians, Majesty.”

“Oh. Yes.”

The girl started towards her. Jaennis called her name.

Matylde came back immediately.

“There is a loose thread at your neck. Stand a moment.”

Jaennis turned the tie over between her fingers. The music changed. Isbray was already finding the first step with the boy in the tight collar. Matylde looked round, then held still while Jaennis unpicked the knot.

“You’ve had a good evening,” Jaennis said.

“Yes.”

“I thought you would.”

The knot came free. Jaennis smoothed the tie and began again.

Riarhin went to find Coryd’s parcel.

The dancing began badly.

The musicians had chosen a measure known to most of the older company and almost none of the younger guests. Everybody expected somebody else to lead. At the first turn three couples went one way and two the other; Madra, newly returned from choosing her son’s tutor, put both hands on her hips and ordered the men to stop pretending they knew what came next.

“I knew,” the knight said.

“Then you were concealing it with great skill.”

He bowed. She took his hand and demonstrated the turn so sharply that he nearly lost his footing. On the next attempt the line held together. People at the edges began clapping, some in time.

Riarhin had collected Coryd’s food and meant to leave. Her sister was already in the second line with Sivelle. Coryd could learn a movement after seeing it once, but this particular dance required her to allow people who had not learned it as quickly to pass in front of her. Twice she stopped too soon. On the third turn Sivelle whispered something which made her laugh and move with the rest.

Deliane came to Riarhin’s side carrying two cups.

“You’re looking very severely at our entertainment.”

“I’m waiting for my sister.”

“Then you’d better drink.”

Riarhin accepted one cup. The wine was better than the guesthouse wine and tasted of the dried fruit added to it. She drank more than she intended.

“Would you like to dance?” Deliane asked.

“No.”

“Have you tried?”

“Yes.”

“Successfully?”

“Depending on who was being asked.”

Deliane grinned. “That is the most promising answer I’ve had all evening.”

She took the cup back and held out a hand. Riarhin could have refused again. Instead she laid the parcel on a chair and went with her.

At the first change of partner, the man facing Riarhin saw who she was and nearly stopped. Deliane, passing behind him, caught his wrist and brought him through the turn before he could decide what to do. By the time he returned to his place, the movement had gone on without his permission. He performed the next figure correctly and would not look at Riarhin’s face.

She kept dancing. There was nothing to solve. Each person had somewhere to go and a hand to take. She knew the steps now. The difficulty was allowing herself to remain inside them while people looked.

When the music ended, she was breathing more deeply than the exercise warranted. Deliane had a loose strand of hair across her cheek. She pushed it away, smiled at Riarhin and looked past her towards Jaennis.

The older woman had watched from a chair near the musicians. She held out one hand when Deliane approached. Deliane took it and bent close to hear what she said. Her face changed into a smile Riarhin had seen only in private moments accidentally witnessed.

Madra appeared beside Riarhin with her shoes in one hand.

“Don’t tell anybody.”

“They can see.”

“Not if they are polite.”

She sat on the chair where the food parcel lay, discovered it and handed it to Riarhin.

“My son’s tutor is a solemn young man who knows everything worth knowing about dead rulers and nothing about eleven-year-old boys.”

“You chose him?”

“My son did.”

“Why?”

“Because he discovered the tutor can juggle.”

Riarhin looked at her. Madra sighed.

“He promised to teach him after the lessons. My husband was appalled. I thought it the first sensible educational proposal we’d received.”

“Is your husband still appalled?”

“He’s learning with him. Badly, I hope.”

She flexed her toes beneath the hem of her gown. Sivelle came over with Coryd and stooped to inspect the discarded shoes.

“You wore these to punish yourself.”

“I wore them because you said they were lovely.”

“They are. I didn’t tell you to walk in them.”

Coryd sat on the floor to examine the heel of one. Madra watched her with interest.

“Are you keeping this one?” she asked Sivelle.

“Ask her.”

“I was asking whether you meant to behave badly before I became fond of her.”

Coryd looked up. “I’m already here.”

“Good,” Madra said. “Then you can hear the warning.”

Sivelle’s smile thinned. “Madra.”

The other woman looked at her, then reached for the shoe without quite meeting Coryd’s eyes.

“I’m sorry. Wine and dancing make me consider myself amusing.”

“You were amusing,” Coryd said. “Then you continued.”

Riarhin nearly laughed. She kept her face still because Madra had gone red.

Sivelle put a hand on Coryd’s shoulder. Coryd covered it briefly with her own, then went back to the damaged heel. Madra watched the small movement and looked away.

The musicians began a slower measure. Deliane was dancing with Jaennis, adapting her step so that the older woman need not turn sharply. Jaennis said something which made her stop accommodating quite so carefully. They moved with a better rhythm after that.

Vaelrie stood at the edge of the floor. She was smiling, though one hand worried the clasp of her bracelet. Riarhin went to stand beside her.

“Would you like your turn?” she asked.

Vaelrie glanced at her. “You assume I am being deprived.”

“I asked.”

“Yes. You did.”

She let the bracelet alone.

“She’s good tonight. The knee has been troublesome.”

“Deliane noticed.”

“Deliane notices most things. That’s one reason she wants a job in which she can finish noticing and go home.”

Riarhin looked at her. Vaelrie kept watching the dance.

After a while she said, “Don’t ask me to dislike her.”

“I wasn’t going to.”

“Someone will.”

On the floor Jaennis missed a step. Deliane caught her balance without making it visible to anyone not already watching. Jaennis laughed, a sudden full laugh which made three people turn towards her. Vaelrie’s face softened.

Coryd’s shoe repair held long enough for Madra to attempt another dance. Sivelle chose not to join that one. She remained beside Coryd, speaking quietly. Madra looked at them once, then concentrated on her partner.

Riarhin found the parcel again and asked the servant whether there were more cakes she could take for the escort. There were. He wrapped them separately, pleased to have a request he could fulfil at once.

Matylde and Isbray had gone upstairs. Margery arrived while the musicians were deciding whether to play another measure.

She had changed her dress. Riarhin noticed that before Jaennis saw her: a darker colour, the neck open, a pin which required more care than Margery usually gave her clothes. She stopped beside Jaennis’s chair and put her hand on its back.

“You said you weren’t coming,” Jaennis said.

“I changed my mind.”

“I’m glad.”

Margery leaned down to kiss her. Jaennis caught her face between her hands and kept her there a moment longer. When Margery straightened, she was laughing, a little breathlessly.

“What?” Jaennis asked.

“Nothing.”

“Then sit here and tell me about it.”

A servant brought a chair. Margery sat close enough to lay her arm along the back of Jaennis’s, and did. Riarhin saw her look across the room, see her and stop smiling. She looked away at once. Jaennis said something and Margery bent to hear it.

Riarhin went to find water. When she returned, Margery had gone to speak to the woman arranging the next day’s visit to her house. Jaennis followed her with her eyes until Aluen touched her sleeve.

The musicians were resting when Aluen brought her cousin to Jaennis.

The cousin had been widowed three years and had come to Valora to settle her husband’s remaining business. Riarhin had learned both things from Madra, together with an estimate of the husband’s competence which his surviving accounts seemed to support. She was a woman in her thirties, heavy-haired, wearing dark silk which turned copper where the candles caught it. One earring was missing. She had removed the other rather than let the loss announce itself.

Aluen wanted an introduction to someone who could read the disputed accounts. Her cousin wanted to speak to Jaennis. They were not quite the same request.

When Aluen had gone to find the man she wanted, the younger woman remained. She leaned down to hear something Jaennis said, laughed, and answered close to her ear. Jaennis’s head turned towards her. Riarhin, passing with a cup to return, saw her take the woman’s wrist and hold it between both hands.

“Sit,” Jaennis said. “You make me feel I must rise, and I have spent the evening being foolish with my knee.”

“Would you dance again if it didn’t hurt?”

“With you? Certainly.”

“Then I shall sit. I don’t want you remembering me tomorrow as a bad idea.”

Jaennis laughed. She moved her own cup off the neighbouring chair. The woman sat so close that their sleeves lay together.

Riarhin returned the cup. When she looked again, Jaennis was listening with her chin tilted, the attention in her face entirely unlike the patient welcome she offered a petitioner. The woman’s fingers had settled against the edge of her sleeve. She was saying something about the rooms her cousin had found her. Jaennis looked at her mouth as she spoke.

Sivelle came for Coryd. They wanted to leave before the final dance made leaving a public announcement. Coryd gathered her things and asked Riarhin whether she would mind going back with the escort alone.

“No. Go.”

She meant it, and was glad she meant it. Sivelle thanked her for the loan of a cloak which Riarhin had forgotten lending. They went out arm in arm.

At the far end of the room the widow rose to let a servant move the cold brazier. Jaennis caught her sleeve.

“Where are you going?”

“Nowhere yet.”

She bent and kissed Jaennis, slowly enough that a man speaking beside them lost the next word of his sentence. When she straightened, Jaennis’s hand remained at her waist.

“Come tomorrow.”

“I have the accounts.”

“After them.”

“If they finish.”

“They will finish.”

The woman smiled. “Will you make them?”

“I may be able to make them less disagreeable.”

“I would prefer you disagreeable, if it meant I could have you to myself.”

Jaennis drew her down again. This kiss had no audience in its intention, though the room supplied one. Riarhin looked away before the younger woman stood.

Aluen was waiting by the door. Her cousin went to join her, stopping once to look back. Jaennis lifted her hand.

A servant came to collect the cups. Jaennis asked him to leave the second one.

Riarhin was looking for her escort when Aluen returned, without her cousin, for a parcel she had forgotten. Jaennis beckoned her.

“Where is she staying?”

Aluen named the lodging.

“Those rooms are noisy. I can find something better.”

“She likes them. There’s a balcony.”

“And the cart yard beneath it.”

“She says she slept through worse when her husband was alive.”

Jaennis smiled, but the smile had arrived a little late. “Ask her to come at noon. I’ll have someone look at the papers first.”

“She may still be with the accountant.”

“Then send for her.”

Aluen tucked the parcel under her arm. “I’ll tell her you’re expecting her.”

The servant had returned for the remaining cup. Jaennis gave it up. A drop of wine had fallen on her cuff where the widow’s hand had rested. She touched the stain, then looked across the room for Deliane.

Deliane was laughing beside the musicians. She had one shoe in her hand and her head thrown back. Jaennis watched until somebody came to ask her whether the northern visitors should be given chairs at tomorrow’s dinner. She asked which visitors. The servant had to begin again.

Chapter 21 The debt after relief

Velden arrived at their lodging with a letter he would not put down.

Rain had soaked the shoulders of his coat. The rest of him was dry; he had evidently spent some time sheltering within sight of the door before deciding to knock. The cook made him stand on a mat while she fetched a cloth for the chair. He obeyed with an attention that distressed her more than the water had.

“Sit, man. I meant the coat.”

He sat with the coat on.

Coryd took it off him. Riarhin brought another chair. There were three seats at the kitchen table, and they arranged themselves so that nobody had to sit at its head.

The letter asked him to attend the registry that afternoon. His testimony about the grey horse had given the clerks two different dates for an order. They wished to establish which was correct. It offered an allowance for his time.

“They’ll ask it again,” he said.

“They may.”

“I told it.”

“They have a written order which may have been copied later.”

“I told it how it happened.”

“I believe you.”

He looked down at the letter.

Riarhin had used the words to settle him. They did not. He held the paper tightly enough to bend the lower corner, and she remembered him in the room with Jaennis, struggling to breathe before the cut thread fell. He had been able to speak after that. She had watched the relief take his whole body.

Now his wife had been offered different work. Better work, if the account could be trusted: fewer early mornings, a room not shared with the dairy’s night man, and wages paid from the household’s central purse. She wanted to take it. Velden did not know whether she should.

“Who made the offer?”

“The steward’s woman. Lady Jaennis had written.”

“Has your wife seen the room?”

“Yes.”

“What does she say?”

“She says it’s dry.”

Coryd put bread between them. Velden thanked her and left it untouched.

“You needn’t ask Lady Jaennis before speaking to the clerk,” Riarhin said.

He rubbed the bent corner flat.

“I said I’d come to her.”

“If there was trouble.”

“Yes.”

“Is this trouble?”

He looked up then, as if she had accused him of inventing it.

Riarhin leaned back. She could hear the cook chopping something behind her. One cut, two, a pause to gather the pieces. Through the window the rain lay across the yard in a pale sheet.

“Would you like me to go with you?” she asked.

He shook his head quickly.

That hurt, absurdly. She had done nothing to earn his comfort. At the hearing she had sat with the people who could command an answer, and he knew why she had come to Valora.

“Edrune?” Coryd asked.

He considered it. “She’d be busy.”

“We can ask.”

“She doesn’t talk over a man.”

“No,” Riarhin said. “She doesn’t.”

He ate some bread while they sent a message. When the cook put a bowl beside him, he began to get up; she pushed his shoulder down and said there would be no sense washing a bowl twice. He smiled for the first time.

Edrune came with mud on her hem and anger directed at an entirely different person. A shipping clerk had decided that damaged timber should be valued as firewood before she could object to the storage that had damaged it. She had made him write the proposal out.

“He lost enthusiasm when he came to the signature.”

Velden listened. The kitchen grew less still around him.

Edrune read his letter and asked him how long the interview had taken before. He said two hours. She said she had one, and if they needed him for longer they could give another appointment. He was to ask for his allowance at the beginning, because she was not walking back through that rain to collect it afterwards.

“Will they think I came for the money?”

“You came because they asked. You need the money because you came.”

She gave him his letter. Riarhin watched him fold it properly this time.

They left together under Coryd’s least damaged cloak. An hour and a half later Edrune returned it.
