The clerk had agreed that Velden was remembering the original spoken order and the paper a later repetition. He had been paid. His wife could take the dry room without making a declaration about her husband’s evidence. Edrune had asked for that in writing and received a look she seemed to have enjoyed.

“Where is he?”

“Gone to tell Lady Jaennis it went well.”

Riarhin let out her breath through her nose.

“He wanted to,” Edrune said.

“I know.”

“Do you?”

Riarhin folded the damp cloak. She had made a space on the table for Velden to sit in, and already she was deciding where he ought to go next.

“Thank you,” she said.

“Pay the message boy. He says you haven’t.”

That evening she wrote to Lyra.

The first sheet began with a question about Jaennis. Riarhin read it, turned it face down and tried again. She asked whether the book had reached her, whether the winter had been kind, and whether she wanted letters at this address at all. It sounded like a person making a courteous approach before requesting something unpleasant. She put her pen down.

Coryd, sitting near the fire with a stocking stretched over her hand, said, “You can send an unpleasant letter.”

“I know.”

“You’ve been polishing it for half an hour.”

Riarhin turned the first sheet up again. There was nothing false in it. She had described the released oath and Velden’s difficulty afterward. She had not named him. She had written that Matylde was trying to establish what could be required of a person who wanted to leave a household, and that an account from someone who had suffered a charm might help her understand the limits of an apparently willing answer.

Lyra might reasonably tell her to find another witness. Riarhin wrote that too.

She asked for no account of the words Lyra had spoken under the charm. No confession, no inventory. If Lyra wished to say what asking should have looked like, that was enough. She could address her answer directly to Solenne, who would receive it under seal for the queen, or send nothing.

At the bottom, after signing, Riarhin wrote that she was glad Lyra was alive.

She nearly crossed it out. It was a poor, enormous thing to put at the foot of a request.

She left it.

The courier was leaving before dawn. Riarhin carried the letter down herself and waited while he wrote its destination into his book. He asked whether she required an answer.

“Only if there is one.”

He made a mark beside the entry and put the letter with the others.

Two days after the letter went, a message came from the guesthouse asking whether Riarhin would attend the clerk’s return.

Velden was not there. His wife had arrived in his place.

She was smaller than Riarhin expected, with reddened hands and a steady way of looking at anyone who asked about her work. Her youngest sat beside her eating a piece of bread too large to bite comfortably. The child kept turning it, searching for a more accommodating edge.

“Where is he?” Riarhin asked.

“Buying what we came for. I wanted to say this without him answering.”

The clerk set out a fresh sheet. Riarhin took the chair nearest the door.

The wife wanted the dry room. She had inspected it twice. The new work would keep her away from the coldest part of the dairy and allow her to have the child nearby in the afternoon. She did not want her acceptance entered as a request by her husband, nor did she wish it treated as proof that everything said about the old household was true.

The clerk asked how she wished it described.

“As my work.”

He wrote.

“Will I still be paid if Velden is required to travel?”

“Yes,” the clerk said.

“Will I still be paid if he says something Lady Jaennis doesn’t like?”

The pen stopped. Riarhin kept her eyes on the woman. A pulse was visible in the hollow above her collar.

“The appointment is to the work,” the clerk said at last. “I can have that specified.”

“Do.”

The child offered the bread to her mother, who took a bite without thinking and handed it back. Then she looked at Riarhin.

“He says you offered to take us away.”

“I offered to help if you wanted to leave.”

“He heard the first part.”

“I know that now.”

“He’s been frightened for a long time. When somebody important offers something, he thinks there won’t be another chance to say yes.”

Riarhin nodded. The woman turned back to the clerk before the nod could become an answer on her behalf.

She asked about the room’s stove. She asked whether the child could remain there when a visiting officer occupied the adjoining chamber. She asked who would pay for the fuel. The clerk knew two of the answers and sent for the third. They waited while the child finally worked out how to tear the bread with both hands.

Velden returned with a parcel under his arm. He stopped when he saw his wife at the table. She beckoned him over and showed him what she had asked to have written.

“You needn’t trouble them with all of it,” he said.

“I am troubling them now.”

He stood beside her, uncertain. Riarhin saw him glance at the doorway, perhaps expecting Jaennis or someone sent by her. Nobody came. After a moment he sat down and unwrapped the parcel. It contained a little pair of shoes.

The child abandoned the bread.

“They’re too big,” the mother said.

“They’ll last.”

“They’ll fall off.”

“They can be tied.”

The clerk smiled. Riarhin looked out of the window while the parents argued about something they both knew how to dispute.

Margery’s message asked Riarhin to come before the afternoon bell. It said that Tessart was visiting and that she did not wish to hear him alone.

Riarhin took it to Solenne. The officer read it, asked whether she would consent to having two men accompany her and looked irritated when Riarhin said she had come to request them. One would remain at the house gate and one within call. The visit was entered among the day’s approved movements. Remorse was returned to her at the outer store.

The road to Margery’s house passed between a walled orchard and a field from which the grain had been cut. Stubble showed through the wet wheel marks at the field’s edge. A man was lifting fallen pears into a basket beneath the orchard wall. Riarhin remembered another afternoon here, Margery changing her shoes because the earth beneath the trees had swallowed one. She had followed her into the garden wearing a dead woman’s face.

Now the gate servant knew her own. He hesitated, checked the escort badge and admitted them.

Tessart’s horse stood in the yard beside a hired vehicle. Its reins had been looped loosely through a ring rather than tied. A groom was rubbing mud from the vehicle’s side, where a painted device had been covered with a cloth. There was nothing unusual in either arrangement. Riarhin noted them together and asked her escort to wait beside the vehicle.

Margery met her at the house door.

“He has brought a secretary.”

“Do you want them to leave?”

“I want to know why he thinks I can settle this.”

“Shall I stay?”

“Yes.”

She said it with enough force that Riarhin looked at her. Margery had dressed for receiving a councillor. One fastening at her cuff was undone; the two halves moved apart when she raised her hand to show Riarhin through.

Tessart was in the small room overlooking the orchard. He stood when they entered. His secretary stayed beside the window with a writing case open on his knee. A third man occupied the place near the passage, broad in a coat which did not quite conceal the stiff leather beneath it. Riarhin had seen men wear such coats when they wished to arrive as servants and leave as something else.

Tessart offered her a chair.

“I shall stand,” she said.

“We have no quarrel with you here.”

“Then you won’t mind.”

Margery sat at her own table. A packet of household letters lay in front of her. Tessart glanced at it, then began explaining that distress had led several people to seek arrangements which might not survive examination. Riarhin had come at Margery’s request. There had been private discussion before the sovereign summons. It was understandable that the councillors administering the older restrictions should wish to know whether the hearing had been solicited for a different purpose.

“The queen knows I asked her to attend,” Margery said.

“Knows everything?”

“She knows that.”

“And Lady Jaennis?”

“Knows I wanted Matylde to have her answers.”

Tessart turned slightly towards Riarhin. “All of us want answers. Some have been waiting rather longer than others.”

The secretary took a sheet from his case. Riarhin could see that only the last lines were blank. He had brought a statement to be signed.

“Read it,” Margery said.

He began. The first paragraph accurately described the invitation. The second suggested that private assurances had been made about the outcome of the hearing. Margery stopped him there.

“I made no assurance.”

“We can alter the word,” Tessart said.

“What did you think I had promised?”

“Please. We need not begin by misunderstanding each other.”

Riarhin watched Margery’s hand move over the packet of letters. She had brought them to establish when she had written, Riarhin thought, and had found herself being asked to explain something else.

Tessart said that an acknowledgment of improper approaches would allow the council to pause the hearings without appearing to repudiate the queen. The petitioners would retain their right to be heard later. The returned property could be held by agreed parties meanwhile. Jaennis would be spared a public accusation that her household had introduced a private quarrel into the queen’s business.

“Who is accusing her?” Margery asked.

“People will.”

“Will you?”

He looked at the secretary. The man lowered the page.

Riarhin said, “Let him finish reading.”

Margery turned towards her, startled. Riarhin held her gaze until she nodded.

The last paragraph asked Margery to affirm that any meeting between Riarhin and aggrieved former servants had been within the purpose of the visit. No officer ought to have mistaken a request for such a meeting for an attempt to obstruct the queen’s protection.

Riarhin looked at Tessart.

“Kerros nearly lost his hand.”

“Which is why it is important to establish what each person intended.”

“Who asked you for that paragraph?”

He did not answer. Margery pushed the page back across the table.

“I won’t sign it.”

“Then we must ask for the correspondence.”

“You may ask.”

“I am asking.”

“No.”

Tessart’s secretary rose. He had put down the writing case. His hands were empty until he reached the table and picked up the packet beneath Margery’s palm.

She held it down.

“These are mine.”

“Copies will be returned, mistress.”

The broad man came away from the passage. Riarhin moved before he reached Margery. She placed herself beside the table, leaving Tessart behind her and the man in front of her, and told him to stay where he was.

He stopped just beyond reach.

Margery pulled the packet towards herself. Several sheets slid free. The secretary caught one. She tried to take it from him and he lifted it above her hand, an ugly, easy movement that brought her halfway out of the chair.

Riarhin called the escort.

The broad man drew his knife.

There was no room to draw Remorse towards him. Riarhin moved backwards along the table, pulling the scabbard behind her right hip and bringing the hilt out left-handed as she turned. The blade cleared the edge of the table. The man came fast enough to strike before she had completed the turn.

She gave ground into the doorway.

His knife cut through the cloth above her right wrist. She felt the scrape without knowing its depth. He had followed the retreat rather than the sword; now he stood with one foot over the threshold, unable to turn towards Margery without showing his side.

Riarhin stepped outside his forward foot. Remorse went across the meat of his thigh, a short cut made with both of them moving. His leg failed to take the next step. He caught the doorpost with his free hand and struck at her again.

She knocked the knife arm into the wood with the sword’s guard. He dropped the weapon, tried to recover it and fell into the passage.

“Stay down.”

He was trying to get his hand beneath him. She put the point where he would have to rise into it. The escort reached the door with his own blade half drawn and stopped at the sight of her.

“His knife,” she said. “Under the table.”

Behind her Tessart was speaking quickly. The secretary had let go of the packet. Margery stood with the loose sheets pressed against her body, looking at the blood appearing beneath the man’s knee.

“I said take the papers,” Tessart said. “There was no instruction to draw anything.”

The escort looked at him, then at Margery.

“Did you ask these men to remove your papers?”

“No.”

“Did you tell them to stop?”

“Yes.”

The man on the floor made a sound which turned into a groan. Riarhin looked at the wound. More blood was coming than she liked. She lowered the sword when the escort had his hands secured and asked Margery for linen.

Margery did not move.

“From the kitchen. A towel.”

She went. Riarhin knelt beside the injured man, careful of the dropped knife until the escort kicked it away. He tried to pull his leg from her. She caught his knee and told him that he would bleed if he kept moving.

“You cut me.”

“Yes. Hold still.”

Margery came back with three towels. She dropped the first when she saw how quickly it darkened. Riarhin took the second from her hand and pressed it into the wound.

“Get a healer,” she told the escort.

“My man is at the gate.”

“Send him.”

Tessart made for the other door.

“Remain here, sir,” the escort said.

“I will attend before the council. You know where to find me.”

“The mistress has made a complaint. Please remain.”

The councillor looked at Riarhin kneeling in his man’s blood. She could feel what he would make of the sight before he said anything.

Margery stepped between him and the door.

“I’ll make it again if you didn’t hear it.”

He stopped.

Riarhin sat in the kitchen while they questioned Margery. The cut at her wrist was shallow; the cook washed it, shook her head over the ruined cuff and tied a strip around it. It hurt more once it was clean.

The injured man lay in the room off the passage. She could hear him complain each time the healer moved his leg. Tessart had been taken to the guard office with his secretary. Neither had been put in irons. The letters remained on Margery’s table beneath an upturned bowl, where the draught from the open door would not scatter them.

Solenne came herself. She asked Riarhin to describe the knife, the hand holding it and the first movement she had made. Riarhin told her. When she reached the cut to the thigh, the officer asked whether she had intended it.

“Yes.”

“You had room to retreat?”

“Into the passage. He would still have been in the room with her.”

“Did you attempt to kill him?”

“No.”

“Could that wound have killed him?”

“Yes.”

Solenne wrote. Riarhin looked at the blood in the basin where she had washed her hands.

“The healer believes he will keep the leg,” the officer said.

Riarhin nodded.

Margery came into the kitchen after Solenne left. She sat on the stool and took the cup the cook gave her. She did not drink.

“I have never had to tell someone twice to leave my table,” she said.

Riarhin could not tell whether she meant Tessart or his secretary. She let the words stand.

“He said Jaennis had told him to speak to me.”

“When?”

“Before you arrived. She’d said that I knew why you came, that I could clear up his anxieties.”

“Did you ask her?”

“She had sent him. I thought I knew.”

Margery set down the cup. Her hands were shaking too much to hold it without spilling.

“I want her,” she said. “I know what you’ll think of that.”

Riarhin pushed the basin away.

“Shall I send?”

Margery looked at her, searching her face for the qualification. Riarhin waited.

“Yes. But tell her I am staying here tonight. She can come here if she wants to.”

“I’ll tell the officer.”

Margery’s hand moved towards Riarhin’s bandaged wrist, then stopped short of touching it.

“Thank you,” she said.

The maid came in to ask whether the papers were to be locked up. Margery rose and went to put them away herself.

Chapter 22 The promise about leaving

Deliane had bought the curtains herself.

They were a dull yellow in the shop and a sickly green in the guesthouse’s morning light. She stood in the middle of the larger front room with two lengths of the stuff over her arm, while the linen woman held a third against the window and said nothing at all.

“You may say it,” Deliane told her.

“They’ll keep the draught out.”

“Cruel woman.”

“I could be crueller. I haven’t hung them.”

Coryd was on the floor fitting a wedge beneath a table leg. She gave a laugh so low that it scarcely carried beyond the table. Deliane heard it and pointed at her with the curtains.

“You liked them in the shop.”

“In the shop they were behind you.”

Riarhin stood by the door holding a packet from the appointments office in her left hand. The dressing beneath her right cuff caught when she bent the wrist; she had already been told twice to leave it alone. She had been asked to bring it because she was already coming; the clerk had made the distinction twice, evidently fearing that she might otherwise regard herself as part of the office. Inside were Deliane’s salary, the authority to engage two resident servants and the date on which she would take responsibility for the house. The date was in six days.

Deliane read it without sitting down.

“Six days,” she said.

“Is that too soon?”

“No.”

The linen woman had begun counting hooks. Coryd tapped the wedge farther beneath the leg and tested the table with both hands.

“No,” Deliane repeated. “It’s quite enough time.”

They spent the morning finding that it was not enough time for everything. The kitchen had three pots and none large enough for the number of guests the office proposed to send. A resident porter had been promised wages which the appointments paper did not name. The new cook would not come until she knew where her sister could sleep. Deliane wanted to say that sisters were not part of the arrangement, caught Coryd’s glance and went to inspect the space over the stable.

It was dry. The sister could sleep there. The porter had his wages entered on a separate sheet. The pots would have to be borrowed.

Riarhin followed Deliane into the little office at the back. There was a narrow window overlooking an empty yard, a desk with an ink burn and a shelf whose brackets had once been used somewhere else. Deliane laid her appointment on the desk and smoothed it with her palm.

“Will he find you here?” Riarhin asked.

Deliane’s hand stopped.

“I mean the man who—”

“I know who you mean.”

Riarhin waited.

“He knows where the house is. Everyone knows where the house is. His brother no longer supplies the wine. Solenne has spoken to the replacement supplier, who has a wife and five children and no desire to be killed over my private life. The porter will not admit him. If he writes, I can send the letter to the officer without opening it.”

She said it quickly, as if somebody had made her recite it until she could do so without a breath.

“And Lady Jaennis?”

“Will tell you I have never understood what men like him can do.”

Riarhin had herself thought something close to that. She looked through the little window. A child on the other side of the yard was coaxing a cat down from a wall by offering it a broken feather.

“Do you understand?”

Deliane sat. “I know him.”

Deliane’s gaze held hers. Riarhin gave a small nod.

“I’ve asked Orven for a cart,” Deliane said. “And I shall speak to her this afternoon.”

“Do you want someone with you?”

“For moving the chest, yes. For speaking, no.”

Riarhin left the appointment on the desk.

They went back through the kitchen. The cook had taken possession early and was standing over the smallest pot, regarding it with the dislike usually reserved for another person’s child.

“I can feed six from that,” she said. “Provided three have eaten.”

Deliane set down her bag. “Show me.”

The cook looked at her armful of papers.

“Put those somewhere dry, then.”

Riarhin expected Deliane to explain that she meant the list of missing things. Instead she put the papers on a shelf and went to the water bucket. The cook gave her a measure. They filled the pot together, counting how much would disappear into grain and how much must remain to keep it from catching. Riarhin stood aside when the kitchen girl came in with wood. The girl came a second time and Riarhin stood aside again, then asked where the rest was.

“In the rain, if nobody has covered it.”

Nobody had. Riarhin and Coryd moved it under the overhang while the cook demonstrated three different ways in which a dinner promised by people in offices could fail before any guest reached the table.

Deliane came out with flour on her cuff.

“I shall have to buy one.”

“A pot?” Coryd asked.

“A large one. She says borrowing makes us eat when the other kitchen has finished.”

“How much?”

Deliane named a figure. Coryd whistled.

“I could have bought brown curtains and nobody would be hungry,” Deliane said.

They went to the metalworkers’ street after putting the wood away. Deliane carried a piece of string the cook had tied to the necessary circumference. Riarhin had seen similar shopping accomplished by servants who returned to be told that the master had meant a different thing; Deliane made Coryd hold the string round two pots and lowered a third from its hook herself.

The seller wanted to address Riarhin. He offered the weight and capacity to her, and then the price.

“I am not buying it,” she said.

He explained the same things to Coryd, who was turning the pot to inspect its bottom.

“She is,” Coryd said, and pointed.

Deliane leaned on the counter. “Are you waiting for a woman with a husband? We can come back when you’ve found one.”

The seller made an apology which included the difficulty of knowing anybody’s business these days. Deliane let him finish, then asked about the patched place beside the foot.

He answered before she had finished asking. Coryd crouched and scraped the edge with her nail. The patch was sound, she thought, but the pot should cost less than one that had never leaked.

They argued about how much less. Outside, somebody began sharpening a blade against a turning stone. The noise rose and fell while Deliane and the seller moved towards a figure neither wished to be the first to name. Riarhin went to the doorway. She had offered to carry the pot.

When Deliane joined her, she had bought the pot and paid to have it delivered.

She looked pleased, until she looked down at what remained in her purse.

“Lunch?” Coryd said.

“Bread.”

“Splendid. My favourite meal that is not a meal.”

They bought bread and cheese and ate standing beside a wall where the sun had dried the stone. Deliane broke off the hardest crust for herself. The string from the cook hung out of her pocket.

Deliane brushed the crumbs off her cuff and found the flour underneath.

At the palace, an hour later, she passed Aluen’s servants carrying two repaired cases through the lower court. One had a new leather corner so much paler than the rest that it looked like a patch of skin. Orven was telling the carter to use the household’s covered way if it rained; Lady Aluen’s horses were lodging there until her road party left. He interrupted himself to ask Riarhin whether she had business upstairs. She gave him the name of the clerk she had come to see.

He knew it already. He let her pass.

She did not see Deliane again until near dusk.

The younger woman was sitting on the stair outside a room used for mending hangings. No lamps had been brought there yet. In the blue light from the landing window her hands looked cold, though she had them pressed flat between her knees.

Riarhin stopped two steps below her.

“Shall I go?”

Deliane shook her head.

For a little while they listened to women talking inside the mending room. Someone had lost a needle and was accusing the youngest of having eaten it. The girl replied that she preferred silver, if she was to be suspected of extravagance.

“I promised,” Deliane said.

Riarhin stayed where she was.

“After that man. When she let me stay. I said I would come to her before I went anywhere else.”

“You did.”

“Yes.”

Deliane lifted her hands from between her knees and looked at them. They were shaking.

“She says I am making the words smaller now that I don’t need her.”

“Did she forbid you to go?”

“No.”

“Then—”

“Don’t.”

Riarhin closed her mouth.

Deliane drew breath, failed to finish it, and leaned forward. Riarhin moved up one stair and stopped herself from taking hold of her. Deliane shook her head again, more violently, and put her hand against the wall.

“It happens when I say the date.”

“Has it happened before?”

“Sometimes. When we’ve quarrelled. I thought I was frightened of being foolish.”

The women in the next room laughed. A bell began somewhere below, slow and irregular; somebody was testing a cracked mounting.

Deliane tried to say something. Nothing came. She swallowed and stared down at Riarhin with a furious, wet look.

A woman came out of the mending room for water. She paused when she recognised Deliane, then set her empty jug down and crouched on the stair above them.

“Shall I fetch her?”

Deliane shook her head.

“She’ll be waiting. They’ve kept your place.”

“I know.”

“My mother always said an empty chair was worse than an empty bowl. You can fill a bowl yourself.”

Deliane pressed her lips together. Riarhin could see the answering kindness assembling in her face. It did not survive another breath.

The woman touched her shoulder. “She wouldn’t have had the chair left there if she meant you unwelcome.”

“Please get your water,” Riarhin said.

The woman rose, affronted. She picked up the jug and went down past them. At the bottom she turned, apparently expecting Deliane to call her back. Deliane had closed her eyes.

“Now she’ll think I’m afraid to speak in front of you,” she said.

From below came the soft collision of the jug with the pump.

“Six days,” Riarhin said softly.

Deliane breathed out.

“I know the date.”

“Yes.”

“I am not asking you to tell it to me.”

“I know.”

They sat until the women came out with their baskets. One offered Deliane a sweet from her pocket. Deliane took it and put it in her mouth. She kept the wrapper crushed in her fist all the way down the stair.

The next evening, Deliane asked her friends to help pack.

She had said friends, in the plural, and had sent word to Riarhin separately. Riarhin brought Coryd because Coryd had been asked too; she did not mistake the invitation for an instruction to keep watch. Nevertheless she took the chair from which the door was visible, and Sivelle noticed.

“You can see it from here,” she said, moving a basket off the window seat. “And this one’s kinder to your back.”

Riarhin sat where she suggested. A fire burned low in the grate. It was too warm to need one, but Deliane had papers to destroy, and had refused to let the servants decide which of her old letters should be read a final time by the kitchen boy.

Madra held up a narrow silk scarf.

“This is mine.”

“Then why is it in my chest?” Deliane asked.

“You took it off me.”

“That explains why it is in my chest.”

“You said you needed it for a day.”

“I did.”

Madra wound it round her throat with a satisfied expression. Deliane waited until she had tied it and then said, “It has that burn at the end.”

Madra pulled the knot loose again.

Sivelle was making three heaps on the bed. Clothes for the guesthouse, clothes to leave in storage, clothes nobody could explain having purchased. The last heap was largest. Coryd tried on a hat from it and looked sufficiently solemn that Riarhin laughed before she could stop herself.

“There she is,” Coryd said, turning towards the small mirror. “I knew she was in there.”

Madra said the hat would suit a woman expecting to be accused of poisoning a husband. Deliane said it had been fashionable for nearly a week.

The afternoon went easily until Sivelle took a little embroidered shift from beneath a stack of linen. Deliane held out her hand before Sivelle could decide which heap it belonged in.

“That stays with me.”

Madra’s eyes went to the fire.

“What?” Deliane asked.

“Nothing.”

“Something.”

“I don’t think you’ve thought about how she will feel.”

Deliane folded the garment. It took longer with everybody looking at her.

“I am taking a position across the city.”

“Yes. I can see that. We are putting things in boxes.”

Sivelle said Madra’s name, without warmth.

“No, let her,” Deliane said. “How will she feel?”

Madra sat on the edge of the bed. “As though you’ve been waiting for somebody important to say you needn’t come home.”

“Somebody important has given me a salary.”

“And a house.”

“For work.”

“I know.”

“You don’t sound as though you do.”

Riarhin put down the stocking she had been mending. She had made four crooked stitches while deciding whether the conversation required her. Deliane caught the movement.

“Please don’t,” she said.

Riarhin picked it up again.

Madra rubbed the place where the scarf had sat against her neck. “When I went home last week, I knew where my things would be. Nobody had put somebody else’s comb on my table. I was glad to go. I was glad to come back. It was easier because I knew that.”

“You may have my table,” Deliane said.

“I don’t want your damned table.”

For a moment Riarhin thought one of them would cry. Madra stood instead and went to the basin. She washed her hands although there was nothing on them. The water had gone cold.

Deliane laid the folded linen in the travelling chest.

“I want to see her on a day when I have something to tell her that she hasn’t arranged.”

Madra dried each finger separately.

Sivelle handed Deliane a ribbon. “Then you should keep that appalling hat. There’s a story to it somewhere.”

“Not a flattering one.”

“Tell her anyway.”

They went back to packing. Madra returned to the bed and folded two dresses with an angry care that gradually became ordinary care. When she found a missing button, she searched the floor until she had it. She did not apologise. Deliane did not make her.

Near the end, a servant came to ask whether they would dine upstairs. Deliane said yes and asked if there was space for all of them. The servant counted, including Riarhin, and went to find out.

Coryd was still wearing the hat. Sivelle took it off her, put it on herself, and asked whether she looked widowed enough.

“You look expensive,” Coryd said.

“Better.”

They kissed beside the bed. Madra told them they were obstructing the drawers. Coryd moved an inch and kissed Sivelle again.

Riarhin’s needle had caught in the lining. She pulled it free too hard and broke the thread.

Deliane took the stocking from her. “That will do. Nobody is going to inspect my heel.”

“You don’t know that,” Madra said.

“Oh, I do hope someone will.”

This time they all laughed. The chest remained open when they went to supper.

Chapter 23 The people she wants

The following morning Jaennis’s new guest was using the window as a desk.

Two account books lay open on the deep sill, held flat with a glove and a little bronze dog which had been brought from another room. The widow stood between them in her travelling dress, her hair pinned loosely enough that a dark strand kept falling against the page. She tucked it behind her ear without looking up when Riarhin entered. An older woman in a grey gown sat by the fire, writing figures on a scrap. Aluen was nowhere to be seen.

Riarhin had come to return Deliane’s small scissors. The servant who had admitted her said that Deliane was with the linen woman and would be down presently. He offered a chair. The widow glanced up.

“You’re the one who didn’t watch.”

Riarhin looked at the older woman, who went on writing.

“At the dance,” the widow said. “I noticed.”

“I was looking for my escort.”

“Were you? How disappointing.”

She pushed back the strand again. There was no embarrassment in her face, and Riarhin liked her better for it. The missing earring had been replaced by a different pair, small and silver. One hung crookedly because its clasp had caught the hair beneath her ear.

“Your earring,” Riarhin said.

“Which?”

She pointed. The woman put her hand up, failed to find the catch and came closer, turning her head. Riarhin released the trapped hair. It was an ordinary intimacy, so readily offered that she found herself being careful with it.

“Thank you. Adelis.”

“I know.”

“No, that’s my name. You may have heard half the history of my marriage from Madra, but I doubt she remembered to give you that.”

Riarhin smiled. Adelis turned back to the books.

The older woman asked whether the cart hire included the return journey. Adelis looked for an entry, could not find it and fetched another book from the floor. Riarhin sat with the scissors in her hand. Outside the window the court was filling with people arriving for business; she heard an escort challenged at the gate and the answering impatience of a man who believed the challenge should have ended when he gave his name.

Adelis found the entry.

“Both ways.”

“Then you have been charged twice for the empty return.”

“I told him that.”

“You told his son. The son has copied his father’s account.”

“Can we tell the father without his son?”

“He is dead.”

Adelis shut the smaller book with enough force to make the bronze dog jump. “They all become reasonable as soon as you can’t speak to them.”

The accountant smiled but did not look up. Riarhin recognised a woman who had heard worse and been paid less to hear it. Her cuffs had been darned with a colour chosen for its availability. On the chair beside her lay the remains of an early meal which she had forgotten to finish.

Jaennis came in carrying a shallow bowl of dried apricots. She had a cane in her other hand and had refused help with one or the other, for the servant followed with both hands empty and a helpless expression. Adelis took the bowl before he could offer again.

“You weren’t meant to get up.”

“I was told to walk.”

“You were told to walk to the window.”

“This is a window.”

Adelis set down the dried apricots and brought the chair with the high cushion. Jaennis lowered herself into it, allowed the younger woman to move the footstool and touched her hand as she straightened. The touch lingered. The accountant took an apricot, opened the next page and gave no sign of seeing either of them.

Riarhin rose to go. Jaennis asked her to stay until Deliane returned with the list. There were curtains to decide about, apparently. Riarhin had already expressed an opinion on those curtains and hoped nobody required it again.

Adelis explained the account. She did it badly at first, beginning with the cousin who had introduced the dead carrier and pausing to describe the carrier’s son. Jaennis listened, ate two dried apricots and asked which figure they wanted altered. Adelis pointed to the return hire. The accountant held out her scrap.

“There,” Jaennis said. “Begin with that when he comes. Leave his father out of it.”

“He keeps bringing his father into it.”

“Let him. He will be grateful to you for listening, and by then this woman will have written the correction.”

The accountant’s mouth moved. “I can write it before.”

“Better still.”

Jaennis remembered another account in which the same carrier had been paid for waiting days at a flooded bridge. She named the clerk who had checked it. The accountant knew him. His copy would show whether a lump sum had been settled against all three journeys, which would make the second charge indefensible. She set aside her scrap and asked for a messenger.

Adelis looked at Jaennis with open delight.

“You kept that in your head?”

“He had a very dull voice. I had to listen to the figures to stay awake.”

“You could have sent him away.”

“He had walked all morning.”

For a little while Riarhin watched a problem diminish in the company of someone who could actually help with it. The accountant sent her message. Adelis ate dried apricots from Jaennis’s bowl and described the balcony at her lodging, from which she could see two women conduct a quarrel entirely by the way they hung their washing. Jaennis asked questions which made the description worse, and Adelis laughed until she had to put the bowl down.

Riarhin remembered another room, long ago, where a person capable of all the harm she knew had been good company. She set the scissors on the table so that she would not keep turning them.

The servant returned with word that the clerk’s copy could be brought after dinner. Jaennis invited Adelis to stay. The accountant said she would return at that hour and began gathering her things.

“Leave the books,” Jaennis told her. “No need to carry them twice.”

The accountant looked at Adelis.

“Will you need them?”

“I have an appointment at my lodging before dinner.”

“With whom?” Jaennis asked.

“The steward from my house. He has to leave tomorrow.”

“Have him brought here.”

“He dislikes coming to the palace.”

“Then he can learn there’s nothing to fear.”

Adelis reached for the smaller book. “He has been here. That’s why he dislikes it.”

Jaennis looked up at her. The accountant stopped tying the string around her papers.

“I could make it easier.”

“I know. He has his sister’s boy with him, and the boy has never seen the city. I promised them dinner at the lodging. There’s a cook who does a fish he likes.”

“You will spend your whole visit feeding other people.”

“I’m hoping you will feed me tonight.”

Adelis leaned down and kissed the corner of her mouth. Jaennis put up a hand, catching the loose strand of hair. For a moment it seemed that she would accept the answer. She tucked the strand behind the earring Riarhin had freed and asked what hour.

“After the copy arrives,” Adelis said. “If that’s convenient.”

“Of course.”

The accountant finished her knot. Adelis gathered the books, but Jaennis laid a hand on the larger one.

“This may be needed when the copy comes.”

“Then I’ll bring it back.”

“Suppose you’re delayed.”

“I’ll send it.”

The hand remained on the cover. Riarhin watched Adelis’s fingers loosen beneath its weight. Then the younger woman laughed, put the other book down and leaned both arms on the sill.

“Are you keeping me through my accounts?”

“I am trying to get them finished for you.”

“I want to come back. You needn’t make it difficult to go.”

The accountant looked at the floor. Jaennis withdrew her hand.

“Take them, then.”

Adelis lifted the book, hesitated and bent again, but this time Jaennis had turned to ask the servant about the dried apricots. The kiss touched her hair.

When Adelis had gone, Riarhin picked up the scissors.

“I can leave these for Deliane.”

“Do you find her amusing?” Jaennis asked.

“Deliane?”

“Don’t.”

Riarhin set the scissors down again.

“She wants to dine with you.”

“She wants a great many things.”

“Yes.”

Jaennis examined an apricot, found a dark place along its edge and put it back. “I have spent the morning helping her. I don’t think you need explain her to me.”

Riarhin rose.

Deliane arrived in the doorway carrying the curtain list. Jaennis asked which room would take the yellow, then listened with such care that Deliane began to look hopeful about the colour again. Riarhin left them to it.

Margery’s invitation came while Riarhin was having the dressing on her wrist changed.

The messenger waited outside the healer’s room. Riarhin read the note with her sleeve rolled above the elbow and the old linen in a bowl beside her. Margery wanted Jaennis to come to the house the following afternoon. She asked Riarhin to call first and remain until the household guards arrived, then leave. She had underlined the last word once and scratched the line out.

The healer told Riarhin to stop bending her wrist. She laid the letter on her knee.

“Will it hold?”

“If you stop asking by trying it.”

She held still. He cleaned the cut and said the edge was joining well. She would keep a scar. There was no reason she should lose the use of the hand, provided she did not pull it open whenever she disliked an answer.

The next afternoon Margery had moved the chairs herself. The secretary’s chair was gone from the room where Tessart had sat. A smaller table stood beside the hearth, with space enough to set down a cup and no space for a packet of papers. Through the open garden door came the smell of wet leaves and the slow scrape of a rake.

Riarhin stopped on the threshold. She could see the post against which she had held the hired man’s arm. Someone had rubbed the wood with oil. The mark of the guard was still there.

Margery followed her gaze.

“I had the table moved so I wouldn’t look at it all evening.”

“I can wait outside.”

“Come in. If I keep everyone outside, I shall end by giving him the house.”

She was wearing the dark dress from the celebration. This time the pin was crooked. Riarhin told her. Margery put her hand to it, failed twice to find the fastening and let Riarhin do it for her.

“I want to say something before she arrives,” Margery said. “I have rehearsed it so often that I no longer know whether it means what I meant.”

“You don’t have to tell me.”

“I need to hear it with somebody in the room.”

Riarhin released the pin and stepped back.

Margery went to the window. She looked at the gardener, waited until he had turned away, and began.

“I won’t have you send people here to discover that you disagree with them.”

Riarhin waited.

“I won’t do it. I am glad to see your friends. I won’t be the place where you teach your enemies a lesson.”

“That seems plain.”

“She’ll say she didn’t know he’d behave like that.”

“She may not have.”

“I know.” Margery turned sharply. “I know that. Do you think I haven’t asked myself? I keep seeing the little supper we might have had if he’d been the sort of man she thought he was. He would have been embarrassed. I’d have felt useful. She’d have come afterward and thanked me, and I’d have been happy.”

Riarhin looked at the chair which had replaced Tessart’s.

“What would you have wanted her to do first?”

“Ask me.”

“Then say that.”

Margery drew a breath. The gardener had reached the far end of the path and was pulling the rake towards him in short, stubborn strokes. She watched him for a while.

“I wanted you to be angrier with me.”

“Why?”

“It would make this easier.”

Riarhin had no answer which would help. She asked whether the guard knew where to stand. Margery said she had shown him the covered walk, from which he could see both doors without looking into the room. Riarhin went out to inspect it.

Jaennis came early. Her carriage entered while Riarhin was speaking to the guard. Margery came to the step before the footman had lowered the stool. Jaennis saw her and smiled, an unguarded smile which took years from her face. She ignored the offered hand and reached for Margery instead.

Riarhin moved aside. Jaennis noticed her only when she had reached the path.

“Are we to have witnesses?”

“No,” Margery said.

Riarhin started towards her escort. Margery stopped her with a hand on her sleeve.

“She has been waiting until you arrived. I asked her.”

“I didn’t object.”

“I know. I am telling you.”

Jaennis looked between them, then bent to kiss Margery’s cheek. “Come and sit. You’ve been standing in the cold.”

Margery did not move.

“I have something to say first.”

Riarhin looked towards the gate. She could not get there without stepping around them. She waited, her attention on the gardener’s heap of leaves.

Margery said what she had rehearsed. The words came less evenly now. She lost the phrase about enemies, started it again and abandoned it.

“Ask me,” she finished. “Before I have the man in my room.”

Jaennis had drawn back. Her hand remained on Margery’s arm.

“I should have asked.”

Margery stared at her.

“I thought you would know what to say to him. You did. That was not enough.”

There was no qualification after it. Riarhin waited despite herself, until she felt Margery’s fingers leave her sleeve.

“I was frightened,” Margery said.

“I know.”

“No. I was frightened afterward. While it was happening, I was angry about my papers. When he’d gone, I couldn’t pick up the cup. I wanted you. And I hated that I wanted you.”

Jaennis’s face changed. She drew Margery closer, then stopped before completing the embrace.

“May I?”

Margery went into her arms. Riarhin turned away. At the gate she gave the officer her return route and waited while he checked it. When she looked back, Jaennis and Margery were going into the house together, slowly because of the step. Jaennis let Margery choose the chair.

Riarhin did not see their evening. Later, as her escort brought her home by the river road, the sitting-room curtains were still open. A lamp stood on the smaller table. Jaennis was leaning back with her shoes off; Margery sat on the rug beside her, holding an open book. Riarhin saw Jaennis laugh and put out her hand. The carriage moved on before she saw whether Margery took it.

Chapter 24 When the door opens

Matylde had chosen the small map room because it had only one entrance.

Riarhin noticed this before she noticed the map. An old habit: door, window, space beside the hearth, the place a second person might stand unseen. Then the map took her attention. Somebody had laid a clean sheet across its northern quarter to keep ink off the borders. A plate weighted one corner. There were three unfinished biscuits on it.

“Don’t stand,” Matylde said. “Everybody stands when I want to talk and then I can’t sit without seeming to dismiss them.”

Riarhin took the chair indicated. Solenne remained near the door. The queen saw Riarhin glance at her.

“She is here because I asked.”

“Good.”

Matylde’s mouth tightened.

Riarhin looked at the map.

“You needn’t tell me every time I do something good.”

“No, Majesty.”

The girl moved the plate. Beneath it was a road Riarhin knew, drawn straighter than it ran.

“My mother made this safer.”

“Yes.”

“And the road west. And the crossings here.” She touched two little black marks with a nail. “People used to pay separately to get over each one. Now they pay once. Or they should. The chancellor says there are still men collecting the old toll because people don’t know.”

“There are.”

“Did you use it? What she built?”

“Yes.”

“Then why do people speak as if nothing was any good?”

Riarhin had expected a question about the amulet, a request for the lost instrument which might make a dead mother easier to understand. The girl’s finger remained on the second crossing. There was ink beneath the nail.

“Some don’t speak that way.”

“The ones who loved her say everything was good. The others begin with the people she killed. I know about the people she killed. I can’t ask whether a bridge was worth building without someone thinking I mean to begin killing again.”

Solenne moved slightly at the door. Matylde heard her and flushed.

“I don’t.”

“I heard you,” Riarhin said.

She pulled the map a little closer, then looked up to ask permission. Matylde nodded impatiently.

“This crossing used to take most of a day in flood. I went round it once because a man with sick cattle had the ferry before me and nobody wanted to take anything after him. The new bridge would have saved him time too.”

“Do you know it would?”

“No. I know I would have used it.”

Matylde leaned her elbows on the table.

“Was there anything you liked about her?”

Riarhin could remember Veralyn young, could remember the attention she gave a story and the sudden impatience when somebody tried to turn it into a lesson. Those memories had acquired too much weight. She handled them badly whenever asked to produce one.

“She laughed readily when I first knew her.”

“So does Jaennis.”

“Yes.”

“And you think I shouldn’t trust her.”

“I think you should be able to finish a conversation you chose to begin.”

Matylde sat back. A knock sounded.

Solenne opened the door a little. A voice outside announced Jaennis. Matylde said, “Come in,” so quickly that the officer had scarcely turned to ask her.

Jaennis carried a folded note. She greeted Riarhin and asked Solenne to have a chair brought, though there were two unused ones against the wall. Solenne moved one herself.

“I shan’t stay. The northern visitors have changed their day. If you mean to receive them together, the evening will have to move.”

“Can they wait until after supper?” Matylde asked.

“The messenger is waiting.”

“Then let him have supper too.”

Jaennis smiled. “You will bankrupt the kitchens before the month is out.”

Matylde smiled back. Her shoulders had relaxed. She reached for the note.

Jaennis did not give it to her at once.

“You’ve been here a long time.”

“We were looking at the bridges.”

“Then perhaps you’ve done enough. There are people who would like to see you while you still have a little cheerfulness to spare.”

Riarhin stared at the plate. A crumb had lodged in the engraved border of the map weight. She wanted to scrape it out with her nail. She kept her hands in her lap.

“I haven’t finished,” Matylde said.

The room was still.

Jaennis gave her the note. “Of course. I only thought you might be tired.”

“I am a little.”

“Then—”

“I would like to finish anyway.”

Jaennis looked at Riarhin, an assessment and perhaps a request: surely an adult would know when enough had been asked of a girl.

Riarhin said nothing.

“Very well,” Jaennis said. She rose. “I shall see whether Margery can be persuaded to take some air. She’s been indoors all day.”

Matylde half rose too. “You said your knee was bad.”

“Then I shall sit with her.”

“I was going to.”

“You have company.”

When the door closed, Matylde remained standing with the note in her hand.

“She isn’t being unkind.”

“I didn’t say she was.”

“You looked.”

Riarhin looked up at her. “I am trying to learn when looking is all I should do.”

Matylde gave a short, irritated laugh. “Well, don’t practise on me.”

She sat and unfolded the note. The northern visitors could have been answered by a clerk. Riarhin knew this from the first line, visible upside down. So did Matylde. She folded it again very carefully.

“What did you want to ask next?” Riarhin said.

“I ought to see whether Margery needs anything.”

“Solenne could send someone.”

“She doesn’t like being fussed over.”

“Matylde.”

The girl looked sharply at her. Riarhin had not used the name without its title since the private audience.

“What did you want to ask?”

For a while Matylde studied the map. Then she moved the plate completely off it and touched the road west.

“If I leave this toll as it is, who will lose?”

They spent the rest of the hour finding that neither of them knew enough to answer. Solenne fetched the relevant account. It named a house whose bridge duties had been bought out and three villages still required to supply men for repairs. The villages had petitioned twice. Matylde wrote their names on the back of Jaennis’s note.

At the end, she sent for the clerk who knew the old arrangement. She did not ask Riarhin to stay.

On the following morning the clerk sent for her.

He had a question about the crossing she had used. Riarhin found him outside the map room with three different drawings spread on a bench. A boy was sitting on the last inches of the bench, holding the ink and trying not to fall asleep.

“Which bank did you approach from?”

She pointed. He turned one of the drawings round.

“This way?”

“Yes.”

“And where did the cattle wait?”

Riarhin looked at the curve of the water. The waiting place had been a patch of trampled ground with a broken fence. She could remember the fence, the sick animal’s labouring flanks and the ferryman refusing to let anybody lead it down the slope. She could not put them on the clerk’s neat bank.

“There was an alder. Here, perhaps.”

“Here is under water.”

“Then not there.”

He put down his pen.

“How long ago?”

She told him as nearly as she knew. He pulled the oldest drawing free and laid it on top. The channel had moved. Her waiting place stood beneath the second pier of the new bridge.

Matylde came to the open door. She had heard the clerk say that the cattle would have been on the queen’s approach road now.

“Can people still wait there?”

“No, Majesty,” he said. “That is part of the problem.”

The room filled slowly. A bridge keeper who happened to be in Valora had been summoned; he had arrived with a bag containing two shirts because nobody had told him whether he must remain overnight. A woman representing one of the three villages brought the oldest toll agreement wrapped in oilcloth. A deputy from the house whose duties had been purchased brought a much newer paper, held flat between boards.

Riarhin made to leave. Matylde asked her to stay until they had settled where the animals had stood, then forgot to dismiss her. She remained against the wall while the keeper explained why the toll was still being collected twice.

“The first payment is for crossing. The second is for holding stock on the far bank.”

“The queen bought that,” the village woman said.

“The queen bought the ferry duty,” said the deputy. “She did not buy a meadow.”

“Your meadow was under the ferry duty.”

“It was used with the ferry duty.”

The boy with the ink had come inside. He stood behind the clerk, wide awake now, following the papers as they moved.

Matylde asked the keeper whether he collected both payments. He said he did, which made the deputy look sharply at him.

“Your Majesty, his account may suggest—”

“I asked him.”

“Yes, Majesty. I only wish to clarify that he collects on two authorities.”

Matylde’s fingers tightened round the edge of the table. The girl looked at the keeper again.

“Where does each payment go?”

He answered. The crossing money repaired the bridge and paid his two assistants. The meadow payment went to the house, less the sum he retained for keeping the gate. In winter, when nobody could stand animals in the meadow without losing them in mud, he still collected it, because the second agreement required the payment from every drove.

“Then they are paying for somewhere they cannot use,” Matylde said.

“For the right to use it,” the deputy began.

The village woman slapped her old agreement onto the table.

Matylde flinched. Riarhin saw it and wanted to move. The clerk told the woman to take her hand off the paper, for the corner was splitting under her palm. The woman apologised to him, then to the queen. She did not apologise to the deputy.

“My sons bring stock in the winter. They stand them on the road. Men coming the other way curse us, and the keeper says we obstruct the approach.”

“You do,” the keeper said unhappily.

“Where shall we put them?”

He spread his hands.

The deputy suggested that the village arrange its market days differently. The keeper looked at him as though he had offered to rearrange the winter.

Matylde began an instruction, stopped in the middle and asked for a clean sheet. Riarhin watched the girl write down the questions she wanted answered. Who had the right to the meadow. What the purchase included. Whether the road could be widened. Whether there was another waiting place.

“The last one is easy,” the village woman said. “There is. It’s mine.”

The deputy gave a little laugh.

The woman turned on him. “Yes. Mine. That’s why I came myself.”

She wanted a payment, and a new gate, and someone answerable when a drover broke it. She would not have the whole meadow used. There was hay to cut. Her children had thought her mad to come to Valora over a gate, and the youngest was doing both her work and his while she was away.

Matylde asked how much she wanted. The deputy objected that no one had established whether the house would lose its present right.

“We can find out both,” Matylde said. “Can we not?”

He bowed. The village woman named a sum so large that the keeper whistled before remembering where he was.

“It is good meadow,” she said.

Riarhin was dismissed before any settlement. At the door, Matylde caught her eye and almost smiled. Then the deputy asked whether the proposed holding ground would count as an extension of the royal approach. The smile went. The girl turned back to the drawing.

Two hours later Riarhin saw the village woman eating a pie in the lower court. Nothing had been settled, she said. The clerk was sending for the survey made when the bridge was built. She might have to stay another day.

“Would you have come,” Riarhin asked, “if your meadow hadn’t been beside it?”

The woman licked gravy off her thumb. “Would you?”

Riarhin considered that.

“No.”

“There, then.”

She broke off the untouched half of the pie and offered it. Riarhin declined. The woman wrapped it up for the keeper, who had gone to inquire about a bed.

Chapter 25 The account of the knife

The council required Riarhin’s account of the visit to Margery before it would resume the northern business.

She entered without Remorse. Her cuff had been mended, but the repair was visible. Tessart sat on the opposite side of the room with a man who had come to advise him and another who stood behind his chair wearing the queen’s badge. The councillor looked rested. Riarhin had slept badly and resented the difference.

Margery had asked to give her account separately. Matylde had agreed. The secretary’s version was already on the table, together with the page he had meant Margery to sign.

Tessart’s adviser said that the safe-conduct did not shelter violence committed after entry.

“No,” Matylde said.

Riarhin waited to be questioned.

The adviser asked why she had attended with a sword. She had been invited to a house outside the palace. Her route and escort had been agreed. He asked why she had not summoned an officer before intervening. She had called the officer when the papers were taken. The knife had been drawn before he reached the room.

“Yet you had room to withdraw.”

“She had not,” Riarhin said.

“Mistress Margery could have handed over the papers.”

Matylde reached for the secretary’s account. The adviser stopped speaking while she found the line she wanted.

“She had been told copies would be returned,” he added.

“Had she agreed?” the queen asked.

“There was a dispute.”

“I can see that. Had she agreed?”

He asked leave to consult Tessart. They spoke so quietly that only the movement of the councillor’s mouth reached Riarhin. The officer behind the chair looked towards the window.

The answer, when it came, acknowledged that Margery had refused. Tessart had thought the originals necessary to establish what had been promised. The secretary had believed he was collecting evidence which would be required by the council in any event.

Matylde put down the page.

“Did we send him?”

“No, Majesty.”

“Then let us hear what he did, without saying he had already been asked to do it.”

Riarhin was required to describe the wound. She could not say how well the man would walk. The healer had supplied an account and would supply another. She had acted to stop him, and had known that cutting his leg could kill him.

Tessart looked directly at her then. She did not look away.

The council retained the separate inquiry into the injury. The officer would remain available as a witness. For now the safe-conduct stood; the councillor’s presence in the house had given neither his secretary nor his hired man authority to take Margery’s papers by force.

Tessart asked whether his offer of financial assistance would be heard.

“By the treasury,” Matylde said.

“It was made through Lady Jaennis.”

“Then she may attend when it is heard.”

He took a breath. Riarhin saw him look towards the empty chair beside the wall before he answered.

“Yes, Majesty.”

Jaennis was waiting in the covered walk outside. She had brought no papers. When Tessart appeared under escort, she rose and asked after his health as if he had been detained by an illness.

He stopped so suddenly that the man advising him walked into his shoulder.

“You said she would hear me.”

“She has.”

“That wasn’t—”

“Did you give her the original offer?”

He looked at her, then at Riarhin behind him. He adjusted his coat and went on without answering.

Jaennis watched him go. There was colour in her cheeks. She turned to Riarhin with a quick smile which faded when she saw the bandage showing at the cuff.

“Let me see.”

“No.”

The refusal checked her. Riarhin could have made it courteous. She did not.

“Margery was frightened,” Jaennis said.

“Yes.”

“She tells me you were very quick.”

“She asked me to be there. Why did you send him?”

“He wished to know who had requested your visit. Margery could tell him.”

“What did you think he would ask her to do?”

“I thought he would discover that I had not given him control of the hearing.”

“Through her.”

Jaennis looked down the walk. A servant carrying a cushion had paused at the turn, unwilling to approach while they spoke.

“Margery has managed a household through worse things than a councillor’s displeasure.”

“He brought a man with a knife.”

“And you stopped him. I am grateful.”

Riarhin’s left hand closed. She made herself open it.

“You could have told him yourself.”

“He has been told. He prefers to believe I must agree with him in private.”

“Let him believe it.”

“And let him promise my support to everyone he approaches? He has used that belief to frighten half the people who ought to have spoken at your hearing.”

Riarhin could hear the usefulness of the result assembling around what had happened in Margery’s house. She could also hear the injured man telling her that she had cut him. She turned to the waiting servant and asked whether the cushion was for Jaennis.

“Yes.”

“Bring it, then.”

Jaennis accepted it. By the time she had settled herself, Riarhin had gone to the officer at the end of the walk.

She asked to have Margery’s household included among the witnesses offered protection. Her request was entered. Whether Margery wanted a guard, and where one could stand, would be asked of Margery.

Matylde sent for Riarhin before she left.

The audience was short. A clerk waited beside the door with the next day’s papers. Riarhin could hear a horse refusing to enter the court below them and a groom explaining, with increasing bitterness, that it had gone through that gate yesterday.

“Did you think I would thank you?” the queen asked.

“For what?”

“For Margery.”

“No.”

“I am glad you were there. I hate that she needed you to be.”

Riarhin kept her hands still.

Matylde picked up the original escort direction from the yard attack. Beside it lay the account of the men waiting at the west gate.

“Solenne says I should decide what happens next.”

“Yes.”

“Everyone says that now. After deciding what I will have to decide about.”

She pushed the papers away. Riarhin looked at the faint impression the seal had left on the sheet beneath it.

“I would ask for the instructions sent before either visit,” she said.

“Whose?”

“All that concern the visits. Mine too.”

“Jaennis will say she can tell me.”

“You can listen.”

“And then?”

Riarhin thought of the protected guest’s room Jaennis had offered, of the six men waiting at the wrong gate, of Margery setting down a cup because she could not hold it.

“Have the copies brought here first.”

Matylde studied her. Then she asked the clerk to write the instruction and name the offices from which the originals were to be obtained. She did not send him to Jaennis to discover which offices those were.

Chapter 26 A quarter’s work

Sivelle had asked them to eat at her house because the stair was finished.

Riarhin would have declined if Coryd had not said she wanted her there. Her wrist ached, and she had spent the afternoon hearing people discuss the exact circumstances in which she had been permitted to cut a man. The prospect of being agreeable to someone who might ask about it exhausted her. Coryd made no attempt to promise that Sivelle would not ask. She held out Riarhin’s clean coat and waited until she took it.

The house stood behind a courtyard too small for the carriage its former owner had kept. He had solved the difficulty by removing a pear tree, leaving a circle of bare earth in the paving where nothing had since been planted. Sivelle had put a bench there. The escort sat on it while a servant brought supper, and the two men ate with their feet in the old tree’s space.

Coryd stopped at the stair to inspect the rail. Riarhin went up behind her. The new wood had been rubbed with oil, but its pale grain still showed beside the dark uprights. At the turn Coryd set her weight against it and listened. Satisfied, she let go.

“Will you do that every time?” Sivelle asked from above.

“Until something else needs fixing.”

“Then I shall stop pointing things out.”

She wore a loose gown fastened at the throat and had left her hair down. Coryd reached the landing and put a hand at her waist, as naturally as she had tested the rail. Sivelle kissed her, then kept hold of her sleeve while welcoming Riarhin.

The room had been changed for the supper. A writing table stood against the wall instead of beneath the window. The small table now in its place was set for three, though a fourth chair had been left beside the hearth. On it lay an old dog’s blanket, brushed clean and folded. Riarhin wondered whether the dog was upstairs or dead. She did not ask.

Sivelle served a pie whose crust had collapsed at one side. She said the cook blamed the dish. Coryd said the dish had a shallow foot and rocked when it was set down. Riarhin watched Sivelle look from one to the other as though expecting a verdict about the cook. Then she laughed and cut through the broken side first.

For the first part of supper they spoke of Harissa. Coryd had written to ask whether a length of wood she had left behind was still beneath the neighbour’s eaves. Harissa’s reply began with an account of why the neighbour no longer spoke to her and ended by assuring her that the wood was dry. Sivelle wanted to know why she had kept it. Coryd described the shape of a chair she had thought it might make.

“Will it?”

“It may make firewood. I haven’t seen it split.”

“You asked someone to keep it for a chair you may never make?”

Coryd lifted a shoulder. “He needed the space filled. His wife was putting things there.”

Riarhin remembered the neighbour and knew there was more to it, but Coryd had chosen the part she wanted to tell. Sivelle asked about the chair’s curved back. Coryd reached for a knife to draw the shape in the crumbs, thought better of it and used her finger.

There was a steadiness in the way they attended to one another which Riarhin had not seen at the palace. Sivelle waited for Coryd to finish a description without turning it into a joke. Coryd asked about the house’s old roof and listened to an answer which was mostly about a difficult winter and a husband afraid to spend money. Riarhin ate more than she had intended. When Sivelle offered another piece of pie she accepted it.

The servant cleared the dishes and brought wine. Coryd got up to help, knew which door would catch the tray if opened too far, and held it back. Riarhin watched her cross the room without looking for a place to put things.

Sivelle followed Riarhin’s gaze.

“It’s pleasant,” she said.

“What is?”

“Having someone who doesn’t ask where every cup belongs.”

Coryd came back with the last dish. “I’ve put three in the wrong place.”

“Then we’ll find them when we need three.”

Riarhin smiled. Sivelle sat with her feet tucked beneath her gown. She had been careful not to look at Riarhin’s dressing. Now she asked whether it hurt, and accepted yes without asking what had happened. Riarhin was grateful enough to feel a little ashamed of her reluctance to come.

Then Coryd found the paper beneath the wine bottle.

She lifted it to save it from the damp ring and saw her own name. Sivelle put out a hand, stopped and let her read.

“It was meant to wait until tomorrow.”

The paper named a wage, a room, wood for the fire and the repairs to be undertaken before winter. There were two copies. Coryd read both. Riarhin saw the date at the top: a full quarter, beginning when the work at the guesthouse ended.

“You said there were things to do,” Coryd said.

“There are.”

“You didn’t say you’d have this written.”

“I wanted you to see what I meant.”

Coryd set it down. Sivelle unfolded her legs and sat upright.

“Tell me if the wage is wrong.”

“It isn’t.”

“Or the room. There are others.”

Riarhin took a slow drink. She could leave, but leaving would declare what sort of conversation this had become. Coryd did not look at her. She was studying the line which named the room.

“The one beside yours.”

“It has the best light.”

“It has a door through.”

“That can be kept locked.”

“Who wrote this?”

“My man of business. He writes all the service agreements.”

“Did you tell him we were together?”

“He has eyes.”

Sivelle’s colour rose. Coryd looked up. The paper remained between them, its lower corner damp from the bottle.

“I don’t need a servant who sleeps with me,” Sivelle said.

“I didn’t say you did.”

“You asked what he knew.”

“I wanted to know why he put me there.”

“Because I asked him to.”

Coryd turned the paper face down.

“I like the room.”

“Then what is wrong?”

“I don’t know whether I want a quarter.”

“You needn’t decide tonight.”

“The room isn’t the part that makes me stay.”

Riarhin looked towards the dog blanket. The hearth had been swept so thoroughly that the edge of a darker stone showed where the grate had once been wider. She concentrated on that stone while Sivelle asked whether Coryd was waiting for her sister to decide when they would leave.

“No.”

“But you go together.”

“We have been.”

“Will you this time?”

Coryd looked at Riarhin then. Riarhin put down her cup.

“I don’t know,” she said.

Sivelle’s eyes filled. She looked furious about it. Coryd reached for her, but Sivelle moved the paper out of the way first, almost angrily, and the offered hand remained on the table.

“I thought if you had work of your own here, it wouldn’t be a question of staying on my kindness.”

“It wouldn’t.”

“Then I have understood nothing.”

“You’ve understood that part.”

Sivelle laughed without pleasure. “Thank you.”

Coryd winced. Riarhin stood, saying she would see whether the escort had finished. Coryd nodded without looking at her. Sivelle began an apology. Riarhin told her the meal had been good and went down before it could become an apology for anything else.

The two men were still on the bench. One had taken off his boot and was rubbing a stone from the sole. Riarhin stood beneath the porch where she could not hear the voices upstairs. The circle where the pear tree had been removed was full of fine grass between the stones. A seedling had found the crack beside the bench. She bent, touched one of its leaves, and straightened.

After a while Sivelle’s servant came down with a candle and said they might be some time. He offered Riarhin a seat in the kitchen. She accepted. The cook was covering the remaining pie. She gave Riarhin a plate with the broken crust and some of the filling, though Riarhin said she had eaten enough.

“You’ll want it later.”

Riarhin put it beside her. The kitchen was warm and ordinary. A pan soaked in the sink. Near the door, lengths of new wood stood upright, waiting for a hand which might now go elsewhere. Coryd had marked the ends in charcoal. Riarhin could read the shapes she meant them to make and nothing of what she was saying upstairs.

When Coryd came down, the candle had burned a quarter of its length.

“I’m staying,” she said.

Riarhin nodded.

“Tonight.”

“Yes.”

“You needn’t make such an effort to sound as if you don’t mind.”

“I don’t know what I mind.”

Coryd sat beside her. She took a piece of crust from the plate and ate it, looking at the stove. Her face was tired. There had been tears, though Riarhin could not tell whose by looking at her.

“We haven’t decided anything.”

“All right.”

“That’s allowed.”

“I didn’t say it wasn’t.”

Coryd put her head against Riarhin’s shoulder. Riarhin held still. The cook went out to the yard and remained there longer than was necessary to empty the basin.

“I don’t want you to tell me what you think of her,” Coryd said.

“I like her.”

“I know. Don’t tell me the rest.”

Riarhin put her hand over Coryd’s, careful of the dressing. For a while they sat without speaking. Then Coryd lifted her head and went to ask the servant for Riarhin’s coat.

Sivelle came down to say goodnight. She had washed her face. She asked Riarhin to come again, more formally than before, and Riarhin said she would like to. The offer was not repeated. At the door Coryd kissed her sister’s cheek and told the escort which lane would be dry.

Riarhin went home. In their room she found the half-finished chest runner beside the window and put a cloth over it so that the morning damp would not settle on the cut edge. She did not move it from Coryd’s place.

Chapter 27 The household passage

Six days after the appointment arrived, the cart had gone when Deliane’s chest came down.

Two men carried it between them, one walking backwards and swearing softly at each turn. The lower passage smelled of rain and lamp oil. A kitchen boy with a basket of eggs flattened himself against the wall to let them pass. Deliane followed carrying a smaller bag; Riarhin walked beside the officer assigned to witness the transfer of her appointment papers.

Outside, where the cart should have stood, rain dripped from an empty awning.

Deliane stopped.

“There was a cart.”

Orven came through the side door. He had a ring of keys in his hand and a piece of bread which he put into his pocket when he saw them.

“I sent it back.”

The man walking backwards lowered his end of the chest too quickly. His companion grunted. They set it down crookedly across the passage.

“Why?” Deliane asked.

“Her ladyship wishes to speak to you.”

“I have spoken to her.”

“She asked that you wait.”

“I said goodbye this morning.”

“I wasn’t there.”

Riarhin glanced at the officer. His name was written on the outside of the appointment packet, together with the hour he had been instructed to attend. He was young enough to be troubled by a man who had keys to more doors than he did. He took the packet out.

“The transfer is to be witnessed at the guesthouse,” he said.

“Then witness it when she gets there.”

“We have an hour.”

“You can see it’s raining.”

Deliane moved towards the arch. Orven stepped across her path.

“Wait a little. It won’t harm you.”

She put the bag down.

Her breathing had changed. She looked past Orven at the wet stones outside.

“Please move.”

“I promised her I’d see you before you went.”

“You have seen me.”

“Don’t make a game of it.”

His voice had risen. The boy with the eggs stared at the floor. One of the chest carriers began edging towards the door through which they had come.

Riarhin said, “Is she under restraint?”

Orven turned to her with immediate dislike.

“This is a household matter.”

“The queen’s officer asked you a question.”

“I didn’t,” the young man said, then coloured. “But I do ask it now.”

Orven held up his keys. “Nobody is restrained. Her ladyship wants a word. She has done more for this girl than any of you.”

Deliane reached for her bag. Riarhin thought she meant to sit on the chest and wait, and the despair that rose in her told her how much she had wanted the opposite.

Deliane straightened with the bag in her left hand.

“Send her to the house,” she said. “She knows where it is.”

Then she tried to walk past.

Orven caught her right wrist.

It happened plainly. No concealed working, no sudden darkness. A man grasped a woman’s arm because she had moved before he gave permission. She pulled back. His hand tightened and turned to keep her beside him. Her foot slid on a wet place near the arch.

The sound she made was brief and incredulous.

Riarhin had his hand before she had decided to take it. Her left thumb pressed into the joint below his forefinger; her right hand held his elbow against his coat. The healing cut beneath her right cuff pulled open enough to sting. He began to turn towards her and found that he could not without bringing his face down into the edge of the chest.

“Open your hand,” she said.

He did.

The officer caught Deliane as her knees gave. The bag fell, its clasp opening. A brush, two folded stockings and a little blue pot rolled onto the floor.

Riarhin kept Orven where he was for one breath longer than necessary. He was panting. She could feel the force with which he wanted to pull free, and the knowledge of what she could do to him rose pleasantly through her anger.

She let go.

“Stand there,” the officer told him.

“I didn’t—”

“Stand there.”

Deliane cradled her wrist against her body. The right hand had already begun to swell. She tried to move her fingers and stopped.

“Don’t,” Riarhin said.

Deliane looked at her with tears running down her face. “Get the things.”

Riarhin knelt. She gathered the stockings, the brush, the blue pot. The pot’s lid had come off. Some of its contents had spilled into the rainwater: a white ointment, sharp with herbs. She put the lid back on without trying to save it.

“Where is she?” Orven said.

Nobody answered him.

“Somebody should fetch her ladyship.”

The boy with the eggs ran.

The officer sent one of the chest carriers to fetch his senior. The man asked whether someone would sign for the chest first.

“For the love of—” the officer began, then stopped. “Yes. Put it beside the wall. I will sign.”

“They’ll say I dropped it. If something’s broken inside.”

Deliane closed her eyes. “He didn’t drop it.”

The other carrier moved the chest without waiting for help. He was red in the face with the effort. When Riarhin stood to help him, Deliane caught the edge of her coat.

“Stay.”

She stayed. Rain blew through the arch and spotted the bag on her knees. She moved her chair, then realised there was no chair; she had been crouching on her heels long enough for the position to feel settled. She stood slowly and sat beside Deliane on the chest instead. The movement put them at nearly the same height.

Orven said, “I never meant to hurt you.”

Deliane stared at her hand.

“I wasn’t holding you hard.”

The officer turned. “Stop speaking to her.”

“She knows me.”

“I know her too,” Deliane said.

Her voice was so quiet that Riarhin thought at first she had been speaking to herself. Orven took half a step forward.

“You know what she’s done for you.”

Deliane drew a breath through her teeth. Riarhin felt it catch. She wanted to put her own body between Deliane and the words, as she would have stepped between her and another blow.

The officer opened the side door and pointed through it.

“In there.”

“I work here.”

“Then you know where it goes.”

Orven went reluctantly. The officer followed and called to a passing guard. For the first time since the grasp at her wrist, Deliane lifted her head. She looked at the closed door and then towards the arch.

“The cartman,” she said.

“We’ll find another cart.”

“No. He came. He should be paid.”

Riarhin looked at her.

“He came when I asked.”

“I will tell the officer.”

Deliane nodded. Her lips had lost their colour. Riarhin pulled the cloak from her own shoulders and arranged it round her, keeping the wool clear of the injured arm. Deliane leaned against her for a moment, then straightened again when footsteps sounded on the stair.
