Jaennis came before the healer. She took in Deliane sitting on the chest, the officer standing between her and Orven, the bag on Riarhin’s knee. For a moment she looked older than she had at supper. She reached Deliane and knelt awkwardly enough that Riarhin almost put out a hand to help her.

“My dear.”

Deliane’s face folded. She began to cry harder.

Jaennis held her uninjured hand and looked at the swelling. “Who did this?”

Orven spoke at once. “I was only keeping her until—”

“Be quiet.”

He stopped.

“We’ll get you upstairs,” Jaennis said. “There is a fire in my room. We can have the physician come directly there.”

Deliane shook her head.

“It will be warmer.”

“No.”

The word was scarcely louder than her breathing.

Jaennis stroked the knuckles of her left hand. “You’re frightened. Of course you are. Let us see to this first.”

“I want the other room.”

“What other room?”

“The guesthouse.”

“You cannot be jolted across the city with that arm.”

“Then a room here. Not yours.”

Jaennis looked up at Riarhin. Riarhin did not speak. She was afraid that if she did she would make Deliane’s words hers.

The officer said, “There is a receiving room by the guard office. It has a fire.”

“It is full of men.”

“They can leave.”

Deliane nodded.

The healer arrived carrying a roll of linen and a little case. He asked everyone to stop speaking. Jaennis moved aside to let him examine the wrist. Deliane cried out when he pressed above the joint. Orven looked away.

The healer asked whether she wished to be treated where she sat or in another room. She said another room. He offered his arm.

Jaennis stood with difficulty. Riarhin took her elbow before she could fall. For an instant the two women held each other, the older woman’s weight unexpectedly light against Riarhin’s hand.

“Thank you,” Jaennis said.

Riarhin released her.

The officer took Deliane’s bag. Riarhin followed him with the appointment packet. Behind them, Orven began to explain what he had been told.

Chapter 28 Who may sit beside the bed

The healer cut Deliane’s sleeve because she could not bear having it pulled over her hand.

She watched the scissors enter the cloth. “It’s new.”

“I can stop.”

“No.”

He continued. Riarhin folded the severed piece and laid it beside the blue pot on the table. The pot had lost most of its ointment. Deliane had asked for it to be kept anyway.

The injury was above the wrist. The healer said that one bone was cracked and that the joint had been twisted badly enough to keep her from useful work for some time. He would set it and bind it; there would be pain, and she ought not to be surprised by that. A second practitioner could be asked to assist the mending once the swelling settled, but no one would make a whole night’s recovery out of the afternoon.

“Tomorrow?” Deliane asked.

“Tomorrow it will still hurt.”

“I have a house.”

“So I understand.”

“It opens tomorrow.”

“Then someone else will have to open the doors.”

She looked at Riarhin. Riarhin said, “There are two people already employed to do it.”

Deliane shut her eyes.

The setting was worse than Riarhin had expected. Deliane tried not to make noise, failed, apologised and then became angry when the healer told her that she need not apologise. Riarhin held her good hand. She could feel the nails digging into her palm. She was glad of it; it gave her something to do which did not involve looking at the white face on the pillow.

When it was finished, Deliane asked for water. Riarhin lifted the cup too quickly and spilled some down her front.

“Sorry.”

“Now you’re doing it.”

Riarhin brought a cloth. Deliane gave a shaky laugh which ended in a sob. She turned her face towards the wall.

There was a knock.

The officer outside said that Lady Jaennis had come.

Deliane’s good hand tightened on the sheet.

Riarhin waited. The healer was washing his fingers in a basin. He did not look towards the door.

“Not now,” Deliane said.

Riarhin went to convey it. Jaennis stood in the passage with a folded blanket over one arm. The blanket was soft, a pale grey worked with a narrow border; Riarhin had seen it across Deliane’s knees on an earlier evening.

“She doesn’t want a visitor yet.”

“I brought this.”

“I can take it.”

Jaennis held it for a moment longer before giving it up.

“Tell her I’m here.”

“She knows.”

“Tell her I can wait.”

Riarhin looked at the old woman’s face. The anxiety in it was real. There was no satisfaction to be had in making her stand outside a door while somebody cried beyond it.

“I will tell her,” she said.

Inside, Deliane looked at the blanket and began crying again. Riarhin laid it within reach. She did not put it over her.

“She’s waiting?”

“Yes.”

Deliane picked at a thread on the coverlet with her good hand.

“Would you like her to come in?”

Her mouth opened. She drew a small breath and stopped. The healer lifted his head.

Riarhin sat down.

“We can wait too.”

They waited. A cart rattled in the court outside. Someone laughed beneath the window and was shushed by another voice. Deliane’s breathing slowly became easier.

“No,” she said at last.

Riarhin went to the door again.

Margery brought a clean basin from the passage. She had asked whether anything was needed and been given something to carry before anyone thought to explain who she was.

Deliane opened her eyes when she heard the water.

“Does she know I’m not coming up?”

“Yes,” Margery said.

“Will you tell her again?”

Margery set down the basin. “You needn’t answer anyone tonight.”

“Then tell her.”

“I will.”

She stood beside the bed until Deliane looked away. In the passage she drew Riarhin a little farther from the door.

“She has sent for someone who may be able to help the bone.”

“The healer knows.”

“Orven had no right to do this.”

“No.”

“Jaennis will tell him so herself.”

Riarhin looked through the doorway. The healer was cutting linen into strips; he had arranged the shears so that Deliane would not see them coming towards the arm again.

“She asked you to tell Jaennis she isn’t coming upstairs,” Riarhin said.

“I heard.”

Margery’s face closed. For a moment Riarhin thought she would leave without another word. Instead she took a step towards the stair and stopped.

“If they ask how it began, you will tell them about my letter?”

“Only if you want me to.”

“I don’t want her to read it aloud in that room. Before everyone.”

“Then tell them yourself.”

Margery stared at her.

“Say what you saw. You needn’t give them what you wrote to me.”

“She’ll know.”

“Yes.”

A servant came between them carrying hot water. Margery moved aside. Riarhin saw her reach towards the basin, ready to find another errand.

“You can say no,” Riarhin told her.

Margery let the servant pass.

“I’ll speak to Solenne.”

“She’s coming here.”

“Then I’ll wait.”

She sat on the passage bench. When an attendant came to ask who had brought the spare basin, Margery said she had, and stayed seated.

Solenne came while the healer was writing his instructions.

She had heard about the breathing on the stair. Deliane gave permission for Riarhin to confirm it, then lay looking at the ceiling while the officer asked whether the same thing happened when Jaennis was elsewhere in the palace. Riarhin could not answer. She had seen one attack. Deliane said she did not know where Jaennis had been every other time.

“Does she give you things to wear?” Solenne asked.

“Yes.”

“Anything tied?”

“Clothes usually fasten.”

Solenne accepted the rebuke without answering it. She asked the healer what he knew about such attachments. He said he knew enough to ask another practitioner before touching one, and that he had come to set a bone.

“The safest course is to keep her gifts outside the room until we have an answer,” Solenne said.

Deliane turned her head.

“For how long?”

“Until tomorrow, perhaps.”

The officer asked a servant for a clean box. Riarhin watched the girl collect the blue pot, a ribbon from Deliane’s bag and the small comb. Then she reached for the blanket.

“Leave that,” Deliane said.

“Only for tonight,” Solenne said. “You will have another.”

Deliane’s uninjured hand moved onto the wool.

The girl stopped. Solenne told her to wait outside, took the box herself and set it on the table.

“I am trying to prevent further harm.”

“I know what you are trying.”

“If these things have been used—”

“I haven’t said they have.”

“No. But we do not know.”

Deliane pushed herself upright too quickly. Pain struck across her face. The healer moved to support the arm and told her to lie back, but she would not. She looked at Riarhin.

“Must I?”

“No,” Riarhin said.

Solenne turned towards her. “You do not know that either.”

“She asked you to leave her blanket.”

“This is not a question about blankets.”

“It is to me,” Deliane said.

Riarhin took the box off the table and put it on the floor, where nobody could lift it without another deliberate movement. She laid the comb beside the pillow. Deliane watched her do it. Her breathing was shallow with the effort of holding herself up.

“May I help you down?” Riarhin asked.

“Yes. Please.”

The healer held the bound arm clear while Riarhin supported her shoulders. Deliane shook after they had settled her. Riarhin drew the blanket over her legs when she asked for it. A servant brought another pillow; it was too thick, and Riarhin folded a towel instead.

Solenne stood at the foot of the bed. “I cannot promise there is no risk.”

“Neither can I,” the healer said. “I can tell you she needs to sleep.”

The officer closed her mouth. She waited until Deliane’s shaking had eased, then apologised for attempting to remove the things without her agreement. Deliane nodded once. She did not tell her it was all right.

Solenne took the empty box out herself.

When the door had closed, Deliane pulled the comb nearer.

“I bought this,” she said.

Riarhin sat beside the bed.

“In a shop. With money.”

“Yes.”

“I couldn’t remember whether she’d given it to me. For a moment. After they asked.”

Riarhin wished she had stopped the servant sooner. Deliane reached for her with the left hand.

“Thank you for making them leave it.”

Riarhin let her hold on. The grip loosened gradually. Before Deliane slept, she asked that the blanket be tucked around her feet.

By evening Orven’s wife had arrived.

Riarhin encountered her at the guard office, arguing with the officer over whether she could take her husband’s coat. She was a broad woman with wet hair pinned badly beneath a cap. Her youngest child slept against her shoulder, open-mouthed and heavy. She had come from the other side of the city without money for a chair.

“His coat is in there,” she said. “He won’t need it if you’re keeping him, will he? And I have to get this one home.”

The officer sent a boy for the coat.

Orven heard her voice and came as far as the inner door. He had not been put in irons. He looked smaller without the keys on his belt.

“Lett, listen.”

She turned. “I have been listening since I got here.”

“She was going out without—”

“I know what you say she was doing.”

“Lady Jaennis asked me—”

“Did she ask you to break the girl’s arm?”

He stared at her. Riarhin looked away, but there was nowhere far enough to look in the little office.

Lett shifted the sleeping child. “I left our supper on the table. The fire wasn’t banked. I had to ask the neighbour to take the other two. I didn’t know whether you were dead.”

“I’m here.”

“Yes. You’re here.”

She wiped her face with the heel of her hand. The child stirred, rubbed its nose against her neck and settled again.

Orven said, more quietly, “She saved us.”

Lett’s mouth trembled. “I know she did.”

For a moment neither spoke. The boy came back with the coat. Lett took it and arranged it around the child, then asked the officer when she would be allowed to see her husband properly. He gave her a time the next morning. She made him repeat it.

After she left, Riarhin found that she was still holding Deliane’s appointment packet. She had brought it from the receiving room and forgotten why.

Solenne came out of the inner office. She took one look at Riarhin and led her into the court.

“Go to your lodging.”

“I said I’d stay.”

“Deliane has the healer and the woman from the linen office. The queen has required an account in the morning. You will give yours. Tonight you can do something useful by not frightening every man who comes through that door.”

Riarhin looked back. Two guards were watching her while pretending not to.

“Orven has children,” she said.

“Yes.”

“I know that doesn’t—”

“Go home.”

At the outer door she met Madra and Sivelle coming in together. Madra carried a jar of preserved cherries. Sivelle had brought a clean shift, and asked at once whether Deliane could raise her arm enough to change.

“Not that one,” Riarhin said.

“It opens down the front.”

“Then perhaps.”

Madra looked past her towards the stairs. “Is she alone?”

“The linen woman is with her.”

“I meant Jaennis.”

“No.”

“Poor old thing. She must be beside herself.”

Sivelle shut her eyes briefly. “Madra.”

“She will be. I am allowed to know that.”

Riarhin was too tired to soften her answer. “Deliane doesn’t want her in the room.”

“And are we to ask you for admission now?”

“No. Ask the officer. He will ask Deliane.”

Madra hugged the jar against her. Riarhin could have explained the blanket and the box, the way pain had kept Deliane from finishing a sentence, the fact that Jaennis had already waited outside. She did not want another frightened person made the evidence for an argument in this doorway.

Sivelle touched her arm. “Go home. We have the shift.”

Madra was still looking at her when she left.

At the lodging, Coryd had kept supper warm by setting a second plate over the first. Riarhin lifted it and found bread, onions and a little meat. She sat down still wearing her coat.

“Do you want me to ask?” Coryd said.

Riarhin shook her head. She took a mouthful, chewed and found she could not swallow. Coryd moved her own chair closer and began cutting the onions into smaller pieces.

In the morning Coryd told her what had happened afterwards. Sivelle had come to the lodging early to ask for help opening the shift’s other sleeve. Madra had gone to Jaennis first and had remained with her for more than an hour. Deliane had waited for the cherries, then said she didn’t want them. When Madra finally came, she cried at the sight of the splint. Deliane cried too. Neither had eaten anything.

“Did they quarrel?” Riarhin asked.

“Not while Sivelle was there.”

Riarhin sat on the bed with her coat across her lap. She could see Madra outside the receiving room, deciding which door needed her most. She had left her to make the decision and had known, even then, which one she would choose.

Coryd handed her the repaired sleeve.

“Will you take it?”

“Yes.”

“The cherries are still in the jar.”

The northern guarantor was waiting when Riarhin came downstairs to leave. He had put his hat on the window ledge and forgotten it was there. Edrune stood beside him with an expression Riarhin had not seen before: anger held back because it might frighten away something useful.

“He wants someone to walk with him,” she said.

“To where?”

“The records office.”

Riarhin looked at him. He held a thin book against his coat.

“They’ll say I made copies after the inquiry began.”

“Did you?”

“No.”

Edrune lifted her eyes towards the ceiling. “He has the keeper’s receipt. He has two witnesses. He wants a third because he doesn’t like the first two.”

The guarantor coloured.

“One is employed by Tessart.”

“Was,” Edrune said.

“Still has a sister in the house.”

Riarhin asked what was in the book. He said payments. She waited for him to say what kind, and he did not. Edrune took her arm and drew her a little way aside.

“Charges paid from the restricted cargo. Guarantees made against the same money. He thought they would share the loss if the orders changed.”

“They won’t?”

“They’ve told him he misunderstood.”

Riarhin looked back at the man. He was rubbing the book’s corner with his thumb.

“Does he expect the debt to disappear if he gives it to us?”

“To the queen,” Edrune said. “And probably. I’ve told him what I think of that.”

They walked to the records office with the escort. The guarantor asked twice whether the book could be kept from Jaennis until his account had been heard. He had promised to speak to her first if difficulties arose. Riarhin asked whether he was unable to break the promise. He looked offended.

“I have already broken it.”

At the office, Edrune required a receipt listing the number of leaves. The guarantor asked for a copy. The clerk made one for each of them.

On the way out he reached for Edrune’s hand. She let him take it, listened to his thanks and then asked when he would pay what he owed for the damaged timber.

His hand dropped.

“After the accounts are settled.”

“I’ll be here,” she said.

At the gate the harness maker was waiting for the treasury window to open.

She had no saddle with her this time. A boy sat on the wall beside her with a bag between his knees. Riarhin recognised the stiffness of a first journey: the bag tied too tightly, the good boots held clear of the mud even though the road would soon make that impossible. The woman saw Riarhin looking and said he was leaving that afternoon.

“The apprentice?”

He answered before she could.

“I can speak.”

“I know,” Riarhin said.

The harness maker looked amused for the first time since Riarhin had met her. “He’s had plenty of practice with me.”

He was old enough to resent the joke and young enough to show it. Riarhin asked whether his new master made saddles. He said cart harness first, then saddles if there was work. His father had found a room near the shop. He would pay for it from the first quarter’s wages.

“What about the room you’ve paid for here?”

“Gone.”

The woman told him it wasn’t gone, that she would settle it when the company account came through. He looked down at the bag and tightened its strap.

“The food is still coming,” the harness maker said to her. “Good oats last night. Not the sweepings.”

“And the horses?”

“Some. Enough to move the company. The sergeant says he can work with them.”

Jaennis’s letters had gone out and been answered. A dealer had agreed to supply the balance on an undertaking to be accepted by the treasury. He wanted the captain to sign as well. The captain had refused to pledge his wife’s work again. They were still arguing about which signature went beneath the other.

The boy slid off the wall. His father’s carrier had entered the court.

The harness maker bent to check the strap on his bag. He let her, though his face tightened. She had made the strap; Riarhin could see the fresh cut of its edge. When the woman straightened she put something in his hand.

“Not a loan,” she said.

“I’ve had my wages.”

“That’s for supper on the road.”

He closed his hand. They stood awkwardly. Then he put the bag down and embraced her, too hard and too briefly. She pressed her cheek against his hair. When he stepped back, she checked the strap once more.

Riarhin moved away while they said their farewells. She stood by the water trough with her escort, watching a horse stir the surface with its breath before drinking. The boy passed her carrying the bag over the wrong shoulder. She nearly corrected him and stopped herself.

The treasury window opened. By the time the harness maker reached it, three men had crowded before her. She let the first speak, then set her book on the sill between the second man’s hands and told the clerk she had been waiting since the bell.

Riarhin could not hear the answer. She saw the clerk turn a page and look towards the door behind him. The woman lifted the book off the sill, waited, and put it back. This time he took it.

Matylde asked about the company that evening.

Riarhin had been summoned to confirm the guard office’s account of a date, a task which took less time than finding the room. The queen was there when she arrived. A packet of ordinary household business lay beside the enquiry papers. Matylde moved it out of the clerk’s way and told him to begin.

When the account had been checked, she asked whether Riarhin had seen the captain again.

“His wife.”

“Are they leaving?”

“The boy left today. The company is still arranging the guarantee.”

“What boy?”

Riarhin told her. Matylde listened without interrupting, then asked whether he could be offered a place in the royal saddlery.

“He has a place.”

“A better one.”

“Perhaps. He was glad to go.”

The girl looked at the packet she had moved. “He shouldn’t have had to.”

“No.”

“Would it be wrong to send?”

Riarhin leaned back. She could feel the question becoming another duty the girl wanted her to settle. She had once sent men to collect people who would certainly be safer under her protection. Some of them had been grateful. Some had never forgiven her.

“Offer it if you want. Let the letter say he needn’t answer at once. He hasn’t seen the other place yet.”

Matylde wrote the name on a spare sheet. She asked the clerk to find it in the company’s account so that the letter would reach the right boy, then crossed out the words she had begun beneath it.

“Tomorrow,” she said.

She pushed it aside and asked whether the harness maker had received her money. Riarhin did not know. The queen rang for a servant and sent to the treasury rather than asking Riarhin to go back.

While they waited she opened the household packet. The first paper concerned shoes for two pages who had outgrown them. She signed it, then hesitated over the next.

“Jaennis says I could have asked her before I stopped the payment.”

“Yes.”

“Do you think I should?”

“I think she knew the people better.”

“She would have paid the seller from somewhere else.”

“Probably.”

“And afterward told me why I ought to be grateful.”

Riarhin watched the girl rub a spot of ink from her finger. There was no way to disagree without lying, and agreement would put Riarhin into a place she did not want beside her.

“You can thank her for the food,” she said.

“I have.”

“Then ask her which men will lend to the treasury when she isn’t in the room.”

Matylde looked up. “She’ll know why I’m asking.”

“Yes.”

The servant returned. The loan had been accepted as an army purchase claim, but only the principal. There was no interest. The money would come from the payment due on the Crown lease when the buyer’s instalment arrived. The clerk did not promise a day.

Matylde asked for the acceptance to be sent to the harness maker that night. The paper itself, she specified, with a copy retained here.

Part V The queen’s own door

Chapter 29 The accounts taken apart

Adelis was standing outside the records room when Riarhin arrived for the hearing.

She had dressed to travel. The dark silk had been replaced by a wool gown with a stain at the hem; a plain hood lay over her arm. Beside her waited a boy carrying a small wooden box. He had set it down twice and been told twice that the floor was damp. Now he held it against his stomach, his face carefully empty.

Riarhin stopped. “Are you leaving?”

“I was.”

There was no accountant with her. The two books were tied together beneath her cloak, their corners making a hard shape against her hip. Riarhin asked whether she needed someone fetched.

“The man who signed the settlement. He was meant to be here at the bell.”

“He may be at the hearing.”

“I know. Everybody has told me that. At the hearing, or waiting for the hearing, or afraid to come because he may be asked why he isn’t at the hearing.”

The boy shifted the box. Adelis took it from him without looking and put it on the window sill. She told him to go and eat something, then called him back to give him money. He went more quickly the second time.

The disputed accounts had been corrected. Jaennis’s remembered payment had settled the return hire, and the accountant had found another overcharge while comparing the copies. A substantial balance was due to Adelis. It would be paid through a broker who had undertaken to meet it when the final receipt was witnessed. Jaennis had introduced him. He had agreed to arrange the remaining debt against an estate payment expected after the harvest.

“He was very agreeable over dinner,” Adelis said. “I liked him less before. That may have been wise.”

“Did he promise to come?”

“He promised her.”

Riarhin looked towards the room from which the clerk would call her. She could not leave the corridor. Adelis saw the glance and lifted the box from the sill again.

“I haven’t asked you to make him.”

“I know.”

“I’m not sure you do. Everyone here looks as if they’re deciding which person could be made to do something for them.”

Riarhin let it stand. After a moment Adelis put the box back down.

“Forgive me. I have sent my steward home. He waited two days because I wished to stay, and then his wife wrote. One of the cows is down. It isn’t an interesting cow, only theirs.”

“You need the money before you follow him.”

“I need to know the money will follow me.”

The records clerk emerged and spoke to her quietly. The broker could send his man to witness, but had asked for Jaennis’s confirmation that the final account reflected their understanding. Adelis unfolded the paper she had been given. Jaennis’s name appeared near the bottom beside a space where her own would go.

“She isn’t a party to my husband’s debts.”

“No, madam.”

“Then why does she need to confirm them?”

“The arrangement was made through her household.”

“Can it be made without her household?”

The clerk said he would enquire. It meant the broker would have to examine the security without relying on the introduction. There might be a charge. The money might not be released that day.

Adelis closed her eyes, then opened them and asked him to enquire. When he had gone, she sat on the bench with the box on her lap. A ribbon had come loose around it. Inside was a small painted cup wrapped in a scarf.

“Is that hers?” Riarhin asked.

“Mine. It was hers.”

The cup had a thin flaw beneath the rim, carefully repaired. Adelis ran her thumb beside it, not over it.

“She told me how it broke. There were three different versions. In one it was a dog, and in one she threw it. I preferred the dog.”

“Which did she say was true?”

“She wouldn’t. She said a woman ought to be allowed one uncertain cup.”

Adelis smiled. It changed her face. She wrapped the cup again, covering the repaired place last.

A servant came to fetch Adelis. Jaennis had heard she was in the passage. The broker’s man could come to her rooms and settle everything there.

Adelis retied the box.

“Will Lady Jaennis sign the confirmation?”

“She asks that you come, madam.”

“Does she know I have a carriage waiting?”

“Yes.”

The servant offered to take the box. Adelis kept it and rose. Riarhin’s clerk called her name from the other door. She went into the hearing, leaving the widow between the servant and the waiting carriage.

The chair for Jaennis was removed before the hearing began.

Riarhin saw the servant carry it out. It was taller than the other chairs, with a cushion which made rising easier. Nothing had been said against the cushion. Matylde had asked for the witnesses to be heard separately, and Jaennis was a witness.

The queen watched the chair go with an expression Riarhin could not read. She was wearing a plain morning dress. A pin at one cuff had come loose; she kept turning it between finger and thumb while the clerk arranged his papers.

Riarhin gave her account first.

She said where she had stood, what Orven had said, the hand with which he had caught Deliane and the direction in which the younger woman had tried to move. When she reached her own intervention, the clerk asked her to demonstrate the grip without using another person. She put her right hand against her left forearm and showed him.

“Could you have done that before she was hurt?” Matylde asked.

The clerk looked up.

Riarhin heard the word could with an old and unwelcome precision.

“Yes,” she said. “If I had expected him to take hold of her.”

“You expected something.”

“I expected him to argue. I thought the officer could end it.”

“And when you took hold of him?”

“He released her.”

“Did you hurt him?”

“Yes.”

“How much?”

“Enough to make him open his hand. I held him after he had done it. Briefly.”

“Why?”

Riarhin looked at the pin in the girl’s fingers.

“I was angry.”

Matylde set the pin down. The clerk wrote.

The young officer came next. Riarhin remained only because he wished her to hear and confirm his account of the packet. He had been unsure whether a household instruction could delay a transfer ordered by the queen. He said so without making Orven appear more threatening than he had been.

“Did the packet name an hour?” Solenne asked.

“Yes.”

“Did you believe you had authority to insist on it?”

“Yes. I thought if I explained—”

He stopped. His face was hot.

“Go on.”

“I thought he would agree once I explained.”

Matylde asked for the packet. The hour was there, written in a narrow hand. She ran her nail beneath it.

“Perhaps we could add that I mean the hour I give,” she said.

The clerk smiled before he could help himself. The officer did not.

Matylde looked at him again. “I’m sorry. That was unhelpful.”

He bowed his head. She handed the paper to Solenne.

Jaennis’s account took longer. Riarhin was sent out before it began and heard nothing of it through the door. She waited beside a window with a view of a roof on which pigeons were conducting some obscure, violent business. Each time she looked away there seemed to be a different bird in possession of the highest tile.

A councillor came out and spoke to Solenne. He wanted Deliane moved to the new house immediately, the porter dismissed and the affair concluded before people made more of it. The queen’s affection for Jaennis ought not to be used against her by strangers.

Riarhin turned from the window.

“Am I the stranger?”

He looked at her. “You are certainly one of them.”

Solenne said, “The queen has asked what instruction the next porter would receive.”

“Surely that is obvious.”

“To him?”

The councillor made an irritated gesture with his gloves. “We have servants throughout this palace who manage to understand what they are told.”

“Then we should settle what they will be told.”

He went back in. Riarhin watched the pigeon on the high tile slide, recover and begin preening as if it had intended the movement.

At the first adjournment, Riarhin found Adelis in Jaennis’s outer room.

She had been sent there for a copy of a household direction. Solenne’s clerk accompanied her. The servant admitted them and asked that they wait while Jaennis finished speaking to her guest. The inner door stood open; beyond it, Adelis sat in the chair beside the window, the painted cup on the table between them.

“You will lose the good part of the day,” Jaennis said.

“I have already lost it.”

“Then stay tonight. Go rested.”

“Will that change the account?”

“The man is coming. Why must we spend the morning as if I’ve detained you?”

Adelis looked towards the open door. She saw Riarhin and gave a small nod. Riarhin could not tell whether it asked her to remain. She sat with the clerk, her hands on the copied direction he had brought for comparison.

“I don’t think you have detained me,” Adelis said. “I think you don’t want me to go.”

“Of course I don’t. I had hoped that would please you.”

“It does.”

“Then don’t make a grievance of it.”

Jaennis’s cane lay across her lap. She rolled it beneath one palm, slowly. Adelis leaned forward and took the other hand.

“I stayed two more nights because I wanted to. I am leaving today because I want to do that too.”

“Your business is here.”

“My house is elsewhere.”

“Your steward can manage a house for a few days.”

“Yes. I employed him because he can. I still want to see it.”

Riarhin heard the patience beginning to cost her. Jaennis watched their joined hands. She raised Adelis’s fingers to her mouth and kissed them.

“There is room here for your things. You needn’t keep coming with that little bag.”

“I like my little bag.”

Adelis smiled when she said it. Jaennis did not smile back.

“You make a joke whenever I offer you something.”

“I brought the cup.”

“To return it?”

“To keep it from breaking in the carriage.”

The silence after that lasted long enough for the clerk beside Riarhin to change the hand in which he held his pen. He did it without a sound. Adelis released Jaennis’s hand and straightened.

“I can come again. I haven’t promised when.”

“Then what have you promised?”

“I don’t think I have.”

Jaennis drew back. Riarhin saw the younger woman flinch. Her hand moved towards the cup.

“I have enjoyed being with you,” she said. “Very much.”

“As long as it asks nothing of you.”

Adelis’s face went still. She looked at the table, then at the open door.

“I’ve missed my carriage.”

“I can have one found.”

“Yes. You can.”

She rose and picked up the box. Jaennis began to rise too, pushed badly on the cane and gasped. Adelis put the box down at once and went to her. Riarhin was halfway out of her chair before she saw that Jaennis had steadied herself.

“It caught,” Jaennis said. “Sit. Please.”

Adelis helped her back, adjusted the cushion and fetched the footstool. She knelt to place the painful leg more comfortably. Jaennis put a hand on her head. The younger woman stayed where she was while the servant brought a warmed cloth.

Riarhin sat again. Adelis held the cloth against the knee, checked the heat with the back of her wrist and asked whether Jaennis wanted the healer. Jaennis said no; it would pass. Then she asked about the balcony.

“Is it very high?”

“Not very.”

“I used to like being above a busy street. One could listen without being expected to settle anything.”

Adelis laughed softly. “You would have settled it anyway.”

“Perhaps.”

The broker’s man arrived while she was still kneeling. He had a confirmation already prepared. Jaennis read it and signed. The transaction which had occupied half the morning took less time than the servant needed to replace the cloth.

Adelis stood and accepted her copy. Her cheeks were flushed from bending. Jaennis held out a hand, and she took it.

“Stay to dine.”

Riarhin looked down at the paper in her lap. She could hear the clerk breathing beside her.

“No,” Adelis said.

Jaennis let go.

There was another carriage to be hired. The boy had returned and would go with her to arrange it. Adelis made these explanations while wrapping the cup. She did not look at Jaennis again until the ribbon was tied.

“I will write when I get home.”

“Do.”

Adelis bent and kissed her. It was a brief, firm kiss. Then she took the box and went out. At the outer door she stopped beside Riarhin.

“I’m glad you freed the earring,” she said.

“So am I.”

She smiled and followed the boy down the passage.

Jaennis called for the copy Riarhin had been sent to collect. Her voice had recovered its ordinary warmth. She thanked the clerk for waiting and asked whether his wife had returned from her sister’s. He told her she had, surprised and pleased that Jaennis remembered. Riarhin watched the servant carry away the unused place laid for dinner.

Chapter 30 The visit not agreed

When she was called again, Jaennis had gone. Her chair had not been brought back.

Matylde had two papers before her: Deliane’s appointment and a page headed with names of officers. There were arrows between some of the names. Two contradicted each other. She had crossed out one and then, less confidently, crossed out the other.

“Who pays Orven?” she asked.

“The household,” Solenne said.

“Whose household?”

“Lady Jaennis’s.”

“Who appoints the officer who came with Deliane?”

“I do.”

“Who gave Lady Jaennis charge of this passage?”

Nobody answered at once. It had been used by her servants for so long that the question required research. Matylde stared at the arrows.

“She says she didn’t mean he should stop her by force.”

“I believe that,” Riarhin said.

The queen looked up quickly, almost gratefully.

“Did she mean he should let her go?” Solenne asked.

Matylde put the page face down.

Margery’s carriage was standing at the wrong entrance.

Riarhin noticed it as she waited for the next account to be called. The driver was arguing with a palace boy who had been sent to find him. He pointed towards the road, then to the box seat, where a folded shawl lay beside the reins. Riarhin knew the shawl. Margery had worn it when she came out to meet Jaennis at the house.

She went down as far as her escort permitted. The boy recognised her and asked whether she had seen Mistress Margery.

“Why?”

“She was to come this way. The driver says no one told him.”

“Told him what?”

The boy had a note. It changed Margery’s afternoon journey to a visit with a woman whose son had been named among the claimants. She had asked to speak privately. Jaennis thought Margery’s manner would reassure her. There was nothing in it about papers or Tessart; it was precisely the sort of call Margery had made before and might gladly make again.

Riarhin did not take the note. She told the boy where Margery was waiting to give her account. He went inside, clutching it as though the right recipient would make its purpose simple.

Margery came out a few minutes later. She stood beneath the arch and read the note once, then again. The driver took off his hat. Riarhin saw that he was afraid of being blamed for a delay he had spent the morning trying to prevent.

“Did you agree to this?” Riarhin asked her.

“No.”

“You can go home.”

“I know where I can go.”

Riarhin stepped back. Margery looked at her and immediately regretted the sharpness; Riarhin wished she would keep it, because an apology would bring the two of them into another kindness neither wanted.

“It’s a mother,” Margery said. “She’s frightened.”

“Yes.”

“She won’t understand why I’ve refused.”

“You haven’t refused yet.”

Margery folded the note. Her thumb left a dirty mark where the ink had not quite dried.

“We had a good evening,” she said.

“I saw the lamp.”

“Did you watch?”

“No.”

Margery looked at the carriage. “She listened. I thought perhaps I had never said it plainly enough. All these years, perhaps I had expected her to guess and been angry because she couldn’t.”

Riarhin waited. The driver had turned away to check a trace which did not need checking.

“She asked me what I wanted. At the house. After you had gone. She let me finish.”

“You can keep that evening.”

Margery shook her head impatiently. “Don’t offer me permission to remember it.”

Riarhin looked towards the waiting carriage.

A servant came through the arch carrying a reply. Jaennis was sorry if the note had arrived before Margery was ready. The visit need not be made until she had eaten. She would have food sent down.

Margery listened without moving. When he finished, she asked whether Jaennis had understood that she had not agreed to the visit.

“I was told to say it could wait, mistress.”

“Then tell her it isn’t waiting. I am not going.”

The servant bowed and went back. Margery watched him until he was out of sight.

Riarhin said, “Would you like me to leave you?”

“No.”

They stood together. The horses stamped. Somewhere inside a door closed, and Margery turned towards the sound with an expectancy she could not hide.

Jaennis came herself.

She walked slowly, leaning heavily on the cane, and stopped beneath the arch where the light fell across Margery’s face. She saw the carriage, the note and Riarhin, in that order.

“I didn’t mean to hurry you.”

“That’s not what I said.”

“The woman has asked three times. I thought you would be glad to help.”

“You thought I would say yes.”

“I did.”

“Then ask before you send the carriage.”

“It is your carriage.”

Margery laughed once, in disbelief. Jaennis looked hurt, then looked at the driver and saw him study the buckle in his hand.

“I see,” she said.

Riarhin could not tell whether she did. Margery opened the folded note and held it where Jaennis could read the first line.

“You wrote that I was coming.”

“I expected that you would.”

“At the house you said you would ask.”

“We were speaking of Tessart. This is a woman who is afraid for her son.”

Margery lowered the note. She looked tired suddenly, older than she had looked in the house.

“Find someone else,” she said.

Jaennis drew herself upright. “Of course. I won’t force you to be kind.”

Riarhin felt anger move through her so quickly that she nearly stepped between them. Margery lifted one hand without looking at her.

“Don’t,” she said.

Riarhin stopped. Margery’s hand remained raised until she was sure of it, then fell to her side.

“I’m going home,” she told Jaennis. “After I’ve given my account.”

Jaennis’s face altered. She glanced at the door through which the clerk would call the next witness.

“I hadn’t understood you were being asked.”

“I asked them.”

The driver put on his hat, then took it off again. Jaennis turned from the carriage and went inside without calling for help at the step.

Margery watched her until she was gone. She looked at Riarhin only then.

“Don’t be pleased.”

“I’m not.”

“I think part of you is.”

Margery folded the note and put it away before Riarhin answered. When the clerk called her, she went in with her hand still in the pocket where she had put it.

Margery had given her account separately. Riarhin knew only that she had attended, until the older woman found her in the court after the hearing.

“I told them about the walk.”

Riarhin waited.

“And the other times. Only the ones I was there for.”

“Did they ask for your letter?”

“Yes. I said they could ask me questions instead.”

They stood beside a cart being unloaded. A servant carried a basket past, turned and came back because she had been sent to the wrong entrance.

“Will you see her?” Riarhin asked.

“Jaennis?”

“Yes.”

Margery nodded. “If she’ll see me.”

Riarhin thought of the opening line on the back of the letter. Margery watched her face.

“I meant that part too,” she said, and went towards the household stair.

That afternoon Solenne received Lyra’s answer.

It was a thin packet with a separate note for Riarhin. The note said that the book had arrived, that the binding was poor and that Riarhin ought not to have paid whatever she had paid for it. At the bottom Lyra had written: Yes. You may write here.

Riarhin read those five words twice before opening anything else.

The statement was addressed to the queen. Solenne allowed her to read it because Lyra had named her among the permitted readers. The clerk was to record that it existed, its date and the scope of its use. He was not to copy its account into a public register.

Lyra had not described what she had revealed under Jaennis’s charm. She described the ease with which the desire to speak had arrived, and the difficulty, afterwards, of locating the moment when she could have refused it. She had felt close to Jaennis. She had wanted to be useful to her. Some of that feeling had existed before the working. The rest had been given to her with no means of separating it while she spoke.

Do not ask whether I enjoyed the conversation, she had written. That is one of the injuries.

Riarhin sat with the sheet in her lap. She remembered Lyra as she had been before the imprisonment; then, involuntarily, as she had been after. There were times when memory did not present one picture after another but laid them across each other until she could not look directly at either.

Solenne waited.

“She has not accused her of doing this to Deliane,” Riarhin said.

“No.”

“Or to the queen.”

“No.”

“Will they use it that way?”

“Not if I can prevent it.”

Riarhin returned the statement. “Keep it as she asked.”

Before she could leave, Solenne closed the door.

“We have something about your escort.”

Riarhin remained standing.

“The man Coryd struck admits he was paid to help take you aside. He insists he did not agree to a killing. The first man, the one Kerros fought, had been promised a different sum.”

“Cedric.”

“Listen.”

Riarhin put her hands on the back of the chair.

“The intermediary is Audemar, a former household clerk. He had access to an old escort seal and knew which passage schedules were still written by the same office. He says Cedric asked for an hour when you would be beyond the palace guard. He claims to have understood that there would be a confrontation.”

“Of course.”

“He has given us the name of the man who was to take the horses away afterwards.”

Riarhin looked at her.

“A confrontation doesn’t require that,” Solenne said. “Nor does it require payment to be made only when no return escort is needed. We have found the stableman. The payment was lodged. He was told to say that a horse had thrown its rider near the yard wall.”

Riarhin pulled the chair out and sat.

The facts came slowly, one after another, with the steadiness of objects being set on a table. The seal had been real and used without authority. The clerk had met Cedric twice. One meeting had been witnessed by a woman waiting to ask about her dead brother’s papers. She had not heard what was said. She knew Cedric because he had offered to help her request an account from Riarhin.

Solenne took another sheet from the folder.

“The clerk’s first account said he had only arranged an opportunity to speak to you. Then we asked him why he had used an escort direction.”

“What did he say?”

“That you would not have come otherwise.”

Riarhin looked at the paper without reaching for it. The answer had the bare plausibility of a thing Cedric might say while somebody else decided how much of it to believe.

“He knew I hadn’t agreed.”

“Yes. He says he meant no injury. He knew you were to be taken somewhere without a witness of your choosing. He must answer for that much whether he knew about the blade or not.”

“Will he?”

“He is held.”

“By whose men?”

“Mine.”

Solenne gave her a moment to hear the answer before continuing.

“The witness to his second meeting has identified the house. The owner confirms Cedric lodged there. A servant took food to the room while the two men were together. She did not hear the whole conversation. What she did hear concerned a payment and whether the return route would be watched.”

“Enough?”

“Together with the other accounts, enough to ask the queen for an arrest. Not enough to pretend we know every person’s intention.”

Riarhin sat back. “You think I’ll muddle them.”

“I think you were attacked and might like a single person to hate without having to keep track of the others.”

“I’ve had him to hate for a long time.”

Solenne looked at her and said nothing.

The quiet made Riarhin remember the road, the mark, her satisfaction at having left Cedric with something he could not escape. She had not been thinking of what he would do with the years afterwards.

“Who is the witness?” she asked.

“The woman with the glove.”

“Yes.”

“She supplied the day?”

“She told him you would be going to the timber yard. She thought he was arranging another witness.”

Riarhin covered her mouth with one hand. She had looked at that woman’s anger and seen danger. Danger had come.

“Does she know what he intended?”

“She does now.”

Outside the office someone called for more sand. A clerk answered with the exhausted anger of a man who had already sent for it twice.

“Where is Cedric?”

“We’re checking the address.”

“I could—”

“No.”

“You haven’t heard what I was going to say.”

“I’ve heard enough of this sentence from people who want to help me arrest someone.”

Riarhin looked at the floor. “Tell me when you have him.”

“I will.”

“Before someone gives me a different escort.”

Solenne took a sheet from beneath the ledger and passed it across. The next three days’ escorts were named there, in her own hand. Any change was to be confirmed at the guard office by Riarhin herself.

Riarhin read each name.

Chapter 31 The people an order misses

Matylde’s first list transferred twenty-three people.

Her second transferred nine.

The third had six names, two of them written in the margin because the men were married to women not yet on any list at all.

Riarhin carried a copy to the guesthouse with Solenne. They found Deliane sitting at the desk in a shawl, her bound arm propped on a cushion and her face set against the possibility that someone might suggest she return to bed. A hired chair had brought her across that morning. She had vomited after the journey and sent the bowl away herself. The chair was waiting to take her back to the receiving room; the healer had agreed to this visit, not to her spending the night alone.

Coryd stood on a stool fitting a hook. The linen woman had spread three blankets on the floor and was choosing the least worn for the room above the stable.

“Six,” Solenne said.

Deliane looked up. “Six what?”

“People requiring rooms.”

“No.”

“It is a temporary arrangement.”

“There are four beds free. Two are promised to the women bringing the southern accounts. They arrive at the week’s end.”

“Could they be lodged elsewhere?”

“They could. Who is arranging it?”

Solenne looked at the paper. Riarhin had watched armies reach a ford and discover that somebody had counted carts instead of teams. She kept the memory to herself.

Deliane held out her good hand for the list. She read the names, then called the porter. He was an older man with a damaged ear who had been cheerful about the job until he met its first day. Now he listened without expression while she asked him to measure the room beside his own.

“It will take a bed,” he said.

“Will it take two people?”

“People can stand anywhere.”

“People asleep.”

“Not those ones. He’s broad.”

Coryd laughed from the stool. Deliane shut her eyes for a moment, then opened them.

“Measure it.”

The porter went. Solenne took off her gloves and set them on the desk.

“The queen is willing to pay for another room nearby.”

“Today?”

“Yes.”

“Pay today, or promise to?”

Solenne hesitated.

Deliane looked down at the list again. “I can’t ask the woman next door to turn away a paying guest because the queen will be willing at the end of the quarter.”

“I’ll obtain an advance.”

“Thank you.”

Riarhin moved towards the desk. “I have—”

Deliane raised her eyes.

Riarhin stopped. She had some money. She could solve the afternoon and leave the following month to someone else, and they would all remember that she had been generous.

“What can be done before the advance arrives?” she asked instead.

Solenne nodded and resumed checking the list.

They spent the next hour walking through rooms. One bed could be moved without blocking a fireplace. Another could not. The stable loft needed a safer stair before anybody carrying a child ought to use it. Coryd said that a rail could be fitted, but not by her before evening because the timber had not come. The porter found a cot in a locked cupboard and became offended when asked why he had not mentioned it earlier.

“Nobody asked about cots.”

“I’m asking now,” Deliane said.

He brought it out.

The people themselves arrived after midday. A woman carrying a bedding roll refused the stable room because her husband had a cough which grew worse at night. The husband denied this, coughed and glared at her. A boy wanted to know whether his mother could still come through the kitchen when she brought his little sister; his old mistress had permitted it. Solenne had no objection, but the new cook had not been asked. The cook came in and objected to the kitchen being used as a passage while she had hot pots on the floor. They found another door.

Riarhin took the bedding roll from the woman only after asking where it was to go. The woman told her the front room, discovered that it was not theirs and called her back. Riarhin carried it downstairs again.

By the time the advance arrived she was sweating beneath her coat.

Matylde had sent coin from a fund set aside for replacing the palace’s summer hangings. The councillor who accompanied it wished everyone to know that this was exceptional. Deliane asked him to count it again because he had spoken over the first count.

Later, when Riarhin returned to the palace, she found the queen examining a strip of faded cloth.

“Apparently these will last another year,” Matylde said.

“They look sound.”

“They look like boiled cabbage.”

Riarhin waited.

“I wanted the blue ones.”

“Yes.”

“I can say that and still pay for the rooms.”

“You can.”

Matylde rolled the cloth tightly and gave it to the servant. “Take it away before I decide I hate everybody who has a bed.”

The servant smiled. The queen caught herself smiling back and let it happen.

Coryd returned from Sivelle’s house carrying the narrow bundle of tools she had left there.

Riarhin was in the guesthouse kitchen, helping the cook shell peas because waiting upstairs had become intolerable. Deliane had been taken back to rest. Solenne was still finding rooms for the people whose names had been written more quickly than their needs. The cook tipped another handful into Riarhin’s bowl and told Coryd there was food if she wanted it.

Coryd laid the tools beneath the bench.

“Is she paying someone else?” Riarhin asked.

“For what?”

“The work.”

“No.”

Riarhin split a pod too far and sent three peas across the table. Coryd caught one before it reached the edge. She placed it in the bowl and sat.

“I’m bringing my own tools back.”

“Yes.”

“They’re mine wherever I sleep.”

“I understand.”

“I know you do. That’s why I am telling you before you begin behaving very kindly.”

The cook took the bowl to the sink. Riarhin wiped her hand on the cloth, leaving Coryd’s tools beneath the bench.

“Did you finish the window?”

“Yes.”

“Does it shut?”

“Badly. That’s how she wants it.”

Riarhin looked at her. Coryd smiled a little.

“She likes to hear the yard. I told her it would let in the weather. She said she knew what weather did.”

The cook set bread and cheese before her. Coryd ate as if she had forgotten the last meal. She had a shallow scratch on her neck which Riarhin looked away from before her sister could notice.

“We went through the agreement,” Coryd said. “She’s paying for the repairs I’ve done. We crossed out the quarter.”

“Are you doing the rest?”

“Some of it. Before we leave.”

Riarhin kept her attention on the pods. We had arrived in the sentence as quietly as if it had always been there. She felt the relief before she could examine it and hated herself for thinking that examining it would make it harmless.

Coryd saw anyway.

“Yes,” she said. “You may be pleased.”

“I’m sorry.”

“I didn’t say you had to be sorry.”

“What did she say?”

Coryd ate another mouthful and considered whether to answer.

“That she didn’t want to hire me into staying. That she also didn’t want to become a place I visit when the roads are bad.”

Riarhin waited.

“I couldn’t promise she wouldn’t be.”

Coryd turned the rind of the cheese between her fingers, then put it on the plate.

“She has a house. People who expect her at particular hours. A woman who comes to mend the linen and knows where she keeps the good thread. She likes having those things. I kept treating them as if they were things she would be grateful to escape.”

Riarhin looked at the tools beneath the bench.

“Would you have taken her with us?”

“I asked.”

“And?”

“She asked who would see the roof repaired. I said somebody else could. I heard myself say it.”

The cook returned, took the shells away and gave Riarhin another bowl without joining the conversation. Coryd watched her for a moment.

Riarhin held the split pod until the peas dropped into her lap. Coryd rubbed her palms together as though removing dust and stood.

“I’m seeing her tomorrow. Don’t make that face either.”

“What face?”

“The one where you are pleased I haven’t ruined everything.”

The cook called Coryd over to look at the cupboard hinge. Coryd went at once. She knelt, opened the door twice and asked for her narrow chisel.

Riarhin took it from the bundle and passed it to her. It fitted into Coryd’s hand without either of them looking.

At the guesthouse that evening, Deliane offered Coryd a position maintaining the house and supervising repairs. She had worked out the wages on the back of an old provision sheet. They were better than Coryd expected; Riarhin could tell from the way her sister’s eyes rested on the figure.

“No,” Coryd said.

Deliane looked at the sheet. “Is it the room?”

“No.”

“The money?”

“No. I want to leave.”

The younger woman’s face grew still. Riarhin felt Coryd notice it and make herself remain where she was.

“I like working here,” Coryd said. “I don’t want to live here.”

Deliane folded the sheet once. “You’d be good at it.”

“I know.”

That made her laugh, briefly. She rubbed her forehead with her good hand.

“I’m tired. I wanted one thing settled.”

“I can finish the rail when the wood comes. If it comes before we go.”

Deliane nodded. “I’ll have to ask the carpenter again.”

“He’ll be more expensive.”

“He told me.”

They sat together for a while, watching the porter bring in a mattress which was too broad for the door. He insisted that it had come through that door before. The cook said that perhaps it had been hungry then. Coryd got up to help.

The council called Riarhin back before the evening meal. She had been about to leave the guesthouse with Coryd. Her sister looked at the messenger, then at the wrapped portion the cook had given them, and took both parcels upstairs without speaking.

Matylde sat at the long table with three open books in front of her. One was the guarantor’s. Riarhin recognised the worn corner. The queen had placed a paper marker between its leaves rather than turn one down.

Tessart attended under the same conditions as before. Jaennis sat two places from him. Fayd was standing at the window with a clerk. He had put the best light behind the clerk’s shoulder; from where Riarhin stood, the words were invisible.

She was asked to confirm the page the secretary had read at Margery’s house. She did. The final paragraph still said the unwitnessed meeting had been within the purposes of her visit.

“Did you agree to any such meeting?” Matylde asked.

“No.”

“Did Margery tell you that she had agreed for you?”

“No.”

Tessart’s adviser shifted in his chair. Riarhin watched him look towards the guarantor’s book. She had expected him to object to the words unwitnessed meeting. He seemed more concerned about the money.

Fayd’s clerk read the relevant entries. Tessart’s circle had advanced funds to Audemar for bringing claimants to Valora. Carriage hire, lodging, the copying of accounts. The sum paid for the horses waiting beyond the freight yard had come from that advance. A second payment, promised for the absence of a return escort, had been lodged separately by Cedric.

Tessart said that he had not authorised an attack.

The clerk said nothing to contradict him. He read the dates again. One of the payments had been made before Riarhin entered royal territory. Tessart had been assembling the claimants while her safe-conduct was being witnessed.

Jaennis leaned forward to see the entry. Her expression changed. Riarhin had seen that quick interest at the water garden, when the little bird first turned upside down.

“He retained the receipts,” she said.

“The guarantor did,” Fayd said.

“Very sensible of him.”

Tessart turned towards her. “You told me they should be heard.”

“They should.”

“You told me she could not refuse them.”

“Questions properly put to her. You heard me say it.”

She looked at Riarhin. The words returned with the court, the cup and the wallet on Tessart’s knees.

“Yes,” Riarhin said.

Tessart’s adviser put a hand on his sleeve before he could answer. The councillor shook it off.

“You introduced Audemar.”

“I knew you would have matters to discuss.”

Fayd came away from the window. He brought a separate direction, signed by Jaennis’s travelling officer, and set it beside the escort paper. The west gate, six household men, after the hearing. Audemar had been asked to bring the aggrieved former servants there if ordinary requests to question Riarhin had failed. No weapon was to be drawn.

“Whose ordinary requests had failed?” Matylde asked.

The clerk looked down the list. None of the requests had yet received an answer when the direction was made.

Jaennis adjusted the cushion beneath her knee.

“I expected that some would find the conditions unacceptable.”

“So you gave them another place to come.”

“Where they could be contained. I would not have had them approaching her in the street.”

“They approached her in the street.”

“Yes. Before the agreed hour, and at another place.”

“What was I to do at your gate?” Riarhin asked.

“Answer what you could. Refuse what you must. With witnesses.”

“And if they tried to keep me there?”

“My men would have opened the way.”

“You expected me to wait for them.”

“You have endured worse than questions.” Jaennis turned a glove over in her lap. “I thought you would prefer not to hurt another man who came to ask you about his dead.”

Riarhin pressed her good hand flat against her knee. She had stayed beside Kerros in the yard. She would have stayed at the gate, too, while somebody decided whether an accusation had become a threat.

“Would you have told me where your men were?”

“They would have been visible.”

“That isn’t what I asked.”

Jaennis folded the glove into its fellow.

Matylde looked at Riarhin’s bandaged wrist, then down at the paper. “Kerros was hurt at the yard. This was afterward.”

“Yes, Majesty.”

The girl closed her eyes for a moment. When she opened them, she looked at Jaennis.

“Did you know Cedric was among them?”

“I knew he had an account to put before her.”

“And that she had hurt him?”

“Yes.”

“Why didn’t you tell Solenne?”

“I intended to give her the people who acted improperly. An angry man is not yet a prisoner.”

Fayd said, “An officer need not arrest a man in order to be warned about him.”

Jaennis looked at him. “You have spent years knowing things you did not tell the rest of us.”

“I have.”

The answer seemed to irritate her more than a denial would have done. She turned back to Matylde.

“They have been promising my support to people who ought to answer your officers. I wanted you to see who they were. Tessart would not admit the conditions attached to his money until he thought he might lose the chance to offer it. The others would have denied any wish to obstruct you until they stood before the door doing it.”

“You could have told me what you suspected.”

“I did not wish to hand you suspicions dressed as facts.”

Riarhin looked at the table’s edge. She had wanted her enemies committed too, with the ground chosen before they knew she was waiting.

Matylde asked why Margery had been sent Tessart.

“To answer him. She had asked Riarhin to attend. He could hear why from her.”

“You had arranged something.”

“I had arranged protection.”

“For a quarrel you encouraged.”

Tessart began to speak. Matylde raised her hand without looking at him.

Tessart subsided. Matylde waited. Jaennis looked towards Margery’s empty chair, then back at the girl.

Jaennis spoke more quietly.

“If I warned every man who means to use you that we knew his intention, you would never hear an honest word from them.”

“Was the money honest?” Matylde asked.

Fayd’s clerk answered when she turned to him. Parts of it were. Some guarantees could be met without the disputed income. Others depended on charges now subject to the hearing. The first payment of the army settlement had already been made and was not at issue. The next promises required new scrutiny.

“How long?”

“Six weeks for these accounts, Majesty. Longer if others must be opened.”

Tessart’s adviser asked whether the Crown intended to reject money before knowing whether it could lawfully be paid. The guarantor’s clerk, summoned to identify a receipt, made a small sound at that. Riarhin did not know whether it was anger or amusement. Nobody asked him.

The council argued about the six weeks. Jaennis listened, then offered to obtain replacement assurances from households she knew would answer her.

“Tonight,” she said. “There need be no interruption.”

Matylde moved a loose thread from the cuff of her dress. Riarhin saw the temptation pass over her face: the men paid, the quarrel ended, Jaennis able to repair what had happened.

“Give their names to the treasury,” the girl said.

“I can speak to them first.”

“Give their names.”

Fayd asked authority to examine related guarantees. A councillor asked how far the word related would take him. They spent another quarter-hour reducing the authority to named accounts and a fixed reporting day. Fayd wanted to appoint both clerks. He was given one; the other would come from the treasury and report separately.

Riarhin was still standing. Her injured wrist had begun to throb. The council had reached a question about the second clerk’s pay when Matylde noticed her and had a chair brought. It was the first kindness the girl had offered her that evening, and it was accompanied by an impatient gesture to someone who had failed to do it earlier.

The northern hearings would continue. Tessart could answer for the money and the visit to Margery; his connection to the former servants’ requests would be examined separately from Cedric’s intended violence. The queen would not sign the statement he had brought to Margery, nor accept it as a reason to delay the petition.

Then she asked for the old escort seal to be brought from the guard office.

“It has already been recovered,” Solenne said.

“I want the keeper here with it. Tomorrow.”

Jaennis looked up.

“No household direction is to change a royal escort,” Matylde said. “No one is to arrange another place for claimants to meet my guest. They may bring their questions to the officer I appointed.”

“Of course,” Jaennis said.

“And requests for money or office come here. Your recommendation can come with them. It cannot be the answer before I have seen the request.”

For the first time that evening Jaennis did not reply at once.

The clerk asked whether that instruction was to apply only while the present inquiries continued. Matylde looked at him, then at Jaennis.

“No.”

The clerk wrote.

Jaennis gathered her gloves from the table. She did not put them on. When the council rose, she went to Matylde and bent to speak near her ear. Riarhin could not hear the words. She saw the girl’s head turn towards her, then stop.

Matylde asked Solenne to remain.

Jaennis straightened. Fayd had already taken his clerk to the window again. Tessart was being asked to attend another room with his adviser. The guarantor’s clerk had found a loose receipt beneath his chair and was holding it out for somebody to take.

Riarhin went back to Coryd with both of their meals cold.

Chapter 32 Vaelrie’s answer

Vaelrie sent no note. She was waiting in the lodging when Riarhin returned, sitting in Coryd’s chair with a cup she had apparently found for herself.

“Your cook says you aren’t eating enough.”

“My cook is not my cook.”

“She says that too.”

Coryd looked up from the window seat and resumed sewing. She made no attempt to leave. Vaelrie glanced at her, accepted the arrangement and put down the cup.

“What are you going to do?”

“About what?”

“Don’t.”

Riarhin took off her coat. There was a place along its seam which had begun to rub; she had noticed it for days and done nothing. She turned the sleeve inside out, found the knot and pulled at it until Coryd held out a hand for the coat.

“Thank you.”

Vaelrie watched the exchange.

“The queen intends to move her rooms,” she said. “She intends to appoint her own officers. She intends a great many things which will hurt a woman who has loved her since she was a child.”

“Yes.”

“What happens when Jaennis refuses?”

“I don’t decide that.”

“You have a talent for standing beside a decision until it becomes yours.”

Riarhin sat. “Say what you mean.”

“I mean that you come into a house with other people in it. You decide what cannot be allowed to continue. Then some of the people are dead and the rest are told it was necessary.”

Coryd’s needle stopped. Riarhin kept her eyes on Vaelrie.

“I have not come here to kill her.”

“Is that your answer?”

“No.”

“Then give me another.”

Riarhin looked down at her hands. There was a little crescent of dirt beneath one nail from moving the bedding. She began to pick it out and stopped.

“If Matylde asks me to leave, I leave. If she asks me to witness, I witness. If someone prevents her leaving a room, I would get her through the door.”

“And Jaennis?”

“I would leave her alive.”

“Would you?”

Riarhin felt anger rise with a speed she had not expected. It was the question she had asked herself, and she disliked having it asked by someone who could not know how diligently she had been trying to remain still.

“Yes,” she said.

Vaelrie let the word stand between them.

“Then remember you have said it.”

Coryd pulled the thread through the coat. The small scrape of it was audible.

Riarhin asked, “Do you think Deliane should have stayed?”

“No.”

“Do you think Matylde should have to—”

“I am not here to pass your examination.”

Riarhin closed her mouth. Vaelrie looked tired. There was no triumph in the way she had spoken, and Riarhin could neither refute nor forgive her for it.

They went together to the household meeting the following afternoon.

Jaennis received them in a bright room overlooking the gardens. She had dressed carefully. The colour of her gown brought warmth to her face, and she was wearing an old pin which Vaelrie noticed immediately. She went to her, bent and kissed her cheek. Jaennis caught her hand before she could move away.

“You’ve kept it,” Vaelrie said.

“Of course.”

“It used to catch.”

“You used to be impatient.”

Vaelrie’s thumb moved over the pin’s smooth head. “You never complained.”

“I am not complaining now.”

Jaennis tilted her face. Vaelrie kissed her mouth this time, one hand at the nape of her neck, and for a moment Riarhin had the uncomfortable sense of standing in a doorway neither woman had meant to leave open. She turned towards the clerk until paper rustled beneath his fingers. When she looked back, Vaelrie was still beside the chair. Jaennis had not released her hand.

“I wondered if you would come.”

“You asked me.”

“Yes.”

Several others were present: two women of the Fair Company, a household clerk, an older man who had managed Jaennis’s travelling arrangements for years. Riarhin and Solenne had come to clarify the written transfer of staff. The clerk had brought copies. He set them down and looked at Jaennis before opening the first.

Jaennis asked him to wait a moment.

“Vaelrie, will you speak to her?”

“To Matylde?”

“She thinks I object to her having her own people. I should like her to understand what this has felt like.”

Vaelrie withdrew her hand gently.

“You can tell her.”

“She won’t hear it from me now.”

The older man looked at the window. One of the women began smoothing a fold of her skirt. Riarhin could see that both had heard versions of this conversation before, though perhaps with different names.

“I won’t take it to her,” Vaelrie said.

Jaennis’s face changed.

“I am asking for help.”

“I know.”

“Have I become so difficult to love?”

Vaelrie took a breath. “I love you. I won’t carry this to a girl who is already afraid of making you unhappy.”

Jaennis looked away. Her mouth trembled. For a moment Riarhin wanted to intervene on her behalf, to soften what Vaelrie had said and give the room something easier to bear.

Then Jaennis nodded.

“Very well.”

Vaelrie remained beside her. After a little while Jaennis let her hand rest on Vaelrie’s again.

The clerk opened his papers.

The meeting was difficult and mostly ordinary. A maid had been included twice. A man no longer in service appeared on the wage list. Jaennis corrected both, then remembered that the man’s widow was still receiving his allowance and asked whether the new office meant to stop it. Solenne made a note and said she would find the original grant. The travelling officer needed authority to release a pair of horses which had been borrowed for Aluen’s road party; their driver was still waiting for the second animal’s shoe to be fixed.

“Let him wait under cover,” Jaennis said. “He’ll stand in the rain all day rather than appear troublesome.”

The officer went to arrange it.

Afterwards, in the corridor, Riarhin asked Vaelrie whether she would attend if the queen met Jaennis again.

“If I am asked.”

“She might listen to you.”

Vaelrie stopped walking.

“You heard what I said.”

“Yes.”

“And you thought you would ask the other way round?”

Riarhin felt her face grow hot. Vaelrie studied her for a moment and shook her head.

“You do it quickly.”

“I do.”

“Then try noticing sooner.”

She went back into Jaennis’s room. Riarhin remained in the passage until Solenne asked whether she was waiting for someone.

Chapter 33 The agreement that held

The proposal arrived the following morning in Jaennis’s own hand.

Riarhin saw only the copy Solenne brought to the guesthouse. The queen had asked that the practical arrangements be examined before she answered, and some of them involved passages Coryd had worked on. There were no accusations in the proposal. Jaennis accepted that the royal household required a single officer to give directions. Her travelling staff would send their requests through that office. She would surrender the unused escort forms and give the treasury the names of the people approached about the army guarantees. Deliane’s belongings would be released without another inventory in Jaennis’s rooms. The wages of anyone leaving would be paid on the ordinary day, without a personal application.

Solenne read the last provision twice.

“Can they do it?” Riarhin asked.

“The wages? Yes.”

“Then let them.”

“I intend to.”

The sharpness passed between them and subsided. Both had slept badly. Coryd took the copy, read the lines concerning the outer passage and said no building work would be needed. A guard at the turn could hear a call from the inner room without occupying the doorway. The door itself opened freely if nobody left a chair where it would catch.

“There is a request at the end,” Solenne said.

Riarhin had already seen it. Jaennis asked to retain the privilege of going to Matylde when the girl was distressed, without first making a formal application through officers. She could wait in an adjoining room. She did not ask to resume charge of the queen’s papers. She asked that their family relationship should not be treated as an audience between strangers.

Coryd put the sheet down. “Does Matylde want that?”

“She wants to discuss it.”

“Then there is no hinge I can mend for you.”

Solenne almost smiled. Riarhin looked at the fine writing at the bottom of the page. Jaennis had crossed out one phrase and written another above it. She had taken time over the request.

“Who decides she is distressed?”

“That is one of the questions.”

“And whether she wants her there?”

“That is another.”

Solenne held out her hand for the copy. Riarhin gave it back, uneasy at how readily she had assumed the questions had been left for her to find.

The meeting was held in the queen’s little dining room. No chairs had to be removed. There were six places, a fire and a window through which the ordinary service of the palace could be seen proceeding without anyone’s permission. Margery sat beside Matylde. Vaelrie had come at Jaennis’s request. Solenne stood until the girl told her to sit; Riarhin remained near the door because she had been summoned to answer about the passages, not to take a place at the table.

Jaennis was waiting when they arrived. She had brought Deliane’s small sewing case and the wage list. She set both before Solenne.

“The case belongs to her. I thought she would need it before the rest comes.”

“Thank you.”

“The blue cloth in the chest is hers too. It was bought from my account because the man would not wait for hers to be opened. I have marked it so that nobody asks her to pay twice.”

Solenne checked the mark. It was there. Riarhin thought of Deliane trying to remember whether she had bought her own comb and hoped somebody would tell her the cloth was settled without making her prove it again.

Then Jaennis spoke about Orven.

She did not excuse his grip on Deliane’s wrist. She said she had given him reason to think the departure ought to be delayed and had failed to make clear that Deliane might go despite her disappointment. Riarhin watched the girl’s face as she heard it. Margery leaned forward. Vaelrie breathed out.

“I wanted another evening,” Jaennis said. “I thought she would be glad, afterward, not to have left in anger. That was for me to want. It wasn’t an instruction he should have been left to interpret.”

“Will you say that to Deliane?” Matylde asked.

“If she wishes to hear me.”

“Will you write it?”

Jaennis hesitated, then nodded. “Yes.”

The clerk took a fresh sheet. Matylde told him the exact words mattered, and Jaennis repeated them without softening the reference to her own disappointment. Riarhin read the clerk’s account over his shoulder. Even with the words before her, she found herself checking what had been left out.

The ordinary arrangements took an hour. Jaennis named the servants who could transfer without losing their places in the sick fund, corrected an error in the wage list and surrendered two blank escort forms which had remained in a drawer. Solenne counted them and wrote a receipt. Nobody was pleased by the counting, but nobody objected.

The army guarantees were harder. The remount dealer would accept the treasury’s signature now that the first advance had arrived. The field owner had extended the meal contract. Two gentlemen approached by Jaennis wished their names kept from public reading; one had a brother among the northern claimants, and the other did not want every captain who knew him arriving with an account. Fayd had asked for the names to be entered in his office. The treasury wanted them entered in its own.

“Let both know,” Jaennis said. “And let neither publish them merely to show they know.”

Matylde agreed. The clerk wrote it down while Jaennis explained which man would mistake delay for insult and which would use an insult to excuse delay. Riarhin could see Solenne’s impatience ease. For once the older woman’s knowledge was arriving before the mistake.

They reached the last request with the fire burning low.

Matylde read it aloud. At the words about being strangers her voice caught. She began the sentence again, this time without looking at Jaennis.

“I don’t want that,” she said.

“Nor do I.”

“But I need to know who comes into my rooms.”

“You always shall.”

“Before they are there.”

Jaennis bowed her head. “Before.”

Margery put her hand flat on the table. “There are nights when she wakes and wants someone she knows. We shouldn’t make her send through three offices to find them.”

“No one is proposing three offices,” Solenne said.

“It feels like three when you’re frightened.”

The officer accepted the correction. Riarhin remembered standing outside a room after Na’ar’s death, knowing that no one she could call would be the person she wanted. She kept her attention on the door frame. The girl’s need was not an argument Riarhin had earned the right to win.

Matylde asked whether she could send directly for Jaennis.

“Of course,” Solenne said.

“And if she is asleep?”

“Wake me,” Jaennis said.

“If you’re ill?”

“I shall tell the woman at my door. She can fetch Margery.”

“Or ask me who else I want.”

Jaennis smiled, though something in the smile was effort. “Yes.”
