Vaelrie leaned back. She looked exhausted by the amount of care required to arrive at an answer people in an ordinary household might have given without witnesses. Riarhin wondered whether ordinary households were ever quite as ordinary as she remembered them from outside.

Solenne proposed a simple direction. Matylde could summon Jaennis by any servant. The servant would tell the officer at the queen’s door whom she had requested. Jaennis could come without a written application. If Matylde had not called, anyone concerned could send a question through the door. The queen’s answer would govern admission. Illness or immediate danger remained grounds for entering without waiting.

“Whose judgment of danger?” Jaennis asked.

“The officer’s, with the healer when there is time.”

“A girl can be in danger without a fever.”

Matylde moved her cup. “Then knock.”

Jaennis looked at her. The cup had left a pale ring on the wood; the girl put it back precisely inside it.

“I will answer,” she said.

“If you don’t?”

“Then they can send the healer.”

The agreement was read back. It allowed no uninvited presence in the adjoining room, which shared a door with Matylde’s bedchamber. Jaennis asked whether she might wait in the gallery instead. Solenne said anyone could wait in the gallery during its ordinary hours, but not require the queen’s servants to remain there with her overnight.

For a moment Jaennis looked as if she would withdraw the whole proposal. Margery reached towards her, stopped and folded her hands. Matylde was watching.

“The gallery, then,” Jaennis said.

The clerk finished the direction. Jaennis signed first. Her hand shook a little from the awkward height of the table. Matylde held the ink dish steady, then let go.

They ate afterward. The meal had been kept too long; the fish was dry, and the sauce had formed a skin. Nobody complained. Jaennis asked Matylde about a book she had been reading, and the girl answered at length, moving from the book to a question about the western road. Jaennis asked to see the map rather than sending for someone who could settle it. Margery smiled at Riarhin across the table.

Riarhin could not smile back at once. When she did, it felt like conceding something she had not meant to contest.

The new direction worked that afternoon.

Riarhin knew because she was in the gallery when the servant brought Matylde’s request. Jaennis had come to return a book and was speaking to Vaelrie by the window. The servant said the queen would like her to come and look at the repaired music box. Jaennis rose, asked whether she was expected immediately and went with him when he said yes. No one looked to Riarhin for an opinion.

Through the closed door came the uneven tune of something not entirely repaired. Jaennis laughed. Matylde said something too indistinct to hear, then laughed as well.

Vaelrie remained at the window.

“You might look less astonished.”

“I was listening.”

“Yes.”

Riarhin leaned against the stone and looked into the court. A cart had come for the first of the transferred servants’ boxes. The porter checked each name against the list and handed the boxes down without asking who had authorised their departure. One of them was Deliane’s. Riarhin recognised the worn corner.

The next thing she saw was the captain riding through the gate on a horse she had not seen before. His wife walked beside him with her book under her arm. They had come for the accepted guarantee. When they emerged from the treasury room, the captain stopped to read the page in the light, then passed it to his wife. She read it more slowly. He waited.

“Will that do?” Riarhin called down.

The woman looked up. “For leaving.”

“And the loan?”

“Still waiting.”

The captain turned his horse carefully in the crowded court. His wife stepped close to keep her skirt clear of the hoof and he reached down for her hand, not to lift her, only to hold it until the turn was made. They went out through the gate together.

Riarhin returned her attention to the window. Inside the queen’s room the music had stopped. She could hear the two women speaking at an ordinary volume. For the first time since arriving in Valora she allowed herself to imagine leaving without another confrontation.

It was a small, specific imagining. Her papers checked at the crossing, Coryd impatient with the delay, a road on which nobody knew which room she had stood outside the previous afternoon.

At supper Coryd said Sivelle had asked her to stay another month.

Riarhin looked up too quickly. Coryd saw and waited until she had put down her spoon.

“I said no.”

“Is she angry?”

“Yes.”

“Are you?”

“Yes.”

They ate a while. The cook had used more pepper than usual, and Riarhin could not decide whether to ask for water or leave the burning in her mouth. Coryd had brought the second copy of the repairs account. She set it between them, not for Riarhin to read but because her bag was wet.

“She paid everything,” she said. “Even the stair that didn’t take as long as I thought.”

“You could give some back.”

“No. I estimated badly in the other direction on the window. We settled it.”

Riarhin nodded. Coryd pulled the paper away from a spreading drop of water.

“Don’t tell me she’ll come round. She may decide she doesn’t want this. I don’t get to be the person who leaves and then decides how the person staying must feel about it.”

“I wasn’t going to tell you.”

“You were thinking something helpful.”

Riarhin laughed despite herself. “I was trying.”

“Stop. Tell me about the queen.”

She told her about the music box, the signed directions and the captain’s departure. Coryd listened, turned the spoon in her fingers and asked whether Matylde had seemed happy.

“Yes.”

“Then let yourself be glad for her.”

Riarhin drank the water and told her how the music box had missed every fourth note.

Late that night Solenne came to the lodging.

Coryd had already gone upstairs. Riarhin was by the kitchen fire, reading the same part of Ailsa’s letter she had read the night before. She folded it when the officer entered. Solenne saw the movement, asked whether she was interrupting and accepted yes without taking it as a dismissal.

“I need an account of the afternoon,” she said.

Riarhin stood. “What happened?”

“Nothing you can mend by putting on your sword.”

“I asked what happened.”

Solenne sat. Rain had darkened the shoulders of her coat. She unfastened the collar with fingers stiff from cold and said that Jaennis had returned to the gallery after its ordinary hours. One of Matylde’s women had told her the queen was wakeful. The woman had meant to ask advice about a warm drink. Jaennis had gone up herself.

“Was she admitted?”

“No. The guard knocked. Matylde said she didn’t want company.”

Riarhin sat slowly.

“Then?”

“She waited in the gallery. The guard reminded her of the hours. She said she was waiting for an answer from Margery.”

“Was she?”

“She had sent to her.”

The fire shifted. A stick fell against the iron with a dry click. Riarhin waited while Solenne rubbed her thumb along the edge of the table.

“Margery came. They spoke. Margery went in to the queen. Then Matylde asked that Jaennis be admitted.”

“That was allowed.”

“Yes.”

Solenne looked tired enough that Riarhin stopped searching her face for a verdict and listened to what she had actually said.

“Why are you here?”

“The queen says she had asked for Margery only. She knew Jaennis was outside, and that Margery would have to go back out and tell her she wasn’t wanted. She let her in rather than do that.”

“Has she told Jaennis?”

“Not yet.”

“Then she needs to.”

“She has asked for the household meeting tomorrow. With the directions read to everyone before it.”

Riarhin looked at her folded letter. She had imagined the road too soon.

Solenne asked what she had seen in the gallery earlier, whether Jaennis had entered only when called, whether any officer had helped establish an expectation that she could wait there. Riarhin gave the account. The officer wrote it herself, asked her to confirm two details and put the paper away.

“The woman who mentioned the queen was awake,” Riarhin said. “Is she in trouble?”

“No.”

“Will she know that?”

“I shall tell her.”

Riarhin nodded. Solenne remained seated. After a moment she asked whether there was more hot water. Riarhin fetched it. They drank without talking about the palace, and when the officer left, Riarhin waited until she heard Coryd turn over upstairs before unfolding the letter again.

Chapter 34 Instructions before the visit

Cedric was arrested in a house behind a saddler’s yard.

Riarhin learned this from Solenne at breakfast. The officer had come herself, with wet boots and an expression which suggested that she had not yet eaten. Coryd pushed the bread towards her without asking.

“He was preparing to leave,” Solenne said. “One bag packed. A horse promised for this morning.”

“Did he fight?”

“He tried the back stair. There were men below. Then he wanted to know whose authority they had.”

“What did they tell him?”

“The queen’s.”

Riarhin waited.

“He says he has served two reigns and will answer before a proper court. He is being given one.”

Coryd poured water into a cup. Solenne drank, put the cup down and added, “He asked whether you would be present.”

“What did you say?”

“Nothing. I wasn’t there.”

Riarhin looked at the bread. She had imagined seeing him brought in, imagined the face altered by years around the mark she had put upon it. In some versions he accused her. In others she found something precise to say which made all the people listening understand why she had done it.

Now he was in a guarded room somewhere beyond the market. He would eat, sleep and speak to a clerk. She would not be there to govern his account.

“His contacts?”

“The man taken at the yard and the clerk are held. The stableman has given his account. We have a name for the man who used the knife, but he has gone beyond the city. The old seal is recovered, the payment frozen. Nobody named in the inquiry is assigning your men.”

“Fayd?”

“Has given an account of his last employment of Cedric. We will check it.”

“Jaennis?”

“No evidence that she instructed him to kill you. Her arrangements for the confrontation are in the inquiry. They do not excuse his separate preparations.”

Riarhin nodded.

“Don’t make that sound disappointing,” Solenne said.

“It isn’t.”

“Good.”

Coryd broke the bread with unnecessary force. “Can we have one morning when somebody asks whether we’d like more butter?”

Solenne looked at her, then at the plate.

“Would you?”

“Yes.”

They ate for a few minutes without speaking of Cedric.

The woman who had mentioned the queen’s wakefulness was waiting outside Solenne’s office when Riarhin went to confirm the evening’s escort.

She had brought her keys. They lay in a little cloth on her lap, separated from the ring which usually held them. Riarhin saw the marks of the ring in the leather at her waist. She had worked there long enough to wear the surface smooth.

“Have they asked for those?” Riarhin said.

The woman shook her head.

“Then put them away.”

“I’d rather have them ready.”

Riarhin sat on the other end of the bench. She could remember seeing the woman bring a tray, close a curtain, take away a wet cloak without making its owner aware of the weather until it was gone. She had been one of the people through whom the palace remained habitable. Now she was looking at the keys as though an offence might be written on them.

Solenne came out with a clerk. She saw the cloth and stopped.

“You haven’t been dismissed.”

“No, my lady.”

“Has anyone said you will be?”

“No.”

“Then why are these here?”

The woman looked at Riarhin, who wished she had not given her an instruction before knowing the answer. She looked back at Solenne.

“Lady Jaennis asked who was with the queen. I told her. Then she asked whether the queen was asleep. I told her that too. I don’t know which question I’m not to answer.”

“Ordinary household information isn’t an offence.”

“But it brought her up.”

“Yes.”

“If I had said I didn’t know, it would have been a lie.”

Solenne dismissed the clerk for a moment and sat between them. The bench was too narrow. Riarhin rose and took the place beside the window instead.

“Tell me exactly what was said.”

The woman repeated the questions and her answers. Jaennis had not ordered her to fetch anyone. She had asked whether Matylde had eaten, whether the fire had been built up, whether Margery was with her. The servant had answered because answering such questions had been part of caring for Matylde since before she could read.

“She had the bitter drink last night,” the woman said. “She dislikes it. I thought her ladyship might tell me what else to give her.”

“Did the healer prescribe it?”

“He said it might settle her.”

“Then ask him.”

“He had gone home. I wasn’t going to fetch him from his bed because she disliked a cup.”

Solenne rubbed her forehead. Riarhin could see her trying to keep the instruction small enough that it would not make every ordinary kindness a matter for the guard office.

“Keep doing your work,” she said. “If the queen asks you to fetch someone, fetch them. If someone else asks whether she wants company, ask her before you invite them up.”

“I didn’t invite her.”

“I know.”

The woman began gathering the keys, then stopped. “Will the queen know that?”

Solenne opened the inner door. Matylde was standing just beyond it with a book held against her chest. She must have heard some of the conversation. Her hair had been plaited hastily; a loose strand clung to her cheek.

“I know,” she said.

The woman half rose. Matylde asked her to remain seated. She came into the passage, saw the keys and looked away from them as if they embarrassed her.

“I don’t want you to go.”

The woman’s mouth trembled. She held it steady with an effort which made the queen’s face change.

“I wasn’t going to send you away.”

“No, Majesty.”

“I mean it.”

“Yes.”

The girl looked towards Solenne. Riarhin watched without trying to help. She had once thought that if she said something plainly enough, the person facing her would stop being afraid. The servant began threading the keys onto the ring. Her fingers were clumsy with relief.

Matylde waited until she finished.

“Could you leave water beside the bed tonight? Without the drink in it.”

“Of course.”

“And if I don’t sleep, I don’t sleep.”

“I could sit outside.”

“No. Go to bed when your turn ends. The guard is there.”

The woman nodded. This time the answer reached her eyes. She rose, put the ring back at her waist and went down the passage. Matylde watched until the sound of the keys had faded.

“I didn’t know she’d brought them,” Solenne said.

“I didn’t know she’d think she had to.”

The queen went back into the office. She left the door open. Riarhin remained by the window until Solenne asked her to come in; the question about the evening escort was still waiting.

Margery arrived while the permission was being checked. She had come from the queen’s rooms carrying the cup which had held the bitter drink. She put it on the table with an apology for bringing it so far, then stood behind Matylde’s chair.

“I have spoken to her,” she said.

Matylde looked up.

“She says she was waiting to know you were settled. She doesn’t think she asked you to admit her.”

“She didn’t.”

“She says you sent.”

“I did.”

Margery put a hand on the chair back. She looked at Solenne, then at Riarhin, and Riarhin prepared to leave. Matylde shook her head before she moved.

“I want them to hear what I said.”

Margery’s hand tightened.

“I asked for you,” the girl said. “I wanted you to stay. You kept looking at the door.”

“She was tired. Her knee—”

“I know.”

Margery stopped. The words had come too quickly, and she heard them. Matylde turned the book over in her lap.

“I knew she was waiting. I knew you’d have to tell her I didn’t want her. I said she could come because I wanted you to stop worrying about it.”

Margery drew the other chair nearer and sat. She did not take the girl’s hand.

“I should have stayed with you.”

“You did. You both did. For an hour.”

There was anger in it now, and relief at being angry. Riarhin heard the voice of someone kept awake by people trying to help her sleep. Margery accepted the correction with a nod.

“I should have stopped looking at the door.”

Matylde let the book lie still.

“Can you tell her that I meant no before I meant yes?”

Margery looked towards the cup. “I can.”

“No. I should tell her.”

“You needn’t do it alone.”

“I know.”

Solenne asked whether the girl still wished the directions read publicly. Matylde said yes. The households needed to hear the same words, including the words about earned wages and being free to leave. Jaennis’s letter to Deliane could go with the sewing case. It need not wait for the meeting.

Then she asked whether the permission for Riarhin’s sword had been prepared. Riarhin had not known there would be one. She stood very still while Solenne said it would be brought with the rest of the evening instructions.

Margery looked at her. For a moment Riarhin saw her own bloody sleeve in that look, years older than the shallow cut beneath her cuff. She could not promise anyone that the evening would remain gentle. Matylde closed the book and asked Solenne to finish reading the conditions.

The queen’s orders were read that afternoon.

Riarhin stood among the witnesses near the back of the chamber. Her coat had been repaired; the seam no longer rubbed. She wore her hair plainly, her hands visible, and did not turn when somebody behind her whispered the old false name. Beside the clerk’s table stood the sister of the dead guard. She had not brought the glove today. Her hands were clasped so tightly that one thumb had gone white.

Matylde took charge of the royal household through Solenne’s office. Officers receiving contradictory instructions were to seek that office’s answer, in writing if the matter permitted delay. No private promise could authorise detention, obstruct departure from service or prevent attendance upon the queen. Wages earned were to be paid whether a person chose to remain with Jaennis or transfer to the new establishment. Adult members of Jaennis’s household could stay; the honour and support of that household were not withdrawn.

The directions settled at the financial meeting were read next. Household introductions could no longer assign audiences with the queen’s protected guests. Changes to escorts required Solenne’s office. Offers of guarantees and recommendations for appointments would be entered before they were acted upon; Jaennis could recommend, but could no longer conclude those arrangements in advance of the queen’s decision. The limited inspection of Tessart’s associated guarantees was to report in six weeks.

Fayd listened to the last provision with his hands folded. It named the treasury clerk alongside his own. Riarhin saw him turn towards the man when the names were read. The man did not return the look.

Then came the rule about the queen’s rooms.

No person was to be admitted to her private audience, papers or bedchamber without her permission or a specified emergency. Rank did not supply the missing permission.

Matylde read that paragraph herself. Her voice was clear until the last sentence. She had to begin it again.

Jaennis sat in the chair with the raised cushion. She listened without interrupting. When the clerk had finished, she asked whether she might speak with Matylde that evening.

“Yes,” the queen said.

“Alone?”

“No.”

The word made a space in the room.

Jaennis lowered her eyes. “Then with whomever you choose.”

After the chamber emptied, Riarhin was called into the smaller room behind it. Solenne was there with the officer who had escorted Deliane, two other guards and a clerk. Coryd had been asked to attend because she knew the work being done in the outer passage. Deliane had sent word that she wished to be present for the settlement of her departure. Vaelrie would come if Jaennis wanted her; Jaennis had said she did.

Matylde asked Riarhin to keep the way out open.

Riarhin listened to the whole instruction before speaking. She was to act under Solenne. She was not to question Jaennis, search her rooms, remove anyone who wished to stay or prevent Jaennis leaving unless ordered by the queen’s officer. The guards would carry their usual weapons. Riarhin could wear Remorse, under a separate written permission which ended when the meeting ended.

“You understand why I dislike that,” Matylde said.

“Yes.”

“I dislike needing it more.”

Riarhin looked at her. “You could have the meeting in this room.”

“She asked for her own.”

“You can refuse.”

“I know.”

“Then send an officer now. Keep anyone from entering or leaving until you’re there. Take away the household weapons. Give her the order through a closed door if you have to.”

Solenne looked at Riarhin. Coryd leaned back against the wall.

Matylde asked, “Am I putting her under arrest?”

“No.”

“Then why would I do that?”

“To make certain she cannot—”

Riarhin stopped. She had put guards at the doors and taken the household’s weapons; the reason could be supplied afterwards. When she looked up, Coryd had stepped away from the wall.

Matylde waited.

“To make it safer,” Riarhin finished.

“For me.”

“Yes.”

“And less safe for anyone who thinks I can do that because I am frightened.”

The sentence sounded learned. Riarhin looked at Solenne. The officer’s expression did not tell her where the girl had found it.

“I will not arrest her before I know what she does,” Matylde said. “You may tell me that is foolish.”

“It leaves you at risk.”

“I asked you to keep the door open.”

Riarhin bowed her head. “I will.”

Coryd was offered payment for keeping the outer passage clear and checking the workmen’s temporary boards. She asked when the task ended. The clerk said when the queen had returned to her own rooms and the officer released her. She required the instruction to name that officer. He wrote Solenne’s name.

Coryd checked the hour on her copy of the instruction. They walked together to collect Remorse. The custodian checked the tie Riarhin had made at the scabbard mouth, compared the permission with the register and laid the sword across the counter.

Riarhin took it in her left hand. She felt relief, then disliked the relief. Matylde was going into the room of someone who had kept her warm when both her parents were dead. Riarhin was putting on the sword with which she had killed them.

She fastened the belt and asked the custodian where she should return it afterwards.

Chapter 35 The night she remembers

Someone had put another chair beside the door.

Riarhin saw it as soon as she entered. A little chair for a woman who had once waited outside Jaennis’s rooms while her son was ill. The woman sat in it now with a basket on her knees. Riarhin recognised her from supper. She did sewing for the household, and the work in the basket was real: an unfinished cuff, a roll of pale thread, a pair of shears wrapped in cloth.

The chair narrowed the way out.

Riarhin touched Solenne’s sleeve and looked at it. Solenne asked the woman to move nearer the window. The woman rose at once, apologising. Jaennis heard and turned from the hearth.

“There’s no need to put her in the cold. Move the little table instead.”

A servant moved the table. The chair remained by the door, but there was space beside it now. Solenne looked at Riarhin. Riarhin nodded. They had a passage.

The room smelled of apple peel and hot wine. On the hearth stood a shallow pan in which the peel was drying. Riarhin could remember that scent from another winter, another house, though she could not place it at first. Ailsa had dried things near the fire until they grew too crisp, insisting each time that she would remember sooner. Riarhin moved away from the hearth.

Deliane sat with her bound arm on a cushion. She had chosen a chair near the window and was talking to Vaelrie about the new house’s curtains. Her voice was tired but steady. Orven stood near the inner door, without his keys. Solenne had permitted him to attend under guard to identify household instructions and the passages they governed. He was to leave with the guard afterwards. Lett had brought him clean clothes; the collar sat awkwardly where it had been folded damp.

There were others whom Riarhin had not expected. A grey-haired man beside the bookcase, two women she had seen carrying Jaennis’s correspondence, a household guard off duty with his cap beneath one arm. They all had reasons to be there. The grey-haired man had brought a travelling account for signature. One woman needed an answer before the morning’s post. The other had come to return a borrowed book and stayed because Jaennis had asked whether she had eaten.

Riarhin named the unexpected people quietly to Solenne.

“Shall I ask them to leave?”

“Ask Her Majesty.”

Matylde entered before Solenne could do so. She had changed her dress. This one was a dark blue which made the narrowness of her wrists noticeable when she drew off her gloves. She looked round the room and smiled uncertainly at the familiar faces.

Jaennis went to her.

“Your hands are cold.”

“They’re always cold in that passage.”

“I’ve told them about the draught. Come nearer the fire.”

Matylde allowed herself to be moved two steps. Then she stopped and turned to Solenne.

“Were we expecting everyone?”

Jaennis answered. “Only people you know.”

“I know many people.”

The reply came out sharply. She coloured, and Jaennis’s expression softened with a generosity which made Riarhin’s chest tighten.

“You’re tired. Sit down.”

Solenne said, “The queen asked for a small meeting.”

“Of course. Those who have finished their business may go.”

Nobody moved immediately. The woman with the book glanced at Jaennis, who asked whether she had eaten the bread sent up for her. She had not. Jaennis told her to wrap it, then, and take it home. The grey-haired man held his account a little higher. The sewing woman remained because she had not been addressed.

The sewing woman rose last. Her basket caught on the arm of her chair, spilling the roll of thread onto the rug. It unrolled towards the hearth.

“I’ll have that,” she said, and went after it.

The thread had run beneath Jaennis’s chair. Riarhin watched the woman reach down, hesitate and withdraw her hand.

“Leave it,” Jaennis told her. “I’ll keep it safe.”

The woman smiled with an embarrassment too deep for the small accident. “You always do.”

She lifted her basket and moved towards the door. Riarhin stepped aside, and the woman stopped beside her. Her glance went back to the roll beneath the chair. Riarhin could see the cut end resting on the rug; it ought to have been within easy reach.

“Do you need it?” she asked.

“For tomorrow’s work.”

Riarhin bent to retrieve it. The woman caught her sleeve.

“No. Don’t trouble her.”

Jaennis was already speaking to Matylde. She had not noticed them, or gave no sign of noticing. Riarhin straightened. The woman went out with her basket held against her body.

The little roll remained beneath the chair. Riarhin found herself trying to remember whether it had been blue when it fell. In the lamplight it was grey. She looked away and placed her heel against the edge of the threshold, where the wood was firm.

Matylde sat. Solenne took the chair to her right. Riarhin remained beside the door.

Jaennis handed the queen a cup. Matylde sniffed it.

“No wine?”

“You’ve hardly had your supper.”

“I had supper.”

“Then someone has neglected to tell me.”

Matylde looked down into the cup. Jaennis sat opposite her, close enough that there was no need to raise a voice.

“I wanted us to speak without everybody explaining us to each other.”

“We can,” Matylde said.

“Can we?”

The question passed through the room. Riarhin felt the people near her become attentive. Jaennis did not look at them; she looked only at the girl.

“You’ve put so many rules between us.”

“They are rules for everyone.”

“Then everyone has come between us.”

Matylde held the cup in both hands.

Solenne opened the folder on her knee. “The household transfers can be concluded tonight. The question of access—”

“I am not speaking to the folder.”

The officer stopped.

Jaennis’s voice had not risen. She looked at Solenne in distress.

“You have always been careful with her. I know that. I have trusted you with more than anyone in this room understands. Must you make this an office before she has even heard me?”

Solenne looked at Matylde.

“I would like to hear,” the queen said.

Riarhin shifted her weight. Her sword was against her right hip, out of the way of the door. She had checked it twice since entering. Now she wanted to unfasten the belt and put it outside. The sight of it must be causing needless pain. She had brought enough pain into this family.

Her fingers reached the buckle.

She stopped with her thumb beneath the leather.

Across the room Deliane was lifting her cushion, preparing to stand. Jaennis turned towards her.

“Does it hurt?”

“Yes.”

“Put it higher. You’ve let the hand hang.”

Deliane adjusted the cushion. She sat again. A moment later she looked at the door as if she did not quite understand why she remained on the same side of the room.

Riarhin took her hand off the buckle.

Jaennis spoke about the first winter.

Not the killings themselves. She had not been at the nursery door. She spoke of what came afterwards: the mornings when Matylde refused to put on the dress laid out for her because it had been chosen by somebody who did not know which sleeve scratched; the terror of waking in a room where the expected voice did not answer; the effort required to persuade a child to eat when she believed that eating might allow the day to become ordinary.

Riarhin knew some of these things from Margery’s letters. Others were new. She looked at the queen’s hands around the cup and could no longer pretend she had imagined the whole consequence when she went up the linen stair.

“You remember the storm?” Jaennis asked.

Matylde nodded.

“You came to my room in your nightclothes. You wouldn’t let the maid fetch your slippers. You thought if she went away she might not come back.”

“I remember.”

“You told me I mustn’t leave either.”

Matylde’s mouth moved. She seemed about to deny something, then swallowed.

“And I stayed. I sat until the light came, though you had fallen asleep long before.”

“Yes.”

“You said we would keep each other company.”

“I was little.”

“I know.”

“I wanted somebody to stay.”

Jaennis reached for her hand. Matylde let her take it.

“I did stay.”

The sewing woman wiped her cheek with the back of one finger. Orven was staring at the floor. The off-duty guard had put his cap on the table and stood with both hands clasped in front of him.

Riarhin felt the room drawing close around the two women. The fire was not louder. The lamps did not change. Yet she could hear every small movement of Matylde’s sleeve against the chair, and the sounds outside seemed to come from a great distance. A latch clicked in the corridor. She had to turn her head deliberately to place it.

Solenne said, “Her Majesty hasn’t asked you to stop loving her.”

Jaennis looked at her. “I know what she has asked. I have read it.”

The folder on Solenne’s knee slipped. She caught it, then began to explain the order again, more gently this time. Riarhin heard her offering adjustments she had not been authorised to offer: notice before a visit rather than permission; a conversation with Jaennis before disputed appointments took effect.

Matylde looked at the officer.

“That isn’t what it says.”

Solenne stopped.

“No, Majesty.”

Jaennis’s hand remained around Matylde’s. “You needn’t have people tell me I may knock at your door. I’ve sat outside it when you were ill. I’ve sent away men who wished to wake you because their business seemed more important than your sleep. You trusted me to know.”

“I did.”

“Then what has changed?”

Matylde looked towards Riarhin.

The older woman’s gaze followed hers. Riarhin wished the girl had looked anywhere else.

“You can be safe here,” Jaennis said to her. “You have been. I have made certain of it, despite what it costs people to see you walking through these rooms.”

“Yes.”

“Then help me keep her safe.”

Riarhin could answer that. It would be easy. She could assure the woman that none of this removed her place, persuade Matylde to offer the reassurance which would let everybody breathe. There would be time for rules tomorrow. Deliane could still take her room. Solenne could amend the wording. A few sentences would end this misery.

She took one step towards them.

The scabbard touched the chair by the door.

Riarhin looked down at it. The sewing woman moved her basket out of the way and apologised.

She stepped back.

“I am here to keep the passage open,” she said.

Jaennis looked hurt. Riarhin had been expecting anger, and the hurt nearly moved her forward again.

“Is that all you have brought us?”

Riarhin gripped the seam of her coat. The knot Coryd had removed was gone; there was only smooth cloth beneath her thumb.

“For tonight.”

Chapter 36 The unfinished conversation

Deliane stood.

Her cushion fell to the floor. She bent for it automatically and cried out when the movement jarred her arm. Vaelrie picked it up and put it on the empty chair.

“I have to go,” Deliane said.

Jaennis turned, still holding Matylde’s hand.

“We have hardly spoken.”

“We spoke this afternoon.”

“You were in pain.”

“I am in pain now.”

“Then sit down.”

Deliane remained standing. Her good hand had closed over the cloth sling at her shoulder. She looked towards the door, then towards Jaennis, then at Vaelrie as if seeking something she had lost between them.

“I said I would come before I left,” she said. “I’ve come.”

“My dear—”

“I’ve come.”

Riarhin heard the breathing change. Deliane tried to take air and got only a shallow, caught breath. Orven lifted his head. The healer was not in the room. Riarhin had agreed to that: there had seemed no reason to fill a private conversation with people anticipating injury.

Jaennis released Matylde and rose.

“Look what this is doing to you.”

Deliane’s lips parted. She shook her head.

“Sit. Please. We can speak when you are easier.”

The younger woman’s knees bent. She steadied herself against the chair back. Riarhin started towards her and stopped because the way between Matylde and the door would then be empty.

Vaelrie took Deliane’s elbow, the uninjured one.

“Do you want to leave?”

Deliane nodded.

“Then lean on me.”

Jaennis looked at Vaelrie. “She cannot breathe.”

“I can see.”

“Help me.”

“I am helping her.”

For a moment Riarhin thought Jaennis would strike the words out of the room. She stood so still that even the folds of her dress seemed fixed. Then her face crumpled and she sat heavily, one hand catching at the arm of the chair.

Matylde was beside her at once.

“What is it?”

“Nothing.”

“Your knee?”

“No. Sit with me a moment.”

The girl crouched, then looked back at Solenne. The officer had stood. Her folder lay on the chair, forgotten.

“We were going,” Matylde said.

“You have only just come.”

“I said we would speak. We have spoken.”

“Have we? You read me what other people have written and now you will leave before I can answer?”

“I wrote some of it.”

“That is what hurts.”

Matylde closed her eyes. Riarhin saw her reach for the old answer, the one she had heard in corridors and after supper, offered before anyone asked.

I didn’t mean—

The girl opened her eyes again.

“I am going to my rooms.”

Jaennis’s hand moved towards her. Matylde took it and pressed it once between both her own hands. Then she let go.

Riarhin stepped to the side of the doorway.

Orven had moved into it.

He did not appear to know how he had got there. He looked from Deliane to Matylde, then at Jaennis. The guard assigned to him said his name. Orven did not answer.

“Let them finish,” he said. “Just let them finish speaking.”

Solenne came towards him. “Move.”

“She asked me—”

“You are here under my authority.”

He rubbed his hands against his coat. Without the keys they seemed to have nothing to hold. Riarhin could have put him against the wall as she had before. She waited one breath, then another, counting Deliane’s breaths between them.

Deliane lifted her injured hand as far as the sling allowed.

“Orven.”

He looked at it.

The linen was clean. The fingers beyond it were swollen, their colour wrong. She could not point at him. She held the hand between them without speaking again.

Orven stepped back.

The off-duty guard moved at the same time.

“Wait,” he said. “You can’t take her out with that woman.”

He meant Riarhin. His hand went beneath his coat. She saw the movement, the angle of the elbow, the little hitch as his fingers found the weapon too quickly and caught on the cloth.

She had enough time to kill him.

Instead she turned the chair by the door into his knees.

The sewing woman cried out. Her basket fell. The guard struck the chair, stumbled and drew a short blade as he tried to recover. Riarhin was already on his right side. She caught his wrist against the doorpost, close enough that the blade passed outside her sleeve, and put her weight through her hip.

He was strong. His shoulder drove into her and she felt the old pain along her ribs. The point scraped stone. She had Remorse half drawn, then stopped pulling; there was no space to use the edge without someone moving into it. She forced the hilt between his forearm and the post, using the guard of the sword to keep his fist turned away.

“Drop it.”

He tried to strike her with his free hand. She took the blow on her shoulder. He was saying something, a woman’s name perhaps, and she could not distinguish it from the voices behind them.

“Riarhin,” Solenne said.

“I have him. Go.”

The guard twisted towards the queen. Riarhin pressed harder. The little blade fell. She kicked it beyond his reach and held him against the post with both hands, her sword trapped safely between their bodies.

Matylde passed behind her. Riarhin could feel the girl’s sleeve brush her back. Then Deliane, supported by Vaelrie; then the officer who took the guard’s left arm and allowed Riarhin to release some of her weight.

Inside, Jaennis was calling Matylde’s name.

Not loudly at first. The voice was the one used to call a child back for the thing she had forgotten. Matylde stopped in the corridor.

Riarhin saw Solenne stop with her.

The sewing woman had knelt among the fallen things, gathering them without looking up. The grey-haired man stood beside Jaennis’s chair. He was telling her that there had been no need for this, none at all, and his agreement seemed to hurt her more.

“Matylde.”

The girl turned halfway.

Riarhin wanted to say that she must keep walking. She wanted to put a hand on the back of her neck and steer her through the outer door as one moved somebody out of a burning room. The desire came with an old confidence. She knew where the passage led. She knew which doors could be shut.

She kept both hands on the guard.

Solenne said, “Your Majesty, your room is ready.”

Matylde looked at her. “Is she hurt?”

“No one has struck her.”

“That wasn’t what I asked.”

Solenne swallowed. “I know.”

Jaennis stood. The grey-haired man tried to help her, but she moved past him and came as far as the inner doorway. There was no transformation in her, nothing Riarhin could afterwards describe as a sign. She was an ageing woman in a good dress, distressed before people who loved her, and the air seemed to draw towards the sound of her breathing.

“You promised you would not leave me like this.”

Matylde began to cry.

She did it angrily, wiping her face with the heel of her hand. Her other hand remained at her side, fingers opening and closing. Riarhin heard a sound in her own throat and held it there.

“I don’t know how else to leave,” the girl said.

Jaennis stared at her.

Matylde turned and walked towards the outer passage.

Coryd was there with one of the royal guards. The temporary boards had shifted beneath the weight of people stopping on them. She had her foot planted on a raised end and was holding a workman’s lantern low enough to show the gap.

“Left side,” she said. “One at a time.”

Solenne went first. Matylde followed. Deliane had to turn sideways to keep her arm from touching the doorframe; Vaelrie steadied her. Coryd waited until they had passed, then looked back at Riarhin.

The officer had taken the household guard’s weapon and was holding his arm. Orven stood beneath the arch with his head bowed. Riarhin let the sword slide home and stepped away from the man she had restrained.

Jaennis looked directly at her.

“You brought this to her.”

Riarhin could have answered with the room, the promises, the injured wrist. She could have spoken of the child whose parents she had killed and every consequence she had not been there to carry. For a moment she felt all the answers trying to arrange themselves into something she could bear to say.

Coryd called her name.

Riarhin went through the door.

In the passage beyond, the smell of apple peel disappeared. She stopped so suddenly that Coryd nearly walked into her. Her hands were shaking. She pressed them against the front of her coat and found that this made the shaking more visible.

Coryd took the lantern in her other hand.

“Walk,” she said quietly. “You can stop at the next turn.”

They walked.

At the next turn Solenne had paused to check the people behind her. Matylde was holding Deliane’s uninjured hand. Neither was speaking. Vaelrie stood apart from them with her face turned towards the passage they had left.

“I am going back,” she said.

Solenne looked at her.

“Not to take anyone else with me.”

The officer nodded. Vaelrie retraced her steps. Riarhin watched her go until the turn hid her.

Then Solenne asked for the folder she had left on Jaennis’s chair.

Nobody had it.

For a moment they all stared at one another. The queen gave a short laugh which broke halfway through. Solenne closed her eyes, opened them and sent a guard for the clerk’s copy.

“The orders have already gone,” she told Matylde.

The girl nodded. She was still crying.

Part VI What remains in the rooms

Chapter 37 The way she leaves

The queen’s new room had no slippers in it.

It had a bed, a fire, fresh water and two doors whose keys had been accounted for. Solenne had thought of the adjoining servants’ room and the window giving onto the covered walk. Nobody had thought of slippers.

Matylde stood on the rug with her shoes in her hand.

“They’re in the other room.”

“I’ll send someone,” Solenne said.

“I could go.”

The officer looked at her.

“I know. I’m not going. I only meant they’re close.”

Riarhin remained by the table. Her shoulder had begun to stiffen where the guard had struck it. The effort of remaining upright seemed disproportionate to the small injury. She could still hear Jaennis saying that she had brought this to them. When a servant asked whether she wanted water, she nearly asked the woman to go back and see whether Jaennis had any.

Matylde sat on the edge of the bed. She looked smaller there, her feet not quite flat on the floor. The new coverlet had been chosen by somebody else. She ran a finger over its raised pattern and caught a loose thread.

“Don’t pull it,” Riarhin said.

The girl looked up.

Riarhin felt foolish. “It may unravel.”

Matylde let the thread go.

For a while nobody spoke. The servant came with slippers belonging to a woman of the household. They were too large. Matylde put them on and walked to the fire, making a faint dragging sound which would have been funny on another night.

“Is Deliane all right?”

“She is with the healer,” Solenne said.

“Was it the promise?”

“We don’t know everything it was.”

“Don’t do that. I was there.”

Solenne bowed her head. “There was pressure beyond what words should have done. We will take accounts while people remember. We must let them describe it differently if it felt different to them.”

Matylde looked at Riarhin.

“Did you feel it?”

“Yes.”

“What did you want to do?”

“Make you stay long enough to reassure her.”

The girl’s face changed. “You too.”

“Yes.”

“But you didn’t.”

“No.”

“Why?”

Riarhin rubbed the sore place beneath her coat. She could offer a description of the doorway, the chair, the sword catching. It would sound like a trick another person could use.

“I remembered the job you gave me.”

Matylde turned back to the fire.

The council met in the outer room while she rested. Riarhin was asked to wait because her account might be needed. Coryd brought her a bowl of something hot and told her to stop pressing her shoulder; if she wished it to hurt all night, there were quicker ways.

Through the open door Riarhin could see the councillors arriving in various stages of evening dress. One had put on his good coat over a shirt marked by supper. Another complained quietly that the working should have been anticipated, as if anticipation alone would have settled who could prevent it and by what authority.

The order they produced required Jaennis to remain within her apartments pending inquiry into the obstruction of royal officers and the use of coercive workings. Her attendants were permitted to come and go under the guard’s supervision. No one proposed chains. Food, medicine and a chosen companion were to be admitted. She was to have the written grounds and a chance to answer before the council the next day.

The officer taking the order wanted to know which passages counted as her apartments.

Solenne unfolded a plan.

Riarhin saw the covered way at once.

“Send someone there now,” she said.

Solenne was already pointing to it. A guard left at a run.

They were late.

The message came back while Matylde was trying to eat broth. She had asked Riarhin to remain because she did not want another unfamiliar person in the room, then told her not to watch. Riarhin sat facing the window and listened to the spoon touch the bowl.

Jaennis was gone. Vaelrie had left her with the travelling officer and a maid, believing that she was preparing for bed. A chair had been brought to the inner passage to spare her knees. The lower servants knew the driver and had seen Aluen’s repaired cases go the same way for days. He had been waiting beneath the household awning while a horse was shod. Nobody stopped him taking up another passenger.

The first guard sent to the covered way had found the chair folded against the wall. The driver had gone through the south gate before the order closing it arrived.

Matylde set down her spoon.

“She knew.”

Solenne did not ask what she meant.

“She knew you would ask her to stay.”

“She may have expected it.”

“And everyone helped her.”

“We know of two people who arranged it. Others may not have known whom they were carrying.”

Riarhin had risen. She had not realised she meant to rise until she was standing beside the table. She knew the road out, the places a carriage might stop and the distance a rider could make while a household argued over orders.

Matylde looked at her.

“Stay.”

Riarhin stood still.

“Let Solenne send the men whose work it is.”

“I wasn’t—”

“You were.”

Riarhin sat down.

Solenne gave instructions in the outer room. They were sensible instructions: riders to the southern road, notices bearing an actual description rather than a title alone, the driver’s name to the gate officers, no attack on a carriage in which the queen’s dowager might be travelling. The horses were sent for before the copies were finished. A clerk ran after the first rider with the second sheet.

Matylde listened to all of it.

When Solenne returned, the girl asked whether a letter might make Jaennis come back.

“What would you wish to say?”

“That I didn’t want her gone.”

“You can say that.”

“Would she come?”

“I don’t know.”

Matylde looked at Riarhin, angry at the room for failing to produce a better answer.

“Would you?”

“If I were her?”

“Yes.”

“I don’t know how to be her.”

The girl turned away. Riarhin let her. Outside, hooves struck the court stones, one horse impatient with the time being taken to mount.

Eventually Matylde took the bowl again. The broth had cooled. She ate three spoonfuls, then asked the servant to warm it.

By dawn the riders had found Aluen’s carriage at a posting inn. Jaennis was no longer in it. Aluen had gone on with her own people and said truthfully that Jaennis had asked to be put down at a junction before midnight. She had been met there by a smaller vehicle. The innkeeper remembered two women, or one woman and a youth with a hood drawn up. The horses had been changed. A bill had been paid in coin.

Solenne sent the account to the council. Matylde read it sitting upright against the pillows, the borrowed slippers abandoned beneath the chair.

“Keep looking,” she said.

Then she lay down, turned her face to the wall and asked them to leave the room.

Chapter 38 The people still waiting

The woman at the wage desk began crying over a name.

It was her husband’s name, entered on the list with the wrong house beside it. She had brought the paper which showed where he worked. The clerk agreed that it showed where he worked. He could not pay from the other household’s account until someone corrected the instruction.

“My rent is due,” she said.

“I understand.”

“No, you don’t. You keep saying Thursday.”

Riarhin stood behind her holding the receipt for Deliane’s borrowed pots. Coryd had sent her with it because she was going past the office and could perform this errand without becoming responsible for a second one.

The woman put both hands on the counter.

“Lady Jaennis would have sent someone.”

The clerk’s face tightened. “Lady Jaennis is not here.”

“I noticed.”

Riarhin waited until the woman stepped back. Then she asked whom the clerk needed to correct the instruction. He gave a name. She asked where that person was. He said the lower office. She asked whether a messenger had gone.

“Yes,” he said, with understandable irritation. “I have sent twice.”

“Then I won’t make it three.”

The woman looked at her. Recognition came slowly and did not improve her temper.

“Of course you’re paid.”

Riarhin held out the receipt. “I’m returning a receipt.”

The clerk took it, checked the amount and placed it in a tray. He seemed relieved to have completed something.

Riarhin found Solenne in the lower office speaking to the man whose name had been given. He had seven instructions to correct and a boy waiting to carry them up. He was working through them in order. The woman’s husband was sixth.

Riarhin said, “She’s crying upstairs.”

Solenne looked tired enough to be ill.

“I know.”

“Can you—”

“Riarhin.”

She stopped.

The man at the desk crossed out a line, wrote another and sanded it. Solenne took the first four completed sheets and gave them to the boy.

“Take these now. Come back for the rest. Tell them we are sending them as they are finished.”

The boy left. Riarhin leaned against the wall. She wanted something larger to be required of her. She remained until the sixth sheet was ready.

The woman was still upstairs when it arrived. She counted the coin twice, put it away and went without thanking anyone. The clerk rubbed his eyes.

The sewing woman from Jaennis’s room sat on a bench beyond the wage desk. Riarhin recognised the basket before the face and was annoyed with herself for it. A boy sat beside her with his cap held between his knees.

“Did you get your thread?” Riarhin asked.

The woman looked up. “No. They’re examining the rooms.”

“Can you get another?”

“I have another. That isn’t why I’m here.”

She shifted the basket to show the paper beneath it. Her son had been learning household work without a formal place. Jaennis had wanted him kept near while he recovered from a fever. He was well now. Nobody could say which officer ought to decide what happened to him.

The boy studied Riarhin’s boots. “I can work.”

His mother began to explain which kinds of work he ought not to do. He interrupted her, then apologised, then interrupted again. He wanted a place with the horses. She wanted him indoors where somebody knew when he was ill.

“Lady Jaennis understood,” the woman told Riarhin.

The boy pulled at his cap.

Riarhin did not ask what had been understood. The mother turned her paper over, looking for an answer on the back. It was blank.

“I should have gone to her before this,” she said. “She’d have put his name somewhere.”

A clerk came out and called another woman. The boy stood instinctively, saw it was not them and sat again. His mother put her hand over his knee. He let it remain for a moment, then moved his leg away.

Riarhin told them where the appointment clerk was. They already knew. They were waiting for him to return from the council.

She left them on the bench. Halfway down the stair she remembered the roll beneath the chair and the ease with which she could have picked it up. The cuff was still unfinished in the woman’s lap.

At the guard office she asked that it be returned when the room had been examined. She described the roll, gave the woman’s seat in the waiting hall and left before anyone could ask whether she vouched for the son.

Madra was packing when Riarhin brought back the shawl she had lent Coryd.

The room looked larger with the clothes taken down. Sun had faded the wall around the hanging tapestry; its old shape remained behind the bed in a darker rectangle. A maid folded dresses into a chest while Madra sat on the floor beside an open crate of pears. Each pear had been wrapped separately. She was unwrapping them and laying them in rows on a towel.

“Don’t step there,” she said.

Riarhin moved her foot. One had rolled beneath the chair. She picked it up and set it with the others.

“Are these for the journey?”

“They were for Jaennis. Adelis sent them ahead of a letter. Her steward grows them against a warm wall.”

There was a note attached to the crate, addressed to the household. The fruit was to be eaten soon. Adelis had remembered Jaennis saying she disliked the hard varieties served at court. Madra turned one in her hand, feeling a soft place near the stem.

“She had not heard?”

“She must have sent them before she left. The carrier brought them this morning.”

Riarhin laid the shawl on the bed. The maid thanked her and folded it without asking where it belonged. Madra put the bruised pear apart and reached for another.

“What will you do with them?”

“Eat them. Give them away. They’re no use as witnesses.”

Riarhin sat on the edge of the bed. From there she could see a pair of shoes which had not been packed. The heels were worn differently, one at the outer edge, the other almost straight across. She remembered Coryd examining one at the celebration, Madra embarrassed after a joke that had ceased to be funny.

“Did the repair hold?”

Madra followed her glance. “Long enough. She offered to do it properly.”

“Would you like me to ask her?”

“No. I can take my shoes to a man who mends shoes. Your sister has done enough work for people who say they enjoy her company.”

The maid closed one side of the chest and tested the latch. Madra told her to leave it open until the letter from home had been put in. Her son’s latest drawing was inside it. The drawing had arrived without an account of what it represented, and her husband had supplied three contradictory explanations. She took it from the table and looked at it before passing it over.

“He’s grown while I’ve been here,” she said.

“Children do.”

“I know what children do.”

Riarhin lowered her eyes. Madra laid another pear on the towel.

“I could have stayed. She would have asked. Even now, if I knew where she was, I could go. I’ve spent the morning thinking about that instead of being glad to see my son.”

The maid bent over the chest. Riarhin could feel her trying not to hear.

“You will be glad when you see him,” Riarhin said.

“Probably.”

Madra picked up the soft pear and cut it with a little knife. The flesh beneath the bruise was brown. She removed it, divided what remained and offered Riarhin half.

It was sweet enough to catch in the throat. Riarhin had expected to refuse food, to give back the shawl and leave Madra to a departure that did not concern her. Now she sat chewing while the other woman looked at the rows of fruit intended for someone who would not eat them.

“She liked being given things,” Madra said. “I forget that when people talk about everything she gave us.”

Riarhin finished the pear. Madra took two sound ones, wrapped them again and laid them on the shawl for Coryd. The rest went into a shallow basket for the household table.

“Tell her thank you for the shoe. I should have said it before.”

“I will.”

Madra stood with difficulty after sitting so long on the floor. Riarhin offered her arm and let her use it. The maid shut the chest. This time the latch caught.

Orven’s account was taken that afternoon.

He had asked for Riarhin to hear it. She did not know why until he began.

“I believed I had to bring anything that might hurt her to her first,” he said. “She told me that when she gave me the keys.”

The clerk asked whether the instruction was written.

“No.”

“Did it say you could prevent a person leaving?”

“Not in those words.”

“What words?”

Orven gave them. They were tender words. A former employer had accused him of stealing money which another man later admitted taking. By then Orven had lost his place and borrowed for Lett’s care; a child had died before the second winter. Jaennis had found work, rooms and a healer who came without demanding payment at the door. When she made Orven her porter, she had asked him to keep the household’s troubles within the household until she could see to them.

“Did you understand that to include a person asking to leave?”

“Yes.”

“Did you ask?”

“No.”

“Why not?”

He looked at Riarhin. She made herself meet his eyes.

“I knew she’d be hurt.”

The clerk waited. Orven went on, with increasing difficulty. On the night of the meeting he had heard Jaennis ask them to finish speaking and found himself at the door. He remembered moving there. He could not remember deciding that he had authority. When Deliane raised her arm he had seen the injury again, the blue-white fingers which had not moved properly, and stepped aside.

“I could have stepped aside before,” he said.

The clerk wrote it down.

Orven was not returned to his former work. He remained under orders to attend the inquiry and not to enter the royal household without an escort. His earned wages were paid to Lett. Whether he would be charged with unlawful confinement as well as the injury was for the hearing. Solenne refused to let the announcement use the word misunderstanding.

He asked whether he could apologise to Deliane.

“You may write,” Solenne said. “She will decide whether to read it.”

“I’d rather speak.”

“She has not asked to see you.”

He looked down at his hands.

Riarhin found Coryd outside with a bag of tools and the wages for her work. One coin was older than the others, worn almost smooth. She turned it in her fingers, then put it in her purse.

“Finished?” Riarhin asked.

“The rail is. The carpenter lent me his plane. I need to take it back.”

“I can come.”

“You can, if you don’t tell him how to run his shop.”

“I have never told a carpenter how to run his shop.”

Coryd looked at her.

“Not recently.”

They went through the afternoon market. A man was selling late apples out of a cart and objecting to every hand that touched them. Coryd bought two before Riarhin could begin inspecting the bruises. The carpenter was busy and barely looked up when the plane came back. He ran his thumb along its edge, nodded and set it in its place.

Outside, Coryd bit into her apple and made a face.

“Bad?”

“Sour.”

“You chose it.”

“I did. You needn’t defend it.”

Riarhin took a bite of hers. It was sour too. They ate them walking back to the lodging.

Chapter 39 A first day of work

On the morning she finally took up residence, Deliane’s chair arrived before her baggage.

The porter had been told to expect a chest, so he directed the bearers towards the service entrance. Deliane leaned out between the curtains and asked him whether he meant to put her on the baggage scales as well.

He looked at her, looked at the chair and went red.

“Front door, then,” he told the men, as if correcting their mistake.

Riarhin came up the steps behind them with Coryd. The morning was cold and clear. Across the street a woman had spread sheets over a hedge, each corner weighted with a stone. The guesthouse windows stood open despite the chill; the cook had burned something while testing the oven and insisted that the smoke came from old fat in the brickwork.

Deliane was helped into her office. She asked them to leave the door open.

“Otherwise I’ll keep having to get up.”

Coryd moved the chair closer to the desk and placed the cushion beneath her injured arm. The swelling had eased. A practitioner had assisted the healing after the bone was set, and the fingers could now move without the same sharp pain, but Deliane was under orders not to use the hand for lifting. She had already discovered nine things in her own room which counted as lifting only when someone saw her do them.

Riarhin carried in the smaller bag. The blue pot was wrapped in a stocking. Beside it lay Jaennis’s grey blanket.

Deliane saw where she was looking.

“I want it.”

“I didn’t ask.”

“You looked as if you might.”

Riarhin set the bag down. “Where?”

“On the shelf. Not that shelf; the bracket is loose.”

Coryd sighed and fetched her tools again.

The first guest arrived while the clerk was explaining the provision account. She was one of the women bringing the southern records, accompanied by a girl who seemed to be her daughter and turned out to be her supervisor. They had quarrelled on the journey and continued quarrelling while the porter carried their bags.

“You said there would be two beds.”

“There are two beds,” Deliane said.

“In different rooms,” the older woman said.

“No,” said the younger. “I said if there were two rooms we should ask. There was no promise.”

The older woman looked at her with a resentment honed by several days in a cart.

Deliane sent them upstairs together, then instructed the porter to take the larger case to the room above the kitchen.

The linen woman, passing with a basket, stopped.

“That room’s wet.”

“The window was mended.”

“The wall’s wet.”

“I was told it was ready.”

“Not by me.”

Deliane’s face grew hot. Riarhin saw her look towards the open office door, as if someone beyond it might tell her whether she was allowed to be angry. Then she looked back at the linen woman.

“Show me.”

They went up together. The wall was wet. The repaired window had stopped the draught but trapped the damp left by the earlier leak. Deliane pressed her good hand against the plaster, drew it away and examined her fingers.

“Then we won’t put them here.”

There was nowhere else without moving someone. They moved Deliane’s own things into the little office for the afternoon while the smallest sleeping room was aired. She would have it that night. The two guests were given the larger room she had intended for herself, with a screen between the beds.

The older woman inspected the arrangement and said that the screen was thin.

“It is,” Deliane said.

“You can hear through it.”

“Yes.”

The younger woman put down her case. “We shall try not to say anything worth hearing.”

For the first time since arriving, she and her companion laughed.

At midday Deliane tried to sign for the bread. She took the pen in her left hand and produced a mark which made the baker’s boy stare.

“That’s my name,” she said.

“Yes, mistress.”

“It looks better usually.”

“Yes, mistress.”

She looked at him. His ears reddened. She laughed, and he escaped with the receipt.

The wine arrived late.

Riarhin heard the porter arguing at the side gate and went to see whether the cart was blocking the street. Two casks stood on the paving. The carter wanted the gate opened so that he could roll them into the passage. The porter kept his boot against its foot.

“Your name isn’t the one I was given.”

“Then put my name down instead.”

“That isn’t the instruction.”

“It isn’t my fault you were given a stupid instruction.”

Deliane came behind Riarhin, holding her sling with the good hand. She asked the carter who had sent him. He named the new supplier. He had been hired because the usual driver was ill. The cart carried the supplier’s mark. So did the seals on the casks.

“He was told to send word if he changed the driver,” Deliane said.

“He sent me.”

“With a note?”

“With wine.”

The cook had come out. She wanted the passage clear before the flour arrived, and said so. The carter said she was welcome to help. The cook called him a name which made the porter look away from Deliane as if trying not to laugh.

Deliane asked him to take the casks back onto the cart while they sent to confirm the delivery. He refused. He had another load waiting, and would not be paid twice for standing in the same street. If she did not want the wine, he would leave it where it stood and tell the supplier why.

“You cannot leave it in the road.”

“It isn’t in the road.”

He had her there. The casks stood on the house’s narrow apron, outside the gate but clear of passing wheels. Deliane looked at them. Riarhin could see her deciding whether a man annoyed about his wages was worth making an enemy over two casks bearing the right seal.

From above them the older guest called through an open window, “Is that wine for us? They’ve been telling me since yesterday I should wait for the new wine.”

The carter spread his hands towards the window.

Deliane sent the kitchen boy to the supplier. Then she asked Riarhin to stay at the gate.

“Can you keep the casks where they are? I can’t have them inside until I know.”

“Yes.”

“Without frightening him into doing something.”

“I can stand here.”

“Thank you.”

The carter looked her over properly for the first time. His eyes stopped at her ears, then at her empty right hip. He would not know the name from that, she thought. There were plenty of half-Lethai in the city now. Then he looked at her face and knew it from something else.

“Oh,” he said.

Riarhin stood beside the closed gate.

“You’re the reason for all this.”

“No. The driver was to be named.”

“Yes. So I’ve been told.”

He climbed onto his cart and began gathering the reins. The casks stayed where they were. Deliane had gone inside to sit down; the porter had followed her to fetch a stool. For a few moments Riarhin was alone with the man.

“My sister worked in the palace,” he said.

She waited.

“Washing. Years ago. I used to take her bread when she had a long day.”

“Is she living?”

He gave her a look of such dislike that she wished she had not asked.

“She is. Did you want to hear she wasn’t?”

“No.”

“You all think everybody has to know a dead person before you need listen.”

He drove away. The cart’s tail clipped the nearer cask hard enough to make it rock. Riarhin steadied it. Behind her the porter returned with the stool.

“Sit if you like.”

She remained standing until the kitchen boy came back.

The supplier confirmed the substitution and the carter’s name. He had sent no note because he had forgotten. Deliane gave the porter permission to admit the wine. The cook said they might all now return to the work for which they were paid.

The older guest sent down to say the wine was too cold.

Deliane put her forehead against the heel of her good hand. Then she laughed, helplessly, and called for someone to move the guest’s jug nearer the fire.

Coryd finished tightening the bracket and refused payment for that last small job. Deliane insisted. They compromised on a meal, provided it was ready before the sisters had to leave. Riarhin sat at the kitchen table while the cook carved something which had survived the oven test rather better than the smoke suggested.

Through the open door she could see the grey blanket folded on Deliane’s chair. The younger woman came in, took a bowl and sat with them. For a few minutes they ate in a peace interrupted only by the porter asking where to put things whose places he had been shown that morning.

At last Deliane said, “Do they know where she is?”

Riarhin shook her head.

“She shouldn’t travel for so long. Her knee gets stiff in a carriage.”

Nobody answered.

Deliane scraped a little food from the edge of her bowl.

“I hope someone remembers the cushion.”

Coryd said, “Someone probably has.”

Deliane nodded. She ate another mouthful. The cook came in to ask whether the new guest’s companion could have broth without paying separately, as the girl had been sick on the road and could not face the meal. Deliane asked how much broth there was. The cook showed her the pot.

“Yes, then. Put it on the room.”

The cook went. Deliane remained seated until she had finished eating.

Chapter 40 The houses in the petition

The harness maker’s book had finally acquired a line in a different ink.

Riarhin found her at the river yard, where Edrune had asked the escort to stop on the way to the office. The company had gone west. Its remaining purchases were being collected by a hired wagon, and the captain’s wife had stayed behind to finish the accounts. She had a place beside the driver for the journey after them. On the ground at her feet stood the old saddle, repaired but not yet covered, its opened seat exposing the new piece fitted against the broken tree.

“You kept it,” Riarhin said.

“It’s a good saddle.”

She crouched and pressed the new wood. It did not move. Then she stood, wiped her hand on her skirt and opened the book.

The accepted loan had been paid that morning. Not all her outstanding wages and work, which had never been part of the claim; the money she and her husband had advanced towards the replacement horses. She showed Riarhin the entry as though displaying a finished piece of stitching. The treasury clerk had counted the coins twice. She had counted them again before leaving.

“Where did they find it?”

“The lease instalment arrived. Or so they told me. I didn’t ask to see the man pay it.”

Riarhin smiled. The woman closed the book and took a purse from inside her coat. She counted out a smaller sum, tied it separately and gave it to the wagoner for a person who was not travelling with them. It was the apprentice’s remaining room money.

“Will he get it?”

“He’d better. The carrier knows his father.”

“Have you heard from him?”

“A line. He says the new place has a window over the river. He can see boats while he works.”

She looked at the open saddle. Without the covering it resembled a body awaiting its skin.

“Does he want to come back?”

“I didn’t ask him.”

The woman bent to lift the saddle. Riarhin helped before thinking. Her right wrist pulled, and she adjusted her grip rather than let the pain make a spectacle of the kindness. Together they set it against the wagon’s sideboard.

“He sent a drawing,” the woman said. “Of something his master uses for cutting straps. Says I ought to have one.”

“Will you?”

“I’ll see the thing before I spend money copying his drawing. He’s left out the joint.”

She laughed, then looked away. A man came for her signature on the load list. She read the whole of it while he waited, checked the bundle of leather against the description and made him add the saddle as her own property. When he complained that she had already told them, she gave him the pen.

Edrune came from the sheds. She knew the woman; they had argued about a delivery in a winter neither wished to describe now. They greeted one another cautiously and then with affection. Edrune asked after the captain. His wife said he had written that the company was in its new quarters, that two of the borrowed horses were better than the ones they had lost, and that he had no intention of telling the dealer until the last payment was settled.

“The patrol?” Riarhin asked.

“Short for another week. One of the men sent on leave hasn’t come back. His mother is dying. The captain let him stay.”

“Will he answer for that?”

“He already has. He asked the deputy to put the refusal in writing.”

Riarhin could imagine the letter. She could also imagine the deputy deciding that a few more days of a shortened patrol were less dangerous than the account of a mother’s death which the captain would send next. It pleased her, and she did not try to make the pleasure into a judgment about whether the road would be safe.

The wagoner called that he was ready. The woman put her book away and climbed to the seat without accepting his hand. Once there she leaned down to embrace Edrune. The two women struck shoulders awkwardly and laughed. Riarhin stepped clear of the wheel.

“Tell the queen we got it,” the harness maker said.

“Write to the office.”

“I will. You can tell her too.”

Riarhin nodded.

“She looked as if she thought we’d never stop being angry.”

“Have you?”

“No. I’m also going home.”

The wagon moved. The woman held the side rail until it had passed the rut by the gate, then raised a hand. Riarhin stood beside Edrune watching the repaired saddle rock gently against the sideboard.

“I’m not putting that in my account,” Edrune said.

“What?”

“The bit about going home. They’ll make it sound as if everyone was satisfied.”

Riarhin looked at her. Edrune shrugged and led the way to the office.

At the door they encountered the younger treasury clerk. He was carrying a bundle of papers to be copied before the next rider left. Riarhin recognised the red thread round the remount enquiry and asked whether the posting office had received the final direction.

“Yesterday.”

“By express?”

“Yes.”

He smiled without pleasure. “We now have a box to mark if waiting for an answer will cost more than sending the rider.”

“Will anyone mark it?”

“I have.”

She let him go. Edrune had already begun arguing about the order in which their business would be heard. Riarhin joined her with the news of the paid loan still in her mind, trying to remember the apprentice’s name so that if Matylde asked about him she would not have to call him merely the boy.

At the final hearing, Edrune kept her travelling coat on.

She had a cart leaving after midday and did not intend to miss it because a clerk required a second signature which could have been obtained before breakfast. She told Riarhin this outside the chamber, then asked her to check whether mud showed on the back of the coat.

“A little.”

“Enough for somebody to think about instead of my account?”

“No.”

“Then leave it.”

The queen heard the northern petitions from the smaller chair. The tall cushioned one had been taken back to Jaennis’s rooms, where it stood among objects nobody wished to decide about yet. Riarhin had seen it through an open door that morning. A servant was dusting the wooden arms with considerable care.

There were fewer people at the table than at the first hearing. Those who had obtained what they came for had gone. Those who remained had thicker bundles of papers and less patience with explanations of how difficult their cases were.

The first family received full release from a restriction entered under the wrong village. The second retained a bond because a later act was alleged and had not been heard. Riarhin knew the name of the house and wanted to speak. The clerk asked whether she had been present for the later act.

“No.”

“Do you have a witness?”

“Not here.”

“Then we will name the hearing at which one may attend.”

She sat down. The man whose family would remain bound looked at her with disappointment which she found harder to bear than anger.

Edrune’s timber release stood. The storage claim would be heard locally under a copy of the queen’s direction, with the condition of the timber recorded by two witnesses before sale. The spoiled loads were not to be valued as firewood merely because the owner needed to dispose of them before further loss. Nor was the keeper automatically liable for every split and wormhole. Someone would have to examine the boards.

Edrune asked who would choose that person.

The clerk named the office.

“Before the next rain?”

He looked at Matylde. The queen asked whether three days was possible. The clerk said yes, if the instruction went today.

“Then send it today.”

Edrune took her copy, checked the seal and the names, and put it inside her coat. When Riarhin looked for her after the hearing she had already gone to find the carter. Her gratitude had taken the form of a small packet left with Coryd: two road loaves, still warm, which she had bought while they waited for the chamber to open.

There was one more account to hear.

The guard’s sister came forward with the glove.

Her name was Neral. Riarhin had asked for it only after the escort attack. She was ashamed of that now. The woman had brought a dead man into the room with all the attention she could give him, and Riarhin had thought of her as the person with the glove.

Neral laid it on the table.

“I gave them what I knew about Cedric,” she said.

Matylde nodded.

“I didn’t know what he meant to do. If I had, I wouldn’t have helped him.”

“That has been recorded.”

“I still want an answer.”

The room was silent.

Neral looked at Riarhin. “You said you didn’t ask your people about him.”

“Yes.”

“Are they alive to ask?”

“Some are.”

“Then will you?”

Riarhin looked at the glove. One fingertip had been darned with thread that did not match. She did not know which hand it had fitted without turning it over, and would not touch it without permission.

“I can send a question. I cannot promise an answer.”

“Send it.”

“Yes.”

The woman turned to the queen.

“I want the deaths heard. Not only the queen and the Marshal. Ours.”

Matylde held her gaze.

“I will have the accounts kept and decide how they can be heard.”

“Will she be made to stay?”

“No. I promised her departure.”

Neral’s face tightened. She looked as if she might say something which would hurt them all, then picked up the glove and folded it once.

“Then don’t tell me this is finished.”

“I won’t,” Matylde said.

After Neral left, a councillor proposed a form of departure instrument acknowledging Riarhin’s recent service. It would make the escort’s duty easier to explain. He had written a phrase about reconciliation which Riarhin saw from where she sat.

Matylde crossed it out.

“She has not been reconciled to me.”

The councillor bowed his head.

“Her recent work can be recorded separately. This is the promise I gave.”

The new document confirmed ten days for Riarhin to reach the specified crossing, longer if weather closed the road and the officer recorded the delay. It protected Coryd and their hired carriers within the same journey. It required the escort to return an account of their departure. It pardoned nothing.

Riarhin listened while it was read. The words had seemed oppressive when she first saw them at the posting house: dates, witnesses, limits, the repeated insistence that she did not travel under absolution. Now she found the provision about the weather and asked the clerk to repeat it, because Coryd would ask her later.

He repeated it.

She signed the receipt for her copy.

Outside, Kerros was waiting with his arm in a sling. He could move the fingers now, though his grip had not returned. He would not ride the escort out. Another officer would take them to the crossing.

“I’m told you insisted on knowing every change of guard,” he said.

“Yes.”

“Keep doing it.”

She nodded.
