For a moment she wanted to offer him her hand again. She remembered the first refusal and spared him having to make it twice.

“Thank you,” she said.

He looked past her towards the yard. “Be ready at the hour.”

The guarantor had paid part of Edrune’s claim by the time Edrune left the office. She counted it on the lower step while the man who had brought it waited for a receipt.

“Is that all?” Riarhin asked.

“No. It is what’s here.”

Edrune wrote the sum carefully. She crossed out the words saying it settled the claim before she signed.

The messenger objected. She handed him the money back. He considered it, put it on the step again and took the altered receipt.

Tessart’s lease interests had not vanished with his influence at court. His agents still wanted payment for work actually done. The new hearing would have to distinguish that work from charges created by keeping the goods where they could not be sold. Edrune put the coin in her purse and asked Riarhin whether she had ever tried to sell a cart whose buyer had just learned the seller needed to leave.

“No.”

“I’m about to. Come and look as if I don’t.”

Riarhin went with her as far as the yard gate. Edrune’s buyer saw the escort, looked at Riarhin, and asked to speak to Edrune alone.

She waved Riarhin away without turning. The price she named carried across the yard. Riarhin smiled and returned to the waiting officer.

Margery was in the lower garden when Riarhin came down from the yard. She had brought a travelling basket and set it beside her on the bench. Nothing in it appeared to be packed for a long journey: a shawl, a book, a wrapped loaf.

“Are you going home?” Riarhin asked.

“Tomorrow. For a few days.”

“Does Matylde know?”

“She asked whether I would be here when she woke. I said yes.”

Riarhin sat when Margery moved the basket. Above the garden wall, somebody was testing a trumpet. The same three notes sounded twice. On the third attempt only two came out, followed by a distant laugh.

Margery had a letter in her hand. She folded it without putting it away.

“Do you know where to send it?” Riarhin asked.

“No.”

They listened to the fourth attempt. Margery rubbed her forehead.

“I have written that Mattie misses her. I don’t think I ought to send that.”

“Have you asked Mattie?”

“No.”

She unfolded the sheet, looked at it and folded it again.

“I miss her,” she said. “That I could send.”

Riarhin nodded.

“Don’t look so kind. I am still angry with you some mornings.”

“I know.”

“No, you don’t. You aren’t there. I get up and for a moment I think I can go to her room. Then I remember why I can’t. And I remember that I asked you.”

Riarhin looked at the basket. The book had been wrapped against rain, though the sky was clear.

“I could give you back the letter,” she said.

“I haven’t asked for it.”

“No.”

Margery laid her hand on Riarhin’s wrist. The touch was familiar enough to hurt before Riarhin understood why.

“You must go when they release you. Don’t stay because I am unhappy.”

Riarhin turned her hand beneath Margery’s and held it for a moment.

When Margery rose, she took the unfinished letter with her. The basket remained on the bench. She was returning to the palace for the night.

Chapter 41 The hour with nobody in it

Isbray had a toothache.

Matylde received the message as if it were a political reversal. She read it, put it down, picked it up again and asked whether the messenger had actually seen her. The messenger said that he had, and that Lady Isbray had been holding a cloth against her cheek and in a temper with everybody.

“Then tell her I am sorry.”

He bowed.

“And don’t tell her I asked whether you had seen.”

He bowed again with greater care.

Margery was asleep. A woman who had tried to wake her for the queen’s earlier message had been told to take the message away until the world had improved. Matylde had laughed when she heard that. Now she looked towards the window and did not seem able to find anything else in the room which she wanted.

Riarhin had been summoned for a final audience. She had brought no gift. She had considered one for too long and disliked everything she considered.

On the table lay the little wooden rings from the game. Matylde picked one up, fitted it over a finger and rolled it down to the knuckle. It stuck. She pulled until it came free.

“Jaennis would have had somebody here.”

“Yes.”

“Somebody I liked.”

“Yes.”

“You needn’t sound so pleased with how difficult it is.”

Riarhin looked at her. “I’m not pleased.”

Matylde set down the ring. “I know.”

They sat through a silence which nobody hurried to mend. Outside, two gardeners were carrying a tub. One walked backwards. He kept stopping to look behind him, and the other kept objecting because each stop put the weight into his hands.

The queen watched them negotiate the corner.

“I don’t remember his hands properly,” she said.

Riarhin waited.

“My father’s. I remember that I could put both mine round one finger, but I don’t know whether I remember it or someone told me.”

Riarhin could see him at the inspection, Matylde against his shoulder while a woman explained what the requisition of finished cloth would cost her. She had stood there wearing another woman’s face. Every particular came back: the different shade of the sleeve under discussion, the child’s hand catching the fastening at Tylan’s collar, his turning towards the woman when an officer tried to answer for her.

“I saw him give you a finger to hold,” she said.

Matylde looked round.

“When?”

“At a cloth inspection. You were reaching for his collar. He freed your hand and gave you his finger instead.”

“Did I take it?”

“Yes.”

“What did he do?”

“Kept asking about the cloth.”

The girl gave a little laugh. It surprised her enough that she put a hand to her mouth.

“Everyone says he did that.”

“What?”

“Kept asking. Until people wished they’d never brought it up.”

Riarhin smiled before she could stop herself. Matylde saw it.

“Were you yourself?” the queen asked.

“No.”

Matylde looked down at the rings.

Riarhin could have apologised. She had apologised for other things in these rooms and discovered how readily an apology asked to be received. She waited.

“You watched him holding me,” Matylde said.

“Yes.”

“And later.”

“Yes.”

The queen pressed one ring flat against the table with her palm. Riarhin heard the wood creak.

“I keep thinking there must be a part you hadn’t understood.”

Riarhin’s throat tightened.

“I understood that he loved you.”

Matylde turned her face towards the window. Riarhin looked at the garden rather than at her. The men had got the tub around the corner. One was rubbing his wrist while the other stood looking at the empty space where it had been.

After a while Matylde said, “Will you answer if I write?”

“Yes.”

“Even if I ask the same things?”

“Yes.”

“That doesn’t mean I want you here.”

“I know.”

The girl rubbed her palm. The ring had left a red circle on it.

“I want her to know what I did today,” she said. “I want to tell her about the timber woman and the way she made the clerk say the date. I know how she would laugh. Then I think that if I wrote, she would know I wanted her, and she would come back as if none of it had happened.”

Riarhin could not think of an answer that was not an instruction disguised as comfort.

Matylde looked at her. “You don’t have to say anything.”

Riarhin nodded.

The queen gathered the rings and put them in their little bag. Then she took them out again.

“Ask Solenne whether the girl from the records office is still here. The one who beat Isbray before she remembered she ought to lose.”

Matylde rang the bell. When the woman came, she gave the request and added that the girl need not attend if she had already gone off duty. The woman asked whether the queen wished to send for somebody else in that case.

Matylde hesitated.

“No. Come back and tell me.”

The woman left.

Riarhin rose when dismissed. At the door she stopped. Matylde sat alone with the rings spread before her.

Matylde looked up.

“Go,” she said, without anger. “You said you would.”

Riarhin went.

Chapter 42 The road allowed

Coryd had packed badly on purpose.

That was Riarhin’s first conclusion when she saw the bundles. The second was that her sister had packed for herself and Riarhin did not like the arrangement. One roll was thicker than it needed to be. The tools had been wrapped together in a way that would make reaching the smallest one troublesome. The food was divided between two bags rather than kept where a single rider could guard it.

Riarhin stood over them, moving nothing.

Coryd came in with a letter.

“It’s from Harissa.”

“Another?”

“She had more to say.”

“Are they well?”

“Read it.”

Riarhin took the sheet. Harissa wanted a particular kind of green thread which she had been unable to find at home. A woman had promised that the merchant in the northern market at Valora sold it, but the woman had also promised that a certain dye would not run, so Harissa would prefer Riarhin to inspect it before buying. Linias required a small replacement fitting for an instrument and had enclosed a drawing so precise that the lack of any measure seemed almost malicious. Zemia had taken work for a month in a place whose name was written plainly. She would be there if they wished to write.

At the bottom Harissa had added that they were not to come back without the thread and then, in a different ink, that of course they were to come back without it if obtaining it proved troublesome.

Riarhin read the last addition aloud.

“She knows you,” Coryd said.

“Not well enough if she expects me to understand this drawing.”

“That’s Linias.”

“I know it’s Linias. What size is it?”

Coryd looked. “He has drawn his thumb beside it.”

“I don’t have his thumb.”

Sivelle came before the escort.

She had been to the baker and brought more food than they needed. Coryd took the parcel, put it with the others and kissed her before asking whether she had walked. She had. It was a fine morning. Her house was full of workmen and she had no desire to spend another hour being told how much easier everything would be if she had bought a different house.

“They have begun the stair?” Coryd asked.

“They have taken it away. I must believe that is a beginning.”

Riarhin took Linias’s drawing downstairs. The cook asked whether she had found out how large the fitting should be. She said no. They stood looking at the carefully drawn thumb until the cook said she had better wash the breakfast things.

Upstairs, something fell. Sivelle laughed. Then the voices became quiet.

Riarhin sat in the yard, where the morning sun had reached the wall. She could hear a cart being loaded in the next street. A man called for someone to lift their end, and another asked which end he meant. She folded the drawing along its old creases and opened it again.

Coryd came down alone.

“She’s saying goodbye to the cook.”

“Does she know you’re leaving?”

Coryd looked at her.

“I mean—”

“Yes. She has seen the bundles.”

Riarhin put the drawing away. “That was a foolish question.”

“She asked me to stay.”

There was room on the bench. Riarhin moved a little. Coryd sat without quite settling her weight.

“Did you want to?”

“For a moment.”

“For longer?”

“I don’t know.”

They listened to the men with the cart. Somebody had dropped whatever they were carrying. Now both agreed on where it should have been held.

“I would have waited,” Riarhin said.

Coryd rubbed her hands together. “I know. I don’t want to decide because you’re leaving or because she asks. I want to get as far as the first place where I can think about something else.”

“All right.”

“Don’t say it like that.”

“Like what?”

“As if that’s the decision you were waiting for.”

Riarhin looked down at the paving.

Coryd leaned against her shoulder, briefly. “I shall write to her.”

“Do.”

“If she has a stair to go up when the letter comes.”

Sivelle emerged from the kitchen with the cook behind her. They had apparently settled a dispute about the best way to keep pears. The cook looked pleased with the result; Sivelle looked like a woman who had been allowed to leave with her original opinion if she did not state it again.

“I’ll send word when they’ve built it,” she told Coryd. “Then you can find a new excuse.”

Coryd stood and went to her. Riarhin remained on the bench. The two women held each other without speaking. When they parted, Sivelle rubbed at a wet place below her eye and blamed the sun.

“It is very fierce in this yard,” Coryd said.

“Oh, shut up.”

She kissed her again. Then she took her empty basket from the kitchen step and went out through the gate. Coryd watched the latch fall. She stayed there until the escort’s horses sounded in the street.

At the palace store, the custodian brought Remorse wrapped in the cloth in which Riarhin had returned it. He had recorded the temporary permission from the night of the meeting on a separate line. Beneath it was the new departure authority. She checked both while he waited.

The sword came free of the cloth with its familiar weight. She examined the mouth of the scabbard, the tie, the edge near the point. There was a small mark on the guard where she had trapped the household man’s arm against the doorpost. She rubbed it with her thumb and left it.

The custodian asked her to sign.

Outside, Kerros stood beside the departure officer. His injured arm remained supported, but he had dressed for duty. He read the names against the list: Riarhin, Coryd, the carter carrying their heavier baggage, four escort men, the officer. He asked whether anyone else claimed the protection.

“No,” Riarhin said.

He gave her the folded instrument.

“The copy at the crossing has gone ahead.”

“Thank you.”

He stepped back.

A porter new to the outer gate stopped the cart because one parcel had not been entered on the baggage list. It was the food from Edrune, which Coryd had wrapped with the kitchen’s bread. The porter asked whom he should consult.

“The departure officer,” Kerros said.

The officer inspected the bread, amended the list and gave the parcel back. It took less than a minute. Riarhin watched the porter make his mark in the book before opening the gate.

They stopped in the northern market for the thread. The escort officer objected until Riarhin showed him that the approved route passed through it and that the delay could be recorded. He gave them ten minutes with an expression which suggested he would have preferred another attack to a dispute about shopping.

The merchant had three greens. None matched Harissa’s description. Coryd chose two. Riarhin asked about the dye. The merchant looked insulted, then wet a length and rubbed it against a white rag. The rag remained white. Riarhin paid.

The fitting took longer. The man who sold such things looked at the drawing, laughed and asked whether the musician had lost the last one in a tavern. Riarhin said she did not know. He found something near enough in a little box and told her how to file it if it proved too broad.

They left the city behind schedule but within the officer’s patience.

The fields were being turned. A team moved across the dark soil at a steady walking pace, the man at the plough looking back each time the blade caught. A child followed with a sack, collecting stones. Near a drainage channel, two women had set a pot over a little sheltered fire. One called to the ploughman; he pretended not to hear until she called again.

Riarhin settled in the saddle.

At the first long rise Coryd asked where she meant to stop that night.

“The posting house by the split elm, if the road is dry.”

“It was wet coming down.”

“Then the place before it.”

“With the bad soup?”

“You ate two bowls.”

“I was hungry enough for two bowls of bad soup.”

Riarhin smiled. Her shoulder hurt. The place beneath her belt where the sword rested had become familiar again. In the bag behind her were the green threads, the fitting, Lyra’s note and the queen’s promise.

Hooves sounded behind them. The escort moved aside to let three royal messengers pass, their horses fresher and their business elsewhere. One held his dispatch case against his thigh to keep it from striking the saddle. Another was eating an apple and steering with the reins caught between two fingers.

They passed without stopping.

Coryd watched them diminish along the road.

“I could eat,” she said.

Riarhin reached back for the nearer of the two bags.

Coda A place kept

By the time Jaennis reached the house, she had stopped asking how far remained.

At first she had asked at every change of horses. Then she had learned to distinguish answers meant to comfort her from answers intended to get her into the carriage. Neither gave the distance she needed: how long until she could straighten her leg, how long until the next room in which she must decide whether the person offering help could be trusted with her name.

The southern road had taken days. After it there had been a boat, a wait beside water which smelled of tar and rotting weed, another road among hills where the light lay differently on the houses. She had sent letters from two places and regretted the second before the carrier left. There was no way to call him back without explaining why.

On the boat, she had shared a bench with a woman taking her daughter to a new household. The girl was old enough to be in service and young enough to believe she could begin by concealing how frightened she was. She kept asking about the hours. Her mother answered questions about the wages instead.

Jaennis listened until the woman took out the employment letter. The paper had been folded so often that one line was nearly unreadable. She asked whether she might see it.

The woman hesitated, then surrendered it.

There was a lodging deduction which would consume more of the wages than either appeared to understand. Jaennis asked whether they had allowed for it. The mother said the room came with the place. Jaennis showed her the clause.

The woman read slowly. Her face darkened.

“They told us—”

“They may have meant that the room is reserved for her.”

“They said she wouldn’t have to find one.”

“She won’t.”

The girl stared at the water beside the boat. Her shoulders had risen almost to her ears.

Jaennis asked where the household was. She knew the district, though not the family. At the next landing she sent a boy for paper and wrote a note asking the terms to be confirmed before the girl entered service. She did not use her full name. She named a trading house whose factor would recognise the style of the request and ask questions before ignoring it.

The mother thanked her with a gratitude which made the girl look resentfully from one to the other. Jaennis folded the note and gave it to the daughter.

“You should keep this yourself.”

“Yes, my lady.”

The title came easily, even without the name. Jaennis felt a painful warmth in her chest. She told the girl how to present the note, whom to ask for and what to do if the answer seemed different from the letter. The girl listened closely. When they reached the next landing, she helped Jaennis rise and fetched the cushion before anybody else thought of it.

Jaennis watched them go. She wanted to ask for an address at which the girl might write and tell her what happened. She had none to give. By the time she thought of sending word through the factor, the mother and daughter had disappeared into the crowd.

That night, at the inn, she found the unfinished letter to Matylde beneath the clean linen in her case.

She had begun with the girl’s cold hands. It seemed a poor beginning now, full of a tenderness which could be read as a claim. She tried beginning with the council’s order. That was worse. After two lines she was arguing with a person who would not be there to answer.

She put the unfinished letter back.

The maid who brought hot water asked whether there was anything else she required. Jaennis almost said that there must be. There had always been another person to send for, another account to settle before bed. Instead she asked the girl to leave the water close enough that she could reach it without rising. The maid did so and went away.

For a long time Jaennis listened to the sounds beyond the wall. She knew none of the voices.

Aluen’s maid had gone home. The driver who replaced hers had been paid and dismissed. At the last inn, a woman had offered to accompany her the remaining distance, and Jaennis had accepted because her knee had swollen until she could no longer climb into a vehicle unaided.

The woman did not ask about the news from Viterra. She knew it. Jaennis could see that in the care with which she avoided names. When they stopped for food, she set the bowl where Jaennis could reach it without leaning across the painful leg. When Jaennis offered to pay, she said the meal had already been arranged.

“By whom?”

“Someone who keeps a place here.”

Jaennis had heard versions of that answer in half a dozen houses over the years. Sometimes it meant charity. Sometimes a patroness who disliked receiving thanks before witnesses. Sometimes the person at the centre of a household whose needs everyone had learned to anticipate.

She asked no further question then.

The house stood behind a low wall, its upper windows open to the evening. A tree grew close enough that one branch had been tied back from the plaster. Somebody had wrapped the cord where it touched the bark. The garden smelled of watered earth.

At the gate, Jaennis tried to rise before the woman could help her. Pain went through the knee and made her sit again with a small, angry sound.

“There is no hurry.”

“There is always somebody waiting.”

“Yes,” the woman said. “But not impatiently.”

Jaennis let herself be helped down.

Inside, the passage was wide enough for them to walk beside each other. No one stared. A child carrying a folded cloth paused, looked at Jaennis with frank curiosity and was called gently into another room. Somewhere a pot lid rattled. There was a smell of bread cooling, of herbs rubbed between hands.

The chair had been raised.

Jaennis saw it before she saw the woman standing beside it. A block beneath each leg, fitted and stained to match; a cushion firm enough not to swallow her; a little stool placed at the distance her foot would need. Beside it stood a cup with a broad handle and a bowl covered to keep the food warm.

Her companion left them. Jaennis looked towards the door, then back at the woman by the chair.

“Who told you?”

The woman held out a hand.

“Sit first.”

Jaennis did. The relief was so great that she shut her eyes. Her foot settled on the stool. The woman adjusted it a little lower without being asked.

For a while Jaennis listened to the house. There were several people beyond the room, each occupied with something ordinary. She could hear a young man’s voice trying to explain a mistake and another voice making him begin again from the part he had skipped. A child laughed. A door closed softly.

The woman took the cover off the bowl.

“You have eaten poorly.”

“I have eaten what was there.”

“I know.”

Jaennis looked at her then.

The face was not familiar. Later she would be unable to say why this had seemed surprising. It was a face she ought to have been able to place: patient, attentive, with none of the expectant strain of a person deciding how an honoured guest wished to be treated. The woman knew what to do with the cup, the folded cloth, the bowl. She did not wait for Jaennis to teach her.

“I was expected?”

“Yes.”

“For how long?”

The woman smiled a little. “Long enough to have the chair ready.”

Jaennis’s eyes stung. She looked down at her hands, at the fine tremor in the fingers which had grown worse on the road.

“I have been very foolish.”

“You have come a long way.”

“That is not an answer.”

“No.”

For the first time in days Jaennis almost laughed.

The woman sat beside her, near enough to lift the bowl if she needed help and far enough not to insist on it. The food was soft, well seasoned, neither the condescending pap offered to the old nor the rich meal a nervous host might provide. Jaennis ate several mouthfuls before discovering how hungry she was.

“I don’t know where to go,” she said.

“You may stay here.”

“For how long?”

“As long as you take the place offered.”

Jaennis set down the spoon.

There it was. She ought to have been relieved to hear it. A place had terms; terms could be learned. She had spent her life finding room for people, keeping them fed, remembering what hurt them and who had caused it.

“What place?”

The woman turned her hand palm upwards on the arm of the chair. Jaennis watched a fine line of light move along the skin, though no lamp had been shifted. Outside the window a thread stretched from the tied branch to the sill. It had not been there when she entered. Or she had not seen it.

“Inaea has need of a Champion.”

The house continued around them. A bowl was set down in the next room. The young man stopped explaining and asked a question instead. Jaennis could hear the answer beginning.

She looked at the woman’s offered hand.

“Will I be useful?”

“Yes.”

“Will I be permitted to rest?”

“Now.”

Jaennis closed her eyes again. Matylde’s face came to her, wet and furious, the child’s hands becoming unfamiliar as they let hers go. She wanted to reach for the memory and arrange it differently. She had been trying to do so all the way down the road.

When she opened her eyes, the hand was still waiting.

“There is a woman upstairs,” her host said. “Tomorrow she would like someone to sit with her. Her son has left, and she cannot stop listening for him.”

Jaennis nodded before she had decided to answer.

“Tomorrow,” the woman repeated.

Jaennis put her hand into the offered one. Warmth went through the stiff fingers. She let it come.

Her host moved the bowl a little closer and lifted the cover from the bread.
